It should be understood that all drawings reproduced here are in pen and ink unless otherwise stated.

--- Page 25 ---
## XI.

### *The notes on Sculpture.*

Compared with the mass of manuscript treating of Painting, a very small number of passages bearing on the practice and methods of Sculpture are to be found scattered through the notebooks; these are given here at the beginning of this section (Nos. 706—709). There is less cause for surprise at finding that the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza is only incidentally spoken of; for, although Leonardo must have worked at it for a long succession of years, it is not in the nature of the case that it could have given rise to much writing. We may therefore regard it as particularly fortunate that no fewer than thirteen notes in the master's handwriting can be brought together, which seem to throw light on the mysterious history of this famous work. Until now, writers on Leonardo were acquainted only with the passages numbered 712, 719, 720, 722, and 723.

In arranging these notes on sculpture, I have given precedence to those which treat of the casting of the monument, not merely because they are the fullest, but more especially with a view to reconstructing the monument—an achievement which really almost lies within our reach by combining and comparing the whole of the materials now brought to light, both in notes and in sketches.

A good deal of the first two passages, Nos. 710 and 711, which refer to this subject, seems obscure and incomprehensible; still, they supplement each other, and one contributes in no small degree to the understanding of the other. A very interesting and instructive commentary on these passages may be found in the fourth chapter of *Vasari's*

--- Page 26 ---
2
THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

*Introduction to Sculpture* under the title "How models are made for creating large and small figures in bronze, and how the molds for casting them are prepared; how they are reinforced with iron supports, and how they are cast in metal," etc. Among the drawings of models for the casting molds, we find only one that seems to represent the horse in the act of galloping—No. 713. All the other designs show the horse pacing quietly; and since these studies of the horse are accompanied by extensive notes regarding the method of casting, the question of the horse's position in the finally selected model seems to be decided by the weight of the evidence. "The Sforza horse"—C. Boito remarks very appropriately in the *Saggio* on page 26, "must have looked like a brother to the Colleoni horse. And one might say that the latter was the son of the Gattamelata horse, which itself seems to be the son of one of the four horses that perhaps once stood on the Arch of Nero in Rome" (now in Venice). The publication of the *Saggio* also contains a reproduction of a red chalk drawing representing a horse walking to the left and supported by scaffolding, shown here on Plate LXXVI, No. 1. It remains uncertain whether this represents the model as it stood during the preparations for casting, or whether—as seems highly improbable to me—this sketch shows the model as it was exhibited in 1493 in the Piazza del Castello in Milan under a triumphal arch, on the occasion of the marriage of Emperor Maximilian to Bianca Maria Sforza. The only important point here is to prove that strong evidence suggests that, of the numerous studies for the equestrian statue, only those representing the horse **pacing** agree with the final plans.

The second group of preparatory sketches, representing the horse galloping, must therefore be considered separately—a distinction that seems justified by the note under No. 720 when reviewing the history of the monument's origin.

Galeazzo Maria Sforza was assassinated in 1476 before his plan to erect a monument to his father, Francesco Sforza, could be carried out. In the following year, Lodovico il Moro, the young claimant to the throne, was exiled to Pisa; he only returned to Milan in 1479, becoming the Governor (*Governatore*) of the State of Milan in 1480 after the minister Cecco Simonetta had been murdered. It may have been shortly after this that Lodovico il Moro announced a competition for an equestrian statue. It is fairly certain that Antonio del Pollajuolo took part in it, based on this passage from Vasari's *Life of this artist*: "And there was found, after his death, the design and model he had made for Lodovico Sforza for the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza, Duke of Milan; this design is in our Book, in two versions: in one, he has Verona beneath him; in the other, fully armed and upon a pedestal covered with battle scenes, he makes the horse leap upon an armed man; but I have not been able to discover the reason why he did not carry these designs into execution." One of Pollajuolo's drawings, as described here, was recently discovered by Senator Giovanni Morelli in the Munich *Pinacothek*. In this drawing, the profile of the rider is a portrait of Francesco, Duke of Milan, and beneath the horse, which is galloping to the left, we see a warrior who has been thrown and is lying on the ground; this is precisely the same idea found...

--- Page 27 ---
Galeazzo Maria Sforza, who was murdered in 1476, is said to have planned a monument to his father, Francesco, long before. On November 27, 1473, he wrote to the Commissioner of Works at the Castle of Pavia, requesting him to send the masters who had worked on the statue of the "Regisole" (an ancient equestrian monument in Pavia) to Milan, so that they might undertake the monument of his father. After the death of Galeazzo, his brother, Ludovico il Moro, took up the project. We learn from a letter dated June 8, 1489, from the Florentine ambassador in Milan to Lorenzo de' Medici, that Ludovico had asked him to send two masters from Florence who were capable of carrying out the work; for, although he had already given the commission to Leonardo da Vinci, he did not feel confident that the artist was capable of completing it.

The date of this letter is very important, as it shows that Leonardo had already been working on the monument for some time. It is also clear that he had not yet made much progress, or at least had not produced anything that satisfied his patron. This is not surprising when we consider the immense difficulties of the task and Leonardo's own habit of slow and perfectionist work. It was not until 1493 that the clay model of the horse was finally exhibited to the public. This model, which was of colossal size—about twenty-six feet high—excited the greatest admiration. But the bronze for casting it was never provided; it was diverted to the manufacture of cannons for the war against the French. The model itself remained in Milan until the city was taken by the French in 1499, when it was used as a target by Gascon archers and eventually destroyed. Thus, one of the greatest works of the Renaissance was lost to posterity, leaving only Leonardo's numerous drawings and notes to give us some idea of its intended grandeur.

--- Page 29 ---
3
THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

In some of Leonardo's designs for the monument, such as on Plates LXVI, LXVII, LXVIII, LXIX, and LXXII No. 1, and since it is impossible to explain this remarkable coincidence by assuming that either artist borrowed it from the other, we can only conclude that in the terms of the competition, the proposed subject was the Duke on a horse at full gallop, with a fallen enemy under its hooves.

Leonardo may have entered the competition at that time, but the means for executing the monument do not seem to have been immediately available. It was perhaps not until some years later that Leonardo, in a letter to the Duke (No. 719), reminded him of the project for the monument. Then, after he had obeyed a summons to Milan, the plan seems to have been modified to such an extent—perhaps as a result of a protest by the artist—that a pacing horse was substituted for a galloping one; it may have been at the same time that the colossal dimensions of the statue were first decided upon. The designs shown on Plates LXX, LXXI, LXXII, 2 and 3, LXXIII, and LIV, and on pages 4 and 24, as well as three sketches on Plate LXIX, may be studied in relation to the project in its new form, though it is hardly possible to believe that in any of these we see the design as it was actually carried out. It is probable that in Milan, Leonardo worked less on drawings than on making small models of wax and clay as preparation for his larger model. Among the drawings listed above, one in black chalk on Plate LXXIII—the upper sketch on the right-hand side—strongly reminds us of the ancient statue of Marcus Aurelius. If, as it would seem, Leonardo had not visited Rome until then, he might easily have known of this statue from drawings by his former master and friend Verrocchio, for Verrocchio had been in Rome for a long time between 1470 and 1480. In 1473, Pope Sixtus IV had this ancient equestrian statue restored and placed on a new pedestal in front of the church of San Giovanni in Laterano. Although Leonardo was painting independently as early as 1472, he is still described as working in Verrocchio's studio in 1477. Two years later, the Venetian senate decided to erect an equestrian statue to Colleoni; and since Verrocchio, to whom the work was entrusted, did not move from Florence to Venice immediately—where he died in 1488 before the casting was completed—but instead remained in Florence for several years, perhaps even until 1485, Leonardo probably had the opportunity to see all his designs for the equestrian statue in Venice, and the red chalk drawing on Plate LXXIV may be a recollection of it.

The pen and ink drawing on Plate LXXII, No. 3, reminds us of Donatello's statue of Gattamelata at Padua. However, it does not appear that Leonardo was ever in Padua before 1499, but we may conclude that he took a special interest in this early bronze statue and the reports he could obtain of it from an incidental remark found in Codex Atlanticus 145a; 432a, which will be provided in Volume II under Ricordi or Memoranda.

--- Page 30 ---
4
THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

Among the studies—in the broadest sense of the word—made in preparation for this statue, we may include the Anatomy of the Horse, which both Lomazzo and Vasari mention; the most important parts of this work still exist in the Queen’s Library at Windsor. It was undoubtedly compiled by Leonardo while he was in Milan; only a few interesting records found among these designs are reproduced in Nos. 716 and 717; however, it must be pointed out that out of forty sheets of studies of the horse's movements belonging to 그hat treatise, a horse at a full gallop appears only once.

--- Page 33 ---
5
THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

If we may trust the account given by *Paulus Jovius*—around 1527—*Leonardo's horse* was represented as “vehemently spurred on and panting.” *Jovius* had probably seen the model exhibited at *Milan*; but must we, in fact, infer from this description that the horse was galloping? Compare *Vasari's* description of the *Gattamelata* monument at *Padua*: “He [*Donatello*] went there very willingly and made the bronze horse that is in the Piazza di Sant'Antonio, in which are shown the huffing and the snorting of the horse, and the great spirit and pride expressed most vividly by art in the figure that rides it.”

These descriptions, it seems to me, would only serve to mark the difference between the work of the Middle Ages and that of the Renaissance.

We learn from a statement by *Sabbà da Castiglione* that when *Milan* was taken by the *French* in 1499, the model sustained some injury; and this informant, who, however, is not always trustworthy, adds that *Leonardo* had devoted fully sixteen years to this work (“the form of the horse, upon which *Leonardo* had spent sixteen continuous years”). This often-quoted passage has provided the basis for an assumption, which has no other evidence to support it, that *Leonardo* had lived in *Milan* ever since 1483. But I believe it is closer to the truth to suppose that this author’s statement refers to the fact that about sixteen years must have passed since the competition in which *Leonardo* had taken part.

In these remarks, I must confine myself strictly to the task at hand and give no more of the history of the Sforza monument than is needed to explain the texts and drawings I have been able to reproduce. In the first place, regarding the drawings, I may observe that they are all, with the following two exceptions, in the Queen’s Library at *Windsor Castle*; the red chalk drawing on *Pl. LXXVI No. 1* is in the *MS. C. A.* (see *No. 712*) and the fragmentary pen and ink drawing on page 4 is in the *Ambrosian Library*. The drawings from *Windsor* on *Pl. LXVI* have undergone a slight reduction from the size of the originals.

There can no longer be the slightest doubt that the well-known engraving of several horsemen (*Passavant, Le Peintre-Graveur, Vol. V, p. 181, No. 3*) is only a copy after original drawings by *Leonardo*, executed by some unknown engraver; we have only to compare the engraving with the facsimiles of drawings on *Pl. LXV, No. 2, Pl. LXVII, LXVIII and LXIX* which, it is quite evident, served as models for the engraver.

On *Pl. LXV No. 1*, in the larger sketch to the right, only the base is distinctly visible; the figure of the horseman is erased. *Leonardo* evidently found it unsatisfactory and therefore rubbed it out.

The base of the monument—the pedestal for the equestrian statue—is repeatedly sketched on a magnificent scale. In the sketch just mentioned, it has the character of a shrine or aedicula intended to contain a sarcophagus. Captives in chains are represented here on the entablature with their backs turned to that portion of the monument which more

--- Page 34 ---
6
THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

...strictly constitutes the pedestal of the horse. The lower portion of the aedicula is surrounded by columns. In the pen and ink drawing on Plate LXVI—the lower drawing on the right-hand side—the sarcophagus is shown between the columns, and above the entablature is a plinth on which the horse stands. But this arrangement perhaps seemed to Leonardo to lack solidity, and in the little sketch on the lower left-hand side, the sarcophagus is shown lying under an arched canopy. In this version, the trophies and the captive warriors are detached from the corners. In the first of these two sketches, the place for the trophies is merely indicated by a few strokes; in the third sketch on the left, the base is considerably broader, with buttresses and pinnacles added to form three niches. The black chalk drawing on Plate LXVIII shows a base in which the corners are formed by niches with pilasters. In the little sketch on the extreme left of Plate LXV, No. 1, the equestrian statue serves to crown a circular temple somewhat resembling Bramante’s tempietto of San Pietro in Montorio at Rome, while the sketch above to the right displays an arrangement faintly reminding us of the tomb of the Scaligers in Verona. The base is thus constructed of two platforms or slabs, the upper one considerably smaller than the lower one, which is supported on flying buttresses with pinnacles.

On looking over the numerous studies in which the horse is not galloping but merely walking forward, we find only one drawing for the pedestal, and this, to accord with the altered character of the statue, is quieter and simpler in style (Plate LXXIV). It rises almost vertically from the ground and is exactly as long as the pacing horse. The whole base is here arranged either as an independent baldaquin or else as a projecting canopy over a recess in which the figure of the deceased Duke is seen lying on his sarcophagus; in the latter case, it was probably intended as a tomb inside a church. Here, too, it was intended to fill the corners with trophies or captive warriors. Probably only No. 724 in the text refers to the work for the base of the monument.

If we compare the last-mentioned sketch with the description of a plan for an equestrian monument to Gian Giacomo Trivulzio (No. 725), it seems by no means impossible that this drawing is a preparatory study for the very monument concerning which the manuscript gives us detailed information. We have no historical record regarding this sketch, nor do the archives in the Trivulzio Palace give us any information. The simple monument to the great general in San Nazaro Maggiore in Milan consists merely of a sarcophagus placed in a recess high on the wall of an octagonal chapel. The figure of the warrior is lying on the sarcophagus, on which his name is inscribed; this is a piece of sculpture which is certainly not Leonardo’s work. Gian Giacomo Trivulzio died at Chartres in 1518, only five months before Leonardo, and it seems to me highly improbable that this should have been the date of this sketch; under these circumstances, it would have been done under the auspices of Francis I, but the Italian general was certainly not in favor with the French monarch at the time. Gian Giacomo Trivulzio was a sworn foe to Ludovico il Moro, whom he strove for years to overthrow. On the 6th of September 1499, he marched victorious into Milan at the head...

<meta>This page is a scholarly analysis from a 19th-century text, likely Jean Paul Richter’s "The Literary Works of Leonardo da Vinci." It discusses the historical context and artistic commissions of Gian Giacomo Trivulzio, a rival to the Sforza family in Milan, and how these events relate to Leonardo da Vinci’s designs for equestrian monuments.</meta>

### THE TRIVULZIO MONUMENT.

At the head of a French army, Gian Giacomo Trivulzio soon forced the French forces out of the city. In a short time, however, he was forced to leave *Milan* again when *Ludovico il Moro* <note>Ludovico Sforza, the Duke of Milan and Leonardo's former patron.</note> attacked the city with a force of *Swiss* troops. On the 15th of April following, after defeating *Ludovico* at *Novara*, *Trivulzio* once more entered *Milan* as a conqueror, but his hopes of becoming *Governor* <note>original: Governatore</note> of the city were soon wrecked by political intrigue. This victory and triumph, historians tell us, were marked by acts of vengeance against the dethroned *Sforza*, and it might have been particularly satisfying to Trivulzio that the casting and construction of the *Sforza* monument were suspended at that time.

It must have been at this moment—as it seems to me—that he commissioned the artist [Leonardo] to prepare designs for his own monument, which he probably intended should be placed in the Cathedral or in some other church. As the husband of *Margherita di Nicolino Colleoni*, he would have felt he had a claim to the same distinction and public tribute that his less famous relative <note>Bartolomeo Colleoni, whose famous equestrian statue by Verrocchio stands in Venice.</note> had received from the Venetian republic. It was at this very time that *Trivulzio* had a medal struck with a bust portrait of himself and the following remarkable inscription on the reverse: **BY GOD'S FAVOR, 1499. THE AFOREMENTIONED GIAN GIACOMO EXPELLED LUDOVICO SFORZA** <gloss>Sforza</gloss> **DUKE** <gloss>duke</gloss> **OF MILAN** <gloss>of Milan</gloss> **IN THE NAME** <gloss>name</gloss> **OF THE KING OF THE FRENCH. IN THE SAME YEAR** <gloss>year</gloss> **LUDOVICO RETURNED;** <gloss>returned</gloss> **LUDOVICO** <gloss>Ludovico</gloss> **WAS OVERCOME AND CAPTURED BY HIM.** <note>original Latin: DEO FAVENTE . 1499 . DICTVS . IO . IA . EXPVLIT . LVDOVICV̆ . SF . Sfortiam DVC . ducem MLI Mediolani . NOĨE nomine . REGIS . FRANCORVM . EODEM . ANN . anno RED'T redit . LVS Ludovicus . . SVPERATVS ET CAPTVS . EST . AB . EO.</note> In the Library of the *Palazzo Trivulzio*, there is a manuscript of *Callimachus Siculus* written at the end of the 15th or beginning of the 16th century. At the beginning of this manuscript, there is an exquisite illuminated miniature of an equestrian statue with the name of the general on the base; it is, however, very doubtful whether this has any connection with Leonardo's design.

Entries 731—740, which discuss the casting of bronze, likely have only a very indirect relevance to the arrangements made for casting the equestrian statue of *Francesco Sforza*. Some portions evidently relate to the casting of cannons. Still, in our research regarding Leonardo's work on the monument, we may refer to them as giving us some insight into the process of bronze casting during that period.

<summary>This page explores the political context behind Gian Giacomo Trivulzio’s commission of an equestrian monument from Leonardo da Vinci, framed by his rivalry with Ludovico Sforza and his desire for a legacy similar to that of the Colleoni family.</summary>
<keywords>Gian Giacomo Trivulzio, Ludovico il Moro, Leonardo da Vinci, Sforza Monument, Trivulzio Monument, Milan, bronze casting, equestrian statues, Renaissance history</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
# 706.

## ON THE STATUE.

If you wish to make a figure in marble, first make one out of clay. When you have finished it, let it dry and place it in a case that is large enough to receive the marble block after the clay figure is removed, so that you may carve the marble into the likeness of the clay model. After placing the clay figure in this case, take small rods that fit exactly into holes in the case. Push them through each hole until each white rod touches the figure at various points. Color the part of the rods remaining outside the case black, and mark each rod and its corresponding hole with a matching sign so they can be aligned at your convenience. Then, remove the clay figure from the case and insert your piece of marble. Carve away enough of the marble so that all your rods are hidden in the holes up to their marks. To do this more effectively, design the case so that the entire frame can be lifted up while the bottom of the case remains under the marble; in this way, you can use your tools to remove the material with great ease.

--- Page 42 ---
### 707.

Some have erred in teaching sculptors to measure the limbs of their figures with threads, as if they believed that these limbs were of equal roundness in every part where those threads were wound around them.

---

### 708.

MEASUREMENT AND DIVISION OF A STATUE.

Divide the head into 12 degrees, and divide each degree into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and the minutes into minims, and the minims into semi-minims.
Degree—point—minute—minim.

---

### 709.

Sculptured figures that appear to be in motion will, by their very nature, seem to be falling forward when placed in a standing position.

---

### 710.

Iron braces that bind the mold. [If you wish to make simple casts quickly, make them in a box of river sand moistened with vinegar.]

---

**709.** *figure di rilievo*. Leonardo applies this term exclusively to fully detached figures, especially to those standing free. This note apparently refers to a specific case, though we have no knowledge of what that may have been. If we suppose it refers to the first model of the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza (see the introduction to the notes on Sculpture), this observation may be regarded as one of his arguments for abandoning the first design of the Sforza Monument, in which the horse was to be galloping. It also supports this theory that the note is written in a manuscript volume already completed in 1492. Leonardo's opinions regarding the shortcomings of sculptural works when compared with paintings are provided under numbers 655 and 656.

--- Page 43 ---
**709.** *Figures in relief*. Leonardo applies this term exclusively to fully detached figures, especially to those that are free-standing. This note apparently refers to a specific case, though we have no knowledge of what that may have been. If we suppose it refers to the first model of the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza (see the introduction to the notes on Sculpture), this observation may be regarded as one of his arguments for abandoning the first design of the Sforza Monument, in which the horse was to be galloping. It also supports this theory that the note is written in a manuscript volume already completed in 1492. Leonardo's opinions regarding the shortcomings of sculptural works when compared with paintings are provided under numbers 655 and 656.

--- Page 44 ---
**710.** I say that figures in relief, if they are to appear to be moving, must be balanced on their feet so that their weight is distributed over the foot that supports the body. And if this is not done, the figure will appear to be made of wood or stone, and without life. Therefore, it is necessary to observe the movements of the limbs and the torso, and how they are coordinated with the weight of the body, so that the figure does not seem to be falling or out of balance. This is especially true for figures that are meant to be seen from all sides, for if they are not well-balanced, they will appear distorted and unnatural from certain points of view.

--- Page 45 ---
### THE MOULD FOR THE HORSE.

When you have finished the mould upon the horse, you must create the thickness of the metal using clay.

Note, when alloying, how many hours are required for every hundredweight. In casting, everyone must keep the furnace and its fire well sealed. The inside of the entire mould should be soaked with linseed oil or turpentine; then apply a coat of powdered borax and Greek pitch with aqua vitae, and coat the outside of the mould with pitch so that, while it is underground, the dampness does not damage it.

To handle the large mould, make a model of the small mould; build a small room in proportion to it.

Create the vents in the mould while it is still on the horse.

Keep the hoofs soaking and cast them with fish glue. Weigh the parts of the mould to determine what quantity of metal will occupy them, and provide enough to the furnace so that it can supply its metal to that specific part. You will determine this by weighing the clay of that part of the mould to which the furnace, with its specific quantity, must correspond. This is done so that the furnace for the legs fills them, and so that the legs do not have to supply metal to the head, which would be impossible. Cast the small door at the same time as the casting of the horse.

---

### THE MOULD FOR THE HORSE.

Build the horse on iron legs that are firm and stable upon a good foundation; then grease it and create the outer casing over it, letting it dry thoroughly layer by layer. This will increase the thickness by three fingers' breadth; then reinforce it with iron as needed; furthermore, hollow out...

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's detailed technical instructions for casting the Sforza Horse, focusing on creating the thickness for the bronze and the construction of a multi-piece plaster mould.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>NOTES ON SCULPTURE. [712.]</header>

...[remove the outer] mould and then create the thickness <note>The "thickness" refers to the space that will eventually be filled by the molten bronze</note>. Then fill the mould bit by bit, ensuring it is complete and solid throughout. Encircle and bind it with its iron supports and fire it <note>original: "riuoci" (rebake or fire)</note> from the inside where it will come into contact with the bronze.

### ON MAKING THE MOULD IN PIECES
<note>original Italian: "DEL FAR LA FORMA DI PEZZI"</note>

Once the horse model is finished, mark out all the individual pieces of the mould that you wish to cover the horse with. When applying the clay, cut into it at every joint so that when the mould is finished, you can remove it and later reassemble it in its original position using the alignment marks <term>countersigns</term> at the joints.

The square blocks $a$ and $b$ will be placed between the outer casing <term>cover</term> and the inner core—that is, in the hollow space where the molten bronze is to be. These square blocks of bronze will maintain a consistent distance between the mould and the casing; for this reason, these squares are of great importance.

<margin>The clay should be mixed with sand. Acquire wax, keeping track of what is returned and paying for what is consumed.</margin>

Dry the clay layer by layer. Create the outer mould using plaster <note>original: "giesso"</note> to save time in the drying process and to reduce the cost of firewood. With this plaster, secure the iron props both outside and inside to a thickness of two fingers. Use terra cotta.

This mould can be completed in a single day; half a boatload <note>A "navata" or boatload was a common bulk measurement for materials in Renaissance Italy</note> of plaster will be sufficient for you. Good.

Seal it <note>original: "Ritasa" (to stop up or dam)</note> again with glue and clay, or with egg white, bricks, and crushed rubble.

***

C. A. 213*b*; 628*b*] **712.**
All the heads of the large bolts <term>chiavarde</term>.

<note>Scholarly Notes: 
**711.** See Plate LXXV. The figure "40" near the sketch in the middle of the page was added by a later collector. In the original manuscript, a square piece has been cut out below line 21 and a blank piece of paper glued in its place. 

**712.** See Plate LXXVI, No. 1. This drawing was published in the "Essays on the Works of Leonardo da Vinci" (Milan, 1872). The editor suggested: "One can see the wooden frameworks with which the model was perhaps supported when, for the wedding of Bianca Maria Sforza to Emperor Maximilian, it was placed under a triumphal arch in front of the Castle." However, the author of this volume (Richter) finds that suggestion unsatisfactory.</note>

THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

### 713.

These bindings go inside.

### 714.

Salt can be made from human excrement, burned and calcined, then made into a lye and dried over a slow fire; all excrements produce salt in a similar way, and these salts, when distilled, are very penetrating.

**714.** Vasari repeatedly states, in the fourth chapter of his *Introduction to Sculpture*, that in preparing to cast bronze statues, horse-dung was frequently used by sculptors. If, despite this, it remains doubtful whether I am justified in having introduced this text of little interest here, no such doubt can be attached to the sketch which accompanies it.

--- Page 53 ---
721—725.] THE SFORZA MONUMENT.

### 721.
One can see, in the mountains of Parma and Piacenza, a multitude of shells and corals full of holes, still clinging to the rocks. When I was working on the great horse for Milan, a large sackful of these, which were found in that area, was brought to me at my workshop by certain peasants.

### 722.
Believe me, Leonardo the Florentine—who is making the bronze equestrian statue of Duke Francesco—that he does not need to worry about it, because he has enough work to last his entire lifetime; and, since it is such a massive undertaking, I doubt whether he can ever finish it.

### 723.
I will say nothing of the horse because I know the times.

### 724.
Work on the marbles has been going on for ten years; I will not wait for my payment beyond the time when my work is finished.

### 725.
**THE MONUMENT TO MESSER GIOVANNI JACOMO DA TREVULZO.**

Cost of the labor and materials for the horse.

**722.** This passage is quoted from a letter to a committee at Piacenza for whom Leonardo seems to have undertaken to perform some work. The letter is given in its entirety in section XXI; in it, Leonardo protests against certain unreasonable demands.

**723.** This passage occurs in a rough draft of a letter to Ludovico il Moro, without a date (see below among the letters).

**724.** This possibly refers to the work for the pedestal of the equestrian statue, concerning which we have no further information in the manuscripts. See p. 6.

**725.** In the original, lines 2–5, 12–14, and 33–35 are written in the margin. This passage was recently published by G. Govi in Vol. V, Series 3, of *Transunti, Reale Accademia dei Lincei, session of June 5, 1881*, with the following introductory note: "In the meantime, I desire that these few fragments be printed because I know they were recently transcribed and will shortly be published outside of Italy. Let whoever wishes republish them, but let it at least be known that they were also recognized among us, and had been collected for years to compose, whenever possible, an organized edition of Leonardo's writings."

The learned editor omitted line 22 and wrote 3 *feet* instead of 8 *feet* in line 25. There are other less important deviations from the original.

726.

MINT AT ROME.

| | |
|:---|:---|
| 2 It can also be made without a spring; 3 But | The mint of Rome. It can also be made without a spring. |
| the upper screw must always 4 remain | But the screw above must always be joined |
| joined to the part of the 5 movable sheath; | to the part of the movable sheath: |

--- Page 58 ---
# THE MINT AT ROME.

All coins which do not have a complete rim should not be accepted as good. To achieve the perfection of their rim, it is necessary that the coins first be perfectly circular. To do this, a coin must first be made perfect in weight, size, and thickness. From this specific size and thickness, many plates should be made, drawn through the same gauge, which will remain in the form of strips. From these strips, the round coins are to be stamped out, in the same manner that sieves for chestnuts are made; and these coins are then stamped in the way mentioned above, etc.

The hollow of the die should be uniformly wider at the top than at the bottom, but imperceptibly so.

This process cuts the coins with perfect roundness, thickness, and weight; it saves the labor of the man who cuts and weighs, and saves the labor of the man who makes the coins round. Therefore, the metal only passes through the hands of the gauger and the stamper, and it produces very beautiful coins.

---

### POWDER FOR MEDALS.

Incombustible wicks made of fungus reduced to powder, burnt tin and all other metals, alum, scagliuolo, smoke from a brass forge; moisten each of these substances with aqua vitae, malmsey wine, strong grain vinegar, white wine, or the first water of distilled turpentine, or oil—provided there is very little moisture—and cast them into small frames.

---

### ON TAKING IMPRESSIONS OF MEDALS.

A paste of emery mixed with aqua vitae, or iron scale with vinegar, or ashes of walnut leaves, or ashes of straw very finely ground.

--- Page 59 ---
### ON TAKING IMPRESSIONS OF MEDALS.

A paste of emery mixed with brandy, or iron scale with vinegar, or ashes of walnut leaves, or ashes of straw very finely ground.

The metal should be well greased with turpentine and wax; if the metal is cold, it should be greased with oil or cold fat.

To make a beautiful impression, take some fine plaster, and after it has been well dried, mix it with a little finely ground brick dust; then moisten it with a little salt water, so that it becomes like a thick paste, and cast it onto the medal. When it is dry, remove the medal, and you will have a mold in which you can cast any metal you wish.

If you want to make a mold of a medal in relief, take some fine glue and melt it in a little water; then take the medal and grease it with oil, and place it in a small box of the same size as the medal; pour the warm glue over it and let it cool. When it is cold, remove the medal, and you will have a mold of the medal in relief. In this mold, you can cast any material you like, such as plaster, wax, or sulfur.

--- Page 60 ---
To make a mold of a medal in hollow relief, take some fine glue and melt it in a little water; then take the medal and grease it with oil, and place it in a small box of the same size as the medal; pour the warm glue over it and let it cool. When it is cold, remove the medal, and you will have a mold of the medal in hollow relief. In this mold, you can cast any material you like, such as plaster, wax, or sulfur.

If you wish to make a mold of a medal in high relief, take some fine clay and mix it with a little sand and water; then take the medal and grease it with oil, and press it into the clay. When the clay is dry, remove the medal, and you will have a mold of the medal in high relief. In this mold, you can cast any metal you wish, such as gold, silver, or copper.

To make a mold of a medal in low relief, take some fine sand and mix it with a little water; then take the medal and grease it with oil, and press it into the sand. When the sand is dry, remove the medal, and you will have a mold of the medal in low relief. In this mold, you can cast any metal you wish, such as lead, tin, or pewter.

--- Page 61 ---
The diameter is provided in the enclosed lead; it is beaten with a hammer and extended several times. The lead is folded and kept wrapped in paper so that the powder is not spilled; then melt the lead, and the powder will remain on top of the melted lead. This powder must then be rubbed between two steel plates until it is thoroughly pulverized; then wash it with aqua fortis, and the blackness of the iron will be dissolved, leaving the powder clean.

Emery in large pieces may be broken by placing it on a cloth folded many times and striking it sideways with a hammer, which will cause it to break apart; then mix it in little by little, and it can be crushed with ease. If you were to hold it on the anvil, you would never break it, since it is so large.

Anyone who grinds smalt should do so on tempered steel plates with a cone-shaped grinder, and then place it in aqua fortis. This dissolves all the steel that has been worn away and mixed with the smalt, which made it black; it then remains purified and clean. If you grind it on porphyry, the porphyry wears down and mixes with the smalt, spoiling it, and aqua fortis can never remove it because it cannot dissolve porphyry.

If you want to make a beautiful blue color, dissolve the smalt made with tartar, and then remove the salt from it.

Vitrified brass makes a beautiful red.

---

### 729.

#### Stucco.

Place stucco over the prominence of the ...... of plaster, which may be composed of Venus and Mercury; knead it well over that prominence to the thickness of the back of a knife, shaped with the sagoma. Cover this with the lid of a distilling bell, and you will recover the moisture with which you made the paste. Let the remainder dry thoroughly, and then heat and strike it...

#### Stucco.

Place stucco over the prominence of the . . . . which may be composed of Venus and Mercury, and lay it well over that prominence to the thickness of the side of a knife, made with the ruler; cover this with the bell of a still, and you will recover the moisture with which you applied the paste. The rest you may dry...

---

In this passage, a few words have been written in a sort of cipher—that is to say, backwards; as in line 3, *erenev* for *Venere*, line 4, *oirucrem* for *Mercurio*, and line 12, *il orreve cō ecarob* for *il everro (?) cō borace*. The meaning of the word before "di giesso" in line 1 is unknown, and the sense in which *sagoma* is used here and in other passages is obscure. *Venere* and *Mercurio* may mean 'marble' and 'lime', of which stucco is composed.

12. The meaning of *orreve* is unknown.

ON CASTING BRONZE. [730-734.]

...well; afterwards fire it, and beat it or burnish it with a good burnisher, and make it thick towards the side.

### STUCCO.

Powder . . . with borax and water into a paste, and make stucco of it, and then heat it so that it may dry, and then varnish it with fire so that it shines well.

---

## 730.

### STUCCO FOR MOULDING.

Take 6 parts of butter, 2 parts of wax, and as much fine flour as, when mixed with these two melted ingredients, will make them as firm as wax or modelling clay.

### GLUE.

Take mastic, distilled turpentine, and white lead.

---

## 731.

### TO CAST.

Tartar, burnt and powdered with plaster and cast, causes the plaster to hold together after it has been fired; and then it will dissolve in water.

---

## 732.

### TO CAST BRONZE IN PLASTER.

Take one cup of burnt ox-horn for every two cups of plaster, mix them together, and make your cast with it.

---

## 733.

When you want to take a cast in wax, burn the scum with a candle, and the cast will come out without bubbles.

---

## 734.

Two ounces of plaster to a pound of metal;—walnut, which makes it like the curve.

--- Page 63 ---
From this rear side, which moves a short distance away, these internal mechanisms cannot be seen because they are inside a fire; those inside will not see this coming in order to see the fire behind. Because of the lamps inside, it will not be so visible; the internal view is instead achieved by hand with this tension. This internal apparatus is made to give value to the whole; it shall be made of iron to show the wheel. However, one will proceed in this way to see the composition of this

-> mechanical apparatus <-

This particular wheel shall be as much larger than another as the path of the view shall be through the space of the motion. Construct the pendulum with counterweights above these wheels, which, by turning, create the form of the movement of the instrument made in this manner.

--- Page 64 ---
The horse in its galloping motion, to give grace to the figure.

The wheel must be made of iron.

40

The weight of this apparatus shall be very heavy. Time shall be measured according to the amount of the instrument's motion, and it turns itself to observe all the movements necessary for the motion, following the line of its pendulum.

The pose of the horse: when it stops, the front leg should be quite straight to demonstrate the quality of its strength.

--- Page 65 ---
### 735.

[Dry earth 16 pounds, 100 pounds of metal; wet clay 20—of wet 100—half—which increases by 4 lbs. of water—1 of wax, 1 lb. of metal, a little less—linen scrapings with earth, equal parts.]

### 736.

The cast will be exactly as the mould is.

### 737.

### HOW CASTS SHOULD BE POLISHED.
Make a bundle of iron wires as thick as twine, and scrub the casts with water while holding a tub underneath so that you do not create mud on the floor.

### HOW TO REMOVE THE ROUGH EDGES FROM BRONZE.
Make an iron rod in the style of a wide chisel, and use it to rub over those ridges on the bronze that remain on the casts of the cannons, which result from the seams in the mould; but ensure the rod is quite heavy, and let the strokes be long and forceful.

### TO FACILITATE MELTING.
First alloy a portion of the metal in the crucible, then put it into the furnace; this molten mixture will, by its liquid state, help initiate the melting of the copper.

### TO PREVENT THE COPPER FROM COOLING IN THE FURNACE.
If the copper should cool in the furnace, make sure that as soon as you notice it, you cut it with a poker while it is still in a paste-like state; or, if it has cooled completely, cut it as you would lead, using wide and heavy chisels.

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci's technical instructions on metallurgy and the casting of large bronze objects, such as cannons (bombards). It focuses on furnace management, breaking down scrap metal, and preparing the outer layers of a mold.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>ON CASTING BRONZE. [738. 739.]</header>

### IF YOU HAVE TO MAKE A LARGE CAST.
<note>original: "SE AVESSI A FARE VNO GRĀ GIETTO."</note>

If you have to make a cast of one hundred thousand pounds <note>original: "cento mila libbre"</note>, do it with two furnaces and with 2,000 pounds in each, or at most 3,000 pounds.

---

<meta>738.</meta>

## HOW TO PROCEED TO BREAK A LARGE MASS OF BRONZE.
<note>original: "COME FARE BENE A RŌPERE VNA GRĀ MASSA DI BRŌZO."</note>

If you want to break up a large mass of bronze, first suspend it. Then build a brick wall on all four sides around it, like a trough, and build a large fire inside. When the metal is red-hot, strike it with a heavy weight dropped from a height with great force.

---

<meta>739.</meta>

## TO COMBINE LEAD WITH OTHER METAL.
<note>original: "DEL FARE VNIRE IL PIŌBO CON ALTRO METALLO."</note>

If you wish to save money <note>original: "per masseritia"</note> by adding lead to the metal alloy to reduce the amount of tin required, first alloy the lead with the tin and then add the molten copper.

### HOW TO MELT METAL IN A FURNACE.
<note>original: "COME SI DEBE FONDERE IN UNO FORNELLO."</note>

The furnace should be placed between four well-founded pillars.

### OF THE THICKNESS OF THE COATING.
<note>original: "DELLA GROSSEZZA DELLA CAPPA."</note>

The coating <term>cappa: the outer shell or "cloak" of a mold used in casting</term> should not exceed a thickness of two fingers. It should be applied in four layers over fine clay and then well reinforced. It should only be fired <note>original: "ricotta"</note> from the inside and then carefully covered with a thin layer of ashes and cow dung.

### OF THE THICKNESS OF THE GUN.
<note>original: "DELLA GROSSEZZA DELLA BŌBARDA."</note>

Divide it into 6 parts, and one of these parts shall be the thickness at the muzzle; at the breech it must always be half. And if the ball weighs 700 lbs., 1/7th of the diameter of the ball shall be its thickness at the front; and if the ball weighs 800, one-eighth of its diameter at the front; and if 900, 1/8th and 1/2 [3/16ths]; and if 1000, 1/9th.

---

### OF THE LENGTH OF THE BODY OF THE GUN.

If you want it to throw a stone ball, make the length of the gun 6, or as much as 7 ball diameters; and if the ball is iron, make the said gun as much as 12 ball diameters; and if the ball is lead, make it as much as 18 ball diameters. I mean this when the gun has a muzzle fitted to receive a stone ball of 600 lbs. or more.

---

### OF THE THICKNESS OF SMALL GUNS.

The thickness at the muzzle of small guns should be between one-half and one-third of the diameter of the ball, and the length from 30 to 36 ball diameters.

---

740.

### OF COATING THE INSIDE OF THE FURNACE.

The furnace must be coated with earth from Valenza before you put the metal in it, and over that with ashes.

---

### OF RESTORING THE METAL WHEN IT BEGINS TO COOL.

When you see that the bronze is starting to solidify, take willow wood, split into small chips, and build up the fire with them.

---

### THE CAUSE OF THE SOLIDIFYING.

I say that the cause of this solidifying often results from too much fire, and also from poorly dried wood.

---

### TO KNOW THE CONDITION OF THE FIRE.

You will know when the fire is good and useful by the clear flames; and if you see the

--- Page 70 ---
And if you see the tips of the flames are dull and end in much smoke, do not trust it, especially when the metal bath is almost fluid.

### **OF ALLOYING THE METAL.**

Metal for guns must generally be made with 6 or even 8 percent—that is, 6 parts of tin to one hundred parts of copper—and the less you put in, the stronger the gun will be.

### **WHEN THE TIN SHOULD BE ADDED TO THE COPPER.**

The tin should be added to the copper when you have reduced the copper to a fluid state.

### **HOW TO HASTEN THE MELTING.**

You can hasten the melting when two-thirds of the copper is fluid; you can then, with a stick of chestnut wood, repeatedly stir the remainder of the copper that is still solid into the melted portion.

--- Page 71 ---
## *Introductory Observations on the Architectural Designs (XII), and Writings on Architecture (XIII).*

Until now, very little has been known regarding Leonardo’s work in the field of Architecture. No building is known to have been planned and executed by him, though some contemporary writers make incidental allusions to him occupying himself with architecture. His famous letter to Lodovico il Moro—which has long been a well-known document—in which he offers his services as an architect to that prince, tends to confirm the belief that he was something more than an amateur of the art. This hypothesis has lately been confirmed by the publication of certain documents preserved at Milan, showing that Leonardo was not only employed in preparing plans, but that he took an active part, with much credit, as a member of a commission on public buildings; his name remains linked with the history of the construction of the Cathedral at Pavia and that of the Cathedral at Milan.

Leonardo’s writings on Architecture are dispersed among a large number of manuscripts, and it would be scarcely possible to master their contents without the opportunity of arranging, sorting, and comparing the entire mass of materials, so as to have a comprehensive idea of the whole. The sketches, when isolated and considered by themselves, might appear to be of little value; it is not until we understand their general purpose by comparing them with each other that we can form any just estimate of their true worth.

Leonardo seems to have had a project for writing a complete and separate treatise on Architecture, such as his predecessors and contemporaries had composed—Leon Battista Alberti, Filarete, Francesco di Giorgio, and perhaps also Bramante. But, on the other hand, it cannot be denied that possibly no such scheme was connected with the isolated notes and researches treating on special questions which are given in this work; he may have merely been working at problems in which, for some reason or another, he took a special interest.

A great number of important buildings were constructed in Lombardy during the period between 1472 and 1499, and among them there are several by unknown architects,

--- Page 72 ---
of such high artistic merit that it is certainly not improbable that either Bramante or Leonardo da Vinci may have been, directly or indirectly, involved in their construction.

Having been engaged for nearly twenty years in a thorough study of Bramante's life and work, I have taken a particular interest in detecting the distinguishing marks of his style as compared with Leonardo's. In 1869, I conducted research regarding the latter's architectural drawings in the *Codex Atlanticus* at Milan, for the purpose of finding the original plans and sketches of the churches of Santa Maria delle Grazie at Milan and the Cathedral at Pavia, as these buildings have been attributed to both Bramante and Leonardo. Since 1876, I have repeatedly examined Leonardo's architectural studies in the collection of his manuscripts at the Institut de France, and I have already published some of these in my work "Les Projets Primitifs pour la Basilique de St. Pierre de Rome," *Plate 43*. In 1879, I had the opportunity to examine the manuscript in the Palazzo Trivulzio at Milan, and in 1880, Dr. Richter showed me the manuscripts in London belonging to Lord Ashburnham, as well as those in the British Museum. I have thus had opportunities to see most of Leonardo's architectural drawings in the original, though I have deciphered only the notes that accompany the sketches in the manuscripts themselves. It is to Dr. Richter's efforts that we owe the collected texts on Architecture now being published; while he has taken responsibility for the correct reading of the original texts, he has also made it his task to extract all the material from the various manuscripts. It has been my task to arrange and explain the texts under the headings adopted in this work. MS. B at Paris and the *Codex Atlanticus* at Milan are the primary sources of our knowledge of Leonardo as an architect, and I have recently subjected these to a thorough re-investigation specifically for this work.

A complete reproduction of all Leonardo’s architectural sketches has not, indeed, been possible, but as far as the necessarily restricted limits of the work have allowed, the greatest possible completeness has been aimed at, and no effort has been spared to include everything that can contribute to a knowledge of Leonardo's style. It would have been very interesting, had it been possible, to provide at least some general account of Leonardo's work and studies in engineering, fortification, canal-making, and similar fields, and it is only after mature reflection that we have reluctantly abandoned this idea. Leonardo's work in these departments does not have nearly as close a relation to literary work, in the strict sense of the word, as we are justified in attributing to his numerous notes on Architecture.

Leonardo's architectural studies fall naturally into two categories:

I. Those drawings and sketches, often accompanied by short remarks and explanations, which may be regarded as designs for buildings or monuments intended to be built. These are occasionally accompanied by explanatory texts.

II. Theoretical investigations and treatises. A special interest attaches to these because they discuss a variety of questions that remain of practical importance today. Leonardo's theory regarding the origin and progress of cracks in buildings is perhaps to be considered unique in its way in architectural literature.

HENRY DE GEYMÜLLER

### 745.

The facade *a m* will provide light to the rooms; *a e* will be 6 braccia—*a b* 8 braccia—and *b e* 30 braccia, so that the rooms beneath the porticoes may be well-lit. *c d f* is the location where the boats arrive at the houses to be unloaded. To make this arrangement functional, and to ensure that river flooding does not reach the cellars, it is necessary to choose a suitable site, such as a location near a river that can be diverted into canals where the water level will not change due to either floods or drought. ### Studies in Military Architecture and Fortifications

This page features detailed architectural drawings focused on **military architecture** and **fortifications**, showcasing Leonardo’s innovative approach to defensive engineering.

**Central Fortification and Lighthouse**
The primary sketch illustrates a massive fortification complex. It features a prominent, multi-tiered **lighthouse** <note>original: faro</note> with a conical roof. The structure is protected by an intricate system of defensive walls and auxiliary buildings. Leonardo’s use of perspective here demonstrates his ability to visualize three-dimensional defensive systems with great technical precision.

**Tower Design and Internal Structures**
Below the main complex are several analytical sketches focusing on the functional components of a **bastion** <note>original: bastione; a projecting part of a fortification built at an angle to the line of a wall, so as to allow defensive fire in several directions.</note>:
*   **Circular Tower:** A study of a round defensive tower, including drawings of internal stairwells designed for the efficient movement of soldiers and supplies.
*   **Structural Sections:** Cross-sections showing the thickness and internal layout of defensive walls.
*   **Defensive Layouts:** Perspective studies exploring how different building shapes and placements can create a more formidable defense against attackers.

<summary>This page contains Leonardo da Vinci’s technical sketches for military fortifications, featuring a multi-tiered lighthouse and detailed studies of circular bastions and internal staircases.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, military architecture, fortifications, lighthouse, bastion, technical drawing, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
To make a recovery of gold and silver dust, clean and in the shape of a ball.

---

This is the way in which the course of the wall corresponds to each of the centers.

--- Page 88 ---
This is the way in which the course of the wall corresponds to each of the centers. For if the wall is built in a straight line, it will be necessary to make the towers at certain intervals, so that they may defend each other. But if the wall is built in a circular or polygonal form, then the towers must be placed at the angles, so that the curtains between them may be protected by the fire from the towers. And the distance between the towers should not be greater than the range of a musket shot, so that the soldiers in one tower may be able to assist those in the next. Furthermore, the height of the wall must be such that it cannot be easily scaled with ladders, and the thickness must be sufficient to withstand the force of artillery. The ditch also must be wide and deep, and if possible, filled with water, to prevent the enemy from approaching the foot of the wall. All these things must be carefully considered by the engineer, so that the fortress may be strong and secure against any attack.

The sketch of a villa on a terrace at the end of a garden occurs in Codex Atlanticus 150; and in Codex Atlanticus 77b; 225b is another sketch of a villa somewhat resembling the Belvedere of Pope Innocent VIII, at Rome. In Codex Atlanticus 62b; 193b there is a Loggia.
Plate LXXXII, No. 4 (Codex Atlanticus 387a; 1198a) is a tower-shaped Loggia above a fountain. The machinery is very ingeniously screened from view.

---

### 748.

The Prince's palace must have a piazza in front of it.
Rooms intended for dancing, or for various jumping or other movements with a large number of people, must be on the ground floor; for I have already seen some collapse, causing the deaths of many. Above all, ensure that every wall, no matter how thin, has a foundation in the ground or rests upon well-founded arches.
Let the mezzanines of the dwellings be divided by walls made of thin bricks, and without woodwork to prevent fire.
Let all the privies have ventilation shafts within the thickness of the walls, arranged so that they vent through the roofs.
The mezzanines should be vaulted, and these vaults will be stronger the smaller they are.
The oak tie-beams must be enclosed within the walls so that they are protected from fire.

---

748. The remarks accompanying the plan reproduced on Plate LXXXI, No. 2 are as follows: Above, to the left: “In angle *a* let the stable guard be stationed.” Below are the words “road to Amboise,” and parallel with this “moat 40 braccia,” establishing the width of the moat. In the large court surrounded by a portico: “on the ground—80 braccia wide and 120 braccia long.” To the right of the castle is a large basin for aquatic sports with the words: “Jousting with boats; that is, the jousters are to be in boats.”

**749.** On each side of the castle (Plate LXXXII, No. 2), there are drawings of details: to the left, a "chimney" (*camino*); to the right, the central lantern, sketched in red as "8 sides" (*8 lati*), meaning an octagon.

**751.** This passage was first published by <term>Carlo Amoretti</term> <note>An 18th-century librarian and scholar who was among the first to organize Leonardo's manuscripts.</note> in *Memorie Storiche*, Chapter X: <note>original: "Una sua opera da riportarsi a quest’ anno fu il bagno fatto per la duchessa Beatrice nel parco o giardino del Castello. Lionardo non solo ne disegnò il piccolo edifizio a foggia di padiglione, nel cod. segnato Q. 3, dandone anche separatamente la pianta; ma sotto vi scrisse: Padiglione del giardino della duchessa; e sotto la pianta: Fondamento del padiglione ch’è nel mezzo del labirinto del duca di Milano; nessuna data è presso il padiglione, disegnato nella pagina 12, ma poco sopra fra molti circoli intrecciati vedesi = 10 Luglio 1492 = e nella pagina 2 presso ad alcuni disegni di legumi qualcheduno ha letto Settembre 1482 in vece di 1492, come dovea scriverevi, e probabilmente scrisse Lionardo."</note> 

"One of his works to be attributed to this year was the bath made for Duchess Beatrice in the park or garden of the Castle. Leonardo not only designed the small building in the shape of a pavilion (found in the codex marked Q. 3), providing the plan separately, but he also wrote beneath it: 'Pavilion of the garden of the duchess'; and under the plan: 'Foundation of the pavilion which is in the middle of the labyrinth of the Duke of Milan.' No date is found near the pavilion drawn on page 12, but a little above, among many intertwined circles, one sees 'July 10, 1492.' On page 2, near some drawings of legumes, someone read 'September 1482' instead of '1492,' which Leonardo likely intended to write."

The original text, however, hardly supports the interpretation provided by Amoretti. He is mistaken about the manuscript's markings as well as his claims regarding the date, for the manuscript in question has no date. The date he cites occurs, instead, in a different notebook. Finally, it seems to me an open question whether Leonardo was the architect who actually constructed the dome-like pavilion shown here in section, or the ground plan drawn beside it. Must we assume that "the Duke of Milan" mentioned here was, as is generally believed, <term>Ludovico il Moro</term>? <note>Ludovico Sforza, Leonardo’s primary patron in Milan.</note> He did not officially hold this title from the Emperor until 1494; until then, he was only called "Governor" (*Governatore*). When Leonardo speaks of him, he usually refers to him as "il Moro," even after 1494. On January 18, 1491, he married <term>Beatrice d’Este</term>, the daughter of Ercole I, Duke of Ferrara. She died on January 2, 1497, and for the reasons I have given, it seems unlikely that she is the "Duchess of Milan" referred to here. Based on the style of the handwriting, it appears to me to be...

And the chest muscles move to straighten the arm that is bent. For this reason, one sees the muscles of the chest <note>original: "muscoli del pecto"</note> which attach to the "fish muscle" <term>fish muscle</term> <note>original: "muscolo de' pesci"; a Renaissance anatomical term likely referring to the deltoid or biceps due to their tapered shape.</note>. ### IV. Ecclesiastical Architecture.

#### A. General Observations.

**753.**
A building should always be detached on all sides so that its true form may be displayed.

**754.**
In this case, there cannot and should not be any campanile; on the contrary, it must stand apart, like the Cathedral and San Giovanni in Florence, or the Cathedral at Pisa, where the campanile is entirely detached and surrounded by space, as is the Cathedral itself. Thus, each can display its own perfection. If, however, you still wish to join it to the church, make the lantern serve as the campanile, as is seen in the church at Chiaravalle.

---

**753.** The original text is reproduced on Plate XCII, No. 1, at the bottom left.

**754.** This text is written beside the plan provided on Plate XCI, No. 2.

**12.** The Abbey of Chiaravalle, located a few miles from Milan, has a central tower at the intersection of the cross in the style of the Certosa of Pavia, though the style is medieval (A.D. 1330). Leonardo seems to mean here that in a building where the circular form is prominent, the campanile must either be separated or rise from the center of the building and therefore take the form of a lantern.

### B. The theory of Dome Architecture.

This subject has been more extensively treated by Leonardo in drawings than in writing. Still, we may fairly assume that it was his purpose, ultimately, to embody the results of his investigation in a “*Trattato delle Cupole*” (Treatise on Domes). The amount of material is remarkably extensive. *MS. B* is particularly rich in plans and elevations of churches with one or more domes—from the simplest form to the most complicated that can be imagined. Considering the evident connection between a great number of these sketches, as well as the impossibility of seeing in them designs or preparatory sketches for any building intended to be erected, the conclusion is obvious that they were not designed for any particular monument, but were theoretical and ideal researches, made in order to obtain a clear understanding of the laws which must govern the construction of a great central dome, with smaller ones grouped around it; and with or without the addition of spires, so that each of these parts by itself and in its juxtaposition to the other parts should produce the grandest possible effect.

In these sketches, Leonardo seems to have exhausted every imaginable combination.¹ The results of some of these problems are perhaps not quite satisfactory; still, they cannot be considered to give evidence of a want of taste or of any other defect in Leonardo’s architectural capacity. They were no doubt intended exclusively for his own instruction, and, above all, as it seems, to illustrate the features or consequences resulting from a given principle.

---

¹ In *MS. B*, 32b (see Pl. C III, No. 2) we find eight geometrical patterns, each drawn in a square; and in *MS. C. A.*, folios 87 to 98 form a whole series of patterns done with the same intention.

<page-num>39</page-num>

-># THE THEORY OF DOME ARCHITECTURE.<-

*I have already, in another place,<sup>1</sup> pointed out the law of construction for buildings crowned by a large dome: namely, that such a dome, to produce the greatest effect possible, should rise either from the center of a Greek cross <note>A cross with four arms of equal length.</note>, or from the center of a structure where the plan is symmetrically related to a circle, this circle being at the same time the center of the whole plan of the building.*

*Leonardo's sketches show that he was fully aware, as was to be expected, of this truth. Few of them exhibit the form of a Latin cross <note>A cross with one arm longer than the other three, typical of Western European cathedrals.</note>, and when this is met with, it generally gives evidence of his determination to assign as prominent a part as possible to the dome in the general effect of the building.*

*While it is evident, on the one hand, that the greater number of these domes had no particular purpose, not being designed for execution, on the other hand several reasons may be found for Leonardo's perseverance in his studies of the subject.*

*Besides the theoretical interest of the question for Leonardo and his <term>Trattato</term> <note>original: "Trattato"; refers to a formal treatise or written discourse on a subject.</note> and besides the taste for domes prevailing at that time, it seems likely that the intended erection of some building of the first importance—like the <term>Duomos</term> <note>original: "Duomos"; the Italian term for cathedrals.</note> of Pavia and Como, the church of Santa Maria delle Grazie at Milan, and the construction of a dome or central crossing tower (<term>Tiburio</term> <note>original: "Tiburio"; an architectural term for a tower or lantern that covers a dome, common in Lombardy.</note>) on the cathedral of Milan—may have stimulated Leonardo to undertake a general and thorough investigation of the subject. Furthermore, Leonardo's friendship with Bramante for ten years or more can hardly have remained without influence in this matter. In fact, now that some of this great architect's studies for St. Peter's at Rome have at last become known, he must be considered henceforth as the greatest master of dome architecture that ever existed. His influence, direct or indirect, even on a genius like Leonardo, seems the more likely since Leonardo's sketches reveal a style very similar to that of Bramante, whose name, indeed, occurs twice in Leonardo's manuscript notes. It must not be forgotten that Leonardo was a Florentine; the characteristic form of the two principal domes of Florence, Santa Maria del Fiore and the <term>Battisterio</term> <note>The Baptistery of Saint John.</note>, constantly appear as leading features in his sketches.*

*The church of San Lorenzo at Milan was at that time still intact. The dome is to this day one of the most wonderful cupolas ever constructed, and with its two smaller domes might well attract the attention and study—*

---

<sup>1</sup> *The Original Designs for the Basilica of St. Peter in Rome, by Bramante, Raphael, etc., Vol. I, p. 2.* <note>original: "Les Projets Primitifs pour la Basilique de St. Pierre de Rome, par Bramante, Raphael etc."</note>

<summary>This page discusses Leonardo da Vinci's preference for symmetrical, Greek-cross floor plans in dome architecture and explores the likely influences on his studies, including Bramante and the major cathedrals of Milan and Florence.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Bramante, dome architecture, Greek cross, Milan Cathedral, San Lorenzo, Santa Maria delle Grazie, Renaissance architecture, Tiburio</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
of a never-resting genius such as Leonardo. A whole class of these sketches reveals, in fact, the direct influence of the church of S. Lorenzo, and this also seems to have suggested the plan of Bramante’s dome of St. Peter’s at Rome.
In the following pages, the various sketches for the construction of domes have been classified and discussed from a general point of view. On two sheets: Pl. LXXXIV (C. A. 354b; 118a) and Pl. LXXXV, Nos. 1—11 (Ash. II, 6b), we see various dissimilar types grouped together; thus, these two sheets may be regarded as a sort of nomenclature of the different types, which we shall now address.

### Architectural and Mechanical Studies

The sketches on this page illustrate several key areas of Leonardo's research:

*   **Centrally Planned Churches:** The drawings include several studies for churches designed around a central point, featuring prominent domes. This reflects the Renaissance ideal of architectural harmony and divine proportion.
*   **Classical Column Details:** Leonardo provides detailed renderings of classical columns, likely studying the mathematical proportions and aesthetic styles of antiquity.
*   **Mechanical Drawings:** One section of the page depicts a mechanical device with a compass-like arm and a hanging weight, demonstrating his ongoing investigation into the laws of physics and mechanics.
*   **Geometric Floor Plan:** At the bottom of the sheet is a precise floor plan with numerical annotations, showing how Leonardo translated his three-dimensional architectural visions into practical, measurable layouts.

## 1. Churches formed on the plan of a <term>Greek cross</term>. <note>A Greek cross is a cross with four arms of equal length, a shape favored by Renaissance architects for its symmetry.</note>

### Group I.

->*Domes rising from a circular base.*<-

*The simplest type of central building is a circular edifice.*

*Plate 84, No. 9. Plan of a circular building surrounded by a <term>colonnade</term>.* <note>A colonnade is a long sequence of columns joined by their upper parts.</note>

*Plate 84, No. 8. <term>Elevation</term> of the previous building, showing a conical roof.* <note>An elevation is an architectural drawing showing one side of a building as if it were a flat 2D plane.</note>

*Plate 90, No. 5. A dodecagon <note>A polygon with 12 sides.</note>, as the shape that most closely approaches a circle.*

*Plate 86, No. 1, 2, 3. Four round chapels are added at the ends of the two principal axes; compare this plan with figure 1 on page 44 and figure 3 on page 47 (Windsor Papers 5.ᵇ) where the outer wall is octagonal <note>eight-sided</note>.*

### Group II.

->*Domes rising from a square base.*<-

*The plan is a square surrounded by a colonnade, and the dome appears to be octagonal.*

*Plate 84. The square plan shown below the circular building (No. 8), and its elevation to the left, above the plan: here the ground plan is square, but the upper story is octagonal.*

*A further development of this type is shown in two sketches from the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> 3ᵃ <note>original: "C. A. 3ᵃ"; the Codex Atlanticus is Leonardo's largest collection of drawings and writings.</note> (not reproduced here), and in Plate 86, No. 5 (which possibly belongs to No. 7 on Plate 84).*

*Plate 85, No. 4, and page 45, Figure 3: a Greek cross, repeated on page 45, Figure 3, is another development of the square central plan.*

*The remainder of these studies show two different systems; in the first, the dome rises from a square plan—in the second, from an octagonal base.*

<page-num>42</page-num>
# ARCHITECTURAL DESIGNS.

### Group III.

#### *Domes rising from a square base and four pillars.¹*
<note>¹ The ancient chapel of San Satiro, on the Via del Falcone in Milan, is an example of this architectural type.</note>

**a) First type.** *A dome resting on four pillars in the center of a square building, with an <term>apse</term> <note>a semicircular recess, often vaulted, that projects from the wall of a building</note> in the middle of each of the four sides. We have eleven variations of this type.*

*   **aa)** Plate 88, No. 3.
*   **bb)** Plate 80, No. 5.
*   **cc)** Plate 85, Nos. 2, 3, 5.
*   **dd)** Plate 84, No. 1 and No. 4 below.
*   **ee)** Plate 85, Nos. 1, 7, 10, 11.

**b) Second type.** *This consists of adding <term>aisles</term> <note>passageways between or along the sides of the rows of seats or walls</note> to the entire plan of the first type. Columns are placed between the apses and the aisles. The resulting plan is almost identical to that of the Basilica of San Lorenzo in Milan.*

*Figure 1 on page 56 (found in Manuscript B, 75a) shows the result of this layout adapted for a specific purpose, which we will discuss shortly.*

*Plate 95, No. 1, shows the same plan but with the addition of a short <term>nave</term> <note>the central part of a church building, intended to accommodate most of the congregation</note>. This plan seems to have been inspired by the general arrangement of San Sepolcro in Milan.*

*Manuscript B, 57b (see the sketch reproduced on page 51). By adding towers to the four outer corners of the previously mentioned plan, we arrive at a layout that carries the general features of Bramante’s plans for St. Peter’s Basilica in Rome.²* 
<note>² See "The original projects, etc." <note>original: "Les projets primitifs etc."</note>, Plates 9–12.</note>

### Group IV.

#### *Domes rising from an octagonal base.*

*This system, developed according to two different schemes, has resulted in two classes with many varieties.*

*   **In a)** Chapels of equal shape are added to each side of the <term>octagon</term> <note>an eight-sided shape</note>.
*   **In b)** The chapels are different from one another; those that end the principal axes are different in shape from those added to the diagonal sides of the octagon.

##### *a. First Class.*

*The "Santa Maria degli Angeli" chapel in Florence—which was built only to a height of about 20 feet by Filippo Brunelleschi—may be considered the prototype of this group. Indeed, it likely suggested this design to Leonardo. We can see evidence of this in Manuscript B, 11b...*

<summary>This page categorizes Leonardo da Vinci's designs for domes on square and octagonal bases, linking his theoretical sketches to real-world churches like San Lorenzo in Milan and Brunelleschi's projects in Florence.</summary>
<keywords>architectural history, Leonardo da Vinci, church plans, domes, San Lorenzo, Bramante, St. Peter's Basilica, Brunelleschi, octagonal design</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s architectural drawings, specifically focusing on his studies of centrally planned (octagonal) churches and their relationship to the works of other Renaissance masters like Brunelleschi and Bramante.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL ARCHITECTURE</header>

The side-by-side comparison of Plate 94, No. 3 with **Brunelleschi’s** <note>original: "Brunellesco’s"</note> plan for the **Basilica of Santo Spirito** in Florence—a plan almost identical to that of the **Chapel of the Angels** <note>original: "Capella degli Angeli"</note>—confirms this connection. Leonardo introduced only two small differences, which we might consider improvements. First, the back of each chapel contains a third niche. Second, each angle of the octagon features a folded <term>pilaster</term><note>A rectangular column, especially one projecting from a wall, used for decoration or support.</note> similar to those in **Bramante’s** Sacristy of Santa Maria presso San Satiro in Milan. This replaces the gap between the two pilasters seen in the Florence Baptistery, the Sacristy of Santo Spirito in the same city, and the aforementioned chapel by Brunelleschi.

The first set of sketches under consideration appear at first glance to be mere geometrical studies. They seem to have been inspired by the plan given on page 44, Figure 2 (Manuscript B, folio 55a), in the center of which Leonardo wrote "Santa Maria in Pertica, Pavia" <note>original: "Santa Maria in perticha da Pavia"</note> at the location marked **A** on the reproduction.

**a)** (Manuscript B, folio 34b). A column is added to the middle of each side. In the center lines of the spaces between these columns, a second row of columns forms an aisle around the octagon. These are placed at the intersection of a system of semicircles, using the sixteen columns on the sides of the octagon as their centers.

**b)** The previous diagram is expanded into a more monumental style in the next sketch (Manuscript B, folio 35a). An outer aisle is added by using circles with a radius equal to the distance between the columns in the middle sides of the octagon.

**c)** (Manuscript B, folio 96b). This shows an octagon with an aisle around it; the corners of both are formed by columns. The outer walls consist of eight niches that form chapels. The exterior is likewise octagonal, with the corners corresponding to the center of each of the interior chapels.

**Plate 92, No. 2 (Manuscript B, folio 96b).** This is a detail and modification of the previous plan, using half-columns against <term>piers</term><note>Solid masonry supports, often square or rectangular, used to carry the weight of an arch or roof.</note>. This arrangement ensures that the chapel openings in the aisle have the same width as the inner arches between the half-columns. Underneath this sketch, the following note appears: "This will have twelve sides with twelve <term>tabernacles</term><note>In this context, ornamental niches or small structures used as chapels.</note> as a b." <note>original: "questo vole . avere 12 facce . cō 12 tabernaculi . come . a . b."</note> In the remaining sketches of this class, the octagon is not formed by columns at the corners.

The simplest type shows a niche in the middle of each side. This is repeated on several sheets, namely: Manuscript B, folio 3; Codex Atlanticus, folio 354b; and the Ashburnham II Manuscript, folio 6b.

<summary>This page analyzes Leonardo da Vinci's architectural sketches of octagonal buildings, comparing his designs to those of Brunelleschi and Bramante while detailing specific modifications to aisles, columns, and niches.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, architectural drawings, Filippo Brunelleschi, Donato Bramante, Santo Spirito, Santa Maria in Pertica, octagonal plans, Renaissance architecture, pilasters, niches</keywords>
