> This design is to be made with eight domes, of which four are located under the four [main] arches, and the other four are above the four semi-circular chapels. These will be arranged as was mentioned previously. <note>original: "questo s’a a fare con .8. cupole delle quai le .4. sieno sotto i 4 archi e le altre .4. sopra le .4. capelle d’un mezo cerchio e queste vĕga a esser come s’e dīto dadietro"</note>
>
> If you wished to include a bell tower <term>original: "campanile"</term> in this manner, place it within the small chapels. Because they are small, they are well-suited for this and do not interfere with the structural members located in the center. <note>original: "se tu volessi fare questa chāpāile a rido- -tto i questo mōdo dāgli la chāpāilla ne le capele picole le quali per essere pichole stanno bene e nō āno a ffare con i mēbri che sono nel mezo"</note>
>
> If you wished to build this plan upon a rocky foundation <term>original: "rocca"; likely referring to a cliffside or a fortified rocky site</term>, it should be expanded so that the church is set within the rock and the small chapels are on the outer faces; thus, it would still be situated upon the rock in this manner. <note>original: "se tu volessi fare questa pianta sō- -pra unā rocha di soto e s’āpli i modo che la chiesa sia ne la rocha e le capele picole ne le facē di fōra e āchora sarebe sōpra a rocha in questo modo"</note>

E. By chapels formed by four niches:

->b. Second Class.<-

Composite plans of this class are generally obtained by combining two types of the first class—one developed on the principal axes, the other on the diagonal ones.
MS. B. 22 shows a basic combination, without any additions on the diagonal axes, but with the dimensions of the squares on the two principal axes exceeding those of the sides of the octagon.
In the drawing W. P. 5b (see page 44 Fig. 1), only the exterior of the building is octagonal, while the interior is formed by a circular colonnade; round chapels are placed against the four sides of the principal axes.
The elevation, drawn on the same sheet (see page 47 Fig. 3), shows the entire arrangement, which is closely related to the one on Plate LXXXVI No. 1, 2.
MS. B. 21a shows:
a) four sides with rectangular chapels topped by pediments, Plate LXXXVII No. 3 (plan and elevation);
b) four sides with square chapels topped by octagonal domes, Plate LXXXVII No. 4; the plan is underneath.
MS. B. 18a shows a variation obtained by replacing the round chapels on the principal axes of the sketch MS. B. 18a with square ones, with an

ARCHITECTURAL DESIGNS. [756.

*MS. P. V. 39b. In the principal axes are the chapels of MS. B. 19, with semicircular niches on the diagonals. The exterior of the entire building is also an octagon, which hides the shape of the interior chapels, but has its angles aligned with their axes.*

### Group V.
#### Suggested by San Lorenzo at Milan.

*In MS. C. A. 266 IIb, 812b, there is a plan almost identical to that of San Lorenzo. The diagonal sides of the irregular octagon are not shown. If it could be proven that the arches which exist on these sides in the first story of the actual church were added in 1574 by Martino Bassi, then this plan and the following section would be even closer to the original state of San Lorenzo than they are now. It has not been possible to reproduce this lightly sketched plan. However, it can be understood from Plate LXXXVIII No. 3 by removing the four pillars that correspond to the apses.*

*Plate LXXXVII No. 1 shows the sectional elevation corresponding to the plan mentioned above. The recessed chapels are decorated with large shells in the half-domes, similar to the arrangement in San Lorenzo, but with proportions like those of Bramante’s Sacristy of Santa Maria presso S. Satiro.*

*MS. C. A. 266; a sheet containing three exterior views of domes. On the same sheet, there is a plan similar to the one mentioned above, but with continuous aisles and the addition of round chapels on the axes (compare Plate XCVII No. 3 and page 44 Fig. 1), perhaps a memory of the two chapels attached to San Lorenzo. Leonardo has sketched here a way to transform this plan into a Latin cross by adding a nave with side aisles.*

*Plate XCI No. 1. A plan showing a type without aisles, contained within a square building surrounded by a portico. It is accompanied by the following text:*

## 2. Churches formed on the plan of a Latin cross.

*We find among Leonardo’s studies several sketches for churches on the plan of the Latin cross; we shall begin by describing them and shall add a few observations.*

### A. Studies after existing Monuments.

*Pl. XCIV No. 2. (MS. B. 11b.) Plan of Santo Spirito at Florence, a basilica built according to the designs of Brunellesco. Leonardo has added the indication of a portico in front, which is either his own invention or the reproduction of a now-lost design.*

*Pl. XCV No. 2. Plan accompanied by the words: “A is the upper church of S. Sepolcro at Milan”; although it has been considerably damaged since Leonardo’s time, the plan remains the same.*

*The second plan, with its note: “B is its subterranean part [the crypt],” still corresponds with the current state of this part of the church, as I have confirmed by visiting the crypt with this plan. Except for the addition of a few insignificant walls, the condition of this interesting part of the church still conforms to Leonardo’s sketch; however, in the vestibule, the two columns near the entrance of the winding stairs are missing.*

### B. Designs or Studies.

*Pl. XCV No. 1. Plan of a church evidently suggested by that of San Sepolcro at Milan. The central part has been expanded based on the principle of the second type of Group III. Leonardo has placed the “coro” (choir) in the center.*

*Plate XCVI No. 2.* In the plan, the interior of the dome belongs to the *First Class of Group IV*, and may be categorized with the one in *MS. B. 35a*. The nave appears to be a development of the type shown in *Plate XCV No. 2, B.*, created by adding towers and two side porches¹.

On the left is a view of the exterior of the previous plan. It is accompanied by the following note:

B. 24 a] 757.

This building is inhabited both above and below; the way up is by the bell towers, and in going up one must use the platform where the drums of the four domes are located. This platform has a railing in front, and none of these drums open into the church; rather, they are entirely separate.

*Plate XCVI No. 1 (MS. C. A. 16b; 65a).* A perspective view of a church seen from behind; this calls to mind the Cathedral at Florence, but with two bell towers².

*Plate XCVII No. 3 (MS. B. 52a).* The central part is a development of *S. Lorenzo at Milan*, similar to what was built at the *Cathedral of Pavia*. There is enough similarity between the actual building and this sketch to suggest a direct connection between them. *Leonardo* accompanied *Francesco di Giorgio*³ when the latter was consulted on *June 21st, 1490* regarding this church; the fact that the only word accompanying the plan is "sacristy" seems to confirm our theory, for the sacristies were added only in 1492—that is, four years after the start of the Cathedral, which at that time was likely still unfinished enough to be adapted to the form of the present sketch.

*Plate XCVII No. 2* shows the exterior of this design. Below is the note: "building suitable for the ground plan illustrated below."

Here we may also mention the plan of a *Latin cross* drawn in *MS. C. A. folio 266 (see page 50)*.

*Plate XCIV No. 1 (MS. L. 15b).* External side view of Brunelleschi's Florentine basilica *San Lorenzo*, seen from the North.

### *C. Studies for a church design most suitable for preaching.*

The problem of which church design might meet the requirements of acoustics seems to have occupied Leonardo’s very specific attention. The label “teatro” (theatre) given to some of these sketches clearly shows which plan seemed to him most favorable for hearing the preacher’s voice.

Pl. XCVII, No. 1 (MS. B, 52). A rectangular building divided into three aisles with an apse on either side, ending in a semicircular theatre with tiered seating, as seen in ancient buildings. The pulpit is in the center. Leonardo wrote on the left side of the sketch: “teatro da predicare” (Theatre for preaching).

MS. B, 55ᵃ (see page 56, Fig. 1). A domed church based on the type in Pl. XCV, No. 1, shows four theatres occupying the apses and facing the square “coro” (choir), which is in the center between the four pillars of the dome.¹ The tiered arrangement of the seats is shown in the sketch above. At the spot marked B, Leonardo wrote teatri per uldire messa (rows of seats to hear mass), at T teatri, and at C coro (choir).

In MS. C. A. 260, there are slight sketches of two plans for rectangular choirs and two elevations of the altar and pulpit which seem to be connected to these plans.

In MS. Ash II, 8ᵃ (see p. 56 and 57. Fig. 2 and 3). “Locho dove si predica” (Place for preaching). A most unusual plan for a building. The interior is a portion of a sphere, the center of which is the top of a column intended to serve as the preacher’s pulpit. The inside is somewhat

---

¹ *The note teatro de predicar on the right side is, I believe, in the handwriting of Pompeo Leoni. J. P. R.*

--- Page 144 ---
like a theater, with many rows of seats arranged in steps. The preacher, standing on his high pedestal, would be heard by everyone. The exterior of the building is shown in Fig. 2. It is a circular structure with a diameter of about 140 feet. The lower part is a massive base, and the upper part is a dome. Numerous staircases on the outside lead to the various levels of the interior.

Fig. 1.

Fig. 2.

### D. Design for a Mausoleum.

Pl. XCVIII (P. V., 182. Order No. 2386). In the middle of a hilly landscape rises an artificial mountain in the shape of a gigantic cone, topped by an imposing temple. At two-thirds of the height, a terrace is carved out with six doorways forming entrances to galleries, each leading to three burial halls, designed to hold about five hundred funeral urns arranged in the traditional ancient style. From two opposite sides, steps ascend to the terrace in a single flight and continue beyond it to the temple above. A large circular opening, like the one in the Pantheon, is located in the dome above what may be the altar, or perhaps the central monument on the level of the terrace below.

The cross-section of a gallery shown in the sketch at the bottom right shows the roof to be built on the principle of stacked horizontal layers, each projecting beyond the one below it, and each fitted with a type of ledge that appears to be undercut to give the appearance of a beam from the inside. Only granite would be strong enough for the dimensions given here for the keystone, as the thickness of the layers can hardly be less than a foot. Using this as the basis for calculating the dimensions of the entire structure, the width of the chamber would be about 25 feet; however, judging by the number of urns it contains—and there is no reason to assume these urns were larger than usual—it would seem to be no more than about 8 or 10 feet.

The construction of the vaults resembles those in the galleries of some Etruscan burial mounds, such as the Regulini Galeassi tomb at Cervetri (recently discovered), as well as the chamber and passages of the pyramid of Cheops and the treasury of Atreus at Mycenae.

The upper cone shows similarities not only with the monuments mentioned in the note, but also with Etruscan burial mounds, such as the Cocumella.

--- Page 151 ---
Page 58'
PLATE XCVII.
Printed by Eudes.

[Keywords: engraving, architecture, vault, archaeology, landscape]

The upper cone shows similarities not only with the monuments mentioned in the note, but also with Etruscan burial mounds, such as the Cocumella at Vulci, and even more so with the tomb of Porsenna at Clusium, as described by Varro. The entire design is a highly original attempt to revive the architectural forms of the earliest antiquity. In the foreground, a wide, vaulted passage leads into the interior of the mountain. The masonry is composed of massive, horizontal stone blocks, and the entrance is shaped by a sturdy arch that anchors the structure into the surrounding terrain. The landscape is rendered with simple, expressive lines, suggesting a rugged and hilly environment that emphasizes the monumental scale of the construction. This plate serves as a technical study of primitive structural principles applied to a grand, visionary scale, blending the fields of archaeology and architectural design.

### *E. Studies for the Central Tower, or Tiburio of Milan Cathedral.*

Toward the end of the fifteenth century, the Board of Works for the Cathedral <note>original Italian: "Fabbricceria del Duomo"</note> had to decide on a model for the central tower crowning this massive building. We learn from a notice published by G. L. Calvi¹ that the artists who submitted models in 1488 included: Bramante, Pietro da Gorgonzola, Luca Fancelli <note>original: "Paperio"</note>, and Leonardo da Vinci.

Several of Leonardo's sketches refer to this important project:

*Plate CXIX, No. 2 (Manuscript S. K. III, No. 36ᵃ): a small plan of the entire building. The projecting chapels in the middle of the transept are missing here. The nave appears to be shortened and seems to be entered through an inner "vestibule" <note>original Italian: "vestibolo"</note>.*

*Plate C, No. 2 (Tr. 21): Plan of the octagonal tower, showing the arrangement of the buttresses. These start from the eight pillars adjoining the four principal piers and are intended to support the eight angles of the <term>tiburio</term> <note>the lantern or decorative tower built over the crossing of a cathedral</note>. These buttresses correspond exactly with those described by Bramante as existing in the model presented by Giovanni Antonio Amadeo <note>original: "Omodeo"</note>.²*

*Plate C, 3 (Manuscript Tr. 16): Two plans showing different arrangements of the buttresses, which seem to be formed partly by the intersection of a system of pointed arches such as that seen in:*

*Plate C, No. 5 (Manuscript B, 27ᵃ): intended to give a broader base to the drum <note>the circular or polygonal wall supporting a dome</note>. The text underneath is provided under No. 788.*

*Manuscript B, 3: three slight sketches of plans related to the preceding ones.*

---

¹ G. L. CALVI, *Notes on the life and works of the principal architects, sculptors and painters who flourished in Milan* <note>original Italian: "Notizie sulla vita e sulle opere dei principali architetti scultori e pittori che fiorirono in Milano"</note>, Part III, 20. See also: H. DE GEYMÜLLER, *The original projects etc.* <note>original French: "Les projets primitifs etc."</note> I, 37 and 116–119. The <term>Board of Works for the Cathedral</term> <note>original Italian: "Fabbricceria of the Duomo"</note> has recently begun the publication of its archives, which may possibly tell us more about the part taken by Leonardo than has been known until now.
² Bramante's opinion was first published by G. MONGERI, *Lombard Historical Archive* <note>original Italian: "Arch. stor. Lomb."</note> V, volume 3, and afterward by me in the publication mentioned in the preceding note.

ECCLESIASTICAL ARCHITECTURE.

Plate XCIX, No. 1 (MS. Tr. 15) contains several small sketches of sections and exterior views of the Dome; some of them show buttress-walls shaped as inverted arches. Regarding these, Leonardo notes:

758.

The inverted arch is better for providing a support than the ordinary one, because the former finds beneath it a wall resisting its weak point, while the ordinary arch finds nothing but air at its weak point.

Three slight sketches of sections on the same leaf—above those reproduced here—are more closely connected with the large drawing in the center of Plate C, No. 4 (MS. Tr. 41), which shows a section of a very elevated dome with double vaults, connected by ribs and buttresses ingeniously arranged so as to bring the weight of the lantern to bear on the base of the dome.

A sketch underneath it shows a round pillar on which is indicated which part of its summit is to bear the weight: “The column will bear the weight at a b.” Another note is above on the right side: “The arch will support as much below it [i.e., a hanging weight] as above it.”

Plate C, No. 1 (C. A. 303a). A larger sketch of a half-section of the Dome, with a very complicated system of arches and a double vault. Each stone is shaped so as to be joined or dovetailed to its neighbors. Thus, the inside of the Dome cannot be seen from below.

MS. C. A. 303b. A repetition of the preceding sketch with very slight modifications.

Fig. 1. Fig. 2.

MS. Tr. 9 (see Fig. 1 and 2). A section of the Dome with inverted buttresses between the windows, above which iron anchors or chains seem to be intended. Below is the sketch of the exterior.

--- Page 158 ---
Plate XCIX, No. 3 (C. A., 262a) four sketches of the exterior of the Dome.

C. A. 12. Section, showing the points of rupture of a gothic vault, clearly connected with the sketches described above.

It is worth noting how easily and apparently without effort Leonardo manages to combine gothic details and structure with the more modern shape of the Dome.

The following notes are on the same leaf: "every heavy thing," and "every heavy thing desires to descend"; further below, several multiplications most likely intended to calculate the weight of certain parts of the Dome, such as 16 × 47 = 720; 720 × 800 = 176000, next to which is written: "weight of the pillar 9 diameters high."

Below: 176000 × 8 = 1408000; and below:
"Six million six hundred (?) 80 the weight of the tiburio"
(six million six hundred (?) 80 the weight of the Dome).

[Architectural profiles] Bossi proposed the theory that Leonardo might have been the architect who built the church of Sta. Maria delle Grazie, but there is no evidence to support this, either in documents or in the materials provided by Leonardo’s manuscripts and drawings. The sketch shown at the side displays the arrangement of the second and third socle on the apses of the choir of that church; and it is remarkable that those sketches, in MS. S. K. M. II2, 2a and 1b, appear with the passage given in Volume I as No. 665 and 666 referring to the composition of the Last Supper in the Refectory of that church.

--- Page 159 ---
### F. The Project for lifting the Florence Baptistery and placing it on a basement.

Among the very few details Vasari provides regarding Leonardo’s architectural studies, we read: “And among these models and designs there was one by which he demonstrated several times to many ingenious citizens who then governed Florence, his readiness to lift the church of San Giovanni in Florence (the Baptistery, opposite the Cathedral) without damaging it, in order to place under it the missing basement with steps; he supported his claims with reasons so persuasive that while he spoke the undertaking seemed feasible, although every one of his listeners, once he had left, could see for himself the impossibility of such a vast undertaking.”

In the manuscript Codex Atlanticus, folio 293, there are two sketches which might possibly relate to this bold enterprise. We find there a plan of a circular or polygonal building surrounded by semicircular arches in an oblique position. These may be interpreted as the foundation for the steps and the new platform. In the perspective elevation, the same building, forming a polygon, is shown lifted up and resting on a circle of inverted arches; these rest on another circle of arches in the ordinary position, but placed so that the inverted arches above rest on the spandrels of the lower range.

What seems to confirm the theory that the lifting of a building is the subject here is the indication of machines for winding upward, such as jacks, a rack, and a wheel. As the lifting apparatus represented on this sheet does not seem particularly applicable to an undertaking of such magnitude, we may consider it to be a first sketch or scheme for the engines to be used.

---

1 This latter statement by Vasari must be considered an exaggeration. I may refer here to some data given by LIBRI, *History of Mathematical Sciences in Italy* (II, 216, 217): “In recent times, people believed they had performed a mechanical miracle by carrying out such a transport; however, as early as the year 1455, Gaspard Nadi and Aristote de Fioravantio had transported the Magione tower of Bologna, along with its foundations, a considerable distance, despite it being nearly eighty feet high. The continuator of the chronicle of Pugliola says that the distance was 35 feet and that during the transport, which the chronicler claims to have witnessed, a serious accident occurred that caused the tower to lean three feet while it was suspended, but this accident was promptly repaired (Muratori, *Italian Writers*, Vol. XVIII, col. 717, 718). Alidosi reported a note where Nadi gives an account of this transport with rare simplicity. According to this note, it is clear that operations of this kind were not new. This one cost only 150 livres (currency of that time), including the gift that the Legate gave to the two mechanics. In the same year, Aristote straightened the bell tower of Cento, which leaned by more than five feet (Alidosi, *Instruction* p. 188 — Muratori, *Italian Writers*, vol. XXIII, col. 888. — Bossi, *Milanese Chronicle*, 1492, folio, for the year 1455). It is inconceivable how historians of the fine arts could have overlooked such men.” J. P. R.

--- Page 160 ---
### G. Description of an unknown Temple.

Twelve flights of steps led up to the great temple, which was eight hundred braccia in circumference and built in an octagonal shape. At the eight corners, eight large plinths were placed, each one and a half braccia high, three wide, and six long at the base, with an angle in the middle. Upon these, eight great pillars were founded; they rose from the solid part of the base for a distance of twenty-four braccia. At their tops were set eight capitals, each three braccia long and six wide. Above these followed the architrave, frieze, and cornice, with a height of four and a half braccia, which extended in a straight line from one pillar to the next; thus, with a circuit of eight hundred braccia, it surrounded the temple between each pillar. To support this entablature, ten large columns were established, of the same height as the pillars and with a thickness of three braccia above their bases, which were one and a half braccia high.
   The ascent to this temple was by twelve flights of steps, and the temple was founded upon the twelfth step in an octagonal form. Above each angle rose a large pillar, and between the pillars were placed ten columns of the same height as the pillars.

### V. Palace architecture.

Only a small number of Leonardo’s drawings refer to the architecture of palaces, and our knowledge is limited regarding what style Leonardo might have adopted for such buildings.

Plate CII No. 1 (W. XVIII). A small portion of a palace façade in two stories, somewhat resembling Alberti’s *Palazzo Rucellai*.—Compare this with Bramante’s painted front of the *Casa Silvestri*, and a painting by Montorfano in San Pietro in Gessate at Milan, in the third chapel on the left-hand side, and also with Bramante’s palaces at Rome. The pilasters with arabesques, the rustication between them, and the figures over the window may be painted or in sgraffito. The original is drawn in red chalk.

Plate LXXXI No. 1 (MS. Tr. 42). Sketch of a palace with battlements and decorations, most likely graffiti; the details remind us of those in the Castello at Vigevano.^1

MS. Mz. 0^a contains a design for a palace or house with a loggia in the middle of the first story, over which rises an attic with a pediment reproduced on page 67. The details drawn nearby on the left seem to indicate an arrangement of coupled columns against the wall of a first story.

Plate LXXXV No. 14 (MS. S. K. M. III 79^a) contains a very slight

| FOR MAKING A CLEAN STABLE. |
|----------------------------|
| The manner in which a stable must be arranged: You shall first divide its width into 3 parts, while its length may be of any size. Let these 3 divisions be equal, each 6 braccia wide and 10 high. The middle part shall be for the use of the stablemasters, and the 2 side parts for the horses. Each horse must have a space 6 braccia wide and 6 long, and the floor should be half a braccio higher at the front than at the back. Let the manger be 2 braccia from the ground; the bottom of the rack should be at 3 braccia and the top at 4 braccia. Now, to achieve what I promise—which is to make this place clean and neat, contrary to common practice—regarding the upper part of the stable where the hay is kept: that area must have a window at its outer end 6 braccia high and 6 wide, through which the hay can be easily brought up to the loft, as shown by machine E. This loft should be located in a space 6 braccia wide and as long as the stable, as seen at k p. The other 2 parts that flank this middle section should each be divided into 2 parts: the two parts toward the hayloft shall be 4 braccia wide, p s, intended solely for the duties and movement of the stable servants; the other 2 parts bordering the outer walls shall be 2 braccia wide, as seen at s k. These are for the purpose of feeding hay into the mangers through chutes that are narrow at the top and wide over the mangers, so that the hay does not get stuck along the way. These chutes must be well-plastered and clean, as illustrated where marked 4 f s. Regarding the watering of the horses, the mangers should be made of stone with water supplied above them, so that the mangers can be uncovered like boxes by lifting their lids. |

### *VI. Studies of architectural details.*

*Several of Leonardo’s drawings of architectural details prove that, like other great masters of that period, he had devoted his attention to the study of the proportions of such details. Just as every organic being in nature has its own law of construction and growth, these masters endeavored, each in his own way, to discover and prove a law of proportion in architecture. The following notes in Leonardo’s manuscripts refer to this subject.*

->Fig. 1.<-

->Fig. 2.<-

*MS. S. K. M. III, 47b (see Fig. 1). A diagram indicating the rules given by Vitruvius and by Leon Battista Alberti for the proportions of the Attic base of a column.*

*MS. S. K. M. III 55a (see Fig. 2). A diagram showing the same rules.*

^10 The capital must be shaped in this way: divide its thickness at the top into 8 parts. ^11 At the base, make it 5/7 of a foot, and let its height also be 5/7, so that it forms a square. Then, divide the height into 8 parts, just as you did for the column. ^12 Then, set 1/8 for the *echinus* <term>echinus: a rounded molding just below the abacus of a capital</term> and another eighth for the thickness of the *abacus* <term>abacus: the flat slab forming the topmost member of a capital</term> that sits on top of the capital. ^13 The "horns" <note>original: *corni*; the projecting corners of the abacus</note> of the abacus of the capital must project 2/7 beyond the widest part of the *bell* <term>bell: the central, vase-shaped core of a capital</term>. ^14 This means that 1/7 of the width of the top of the bell is assigned to the projection of each horn. ^15 The truncated <note>original: *mozzatura*</note> part of the horns should be as wide as it is high, which is 1/8. ## 767.

A cylinder is a body with a columnar shape, and its two opposite faces are two circles ^3 set in parallel. ^4 Between their centers extends a straight ^5 line that passes through the middle of the thickness ^6 of the cylinder and ends at the centers ^7 of these circles; the ancients called this line the *axis*. <note>original: *axis*</note>

---

<margin>H.3 73b]</margin>

## 768.

^1 $a$ to $b$ is 1/3 of $n$ to $m$; ^2 $m$ to $o$ is 1/6 of $r$ to $o$; ^3 the *ovolo* <term>ovolo: a convex, egg-shaped molding</term> projects 1/6 of $r$ to $o$; ^4 $s$ to 7 is 1/5 of $r$ to $o$. ^5 Divide $a$ to $b$ into 9 and 1/2 parts. ^6 The abacus is 3/9; ^7 the ovolo is 4/9; ^8 the *astragal* <note>original: *fusaiolo*; a small, rounded bead-like molding</note> and the *fillet* <term>fillet: a narrow, flat decorative band</term> are 2/9 and 1/2.

---

<note>Leonardo wrote entry 767 in the margin of a page in the "Trattato di Architettura" by Francesco di Giorgio Martini. That page contains several drawings of columns, including a profile of a head sketched inside the outline of a capital. Entry 768 refers to Plate LXXXV, No. 16; in the original manuscript, both the drawing and text were created using red chalk.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of architectural elements, shifting from the geometry of column capitals and cylinders to the structural integrity of architraves and decorative motifs used in Milanese architecture.</meta>

<header>ARCHITECTURAL DESIGNS. [769.]</header>

*Plate LXXXV No. 6 (Manuscript Ashburnham II 6<sup>b</sup>) contains a small sketch of a capital with the following note, written in three lines:*

"The horns <note>original: *corni*; the projecting corners of the abacus</note> of the capital must be the fourth part of a square." <note>original Italian: *I chorni del capitelo, deono essere la quarta parte d'uno quadro*</note>

*Manuscript S. K. M. III 72<sup>b</sup> contains two sketches of ornamentation for windows.*

*In Manuscript Codex Atlanticus 308<sup>a</sup>; 938<sup>a</sup> (see Plate LXXXII No. 1), there are several sketches of columns. One of the two columns on the right is similar to those used by Bramante at the Canonica di San Ambrogio <note>Donato Bramante (1444–1514) was a key architect of the High Renaissance; the Canonica is part of the Basilica of Sant'Ambrogio in Milan</note>. The same columns appear in the sketch beneath the plan of a castle. There, they appear in pairs, stacked in two stories one above the other. The archivolts <note>the ornamental molding or band following the curve on the underside of an arch</note>, which seem to spring out of the columns, are shaped like twisted cords, perhaps intended to represent twisted branches. The walls between the columns appear to be made from blocks of wood, and the pedestals are decorated with a reticulated <note>net-like or crisscross</note> pattern. From this, we may assume that Leonardo was imagining either a decoration for a festival or perhaps a pavilion for a hunting lodge or park. The sketch of columns marked "35" provides an example of columns shaped like candelabras, a style frequently used at that time, particularly in Milan and the surrounding areas—for example, in the courtyard of Casa Castiglione (now Silvestre), in the Cathedral of Como, at the Porta della Rana, and elsewhere.*

---

<margin>G. 52 a]</margin>

### 769.

| CONCERNING ARCHITRAVES OF ONE AND OF SEVERAL PIECES. | CONCERNING ARCHITRAVES OF ONE OR SEVERAL PIECES. |
| :--- | :--- |
| <sup>3</sup> An architrave made of several pieces is stronger than one made of a <sup>4</sup> single piece, provided those pieces are positioned with their lengths <sup>5</sup> directed toward the center of the world <note>the center of the Earth, representing the direction of gravity</note>. ### XIII.
## Theoretical writings on Architecture.

*Leonardo's original writings on the theory of architecture have come down to us only in a fragmentary state; nevertheless, there seems to be no doubt that he did not complete them himself. It appears that Leonardo intended to write a large and connected book on architecture; it is quite evident that the materials we possess, which can be proven to have been written at different periods, were noted down with a more or less definite aim and purpose. They might all be collected under the single title: "Studies on the Strength of Materials." Among them, the investigations on the subject of fissures <note>cracks or splits in a structure</note> in walls are particularly thorough and very fully reported. These passages are also especially interesting because Leonardo was certainly the first writer on architecture who ever treated the subject at all. Here, as in all other cases, Leonardo carefully avoids all abstract argument. His data are not derived from the principles of algebra, but from the laws of mechanics, and his method throughout is strictly experimental.*

*Though the conclusions drawn from his investigations may not have that precision which we are accustomed to find in Leonardo's scientific labors, their interest is not diminished. They prove, at any rate, his deep wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note> and wonderfully clear mind. No one, perhaps, who has studied these questions since Leonardo has combined a scientific mind with anything like the artistic delicacy of perception which gives interest and clarity <note>original: "lucidity"</note> to his observations.*

*I do not claim <note>original: "assert"</note> that the arrangement adopted here for the passages in question is that originally intended by Leonardo; however, their distribution into five groups was suggested by the titles, or headings, which Leonardo himself prefixed to most of these notes. Some of the longer sections, perhaps, should not, in order to be in strict agreement with this division—* <meta>The text ends mid-sentence as it transitions to the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci's fragmented writings on architecture, highlighting his pioneering experimental research into the "strength of materials" and the causes of structural cracks in walls.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, architectural theory, strength of materials, structural engineering, fissures, experimental method, mechanics</keywords>

*...sion <note>This is the conclusion of the word "discussion" or "version" from the previous page.</note>, have been reproduced in their entirety in the places where they occur. However, because we possess a relatively small amount of material, they will still be easy enough for the reader to understand. Therefore, it did not seem necessary or desirable to further divide the passages just for the sake of strict categorization <note>original: "classification"</note>.*

Only a small selection of these is included in this work, as the majority of them have no accompanying explanatory text.*

### I.

## ON FISSURES IN WALLS.

->770.<-

<margin>British Museum Manuscript, 157 a]</margin>

First, write the treatise on the causes that produce <note>original: "gieneratrici," literally "generating"</note> cracks in walls, and then, in a separate section, discuss the remedies.

Parallel fissures <note>original: "fessi paralelli"</note> are generally produced in buildings erected on hillsides when the hill is composed of stratified rocks with slanted layers <note>original: "obliquo faldamento"</note>. This happens because water and other moisture often penetrate these slanted seams, carrying in greasy and slippery soil. Because these layers are not continuous down to the bottom of the valley, the rocks slide in the direction of the slope, and this motion does not cease until they have reached the valley floor. In the process, they carry with them—as if in a boat—that portion of the building which has been separated from the rest of the structure.

The remedy for this is to build frequent, thick piers <note>original: "pilastri"</note> under the wall that is slipping, with arches spanning from one to another. These must be well-anchored <note>original: "abbarbicati," literally "rooted"</note>, and the piers themselves must be firmly founded in the rock layers that have not broken away.

To find the stable part of these layers, it is necessary to dig a shaft <note>original: "pozzo," literally a "well"</note> of great depth at the foot of the wall, cutting through the layers. In this shaft, on the side from which the hill slopes, smooth and flatten a surface the width of a palm...

---

**770.** <note>These notes refer to specific variations and spellings in Leonardo's original manuscript transcription.</note> 1. causes <note>original: "chause"</note>. 3. [of] are <note>original: "sono"</note>. 4. that are built <note>original: "chessi edifichano"</note> in places. 5. composed <note>original: "chōposti"</note>... with oblique <note>original: "chon obbriquo"</note>. 8. and slippery <note>original: "essdrucciolente"</note>. 9. continuous <note>original: "chontinovate"</note>. 10. such... they move <note>original: "simovan"</note>. 12. until they descend <note>original: "cheddisciendano"</note>. 13. with them <note>original: "chonsecho"</note>... boat <note>original: "barcha"</note>. 16. the remedy <note>original: "lrimedio"</note>... thick <note>original: "spe"</note>. 17. piers <note>original: "pilasstri"</note>... which are <note>original: "chessi"</note>. 18. with <note>original: "chon"</note>. 19. anchored <note>original: "abarbatiati"</note> these. 20. piers <note>original: "pilasstri"</note>. 21. and firm <note>original: "effermi"</note>. 22. broken <note>original: "rutte"</note>. 23. for <note>original: "per [del]"</note>. 24. shaft <note>original: "pozzo [no]"</note> under... with <note>original: "chō"</note>. 25. shaft <note>original: "pozo"</note>... with <note>original: "chō"</note>.

...a space one palm wide from the top to the bottom; and after some time, this smooth portion made on the side of the shaft will plainly show which part of the hill is moving.

### 771.

The cracks in walls will never be parallel unless the part of the wall that separates from the rest does not slip down.

### WHAT IS THE LAW BY WHICH BUILDINGS HAVE STABILITY.

The stability of buildings is the result of the law contrary to the two previous cases. That is to say, the walls must all be built up equally and by degrees to equal heights all around the building, including the entire thickness of every kind of wall. And although a thin wall dries more quickly than a thick one, it will not necessarily break under the weight it may acquire from day to day; because if a wall of half the thickness dries in one day, one of double the thickness will dry in two days or thereabouts. Thus, the small difference in weight will be balanced by the small difference in time.

The adversary says that *a*, which projects, slips down.
And here the adversary says that *r* slips and not *c*.

### HOW TO PREDICT THE CAUSES OF CRACKS IN ANY SORT OF WALL.

The part of the wall which does not slip is that in which the slant projects and overhangs the portion of the wall that has parted from it and slipped down.

### ON THE PLACEMENT OF FOUNDATIONS AND IN WHAT PLACES THEY CAUSE RUIN.

When the crack in the wall is wider at the top than at the bottom, it is a clear sign that the cause of the wall's failure is far from the perpendicular line of that crack.

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 771, which discussed the stability of buildings and the causes of vertical cracks in foundations and walls.</meta>

<page-num>772.], 79</page-num>
<header>ON FISSURES IN WALLS.</header>

## ON CRACKS IN WALLS THAT ARE WIDE AT THE BOTTOM AND NARROW AT THE TOP, AND THEIR CAUSES <note>original: "DELLE FESSURE DE’ MURI, LE QUALI SŌ LARGHE DA PIÈ E STRETTE DA CAPO E LOR CAUSA."</note>

That wall will always crack if it does not dry uniformly over the same period of time.

Even a wall of uniform thickness will not dry in the same amount of time if it is not in contact with the same medium throughout. For example, if one part of a wall were built against a damp slope <note>original: "monte" (literally 'mountain' or 'mound')</note> and the rest were left exposed to the air, the part in the air would shrink in every direction while the damp part would maintain its original size. In this case, the portion drying in the air shrinks and diminishes, but the moistened part does not dry. The dry section will easily break away from the damp section because the damp part lacks the tenacity <note>internal cohesion or strength</note> to follow the movement of the part that is continuously drying.

## ON ARCHED CRACKS THAT ARE WIDE AT THE TOP AND NARROW AT THE BOTTOM <note>original: "DELLI FESSI ARCATI LARGHI DI SOPRA E STRETTI DI SOTTO."</note>

Arched cracks that are wide at the top and narrow at the bottom occur in walled-up doors <note>doorways that have been filled in with masonry after the original wall was built</note>. These shrink more in their height than in their width because the height is greater than the width, and because the mortar joints <note>original: "commessure della calcina"</note> are more numerous vertically than they are horizontally.

The crack narrows less between points *r* and *o* than between points *m* and *n*, because there is less material between *r* and *o* than between *m* and *n*.

Every crack formed in a concave wall is wide at the bottom and narrow at the top. This happens as shown by the figures *b*, *c*, and *d* in the diagram on the side.

1. That which becomes moist expands in proportion to the moisture it absorbs.

2. Everything damp shrinks during the drying process in proportion to the amount of moisture that evaporates from it.

<vocab>fessure: cracks or fissures; muri: walls; secca: dries; arched cracks: curved fractures in masonry; calcina: lime mortar; restrignie: shrinks or contracts; larghezza: width; altezza: height</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how uneven drying and moisture absorption cause walls to crack, specifically noting how the number of mortar joints affects shrinkage in walled-up doorways.</summary>
<keywords>masonry, structural engineering, evaporation, mortar joints, thermal expansion, moisture, architectural failure</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
## ON THE CAUSES OF CRACKING IN PUBLIC AND PRIVATE BUILDINGS.

Walls break through fissures, some of which are vertical and others oblique. Vertical cracks are caused by joining new walls to old vertical walls, whether joined flush or with interlocking teeth fitted into the old wall. Because these interlocking teeth cannot support the unbearable weight of the wall joined to them, they must necessarily break and allow for the settling of the aforementioned new wall. This wall sinks one braccio for every ten braccia, more or less, depending on the greater or lesser amount of mortar placed between the masonry stones and whether the mortar is more or less liquid. Note that one must always build the walls first and then face them with the stones intended to cover them. If this is not done, because the main wall settles more than the exterior facing, the interlocking joints on the sides of the walls would inevitably break. This is because the stones facing the walls, being larger than the stones they cover, necessarily require a smaller amount of mortar in their joints and consequently settle less; this problem cannot occur if such facings are built after the wall is dry.

a b is the new wall, c is the old wall which has already settled; and a b settles afterward. Although section a, being founded upon the old wall c, cannot break in any way because it has a stable foundation on the old wall, the rest of the new wall b will break away. This is because it is built from the top of the building down to the bottom; the remainder of the new wall will then overhang like a corbel above the wall that has sunk.

### 775.

**OF STONES WHICH SEPARATE FROM THEIR MORTAR.**

Stones laid in equal numbers throughout their height, built with an equal quantity of mortar, settle equally during the evaporation of the moisture that softened the mortar.

From the previous point, it is proved that the small section of the new wall placed between $A$ and $n$ will settle very little compared to the section of the same wall placed between $c$ and $d$. The proportion of the settling will be the same as the proportion between the amounts of mortar; that is, the proportion of the numbers or quantities of mortar placed in the joints of the stones built above the various heights of the old walls.

## II.

## ON FISSURES IN NICHES.

| ON FISSURES IN NICHES. <note>original: "DELLE ROTTURE DELLI NICHI"</note> | ON FISSURES IN NICHES. |
|:---|:---|
| ² An arch made from a semicircle, which is loaded at the ³ two opposite thirds of its curvature, will break in ⁴ five places along its curve. This is proven as follows: let the ⁵ weights <note>original: "pessi" (pieces/weights)</note> be *n* and *m*, which break this arch *a b f*. I say, according to what was ⁶ previously stated, that as the ends *c* and *a* are equally burdened by the thrust *n*, ⁷ it follows, by the 5th principle <note>Leonardo refers to his own numbered axioms of mechanics</note>, that the arch will break in the part furthest from the ⁸ two forces that press it, which is the middle point *e*. I ⁹ mean the same to be understood regarding the opposite arch *d g b*. Therefore, ¹⁰ the weights *n* and *m* must descend; but they cannot descend, ¹¹ according to the 7th principle, without coming closer together. They cannot approach ¹² each other unless the arch that is positioned between them brings its ¹³ ends closer; and these ends cannot draw together without the breaking of ¹⁴ its middle. Thus, the arch will break in two places <note>This refers to the two primary failure points at the center of each weighted segment</note> as was first ¹⁵ stated, etc. | An arch constructed on a semicircle and bearing weights on the two opposite thirds of its curve will give way at five points of the curve. To prove this, let the weights be at *n m* which will break the arch *a, b, f*. I say that, by the foregoing rules, *as the extremities c and a are equally pressed upon by the thrust n, it follows, by the 5th principle, that the arch will give way at the point which is furthest from the two forces acting on them—and that is the middle e. The same is to be understood of the opposite curve, d g b;* hence the weights *n m* must sink, but they cannot sink, by the 7th principle, without coming closer together; and they cannot come together unless the extremities of the arch between them come closer. If these draw together, the crown <note>The "crown" is the highest point or top of an arch</note> of the arch must break; and thus the arch will give way in two places as was at first said, etc. |
| ¹⁶ Question <note>original: "Domāda"</note> regarding the weight given at *a*: what portion of it responds along the ¹⁷ *n–f* line, and with what weight must the weight placed at *f* be overcome? | I ask: given a weight at *a*, what counteracts it in the direction *n–f*, and by what weight must the weight at *f* be counteracted? |

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci explains the physics of arch failure, demonstrating how weights placed on the "thirds" of a semicircular arch force the structure to move inward and break at the crown.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, architecture, statics, arch mechanics, structural failure, niches, physics of buildings, thrust and resistance</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
### ON THE SHRINKING OF DAMP BODIES OF VARYING THICKNESS AND WIDTH.

The window $a$ is the cause of the crack at $b$; and this crack is increased by the weight of $n$ and $m$, which sink or penetrate into the soil where the foundations are built more than the lighter portion at $b$ does. Furthermore, the old foundation under $b$ has already settled, whereas the piers $n$ and $m$ have not yet done so. Consequently, part $b$ does not settle vertically; instead, it is thrust outward at an angle. It cannot be thrust inward because such a section, once separated from the main wall, is wider on the outside than on the inside, and the gap in the remaining wall is the same shape. If this separated section were to move inward, the larger part would have to pass through the smaller, which is impossible. Therefore, it is concluded that a section of such a semicircular wall must necessarily separate from the whole by being thrust outward at the bottom, and not inward as the opponent claims.

When full or half-domes are overwhelmed from above by excessive weight, their vaults will crack. This crack will be tapered, being narrow at the top and wide at the bottom, and narrow on the inside while wide on the outside. This is similar to the peel of a pomegranate or an orange divided into several longitudinal sections; the more it is pressed from the opposite ends of its length, the more the joints will open in the areas furthest from the source of the pressure. For this reason, the arches of the vaults of any apse should never be more heavily loaded than the arches of the main building. This is because the heavier part exerts more pressure on what is beneath it and sinks further into its foundations, which cannot happen to lighter structures like the aforementioned apses.

--- Page 191 ---

#### To ensure that the smoke does not escape through the mouth of the kiln

The fire of the kiln should be arranged in such a way that the smoke circulates throughout the entire kiln and is forced to exit through the throat located above at *a* and *d*, so that the heat remains entirely within the kiln and does not escape through the throat.

---

The heat of the kiln shall be circulated in this manner: it shall be forced to circulate through the entire kiln before it exits through the throat. In this way, the kiln will heat up more quickly and with less wood; the heat will be much more intense and will achieve its effect in less time. This shall be the form of the kiln, as is shown above in the drawing.

#### To make a chimney that does not send smoke into the room even if the wind blows into it

Because the throat shall be narrow and the mouth shall be wide, the smoke will be forced to exit through the throat and cannot return back through the mouth.

# 778. ON FISSURES IN NICHES

| | |
|:---|:---|
| Which of these two cubes will shrink more uniformly: cube *A* resting on the floor, or cube *b* suspended in the air, given that both cubes are equal in weight and volume and are made of clay mixed with the same amount of moisture? <note>original Italian: Qual di questi due cubi diminuirà più uniformemente...</note> | Which of these two cubes will shrink more uniformly: the cube *A* resting on the floor, or the cube *b* suspended in the air, when both cubes are equal in weight and volume, and made of clay mixed with equal quantities of water? |
| The cube that rests on the floor shrinks more in its height than in its width. This does not happen to the cube above that is suspended in the air. This is proven as follows: the behavior of the cube resting on the floor is better illustrated below. <note>original Italian: Quel cubo che si posa sopra il pavimento più diminuisce della sua altezza...</note> | The cube placed on the floor shrinks more in its height than in its width, which the cube above, hanging in the air, cannot do. This is proven thus: The cube shown above is better illustrated here below. |
| The final shape of the two cylinders of fresh clay—namely *a* and *b*—will be the tapered <term>pyramidal</term> <note>original: figure piramidali; here referring to a shape that tapers due to deformation</note> figures *c* and *d* shown below. This is proven as follows: cylinder *a*, resting on its base and being made of clay mixed with a great deal of water, sinks down due to its own weight pressing on its base. It will settle and widen more at the parts closer to the base, because the base supports the weight of the entire mass, etc. A similar effect happens to weight *b*, which will stretch more the more weight it has hanging below it; this maximum tension occurs at the point where it is attached to its support. <note>original Italian: Il fine delli dua cilindri di terra fresca cioè a. b sarà le figure piramidali...</note> | The final result of the two cylinders of damp clay, *a* and *b*, will be the tapered figures *c* and *d* shown below. This is proved as follows: The cylinder *a*, resting on a block of stone and being made of clay mixed with a great deal of water, will sink under its own weight as it presses on its base. In proportion as it settles and spreads, all the parts will become somewhat nearer to the base because the base is burdened with the whole weight, etc. The case will be the same with the weight of *b*, which will stretch lengthwise in proportion to the weight hanging below it, and the greatest tension will be near the point where it is suspended. |

***

### III.

## ON THE NATURE OF THE ARCH.

<margin>Manuscript A. 50 a]</margin>
<margin>779.</margin>

### WHAT IS AN ARCH?<gloss>¹</gloss> <note>original Italian: "CHE COSA È ARCO"</note>
### WHAT IS AN ARCH?

² An arch is nothing other than a strength caused by two weaknesses. This is because an arch in a building is composed of two segments <note>original: "quarti," literally quarters or quadrants</note> of a circle; each of these segments, being very weak on its own, tends to fall. However, as they oppose each other's collapse, the two weaknesses are converted into a single strength. <note>original Italian: "Arco non è altro che una fortezza · cavsata da due debolezze..."</note>

The arch is nothing else than a force originated by two weaknesses, for the arch in buildings is composed of two segments of a circle, each of which, being very weak in itself, tends to fall; but as each opposes this tendency in the other, the two weaknesses combine to form one strength.

### ON THE NATURE<gloss>⁷</gloss> OF THE WEIGHT IN ARCHES. <note>original Italian: "DELLA QUALITÀ DEL PESO DELLI ARCHI"</note>
### OF THE KIND OF PRESSURE IN ARCHES.

⁸ Once the arch is constructed, it remains in equilibrium because each side pushes against the other with equal force. If one segment of the circle weighs more than the other, stability <note>original: "permanenza"</note> will be lost, because the greater weight will overcome the lesser. <note>original Italian: "Poichè l'arco fia · cōposto ·, quello · rimane in equilibrio..."</note>

As the arch is a composite force it remains in equilibrium because the thrust is equal from both sides; and if one of the segments weighs more than the other the stability is lost, because the greater pressure will outweigh the lesser.

### ON DISTRIBUTING THE LOAD<gloss>¹²</gloss> UPON ARCHES. <note>original Italian: "DEL CARICO DATO AGLI ARCHI"</note>
### OF DISTRIBUTING THE PRESSURE ABOVE AN ARCH.

¹³ After ensuring the segments of the circle have equal weight, it is necessary to place an equal load upon them from above; otherwise, the error mentioned above will occur. <note>original Italian: "Dopo il peso equale de' quarti circuli è neciessario dare loro equale peso di sopra..."</note>

Next to giving the segments of the circle equal weight it is necessary to load them equally, or you will fall into the same defect as before.

---

ON THE DAMAGE DONE TO THE POINTED ARCH BY PLACING THE PRESSURE ON ITS FLANKS.

An arch with a slight curve is stable on its own, but if it is loaded, the flanks must be well reinforced. An arch with a very large curve is weak on its own, but becomes stronger if it is loaded; it will cause little harm to its abutments, and its points of failure are $o$ and $p$.

781.

ON THE REMEDY FOR EARTHQUAKES.

The arch which directs its weight perpendicularly onto its foundations will fulfill its function regardless of its orientation, whether it is upside down, lying flat, or upright.

The arch will not break if the chord of the outer arch does not touch the inner arch. This is evident from experience, because whenever the chord $a$ $o$ $n$ of the outer arch $n$ $r$ $a$ touches the inner arch $x$ $b$ $y$, the arch begins to weaken; it will become weaker in proportion to how much the inner arch breaks through that chord. When an arch is loaded on only one of its sides, the weight will press upon the top of the other side and be transmitted.

--- Page 197 ---
to the spring of the arch on that side; and it will break at a point halfway between its two extremes, where it is farthest from the chord.

### 783.

### ON THE STRENGTH OF THE ARCH IN ARCHITECTURE.

The stability of an arch built by an architect depends on the tie-rod and on its flanks.

### ON THE POSITION OF THE TIE-ROD IN THE ABOVE-MENTIONED ARCH.

The position of the tie-rod is of equal necessity at the base of the arch and at the top of the perpendicular pier on which it rests. This is proved by the second rule of supports, which states: that part of a support has the least resistance which is farthest from its solid attachment. Therefore, since the top of the pier is the point farthest from its foundation, and the same applies to the opposite ends of the arch, which are the points farthest from the center (its true upper attachment), we have concluded that the tie-rod *a b* must be positioned so that its opposite ends are located between the four aforementioned extremes.

The opponent argues that this arch should be more than a semicircle, and that it will then not require a tie-rod because the ends will not thrust outward but inward, as shown in the extensions *a c* and *b d*. To this it must be answered that such a design is poor for five reasons. The first concerns strength, because it is proven that a circular parallel composed of two semicircles will only break where those semicircles join together, as shown in figure *n m*. Beyond this, it follows that there is a wider space between the ends of the semicircle than between the faces of the walls. The third reason is that the weight placed to counterbalance the strength of the arch diminishes in proportion to how much wider the arch supports are than the space between the piers. Fourthly, the piers are weakened to the extent that their parts at *c a b d* turn outward. The fifth reason is that all the material and weight of the

--- Page 199 ---

...arch which exceeds the semicircle is useless and harmful; and it should be noted here that a weight placed above the arch will break the arch more easily at $a \ b$—where the curve of the excess added to the semicircle begins—than if the pier were straight up to its contact with the semicircle.

### AN ARCH WHICH IS LOADED ABOVE ITS CENTER WILL BREAK AT ITS RIGHT AND LEFT QUARTERS.

This is proved by the seventh rule of this treatise, which says: the opposite ends of the supports are equally burdened by the weight suspended from them; therefore, the weight applied at $f$ is felt at $b \ c$, that is, half at each end. And by the third rule, which says: that part of a support of equal strength will break sooner which is farther from its attachment; from which it follows that... since $d$ is equally distant from the fixed points $f, e \dots$

### ON THE STRENGTH AND NATURE OF ARCHES, AND WHERE THEY ARE STRONG OR WEAK; AND THE SAME REGARDING COLUMNS.

That part of the arch which is flatter will offer less resistance to the weight placed upon it.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of arch mechanics and structural failure, specifically focusing on the points of fracture and the mathematical relationship between an arch's thickness and its load-bearing capacity.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
# WRITINGS ON ARCHITECTURE
<page-num>[786.]</page-num>

When the triangle $a$ $z$ $n$ <note>Referring to the triangular weight or masonry structure resting on top of the arch in the diagram.</note>, by settling, pushes back two-thirds of each half-arch <note>original: "arco"</note>—that is, $a \ s$ and likewise $z \ m$—the reason is that $a$ falls perpendicularly over $b$, and likewise $z$ is above $f$.

---

Every half-arch, if overcome by excessive weight, will break at two-thirds of its height. This point corresponds to a perpendicular line falling above the middle of its base, as is seen at $a \ b$. This happens because the weight tends to fall and pass through point $r$. If, contrary to its nature, it were to fall from point $s$, the arch $n \ s$ would break exactly in its middle. 

If the arch $n \ s$ were made of a single piece of timber <note>original: "legnio"</note>, and the weight placed at $n$ tended to fall along the line $n \ m$, the arch would break in the middle of the arc $e \ m$. Otherwise, it will break at the upper third at point $a$, because from $a$ to $n$ the arch is flatter <note>original: "piv piano"</note> than it is from $a$ to $o$, or from $o$ to $s$. In proportion as $p \ t$ is greater than $t \ n$, the section $a \ o$ will be stronger than $a \ n$; and likewise, $s \ o$ will be stronger than $o \ a$ in the same proportion as $r \ p$ is greater than $p \ t$.

An arch that is doubled in the square of its thickness <note>original: "grossezza"</note> will carry four times the weight that a single <note>original: "scēpio"</note> arch could. It becomes even stronger in proportion as the diameter of its thickness fits fewer times into its length. 

For example, if the thickness of a single arch fits 10 times into its length, the thickness of a doubled arch will fit only 5 times into its length. Therefore, as the thickness of the doubled arch fits into its length only half as many times as the single arch, it is reasonable that it should carry half as much more weight than it would if it were in simple proportion to the single arch. Thus, because this doubled arch has four times the volume of the single arch, it would seem that it should carry four times the weight; however, the rule stated above demonstrates that it actually supports exactly eight times as much.

---

ON THE NATURE OF THE ARCH.
787.

# THE PIER WHICH IS LOADED WITH THE MOST UNEQUAL WEIGHT WILL BE THE FIRST TO COLLAPSE.

Column c b, because it is loaded with an equal weight on each side, will be the most durable; and the other two outer columns require as much weight on the side outside of their center as there is on the side inside of their center—that is, from the center of the column toward the middle of the arch.

Arches that depend on the strength of chains for their support will not be durable.

---

# THAT ARCH WILL HAVE THE LONGEST PERPETUITY WHICH HAS A GOOD ABUTMENT OPPOSED TO ITS THRUST.

The arch by its very nature tends to fall. If the arch is 30 braccia and the interval between the walls that support it is 20, we know that 30 cannot pass through 20 unless 20 also becomes 30. Therefore, when the arch is overcome by excessive weight, it straightens itself out, and the walls, offering poor resistance, open up and provide space between them for the ruin of the arch. But if you do not wish to provide the arch with an iron tie-rod, you must give it such shoulders as will offer resistance to its thrust. You shall do it in this way: load the corners m and n with stones so that the lines of their joints are directed toward the center of the circle of the arch. And the reason why the arch will be durable is this: we know clearly that if one loads the arch at its quarter a b with excessive weight, the wall f g will be pushed outward because the arch will want to straighten itself; and if one were to load the other quarter b c, it would pull the wall f g inward, were it not for the line of stones x y which provides support.

787.

## FOUNDATION.

Here it is demonstrated how the arches made in the sides of the octagon thrust against the piers of the

--- Page 202 ---
of the angles outwards, as is shown by the line $h \cdot c$ and by the line $t \cdot d$ which thrust out the pier $m$; that is, they tend to force it away from the center of such an octagon.

### 788.

An experiment to show that a weight placed on an arch does not discharge itself entirely onto its columns; on the contrary, the greater the weight placed upon the arches, the less the arch transmits the weight to the columns. The experiment is the following: let a man be placed on a weighing scale in the middle of the shaft of a well; then let him spread out his hands and feet against the walls of the well, and you will see him weigh much less on the scale. Give him a weight on his shoulders, and you will see by experiment that the greater the weight you give him, the greater the effort he will make in spreading his arms and legs, and in pressing against the walls, and the less weight will be thrown onto the scale.

--- Page 203 ---
### On water and canals

To know how much water rises in canals due to the backflow of motions, do as follows: if it is a straight and level canal where the water falls into it from above, or from every part of the air; but if it is from vessels on a level surface where it falls evenly, and how much the water rises if it falls either from every part of the level air or if it is back-pressed water; do as was said above.

---

### On the union of water

That water which unites with other water in a stream or on a flat surface will have more weight due to the effect of the impact than that which falls without impact; furthermore, the union of water will be heavier at the center of its fall than at the sides of that column of water.

**Regarding the arch:**
That part of the weight which rests upon the arch exerts less load the more acute the arch is.

**Regarding the cord:**
The weight that hangs by a perpendicular cord pulls with as much force as its own weight.

---

### On motion and weight

**If there is motion:**
That object which moves in a straight line and encounters an obstacle which forces it to bend its straight line will experience as much more strain as the bend is more abrupt; furthermore, that object which falls along a perpendicular line will fall more readily than that which falls along an oblique line.

---

### On impact

The weight of water that falls upon another body of water will have as much more heaviness as its impact is more distant from the beginning of its motion.

--- Page 204 ---
The weight of water that falls upon another body of water will be as much heavier as its impact is further from the start of its motion. Furthermore, the water that falls through the air will be as much lighter as it is more divided into small parts.

**Regarding the impact of water:**
The impact of water that falls into a vessel will be as much heavier as the water is more united and falls from a greater height.

**Regarding the weight of water:**
Water that is in a vessel will weigh as much as the water that is outside the vessel, provided that the water is at the same level.

**Regarding the motion of water:**
Water that moves in a straight line will move as much faster as the line is more perpendicular. Furthermore, water that moves in a curved line will move as much slower as the curve is more abrupt.

**Regarding the pressure of water:**
The pressure of water that is in a vessel will be as much greater as the water is deeper. Furthermore, the pressure of water that is in a vessel will be as much greater at the bottom than at the sides.

--- Page 205 ---
### IV.

## ON FOUNDATIONS, THE NATURE OF THE GROUND AND SUPPORTS.

789.

The first and most important thing is stability. Regarding the foundations of the component parts of temples and other public buildings, the depths of the foundations must bear the same proportions to each other as the weight of material which is to be placed upon them. Every part of the depth of earth in a given space is composed of layers, and each layer is composed of heavier or lighter materials, the lowest being the heaviest. And this can be proved, because these layers have been formed by the sediment from water carried down to the sea by the current of rivers which flow into it. The heaviest part of this sediment was that which was first deposited, and so on by degrees; and this is the action of water when it becomes stagnant, having first carried the mud from where it originally flowed. And such layers of soil are visible in the banks of rivers, where their constant flow has cut through them and divided one hill from the other to a great depth; where in gravelly strata the waters have drained away, and because of this the material has dried—

--- Page 206 ---
...and turned into hard stone, especially in the case of that mud which was the finest; and this leads us to conclude that every part of the earth's surface was once at the center of the earth, and vice versa, etc.

---

### 790.

The part of a building's foundation that bears the most weight sinks the most, leaving the lighter part above it separated from itself. And the soil that is most compressed, being porous, yields the most. You must always design foundations so that they project equally beyond the load of their walls and piers, as shown at $m \ a \ b$. If you do as many people do—that is, make a foundation of uniform width all the way to the surface of the ground, and then place an unequal load on top of it, as shown at $b \ e$ and $e \ o$—the part of the foundation at $b \ e$ will weigh more because it is occupied by the corner pier. It will push its foundation downward more than the wall at $e \ o$ does, which does not entirely occupy its foundation and therefore exerts less pressure and sinks less. Consequently, as the pier $b \ e$ sinks, it disconnects and separates from the wall $e \ o$, as can be seen in most buildings that are cracked around such piers.

---

### 791.

Window $a$ is well-placed under window $c$, but window $b$ is poorly placed under the space $d$, because that space is without support and foundation. Therefore, remember never to make an opening beneath the spaces between windows.

--- Page 207 ---
ON SUPPORTS.

792.
VOL. II.

ON THE SUPPORTS.

A pillar whose thickness is increased will gain more than its expected strength in direct proportion to what it lacks in its proportional height.

**EXAMPLE.**

If a pillar ought to be 9 units of thickness high—that is to say, if it is one braccio thick, the rule sets it at 9 braccia high—and if you join 100 of these together in thickness, the resulting mass will be 10 braccia thick and 9 high. If the first pillar supported 10,000 pounds, because this second mass is only about as high as it is thick, and thus lacks 8 parts of its proportional length, it will support eight times more. That is to say, each joined pillar will be responsible for supporting 8 times more than when it was separate; so, if it previously supported ten thousand pounds, it will now sustain 90 thousand.

--- Page 208 ---

The angle that is most acute will offer the greatest resistance, and the most obtuse will be the weakest.

A beam that is more than 20 times as long as its greatest thickness will have little durability and will break in the middle; and remember that

**793.** The three smaller sketches accompany the text in the original, but the larger one is not directly connected with it. It is found on folio 89a of the same manuscript, and there we read in a note written underneath: *roof of the flagstaff of the castle*. Compare also Plate XCIII, No. 1.

The part that enters the wall should be soaked in hot pitch and encased in oak boards that have also been soaked. Every beam must pass through its walls and be secured on the other side of those walls with sufficient chains, because during earthquakes, beams are often seen to slip out of the walls, subsequently causing the walls and floors to collapse. However, if they are chained, they will hold the walls together firmly, and the walls in turn secure the floors. Furthermore, I remind you never to apply plaster over timber, because as the wood expands and contracts due to moisture and dryness, such floors frequently crack. Once their joints have cracked, they gradually turn to dust and create an ugly appearance.

Again, remember not to build floors supported by both arches and beams; for over time, the floor supported by the beams settles somewhat in its center, while that part of the floor supported by the arch remains in its place. Consequently, floors supported by two different types of structures appear, over time, to be shaped like hills.

## *Remarks on the style of Leonardo's architecture.*

A few remarks may here be added on the style of Leonardo's architectural studies. However incomplete, however small in scale, they allow us to establish a certain number of facts and probabilities, well worthy of consideration.

When Leonardo began his studies, the great name of Brunellesco was still the inspiration of all Florence, and we cannot doubt that Leonardo was open to it, since we find among his sketches the plan of the church of Santo Spirito¹ and a lateral view of San Lorenzo (Pl. XCIV No. 1), a plan almost identical with the chapel Degli Angeli, which was only begun by him (Pl. XCIV, No. 3), while among Leonardo's designs for domes several clearly betray the influence of Brunellesco's Cupola and the lantern of Santa Maria del Fiore².

The beginning of the second period of modern Italian architecture falls during the first twenty years of Leonardo's life. However, the new impetus given by Leon Battista Alberti either was not generally understood by his contemporaries, or those who appreciated it had no opportunity of showing that they did so. It was only when taken up by Bramante and developed by him to the highest rank of modern architecture that this new influence was generally felt. Now, the peculiar feature of Leonardo's sketches is that, like the works of Bramante, they appear to be the development and continuation of Alberti's.

---

¹ See Pl. XCIV, No. 2. Then only in the course of construction after the designs of Brunellesco, though he was already dead; finished in 1481.

² A small sketch of the tower of the Palazzo della Signoria (MS. C. A. 309) proves that he also studied medieval monuments.

--- Page 211 ---
101
ON THE STYLE OF LEONARDO'S ARCHITECTURE.

But a question arises here that is difficult to answer. Did Leonardo, until he left Florence, follow the direction set by the dominant school of Brunellesco, which would have produced his "first style," or had he already felt Alberti's influence before leaving Florence—either through his works (the Palazzo Rucellai and the facade of Santa Maria Novella) or through personal contact? Or was it not until he went to Milan that Alberti's work began to impress him through Bramante, who likely knew Alberti in Mantua around 1470? Bramante not only carried out Alberti's views and ideas but, through his designs for St. Peter's in Rome, proved himself the greatest of modern architects. When Leonardo went to Milan, Bramante had already been living there for many years. One of his earliest works in Milan was the church of Santa Maria presso San Satiro, on Via del Falcone¹.

Now, among Leonardo's studies of domes on Plates LXXXIV and LXXXV, and in Plate LXXX, we find several sketches that seem to me to have been suggested by Bramante's dome for this church.

Manuscripts B and Ashburnham II contain the plans for San Sepolcro, the pavilion in the Duke of Milan's garden, and two churches evidently inspired by the church of San Lorenzo in Milan.

Manuscript B also contains two notes relating to Pavia; one of them is a design for the sacristy of the Cathedral of Pavia, which cannot be dated later than 1492, and it likely relates to Leonardo's summons to Pavia on June 21, 1490². These and other considerations justify the conclusion that Leonardo made his studies of domes in Milan, probably between 1487 and 1492, in anticipation of the construction of one of Italy’s grandest churches, the Cathedral of Pavia. This may explain the decidedly Lombard-Bramantesque tendency in the style of these studies, among which only a few remind us of the forms of the domes of Santa Maria del Fiore and the Baptistery of Florence. Thus, although several of these sketches plainly reveal Bramante's influence when compared to his work, we find among the sketches of domes some that already show Bramante's classical style, of which the Tempietto of San Pietro in Montorio—his first building completed in Rome—is the primary example³.

On Plate LXXXIV is a sketch of the plan for a similar circular building; the Mausoleum on Plate XCVIII, as well as one of the pedestals for the statue of Francesco Sforza (Plate LXV), are of the same type.
