<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>ON THE STYLE OF LEONARDO'S ARCHITECTURE.</header>
<meta>The body text of this page was originally printed in italics to distinguish Jean Paul Richter's commentary from Leonardo’s own notes. This modernization continues the analysis of Leonardo’s architectural relationship with Bramante.</meta>

The drawings on Plate LXXXIV No. 2, Plate LXXXVI No. 1 and 2, and the ground floor <note>original: "ground flour"</note> of the building in the drawing on Plate XCI No. 2—featuring interesting decorations of gigantic statues in large niches—are also, I believe, more in the style Bramante adopted in Rome than in the Lombard style <note>The "Lombard style" refers to the regional architectural tradition of Northern Italy, characterized by ornate brickwork and terracotta, which Bramante used before moving to Rome.</note>. 

Are we to conclude from this that Leonardo, for his part, influenced Bramante by simplifying his style and making it more in harmony with ancient classical art? The answer to this important question seems difficult to give at first, for we are dealing here with Bramante, the greatest of modern architects, and Leonardo, a man comparable to no other. We have no knowledge of any actual buildings erected by Leonardo, and unless we assume they had a personal relationship—which seems probable, though there is no proof—it would be difficult to understand how Leonardo could have influenced Bramante’s style. It is easier to accept the opposite: that Bramante influenced Leonardo. This is because Bramante, as we have proved elsewhere, drew and built in different styles simultaneously. Although there is no building by him in his "classic" Roman style in the Lombardy region, his use of brick for construction in that part of Italy may easily explain why he worked differently there.

Bramante’s name is mentioned incidentally in Leonardo’s manuscripts in two passages (Nos. 1414 and 1448). On both occasions, it is only a brief, passing allusion, and the context provides no sufficient information regarding any close connection between the two artists.

It might be supposed, based on Leonardo’s relations with the East (discussed in sections XVII and XXI of this volume), that some evidence of Oriental influence might be detected in his architectural drawings. However, I do not think any such traces can be pointed out with certainty, with the possible exception of the drawing for a Mausoleum on Plate XCVIII.

Among several studies for the construction of domes <note>original: "cupolas"</note> over a Greek cross <note>A church plan where all four arms are of equal length.</note>, there are some in which the forms are decidedly monotonous. It is clear that these were not designed as models of beauty; they should be regarded as the results of specific investigations into the laws of proportion, harmony, and contrast.

The designs for churches based on the plan of a Latin cross <note>A church plan where one arm is longer than the other three, forming the traditional cross shape.</note> are evidently intended to deviate as little as possible from the form of a Greek cross. They also show a preference for a nave surrounded by outer porticos <note>A porch or walkway supported by columns.</note>.

The architectural forms preferred by Leonardo include coupled pilasters <note>A "pilaster" is a rectangular column, especially one projecting from a wall.</note> (Plate LXXXII No. 1) or grouped pilasters (Plate LXXX No. 5 and XCVI No. 4), often combined with niches. We frequently see superimposed orders—one for each story—or two small orders on a single story combined with one large "giant" order (Plate XCVI No. 2) <note>An "order" refers to the specific style of columns and entablature used; "superimposed" means they are stacked on top of one another.</note>.

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, Bramante, Architecture, Lombard style, Rome, Mausoleum, cupolas, Greek cross, Latin cross, pilasters, porticos, Jean Paul Richter</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses whether Leonardo da Vinci influenced the famous architect Bramante’s move toward a simpler, more classical style. It also examines Leonardo’s architectural preferences, such as his focus on Greek cross plans and his specific use of decorative pilasters and niches.</summary>

--- Page 213 ---
103
ON THE STYLE OF LEONARDO'S ARCHITECTURE.

The drum (*tamburo*) of these cupolas is generally octagonal, as in the cathedral of Florence, and features similar round windows in its sides. In Pl. LXXXVII No. 2, it is circular, like the model actually executed by Michael Angelo at St. Peter's.

The cupola itself is either hidden under a pyramidal roof, as in the Baptistery of Florence, San Lorenzo of Milan, and most of the Lombard churches (Pl. XCI No. 1 and Pl. XCII No. 1); but it more generally suggests the curve of Sta Maria del Fiore (Pl. LXXXVIII No. 5; Pl. XL No. 2; Pl. LXXXIX, M; Pl. XL No. 4, Pl. XCVI No. 2). In other cases (Pl. LXXX No. 4; Pl. LXXXIX; Pl. XC No. 2), it shows the sides of the octagon crowned by semicircular pediments, as in Brunellesco's lantern of the Cathedral and in the model for the Cathedral of Pavia.

Finally, in some sketches, the cupola is either semicircular or, as in Pl. LXXXVII No. 2, shows the beautiful line adopted sixty years later by Michael Angelo for the existing dome of St. Peter's.

It is worth noticing that for all these domes, Leonardo is not satisfied with decorating the exterior merely with ascending ribs or moldings, but also employs a system of horizontal parallels to complete the architectural system. Not the least interesting are the designs for the *tiburio* (cupola) of the Milan Cathedral. They show some of the forms just mentioned, adapted to the peculiar Gothic style of that monument.

The few examples of church interiors recall the style employed in Lombardy by Bramante—for instance, in S. Maria di Canepanuova at Pavia—or by Dolcebuono in the Monastero Maggiore at Milan (see Pl. CI No. 1 [C. A. 181^b; 546^b]; Pl. LXXXIV No. 10).

The few indications concerning palaces seem to prove that Leonardo followed Alberti's example of decorating the walls with pilasters and a flat *rustica*, either in stone or by *graffitti* (Pl. CII No. 1 and Pl. LXXXV No. 14).

By pointing out the analogies between Leonardo's architecture and that of other masters, we in no way intend to diminish his individual and original inventive power. These are, at any rate, beyond dispute. The project for the Mausoleum (Pl. XCVIII) would alone suffice to rank him among the greatest architects who ever lived. The peculiar shape of the tower (Pl. LXXX), of the churches for preaching (Pl. XCVII No. 1 and pages 56 and 57, Fig. 1—4), his curious plan for a city with high and low-level streets (Pl. LXXVII and LXXVIII No. 2 and No. 3), and his Loggia with fountains (Pl. LXXXII No. 4) reveal an originality, a power, and a facility of invention for almost any given problem that are quite wonderful.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leonardo’s architectural style, shifting from the general forms of domes and palaces to his specific technical studies on structural engineering and decorative details.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

# ON THE STYLE OF LEONARDO'S ARCHITECTURE.

*In addition to all these qualities, he probably stood alone in his day in one department of architectural study—namely, his investigations into the resistance of vaults, foundations, walls, and arches.*

*As an application of these studies, the plan of a semicircular vault (Plate CIII, No. 2) may be mentioned here, arranged so as to produce no outward pressure on the columns on which it rests: **a barrel vault that does not push the columns outward** <note>original Italian: "volta ī botte e non ispignie īfori le colone"</note>. Above the geometrical patterns on the same sheet, close to a circle inscribed in a square, is the note: **the ratio of a vault, namely a third of the diameter of its . . . [from the] German at the cathedral.** <note>original Italian: "la ragiō d'una volta cioè il terzo del diamitro della sua . . . del tedesco in domo". This likely refers to technical advice Leonardo received from a German master builder working on the Milan Cathedral.</note>*

*There are few data by which to judge **Leonardo’s** style in the treatment of detail. On **Plate LXXXV, No. 10** and **Plate CIII, No. 3**, we find some details of pillars; on **Plate CI, No. 3** are slender pillars designed for a fountain, and on **Plate CIII, No. 1, Manuscript B**, is a pen and ink drawing of a vase which also seems intended for a fountain. Three handles seem to have been intended to connect the upper parts with the base. There can be no doubt that **Leonardo**, like **Bramante**, but unlike **Michael Angelo**, brought infinite delicacy of motif <note>original: "motive"</note> and execution to the details of his work.*

### XIV.
## Anatomy, Zoology, and Physiology.

*Leonardo's distinguished place in the history of medicine, as a pioneer in the sciences of* **Anatomy** *and* **Physiology**, *will never be fully appreciated until it is possible to publish the mass of manuscripts in which he treated extensively of these two branches of learning. In the present work, I must necessarily limit myself to giving the reader a general view of these labors by publishing his introductory notes to the various books on anatomical subjects. I have added some extracts and such observations as are scattered incidentally through these treatises, as they serve to throw a light on* **Leonardo's** *scientific attitude, besides having an interest for a wider circle than that of specialists alone.*

VASARI <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the influential biographer of Renaissance artists.</note> expressly mentions Leonardo's anatomical studies, having had the opportunity to examine the manuscript books which refer to them. According to him, Leonardo studied *Anatomy* in the company of Marc Antonio della Torre, "**helping and being helped in return**" <note>original Italian: "aiutato e scambievolmente aiutando"</note>. This learned anatomist taught the science in the universities first of Padua and then of Pavia, and at Pavia, he and Leonardo may have worked and studied together. We have no clue to any exact dates, but in the year 1506, Marc Antonio della Torre seems to have not yet left Padua. He was scarcely thirty years old when he died in 1512, and his writings on anatomy have not only never been published, but no manuscript copy of them is known to exist.

This is not the place to enlarge on the connection between Leonardo and Marc Antonio della Torre. I may, however, observe that I have not been able to discover in Leonardo's manuscripts on anatomy any mention of his younger contemporary. The few quotations which occur from writers on medicine—either of antiquity or of the Middle Ages—are printed in Section XXII. Here and there in the manuscripts, mention is made of an anonymous "**adversary**" <note>original Italian: "avversario"</note> whose views are opposed and refuted by Leonardo, but there is no ground for supposing that Marc Antonio della Torre should have been this "adversary."

Only a very small selection from the mass of anatomical drawings left by Leonardo have been published here in facsimile, but to form any adequate idea of their scientific...

->VOL. II.<-
->O<-

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci's pioneering contributions to anatomy and physiology, discussing his documented but mysterious collaboration with the anatomist Marc Antonio della Torre.</summary>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical reception of Leonardo da Vinci's anatomical works, citing 18th-century experts who recognized his genius long before his manuscripts were fully studied.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

<header>ANATOMY, ZOOLOGY, PHYSIOLOGY.</header>

It is only right that they should be compared with the coarse and inadequate illustrations found in the books published during the early part of the 16th century.

William Hunter, the great surgeon—a qualified judge who had the opportunity during the reign of George III to see the original manuscripts in the King’s Library—recorded his opinion as follows: “I expected to see little more than the kind of anatomical designs that might be useful to a painter in his own profession. But I saw, and indeed with astonishment, that Leonardo had been a comprehensive and profound student. When I consider the great effort he took with every part of the body, the superiority of his universal genius, his particular excellence in mechanics and hydraulics, and the attention with which such a man would examine and observe the objects he intended to draw, I am fully persuaded that Leonardo was the best anatomist in the world at that time... Leonardo was certainly the first man we know of who introduced the practice of creating anatomical drawings” (Two Introductory Letters, London 1784, pages 37 and 39).

The illustrious German naturalist *Johann Friedrich Blumenbach* esteemed them just as highly; he was one of the privileged few who, after Hunter, had the chance to see these manuscripts. He writes: “The keen insight of this great researcher and observer of nature had already paid attention to things that remained unnoticed for centuries afterward” <note>original German: "Der Scharfblick dieses grossen Forschers und Darstellers der Natur hat schon auf Dinge geachtet, die noch Jahrhunderte nachher unbemerkt geblieben sind"</note> (see Blumenbach’s Medical Library <note>original German: "medicinische Bibliothek"</note>, *Vol. 3, Part 4, 1795, page 728*).

These opinions were based on the drawings alone. Until now, hardly anything from the text has been made public; therefore, for the reasons I have mentioned, I intend to reproduce here only a selection of extracts I have chosen from the originals at Windsor Castle and elsewhere. In the Bibliography of the Manuscripts at the end of this volume, a short review is provided regarding the valuable contents of these anatomical notebooks, which are currently almost all in the possession of Her Majesty the Queen of England <note>This refers to Queen Victoria, as the Royal Collection at Windsor Castle has held these drawings since the 17th century.</note>. I believe it is possible to date almost all the fragments with approximate accuracy, which leads me to conclude that the majority of Leonardo’s anatomical investigations were conducted after the death of della Torre <note>Marc Antonio della Torre (c. 1481–1511), an anatomist with whom Leonardo collaborated.</note>.

Simply by reading the introductory notes to his various books on anatomy printed here, it is impossible to ignore the impression that the Master’s anatomical studies bear, to a very great extent, the mark of originality and independent thought.

<summary>This page presents historical praise for Leonardo’s anatomical drawings from experts William Hunter and Johann Friedrich Blumenbach, noting that his scientific contributions were far ahead of his time. It also clarifies that most of Leonardo's anatomical writing was done independently after the death of his colleague Marc Antonio della Torre.</summary>

# I.
# ANATOMY.

### 796.

I wish to work miracles; it may be that I shall possess less than other men of more peaceful lives, or those who want to grow rich in a single day. I may live for a long time in great poverty, as always happens, and to all eternity will happen, to alchemists, the would-be creators of gold and silver, and to engineers who want dead water to give motive life to itself through perpetual motion, and to those supreme fools, the necromancer and the enchanter.

And you, who say that it is better to watch an anatomy performed than to see such drawings, you would be right, if it were possible to see all these things that are demonstrated in such drawings in a single figure; in which, with all your cleverness, you will not see and will not obtain knowledge of more than a few veins. To obtain a true and perfect knowledge of these, I have dissected more than ten human bodies, destroying...

--- Page 218 ---
...destroying all the other members, and removing the very smallest particles of the flesh by which these veins are surrounded, without causing them to bleed, except for the imperceptible bleeding of the capillary veins. Since a single body did not last long enough, it was necessary to proceed by degrees with several bodies until I reached the end and gained complete knowledge; this I repeated twice to observe the differences.

And if you have a love for such things, you might be prevented by a weak stomach; and if that does not prevent you, you might be deterred by the fear of spending the night hours in the company of those corpses—quartered, flayed, and horrible to see. And if this does not prevent you, perhaps you might lack the skill in drawing necessary for such a depiction; or, if you had the skill in drawing, it might not be combined with a knowledge of perspective; and if it were, you might still lack the methods of geometrical demonstration and the method for calculating the forces and strength of the muscles. Patience also may be lacking, so that you are not diligent. As to whether all these qualities were found in me or not, the one hundred and twenty books I have composed will give the verdict of "yes" or "no." In these, I have been hindered neither by greed nor negligence, but only by a lack of time. Farewell.

---

W. A. II. 36 a (21)] 797.

### ON THE ORDER OF THE BOOK.

This work must begin with the conception of man, and describe the nature of the womb, how the fetus lives in it, at what stage it resides there, and the way it is quickened into life and fed, as well as its growth and the interval between...

84. Leonardo frequently, and perhaps habitually, wrote in notebooks of a very small size and only moderate thickness; in most of those which have been preserved intact, each contains fewer than fifty leaves. Thus, a considerable number of such volumes must have been required to make up a volume of the bulk of the *Codex Atlanticus*, which now contains nearly 1,200 detached leaves. In the passage under consideration, which was evidently written late in his life, Leonardo speaks of his manuscript notebooks as numbering 120; but we should hardly be justified in concluding from this passage that the greater part of his manuscripts are now missing (see *Prolegomena*, Vol. I, pp. 5–7).

797. The meaning of the word *nervo* varies in different passages, as it is sometimes used for *muscle*.

### THE ORDER OF THE BOOK.

This illustration of mine of the human body will be demonstrated to you just as if you had a natural man before you. The reason is that if you wish to know the parts of an anatomized man thoroughly, you—or your eye—must view it from different aspects, considering it from below, from above, and from the sides, turning it around and seeking the origin of each member. In such a manner, a natural anatomy would satisfy your inquiry; but you must understand that such knowledge will not leave you satisfied, because of the immense confusion that results from the mixture of tissues combined with veins, arteries, nerves, sinews, muscles, bones, and blood. This blood stains every part the same color; and the veins, which empty themselves of such blood, are not recognized because of their diminutiveness. Furthermore, the integrity of the tissues is broken while seeking the parts contained within them, and their transparency, tainted with blood, does not allow you to recognize the parts covered by them due to the similarity of their blood-stained color. You cannot acquire knowledge of one part without confusing and destroying the other. Therefore, it is necessary to perform several dissections, of which you need three to have full knowledge of the veins and arteries, destroying everything else with the utmost diligence; and another three to have knowledge of the tissues; and three for the sinews, muscles, and ligaments; and three for the bones and cartilages; and three for the anatomy of the bones, which must be sawn open to demonstrate which are hollow and which are not, which contain marrow, which are spongy, which are thick from the outside in, and which are thin. Some have great thinness in one part and are thick in another, and in some parts are hollow, or...

--- Page 221 ---
solid, and in others are bone or cartilage. And thus, through my plan, you will become acquainted with every part and every whole by means of a demonstration from three different aspects of each part. For when you have seen a limb from the front, with whatever muscles, tendons, or veins arise from that side, the same limb will be shown to you from the side or from behind, just as if you had that same limb in your hand and were turning it from side to side until you had full knowledge of all that you desire to understand. And so, in the same way, there will be placed before you in three or four demonstrations the internal organs of man from every side.

I tell you that this kind of demonstration is necessary for you to have a complete understanding of the anatomy of man. Therefore, it is necessary to represent the same part from four aspects so that you may see what is in the front, what is in the back, and what is on the right and left sides. Thus, you will be given knowledge of the true shape and position of every muscle, vein, and artery, which will be very clear to you if you apply your mind to it. And remember that to represent the nerves, you must make a demonstration of them alone, without any other thing; and similarly for the veins and arteries, and for the muscles, and for the bones. And then you will make one of the nerves and bones together, and then of the muscles and bones, and so you will proceed, step by step, until you have represented the whole.

--- Page 223 ---
full of bone, or marrowy, or spongy; and so all these things will sometimes be found in one and the same bone, and in some bones none of them. And you must produce three for the woman, in whom there is a great mystery because of the womb and its fetus. Therefore, through my drawing, every part and every whole will be known to you by means of the demonstration of three different views of each part; for when you have seen a limb from the front with any nerve, tendon, or vein that originates from the opposite side, the same limb will be shown to you turned to the side or from behind—exactly as if you had that same limb in your hand and were turning it from side to side until you had a full understanding of what you desire to know. In the same way, three or four demonstrations of each limb from different views will be placed before you, so that you will be left with a true and full knowledge of what you want to know regarding the human figure.

Thus, here with twelve entire figures, the cosmography of the "lesser world" will be shown to you in the same order that was established before me by Ptolemy in his cosmography; and I will then divide those into limbs, just as he divided the whole into provinces. Then I will describe the function of the parts from every direction, putting before your eyes the knowledge of the entire figure and the physical capacity of man regarding local movement by means of his parts. And so, may it please our Author that I might demonstrate the nature of men and their customs in the same way that I describe their figure.

And I remind you that the anatomy of the nerves will not show you the position of their branching, nor into which muscles they branch, by means of bodies dissolved in running water or in lime water; because, although the origin of their beginnings remains visible without such water as well as with it, their branchings tend to unite due to the flow of the water—not unlike how combed flax or hemp for spinning bunches together into a single bundle—so that it is impossible to find into which muscles or with how many branchings the nerves are embedded in the aforementioned muscles.

--- Page 224 ---
## 799.

#### THE ORDER OF ANATOMY.

First draw the bones—let us say, of the arm—and place the motor muscle from the shoulder to the elbow with all its lines. Then proceed in the same way from the elbow to the forearm. Then from the forearm to the hand, and from the hand to the fingers.
And in the arm, you will place the motors of the fingers that open, and you will show these separately in their demonstration. In the second demonstration, you will cover these muscles with the secondary motors of the fingers, and so proceed step by step to avoid confusion. But first, lay upon the bones those muscles which are attached to those bones, without the confusion of other muscles; and with these, you may place the nerves and veins that provide their nourishment, having first drawn the tree of veins and nerves over the simple bones.

Three men complete, three with bones and nerves, three with the bones only. Here we have twelve demonstrations of entire figures.

When you have finished building up the man, you will make the statue with all its superficial measurements.

ANATOMY. [805—808.]

### 805.

Which nerve causes the movement of the eye so that the motion of one eye pulls the other?

Of knitting the brows, of raising the brows, of lowering the brows; of closing the eyes, of opening the eyes; of raising the nostrils, of opening the lips with the teeth clenched, of pouting the lips, of laughing, of wonder.

# 812.

**ANATOMY.**

The divisions of the head (812. 813). There are eleven elementary tissues: cartilage, bones, nerves, veins, arteries, fascia, ligaments and sinews, skin, muscle, and fat.

**OF THE HEAD.**

The divisions of the skull are 10: namely, 5 containing parts and 5 contained parts. The containing parts are the hair, skin, muscular flesh, fascia, and the skull. The contained parts are these: the dura mater, the pia mater, and the brain. Below, the pia mater and dura mater return to enclose the brain within themselves; then there is the rete mirabile, and finally the bone which is the foundation of the brain and from which the nerves originate.

The walking of man is always according to the universal manner of walking in four-footed animals; because just as they move their feet crosswise, in the manner of a horse’s trot, so man moves his four limbs crosswise; that is, if he puts forward his right foot to walk, he puts forward with it his left arm, and always continues in this way.

### 828.

Advantages in the structure of the eye in certain animals (828-831).

Every object we see will appear larger at midnight than at midday, and larger in the morning than at midday. This happens because the pupil of the eye is much smaller at midday than at any other time. In the same proportion that the eye or pupil of the owl is larger relative to its body than that of a human, it can see that much more light at night than a human can; consequently, at midday it can see nothing unless it constricts its pupil; and, in the same way, objects look larger to it at night than by day.

---

### 829.

OF THE EYES IN ANIMALS.

The eyes of all animals have pupils that are adapted to expand and contract on their own according to the greater or lesser light of the sun or other brightness. But in birds the variation is much greater, and it is greatest in nocturnal birds, such as owls, barn owls, and eyes belonging to the owlet species; in these, the pupil expands in such a way that it occupies nearly the whole eye, and it contracts to the size of a grain of millet, always maintaining a circular shape. But the lion species—such as panthers, leopards, ounces, tigers, lynxes, Spanish cats, and other similar animals—contract the pupil from a perfect circle to a two-angled shape, which is as shown in the margin. However, because man has weaker sight than any other animal, he is less bothered by excessive light and his pupil increases less in dark places; but in the eyes of the aforementioned nocturnal animals—specifically the horned owl, which is the largest of the nocturnal bird species—the power of vision increases so much that in the faintest nocturnal light (which we call darkness) it sees...

--- Page 237 ---
with much more vigor than we do in the splendor of noon, at which time such birds remain hidden in dark places; and if they are indeed forced to come out into the air illuminated by the sun, they contract their pupils so much that their visual power diminishes along with the quantity of such light.

Dissect various eyes, and see which are the muscles that open and close the aforementioned pupils of the eyes of animals.

---

### 830.

$a\ b\ n$ is the lower lid that closes the eye from the bottom upward with an opaque cover; $c\ n\ b$ closes the eye from front to back with a transparent cover.

It closes from the bottom upward because it descends from above.

When the eye of birds closes with its two coverings, it first closes the secondary membrane, which closes from the tear duct to the corner of that eye; and the first lid closes from the bottom upward. These two intersecting movements occupy the area from the tear duct first, because we have already seen that they are secured from the front and below, and they only guard the upper part against the dangers of birds of prey that descend from above and behind. They uncover the membrane toward the tail first, because if the enemy comes from behind, the bird has the convenience of fleeing forward. Furthermore, it possesses the aforementioned secondary membrane, which is transparent, because if it did not have such a shield, it could not keep its eyes open against the wind that strikes the eye in the fury of its rapid flight. And its pupil increases and decreases as it sees lesser or greater light, that is, splendor.

## 832.

**Remarks on the organs of speech (832. 833).**

The tongue is found to have 24 muscles which correspond to the six muscles that compose the portion of the tongue which moves in the mouth. And when *a, o, u* are spoken with a clear and rapid pronunciation, it is necessary, in order to pronounce them continuously without any pause between them, that the opening of the lips should close by degrees; that is, they are wide apart in saying *a*, closer in saying *o*, and much closer still to pronounce *u*. It may be shown how all the vowels are pronounced with the farthest portion of the soft palate which is above the epiglottis.

## 834.

**On the conditions of sight (834. 835).**

### ON THE NATURE OF SIGHT.

I say that sight is exercised by all animals through the medium of light; and if anyone adduces, as an argument against this, the sight of nocturnal animals, I must say that this is subject to the very same natural laws in the same way. For it will easily be understood that the senses which receive the images of things do not project any visual power from themselves. On the contrary, the atmospheric medium which exists between the object and the sense incorporates the forms of things into itself, and by its contact with the sense, transmits the object to it. If the object—whether by sound or by odor—presents its spiritual force to the ear or the nose, then light is not required and does not act. The forms of objects do not send their images into...

--- Page 239 ---
the air if they are not illuminated; and the eye, being thus constituted, cannot receive from that air what the air does not possess, even though it touches its surface. If you choose to say that there are many animals that prey at night, I answer that when the small amount of light which suffices for the nature of their eyes is lacking, they guide themselves by their strong sense of hearing and of smell, which are not hindered by darkness, and in which they are far superior to man. If you observe a cat leaping among a quantity of jars and crocks by daylight, you will see them remain safe; but if you do the same at night, many will be broken. Night birds do not fly unless the moon shines, either fully or in part; rather, they feed between sunset and the total darkness of the night.

No body can be perceived without light and shade; light and shade are caused by light.

---

### 835.

**WHY MEN ADVANCED IN AGE SEE BETTER AT A DISTANCE.**

Sight is better from a distance than from nearby in those men who are advancing in age, because the same object transmits a smaller impression of itself to the eye when it is distant than when it is near.

---

### 836.

The Common Sense is that which judges the things offered to it by the other senses. Ancient observers have concluded that the part of judgment given to man is caused by an organ to which the other five senses refer everything by means of the "impressive" faculty; and to this organ they have given the name Common Sense. They say this sense is situated in the middle of the head, between the "impressive" faculty and memory. And they use the name Common Sense solely because it is...

--- Page 240 ---
the common judge of the other five senses, namely: seeing, hearing, touching, tasting, and smelling. The Common Sense is activated by means of the "impressive" faculty, which is placed in the middle between it and the senses. The "impressive" faculty is activated by means of the images of things presented to it by the surface instruments—that is, the senses—which are placed in the middle between external things and the "impressive" faculty. Similarly, the senses are activated by objects; surrounding things send their images to the senses, and the senses transfer them to the "impressive" faculty. The "impressive" faculty sends them to the Common Sense, and from there they are established in the memory, where they are more or less retained according to the importance or power of the thing presented. That sense is faster in its function which is closer to the "impressive" faculty, and the eye, being the highest, is the chief of the others. We will treat only the eye and leave the others aside, so as not to stray too far from our subject. Experience says that the eye extends to ten different natures of objects: namely, light and darkness (one being the cause of the other nine, and the other being their absence), color and substance, shape and position, distance and nearness, motion and rest.

---

**On the origin of the soul.**

Although human ingenuity may produce various inventions, answering the same end with different instruments, it will never find an invention more beautiful, nor easier, nor more concise than Nature's; because in her inventions nothing is lacking and nothing is superfluous. She does not require counterweights when she makes limbs suitable for motion in the bodies of animals, but instead places within them the soul of that body as its composer—that is, the soul of the mother, which first composes the form of the human being in the womb. At the proper time, she awakens the soul that is to be its inhabitant, which previously remained dormant and under the protection of the soul of the mother.

--- Page 241 ---
...and under the tutelage of the soul of the mother, who nourishes and gives it life through the umbilical vein, along with all its spiritual parts; and this continues until the umbilicus is joined to the placenta and the cotyledons, by which the child is attached to the mother. These are the reason why a wish, a strong craving, a fright, or any other mental suffering in the mother has more influence on the child than on the mother; for there are many cases where the child loses its life because of them, etc.

This discourse does not belong here, but will be required for the one on the composition of animated bodies—and the rest of the definition of the soul I leave to the imaginations of friars, those fathers of the people who know all secrets by inspiration.

I leave alone the sacred books, for they are the supreme truth.

# 838.

## HOW THE FIVE SENSES ARE THE MINISTERS OF THE SOUL.

The soul seems to reside in the judgment, and the judgment seems to be seated in that place where all the senses meet, which is called the Common Sense. It is not spread throughout the entire body, as many have believed, but is instead entirely in one part. Because, if it were spread throughout and present in every part, it would not have been necessary for the instruments of the senses to meet in one single spot. On the contrary, it would have been enough for the eye to fulfill the function of its sensation on its surface alone, rather than transmitting the image of the things seen to the sense by way of the optic nerves; for the soul—for the reason given above—could have perceived it on the surface of the eye. In the same way, regarding the sense of hearing, it would have been enough if the voice had merely...

--- Page 242 ---
...sounded in the porous cavities of the petrous bone which lies within the ear, without making any further transition from that bone to the common sense, where the voice meets and is processed by the common judgment. The sense of smell is also seen to be forced by necessity to converge at that same judgment. The sense of touch passes through the perforated cords and is carried to that same sense; these cords spread out with infinite ramifications into the skin which surrounds the limbs of the body and the internal organs. The perforated cords carry command and sensation to the functional members; these cords and nerves, located among the muscles and sinews, command their movement. The muscles obey, and this obedience is put into action by swelling; for swelling shortens their length and pulls back the nerves which are woven through the particles of the limbs. Being infused into the tips of the fingers, they carry back to the sense the cause of their contact.

The nerves with their muscles serve the cords as soldiers serve commanders, and the cords serve the common sense as commanders serve a captain. Therefore, the joint of the bones obeys the nerve, the nerve obeys the muscle, the muscle obeys the cord, and the cord obeys the common sense. The common sense is the seat of the soul, memory is its storehouse, and the faculty of impression is its secretary. Just as the senses serve the soul and not the soul the senses, wherever the functional sense of the soul is lacking, the soul lacks the full function of that sense in this life, as is evident in those born mute or blind.

# 839.

### HOW THE NERVES SOMETIMES ACT BY THEMSELVES WITHOUT COMMAND FROM THE OTHER FUNCTIONS OF THE SOUL.

This is clearly evident, for you will see movement in those who are paralyzed or shivering from cold, even when the mind does not command such movement. This same phenomenon appears in the severed tails of lizards and similar creatures.

--- Page 243 ---
and their trembling limbs, such as the head and hands, quake without permission from the soul; and the soul, with all its power, cannot prevent those members from trembling. The same thing happens in epilepsy, and in parts that have been cut off, such as the tails of lizards. The idea or imagination is the helm and guiding-rein of the senses, because the thing imagined moves the sense. Pre-imagining is imagining things that are to be; post-imagining is imagining things that are past.

# 840.

There are four powers: memory and intellect, desire and covetousness. The first two are rational and the others are sensual. The three senses—sight, hearing, and smell—cannot easily be suppressed; touch and taste not at all. Smell carries taste along with it in dogs and other gluttonous animals.

# 841.

I reveal to men the origin of the first, or perhaps second, cause of their existence.

# 842.

Lust is the cause of generation.
Gluttony is the maintenance of life. Fear or dread is the prolongation of life and the preservation of its instruments.

# 843.

### HOW THE BODY OF AN ANIMAL IS CONSTANTLY DYING AND BEING REBORN.

The body of anything that nourishes itself constantly dies and is constantly reborn; because nourishment cannot enter except into those places where the previous nourishment has expired. And if it has expired, it no longer has life; and if you do not supply nourishment equal to the nourishment that has departed, then...

--- Page 244 ---
which is gone, life will fail in vigor, and if you take away this nourishment, life is entirely destroyed. But if you restore as much as is destroyed day by day, then as much of the life is renewed as is consumed, just as the flame of the candle is fed by the nourishment afforded by the liquid of that candle. This flame also continually, with a rapid supply, restores from below as much as is consumed in dying above; and from a brilliant light, it is converted in dying into murky smoke. This death is continuous, just as the smoke is continuous; and the continuity of the smoke is equal to the continuity of the nourishment, and in the same instant all the flame is dead and all regenerated, simultaneously with the movement of its own nourishment.

# 844.

King of the animals—as you have described him—I should rather say king of the beasts, you being the greatest—because you have not refrained from slaying them, in order that they may give you their children for the benefit of your gullet, with which you have attempted to make a grave for all animals; and I would say still more, if it were permitted for me to speak the entire truth. But let us not go outside human matters in telling of one supreme wickedness, which does not happen among the animals of the earth, inasmuch as among them none are found who eat their own kind, unless through a lack of sense (few indeed among them, and those being mothers, as with men, although they are not many in number); and this happens only among the rapacious animals, as with the lion species, and leo-

--- Page 245 ---
...pards, panthers, lynxes, cats, and the like, which sometimes eat their children; but you, besides your children, devour father, mother, brothers, and friends; nor is this enough for you, for you go hunting on the islands of others, capturing other men, and after fattening the genitals and testicles of these half-naked people, you shove them down your own throat; now, does nature not produce enough simple plants for you to satisfy yourself? And if you are not content with simple plants, can you not, by mixing them, make infinite compounds, as Platina and other authors on gluttony have written?

### 845.

### 852.

A remedy for scratches taught to me by the Herald to the King of France: 4 ounces of virgin wax, 4 ounces of colophony, 2 ounces of incense. Keep each ingredient separate; melt the wax, then add the incense, and then the colophony. Make a mixture of it and apply it to the sore area.

### 855.

### 856.

I teach you how to preserve your health, which you will succeed in doing all the more the more you protect yourself from physicians; because their compositions are a type of alchemy.

--- Page 250 ---
"Perspective" (see Nos. 869 and 870), this being the term universally applied at the time to Optics as well as Perspective (see Vol. I, p. 10, note to No. 13, l. 10).

At the beginning of the 16th century, the Ptolemaic theory of the universe was still universally accepted as the true one, and Leonardo conceives of the earth as fixed, with the moon and sun revolving around it, as they are represented in the diagram for No. 897. He does not explore any theory regarding the motions of the planets; with regard to these and the fixed stars, he only investigates the phenomena of their luminosity. He takes the spherical form of the earth for granted as an axiom from the start, and he anticipates Newton by pointing out the universality of Gravitation, not merely on the earth, but even on the moon. Although his acute research into the nature of the moon's light and the lunar spots did not produce many results of lasting importance—beyond making it evident that they refuted the errors of his contemporaries—they contain various explanations of facts which modern science does not need to modify in any essential point, as well as discoveries which history has previously assigned to a much later date.

The ingenious theory by which he tries to explain the nature of what is known as earthshine—the reflection of the sun's rays by the earth toward the moon—by saying that it is a peculiar refraction originating in the innumerable curved surfaces of the sea waves, may be regarded as absurd; but it must not be forgotten that he had no means of detecting the fundamental error on which he based it, namely: the assumption that the moon was at a relatively short distance from the earth. So long as the motion of the earth around the sun remained unknown, it was, of course, impossible to form any estimate of the moon's distance from the earth by calculating its parallax.

Before the discovery of the telescope, accurate astronomical observations were possible only to a very limited extent. It appears, however, from certain passages in the notes printed here for the first time, that Leonardo was in a position to study the spots on the moon more closely than he could have done with the unaided eye. So far as can be gathered from the mysterious language in which the description of his instrument is wrapped, he made use of magnifying glasses; these do not, however, seem to have been constructed like a telescope—telescopes were first made around 1600. As Libri pointed out (*Histoire des Sciences mathématiques* III, 101), Fracastoro of Verona (1473–1553) succeeded in magnifying the moon's face by an arrangement of lenses (compare No. 910, note), and this lends probability to Leonardo's invention at a date not much earlier.

--- Page 251 ---

### 857.
The equator, the line of the horizon, the horizontal line, the equidistant line; these lines are those which, at their extremities, are equidistant from the center of the world.

The equator, the line of the horizon, the ecliptic, the meridian: these lines are those which in all their parts are equidistant from the center of the globe.

### 858.
Just as the earth is not in the center of the sun’s orbit, nor in the center of the universe, but is indeed in the center of its companion elements, which are united with it; so anyone standing on the moon, when it and the sun are both beneath us, would see this earth of ours with its element of water just as the moon appears to us, and it would perform the same function for the moon as the moon does for us.

The earth is not in the center of the sun’s orbit nor at the center of the universe, but in the center of its companion elements, and united with them. And anyone standing on the moon, when it and the sun are both beneath us, would see this our earth and the element of water upon it just as we see the moon, and the earth would light it as the moon lights us.

### 859.
Force is generated by scarcity or abundance; it is the child of physical motion and the grandchild of spiritual motion, and the mother and origin of weight; and this weight is limited to the elements of water and earth, and this...

Force arises from dearth or abundance; it is the child of physical motion, and the grandchild of spiritual motion, and the mother and origin of gravity. Gravity is limited to the elements of water and...

--- Page 252 ---
...force is infinite, because with it infinite worlds could be moved if instruments could be constructed where such force could be generated.

Force with physical motion, and weight with percussion, are the four accidental powers by which all the works of mortals have their existence and their death.

Force has its origin in spiritual motion; this motion, flowing through the limbs of sentient animals, enlarges their muscles. Once enlarged, these muscles consequently shorten and pull the tendons that are connected to them, and this is the cause of force in human limbs.

The quality and quantity of a man's forces can give birth to another force, which will be proportionally greater as the motion of one is longer than the other.

## 860.

Why does the weight *o* not remain in its place? It does not remain because it has no resistance; and where will it move? It will move toward the center; and why not along other lines? Because the weight, having no resistance, will descend to the bottom by the shortest path, and the lowest place is the center of the world; and why does such a weight know how to find it with such directness? Because it does not wander aimlessly like a mindless thing along various lines.

## 861.

Let the earth move in whatever direction it pleases, the surface of the water will never leave its sphere, but will always remain equidistant from the center of the world.

Given that the earth were removed from the center of the world, what would the water do?

It would remain around that center with equal thickness, but with a smaller diameter than when it contained the earth within its body.

## 865.

How the earth is a star.

## 866.

In your discourse, you must conclude that the earth is a star much like the moon, and the glory of our world; and so you will make a discourse on the sizes of many stars, according to the authors.

--- Page 255 ---
141.] THE EARTH AS A PLANET. 141

Natural perspective diminishes objects by the curvature of the eye, which is forced to intersect, at its surface, the pyramid of every image that comes to the eye at right angles on its spherical surface. But by the method I teach here in the margin, these pyramids are intersected at right angles near the surface of the pupil. The convex pupil of the eye takes in our entire hemisphere, while this method will show only a single star; but where many small stars transmit their images to the surface of the pupil, and those stars are extremely small, this method will show only one star, but it will appear large. And so the moon will be seen in greater size, and its spots will have a more defined shape. For this purpose, one must place close to the eye a glass filled with the water mentioned in number 4 of Book 113, "On natural substances"; for this water makes objects that are enclosed in balls of crystalline glass appear free from the glass.

OF THE EYE.

Among the bodies smaller than the pupil of the eye, the one that is closest to the pupil will be the least noticeable to it. And through this experiment, it has become known to us that the power of sight is not reduced to a single point because if it were, etc.
Read in the margin.
Those objects appear larger which come to the eye at a wider angle.
But the images of the objects that converge at the pupil of the eye are distributed upon that pupil in the same way that they are distributed in the air; and the proof of this follows: when we look at the starry sky without focusing our sight more on one star than another, the sky then appears to us strewn with stars, and they are proportioned in the eye just as they are in the sky, and their spacing does the same.

--- Page 256 ---
# 870.

### PERSPECTIVE.

Among objects moved away from the eye by an equal distance, the one that undergoes the least diminution is the one that was originally the most remote.
When various objects are moved equal distances further from their original positions, the one that was initially farthest from the eye will diminish the least. And the proportion of this diminution will be in the same ratio as the relative distances the objects had from the eye before they were moved.
For example, consider object *t* and object *e*, where the ratio of their distances from the eye *a* is fivefold. I move each from its place and set it further from the eye by one of the five parts into which the ratio is divided. Consequently, the object nearest to the eye will have its distance doubled, and according to the penultimate proposition of this study, its size is diminished by half of its total; meanwhile, body *e*, by the same movement, is diminished by 1/5 of its total size. Therefore, by that same penultimate proposition, what is proposed in this final one is true. I say this regarding the motions of the celestial bodies which, being 3,500 miles more distant when at the horizon than when directly overhead, do not increase or diminish by any perceptible amount.

---

# 871.

*a b* is the aperture through which the sun passes; and if you could measure the thickness of the solar rays at *n m*, you could accurately establish the true lines of the convergence of those solar rays, with the mirror located at *a b*, and then show the reflected rays at...

--- Page 257 ---
reflected rays at equal angles toward $n\ m$; but since you want to capture them at $n\ m$, take them at the inner side of the aperture at $c\ d$, where they can be measured at the point where the solar rays strike. Then place your mirror at the distance $a\ b$, making the rays $d\ b$ and $c\ a$ fall upon it, and then reflect at equal angles toward $c\ d$. This is the correct method; however, you must use this mirror during the same month, the same day, the same hour, and the same instant, and it will work better than at any other time, because the pyramid of rays was produced when the sun was at that specific distance.

### 872.

Side $a$ of the shaded body $n$ sees the entire portion of the hemisphere $b\ c\ d\ e\ f$ and does not see any part of the darkness of the earth. The same occurs at point $o$; therefore, the space $a\ o$ is of one and the same brightness throughout. At $s$, it sees only 4 degrees of the hemisphere $d\ e\ f\ g\ h$, and it also sees the entire earth $s\ h$, which makes it darker by as much as the calculation will show.

### 873.

**PROOF OF THE INCREASED SIZE OF THE SUN IN THE WEST.**

Some mathematicians demonstrate that the sun increases in size in the west because the eye always sees it through a greater thickness of air, claiming that objects seen in mist and in water appear larger. To these I reply: No; because objects seen through a mist are

--- Page 258 ---
similar in color to those at a distance; but because they are not similarly reduced by distance, they appear larger. Furthermore, nothing increases in size in smooth water; and you can prove this by shining a light on a board placed halfway under the water. But the reason why the sun appears larger is that every luminous body appears larger in proportion to its distance.

Show, first, that every light at a distance from the eye throws out rays which appear to increase the size of that luminous body; and from this it follows that 2 . . . .

The moon is cold and moist.

Water is cold and moist. Thus our seas must provide the same influence to the moon as the moon does to us.

876.

It is impossible for the entire side of a spherical mirror illuminated by the sun to reflect its radiance unless that mirror were undulating or covered in small globules.
You see here the sun illuminating the moon, which is a spherical mirror; as much of it as the sun sees, it causes to shine.
From this, it may be concluded that the part of the moon that shines is water, similar to that of our seas and likewise wavy, while the parts that do not shine are islands and solid ground.

This demonstration of many spherical bodies placed between the eye and the sun is made to show that, just as the image of the sun is seen in each of these bodies, so that same image can be seen in every curve of the waves of the sea. Just as many suns are seen in these many spheres, so many reflections are seen in many waves; at a great distance, each individual reflection appears large to the eye. Since every wave does this, the spaces between the waves are lost, and these many reflections merge into a single continuous radiance.

--- Page 261 ---
the spaces between the waves are hidden; and for this reason, it appears as though the many suns mirrored in the many waves were but one continuous sun; and the shadows, mixed with the luminous images, make this radiance less brilliant than that of the sun mirrored in these waves.

### 882.

Epicurus says the sun is as large as it shows itself to be; therefore, since it appears to be one foot across, we must hold it to be so. It would follow that when the moon eclipses the sun, the sun would not exceed it in size as it does; and since the moon is smaller than the sun, the moon would be less than a foot. Consequently, when our world eclipses the moon, it would be less than a foot by a finger's breadth. This is because if the sun is one foot across, and our earth casts a pyramidal shadow toward the moon, it is necessary that the luminous body which causes the shadowy pyramid be larger than the opaque body which causes that same pyramid.

...by the brilliance of the solar body, upon which the human eye cannot fix its gaze; and besides this, it is most clearly demonstrated by its rays reflected from concave mirrors. When the impact of these rays is of such brilliance that the eye cannot endure it, then that impact will have a brilliance similar to the sun in its own position. And to prove this is true, I demonstrate that if such a mirror has the concavity required for the generation of such a ray, then no created thing will withstand the heat of the impact of the ray reflected from such a mirror. And if you say that the mirror itself is cold and yet casts hot rays, I answer you that the ray comes from the sun, and it is the ray of the concave mirror, having passed across the window.

This is abundantly proved by the radiance of the solar body on which the human eye cannot dwell and besides this no less manifestly by the rays reflected from a concave mirror, which—when they strike the eye with such splendor that the eye cannot bear them—have a brilliance equal to the sun in its own place. And that this is true I prove by the fact that if the mirror has its concavity formed exactly as is required for the collecting and reflecting of these rays, no created being could endure the heat that strikes from the reflected rays of such a mirror. And if you argue that the mirror itself is cold and yet sends forth hot rays, I should reply that those rays come really from the sun and that it is the ray of the concave mirror after having passed through the window.

If the sun uses its brilliance with its center to strengthen the power of the whole body, it is necessary that its rays, the farther they move away from it, the more they go on spreading. If this is so, you whose eye is near the water that mirrors the sun, see a very small part of the sun's rays carry the form of that mirrored sun onto the surface of the water; and if you were near the sun—as would be the case when the sun is at midday and the sea is to the west—you would see the sun mirrored on the said sea of very great...

If it is from the center that the sun employs its radiance to intensify the power of its whole mass, it is evident that the farther its rays extend, the more widely they will be divided; and this being so, you, whose eye is near the water that mirrors the sun, see but a small portion of the rays of the sun strike the surface of the water, and reflecting the form of the sun. But if you were near the sun—as would be the case when the sun is on the meridian and the sea to the westward—you would see the sun, mirrored in the...

--- Page 267 ---
...sea, of a very great size; because, as you are nearer to the sun, your eye, taking in the rays nearer to the point of radiation, takes more of them in, and a greater splendor is the result. And in this way it can be proved that the moon must have seas which reflect the sun, and that the parts which do not shine are land.

Every object seen through a curved medium appears to be of a larger size than it actually is.

### III.

## THE MOON.

British Museum 94 a] ->**892.**<-

<margin>On the luminosity of the moon (892-901).</margin>

**OF THE MOON.**

As I intend to treat the nature of the moon, it is necessary that I first describe the perspective of mirrors, whether plane, concave, or convex; and first, what is meant by a luminous ray, and how it is refracted by various kinds of media; then, when a reflected ray is most powerful—whether when the angle of incidence is acute, right, or obtuse; or from a convex, a plane, or a concave surface; or from an opaque or a transparent body. Besides this, how it is that the solar rays which strike the waves of the sea are seen by the eye of the same width as the sun's diameter, and why they appear to stretch from the horizon to the eye of the observer; and why, if the sea were as smooth as a mirror, the image of the sun would appear no larger than the sun itself; and why, on the contrary, when the sea is wavy, the image of the sun appears to be of great width and length, and why this image is composed of many suns. Furthermore, why this image, when seen from a great distance, still appears to be the same width as it is when seen from nearby, and why it appears to be composed of parallel lines. Finally, it must be shown that the moon is a body like the earth, and that the part of it which shines is water.

--- Page 269 ---
The width at the angle nearest to the eye is the same as at the highest point of the waves on the horizon; nevertheless, the solar splendor reflected from the sea waves is pyramidal in shape and, consequently, increases in width at every degree of distance, even though to our sight it appears to be parallel.

1st. Nothing that is very light is opaque;
2nd. Nothing that is lighter can remain beneath that which is less light;
3rd. Whether the moon is situated in the midst of its elements or not;
And if it has no specific location, like the earth, within its elements, why does it not fall to the center of our elements?
And if the moon is not in the midst of its own elements and does not descend, then it is lighter than any other element;
And if the moon is lighter than any other element, why is it solid and not transparent?

Regarding objects of various sizes which, when placed at various distances, appear equal, the proportion of the distances will be the same as the proportion of the magnitudes.

---

### 893.

## ON THE MOON, AND WHETHER IT IS POLISHED AND SPHERICAL.

The image of the sun within the moon is powerfully luminous and occupies only a small part of its surface. You will see the proof of this by taking a ball of burnished gold and placing it in the dark with a light far away from it; although the light illuminates about half of the ball, the eye sees it only on a small part of its surface, and all the rest of that surface reflects the darkness that surrounds it. Because of this, only the image of the light appears on it, and all the rest remains invisible when the eye is distant from the ball. This same thing would happen on the surface of the moon if it were polished, lustrous, and dense, like bodies that act as mirrors.

--- Page 270 ---
Show how, if you were standing on the moon or on a star, our earth would appear to perform the same function with the sun as the moon does.

And show that the image of the sun in the sea cannot appear as a single, undivided sun, as it appears in a flat mirror.

### 894.

How shadows blend together at great distances is proven by the shadow side of the moon, which is never seen.

### 895.

Either the moon has its own light or it does not. If it has its own light, why does it not shine without the help of the sun? And if it does not have its own light, it is necessarily a spherical mirror. And if it is a mirror, is it not proven in the study of perspective that the image of a luminous object will never be equal to the part of that mirror which is illuminated by the light source? And if this is so, as the figure shows here at *r* and *s*, where does such a great quantity of brightness come from as that of the full moon, which we see on the fifteenth day of the lunar cycle?

--- Page 271 ---
### OF THE MOON.

The moon has no light of its own, but is illuminated by the sun to the extent that it faces it. We see as much of that brightness as is facing us. Its night side receives as much splendor as is provided by our waters reflecting the image of the sun, which is mirrored in all the waters that see the sun and the moon. The skin or surface of the water that makes up the sea of the moon and the sea of our earth is always wrinkled, either a little or a lot, or more or less. This roughness causes the expansion of the countless images of the sun that are mirrored in the ridges, hollows, sides, and fronts of the innumerable waves—that is, in as many different spots on each wave as there are different positions of the eyes that see them. This could not happen if the sphere of water, which covers a great part of the moon, were of a uniform roundness; because then there would be only one image of the sun for each eye, and its reflection would be distinct, always appearing as a spherical glow, as is clearly demonstrated by the gilded balls placed on the tops of tall buildings. But if such gilded balls were wrinkled or clustered like mulberries—black fruits composed of tiny round globules—then each part of those globules, seen by the sun and the eye, would show that eye the luster generated by the image of the sun. Thus, in one and the same body, many tiny suns would be seen, which often merge at a long distance and appear continuous. The luster of the new moon is brighter and more powerful than when it is full; this is because the angle of incidence is much more obtuse in the new moon than in the old, where such angles are extremely acute. The waves of the moon mirror the sun in their valleys as well as on their ridges, while the sides remain dark; but at the edges of the moon, the depths of the waves do not see the sun.

--- Page 272 ---
...of the moon, the hollows of the waves do not catch the sunlight, but only their crests; and thus the images are fewer and more mixed with the shadows in the hollows. This intermingling of the shaded and illuminated spots reaches the eye with a diminished splendor, so that the edges will appear darker, because the curves of the sides of these waves are insufficient to reflect the received rays to the eye. The new moon naturally reflects the solar rays more directly toward the eye from these extreme waves than from any other part, as is shown by the diagram of the moon, where rays *a* strike the wave *b* and reflect toward *b d*, where the eye *d* is located. This cannot happen during the full moon when the solar ray, being in the west, strikes the extreme waves of the moon in the east from *n* to *m*, and does not reflect toward the eye in the west, but bounces back to the east, only slightly bending the straight path of the solar ray; thus, the angle of incidence is very wide indeed.

The moon is an opaque and solid body, and if, on the contrary, it were transparent, it would not receive the light of the sun.

The yellow or yolk of an egg remains in the middle of its albumen without sinking in any direction, and it is either lighter, heavier, or equal in weight to that albumen. If it were lighter, it ought to rise above all the albumen and stop in contact with the shell.

--- Page 273 ---
...of the egg; and if it is heavier, it ought to sink, and if it is equal, it might just as well be at one of the ends as in the middle or below.

The innumerable images of the solar rays reflected from the innumerable waves of the sea, as they fall upon those waves, are what cause us to see the very broad and continuous radiance on the surface of the sea.

### 897.

That the sun could not be mirrored in the body of the moon, which is a convex mirror,

--- Page 275 ---
899. THE MOON.

A luminous body appears larger in proportion to its distance. It is easy to understand that every planet and star is farther from us when it is in the West than when it is directly overhead, by about 3500 miles, as is proved by the diagram on the margin. If you see the sun or the moon reflected in water near you, it appears to be the same size in that water as it appears in the sky. But if you move a mile away, it will appear 100 times larger; and if you see the sun reflected in the sea at sunset, the reflected image will appear to be more than 10 miles long, because that reflection occupies more than 10 miles of the shoreline. And if you were located where the moon is, the sun would appear to be reflected in as much of the sea as it illuminates during the day; and the land amid that water would appear just like the dark spots that are on the moon. When viewed from the earth, the moon appears to men exactly as our world would appear to any men who might live on the moon.

---

### ON THE NATURE OF THE MOON.

When the moon is entirely illuminated to our sight, we are seeing its full daylight. At that time, due to the reflection of the sun's rays which strike it and bounce back to us, its ocean sends less moisture toward us; and the less light it gives, the more harmful it is.

---

899.

### ON THE MOON.

I say that since the moon has no light of its own, yet is luminous, it is necessary that such light is caused by something else.

--- Page 277 ---
where it is, it could not support itself, and consequently it would have to descend toward the center of the universe and join with the earth; or if not the moon itself, at least its waters would have to fall and strip the moon of them, falling toward the center and leaving the moon bare and without luster. Therefore, since what reason suggests does not happen, it is a clear sign that this moon is clothed in its own elements—namely water, air, and fire—and thus, in and of itself, it sustains itself in that space just as our earth does with its elements in this other space; and that heavy objects perform the same function within its elements as other heavy objects do within our elements.

When the eye in the east sees the moon in the west near the setting sun, it sees it with its shaded part surrounded by a luminous part, of which light the side and upper portions derive from the sun, and the lower portion derives from the western ocean, which also receives the solar rays and reflects them into the lower seas of the moon. Furthermore, it gives as much brightness to the entire shaded part of the moon as the moon gives to the earth at midnight; therefore, it does not remain completely dark. Because of this, some have believed that the moon has a light of its own in part, besides that which is given to it by the sun, which light derives from the aforementioned cause of our seas being illuminated by the sun.

Again, it could be said that the circle of radiance

--- Page 278 ---
shown by the moon when it and the sun are both in the West is wholly borrowed from the sun, when the moon, the sun, and the eye are situated as is shown above.

Some might say that the air surrounding the moon as an element, catching the light of the sun as our atmosphere does, is what completes the luminous circle on the body of the moon.

Some have believed that the moon has a light of its own, but this opinion is false, because they have based it on that dim light seen between the horns of the new moon, which looks dark where it is close to the bright part, while against the darkness of the background it looks so light that many have taken it to be a ring of new radiance completing the circle where the tips of the horns illuminated by the sun cease to shine. And this difference of background arises from the fact that the portion of that background which borders the bright part of the moon, by comparison with that brightness, looks darker than it actually is; while at the upper part, where a portion of the luminous circle of uniform width is seen, the result is that the moon—being brighter there than the medium or background on which it is seen—looks more luminous at that edge than it actually is by comparison with that darkness. And that brightness at such a time is derived from our ocean and other inland seas. These are, at that time, illuminated by the sun which is already setting, in such a way that the sea then performs the same function for the dark side of the moon as the moon at its fifteenth day does for us.

--- Page 279 ---
sun is set. And the small amount of light which the dark side of the moon receives bears the same proportion to the light of that side which is illuminated, as that . . .
If you want to see how much brighter the shaded portion of the moon is than the background on which it is seen, conceal the luminous portion of the moon with your hand or with some other more distant object.

# 903.

## THE SPOTS ON THE MOON.

Some have said that vapors rise from the moon, like clouds, and are positioned between the moon and our eyes. But, if this were the case, these spots would never be permanent, either in position or form; and, seeing the moon from various angles, even if these spots did not move, they would change in form, as objects do when they are seen from different sides.

# 904.

## OF THE SPOTS ON THE MOON.

Others say that the moon is composed of more or less transparent parts, as though one part were like alabaster and others like crystal or glass. It would follow from this that when the sun casts its rays on the less transparent portions, the light would remain on the surface, and so the denser part would be illuminated, while the transparent portions would show the shadows of their dark depths; and this is how the nature of the moon is composed. This opinion has found favor with many philosophers, and particularly with Aristotle, and yet it is a false view—for, in the various positions that the moon and sun frequently hold in relation to our eyes, we would see these spots vary, at one time appearing dark and at another light. They would be dark when the sun is in the West and the moon is in the middle of the sky, because the sun would then be shining through the transparent parts.

--- Page 281 ---
The illuminated sphere of the earth, in which the ocean and other waters shine, and the land creates spots within that brightness; thus, half of our earth would be seen encircled by the brightness of the sea illuminated by the sun, and on the moon, such a similarity would be the smallest part of that moon. Fourthly, a radiant object is not reflected in another radiant object; therefore the sea, taking its brightness from the sun just as the moon does, could not reflect the earth, nor could the body of the sun or any star opposite to it be seen reflected there specifically.

---

## 906.

If you keep the details of the spots on the moon under observation, you will often find great variety in them, and I have proven this myself by drawing them. This arises from clouds that rise from the waters of that moon, which interpose themselves between the sun and that water, and with their shadow take the sun's rays away from that water; consequently, that water remains dark because it cannot reflect the solar body.

---

## 907.

How the spots of the moon are changed from what they once were, because of the course of its waters.

The larger circle is less red and is in the first, lowest part of these vapors; the second, smaller one is higher and appears redder, because it is seen through...

ASTRONOMY. [913.]

It demonstrates that when the sun is seen through the branches of trees that are bare of their leaves at a great distance, those branches do not occupy any part of that sun in our eyes; the same thing happens with the aforementioned planets, which, even though they are without light of their own, do not—as has been said—occupy any part of the sun from our eye.

### SECOND ARGUMENT.

Some say that the stars appear most brilliant at night the higher they are above us, and that if they had no light of their own, the shadow created by the earth—which is positioned between them and the sun—would darken them, since they would not see, nor be seen by, the solar body. But these people have not considered that the conical shadow of the earth does not reach many of the stars; and in those it does reach, the cone is so diminished that it occupies little of the star's body, and the remainder is illuminated by the sun.

### 913.

Why the planets appear larger on the horizon than they do above us, which should be the opposite, since they are 3,500 miles closer to us when they are in the middle of the sky than when they are at the horizon.

All the layers of the elements through which the images of the celestial bodies pass to reach the eye are equal, but the angles at which the central line of such images penetrates them are unequal, and the distance is greater, as shown by the excess of line *a b* over *a d*; and by the 9th proposition of the 7th book, the size of these celestial bodies on the horizon is proven.
