## XVI.
# *Physical Geography.*

Leonardo's research regarding the structure of the earth and sea was conducted at a time when the long-distance voyages of the Spaniards and Portuguese had also sparked a particular interest in geographical questions in Italy, and especially in Tuscany. Nevertheless, it should hardly surprise us to find that regarding deeper questions, such as the structure of the globe, the primitive state of the earth's surface, and similar topics, he was far ahead of his time.

The number of passages that deal with such matters is relatively large; like almost all of Leonardo's scientific notes, they address partly theoretical and partly practical questions. Some of his theoretical views on the motion of water were collected into a copied manuscript volume by an early transcriber, though without any acknowledgment of the source from which they were taken. This copy is now in the Library of the Barberini palace in Rome and was published under the title: "*De moto e misura dell' acqua*," by FRANCESCO CARDINALI, Bologna 1828. In this work, the texts are organized under the following titles: *Book I. Of the sphere of water; Book II. Of the motion of water; Book III. Of the wave of water; Book IV. Of the eddies of water; Book V. Of falling water; Book VI. Of the breaks made by water; Book VII. Of things carried by water; Book VIII. Of the measurement and channels of water; Book IX. Of mills and other water engines.*

The large number of isolated observations scattered throughout the manuscripts explains why we so frequently find notes for new schemes to organize those relating to water and its motions, particularly in the *Codex Atlanticus*: I have printed several of these plans as an introduction to the *Physical Geography*, and I have actually organized the texts according to the clue provided by one of them, which is undoubtedly one of the latest notes referring to the subject (No. 920). The text provided as No. 930, which is also taken from one of Leonardo's late notebooks, served as a basis for the organization of the first of the seven books—or sections—bearing the title: Of the Nature of Water (*Dell' acque in se*).

--- Page 288 ---
174

PHYSICAL GEOGRAPHY.

Since I have not made it part of this project to print the passages that refer to purely physical principles, it has also been necessary to exclude those practical researches which, according to the indications given in No. 920, should have been included as Books 13, 14, and 15. I can only briefly mention here that Leonardo—as it seems to me, especially in his youth—devoted a great deal of attention to the construction of mills. This is proven by a number of very carefully and minutely executed drawings found in the Codex Atlanticus. Nor was it possible to include his considerations on the regulation of rivers, the construction of canals, and so forth (No. 920, Books 10, 11, and 12); however, those passages in which the design of a canal is directly connected with notes on specific places will be found duly inserted under section XVII (Topographical notes). In Vol. I, No. 5, the text refers to canal-making in general.

On one point only can the collection of passages included under the general heading of Physical Geography claim to be complete. When comparing and sorting the materials for this work, I took particular care not to exclude or omit any text in which a geographical name was mentioned even incidentally. This is because in all such research, the chief interest, as it appeared to me, related to the question of whether these sharp observations on the various local characteristics of mountains, rivers, or seas had been made by Leonardo himself and on-site. It is obvious that the few general and somewhat superficial observations on the Rhine and the Danube, or on England and Flanders, must have been obtained from maps or from informants; in the case of Flanders, Leonardo himself acknowledges this (see No. 1008). But that most of the other and more exact observations were made on-site by Leonardo himself may be safely assumed from his method and the style in which he writes about them. We should bear in mind that in all investigations, of whatever kind, experience is always described as the only basis on which he relies. Incidentally, as in No. 984, he finds it necessary to allude to the total absence of any previously recorded observations.

Let the earth make whatever changes it may in its weight, the surface of the sphere of water will never deviate from its equal distance from the center of the world.

937.

#### WHETHER THE EARTH IS SMALLER THAN THE WATER.

Some assert it to be true that the land which is uncovered by water is much less than that which is covered by water. But considering the thickness of the 7,000-mile diameter which this earth has, one may conclude the water to be of shallow depth.

#### OF THE EARTH.

The fact that the peaks of the mountains rise so high above the sphere of the water may have resulted from this: a very large space within the earth which was filled with water—that is to say, a vast cavern—must have had a great part of its vault fall toward the center of the world, having become detached by means of the course of the subterranean springs which continually wear away the place through which they pass.

The sinking of regions, as in the Dead Sea of Syria—that is, Sodom and Gomorrah.

It is necessary that there should be more water than land, and the visible portion of the sea does not...

--- Page 300 ---
186
PHYSICAL GEOGRAPHY. [942. 943.]

942.

## CONCERNING THE SEA, WHICH TO MANY SIMPLE PEOPLE SEEMS HIGHER THAN THE LAND THAT FORMS ITS SHORES.

*b d* is a plain through which a river flows to the sea; this plain ends at the sea. Since, in fact, the dry land that is uncovered is not perfectly level—for if it were, the river would have no motion—and because the river does move, this area must be called a slope rather than a plain. Therefore, this plain *d b* ends at the sphere of water in such a way that if one were to extend it in a straight line toward *b a*, it would go beneath the sea. From this, it follows that the sea *a c b* appears higher than the uncovered land.

Naturally, no part of the land uncovered by water will ever be lower than the surface of the sphere of that water.

---

943.

## CONCERNING CERTAIN PEOPLE WHO SAY THE WATER IS HIGHER THAN THE UNCOVERED LAND.

Certainly, I feel no small wonder at the common opinion which, contrary to the truth, is held by the universal consensus of human judgment. This opinion is that everyone agrees the surface of the sea is higher than the highest peaks of the mountains, citing many useless and childish reasons. Against these people, I will offer only one simple and brief reason: we see clearly that if the shore of the sea were removed, the water would cover the earth and make it a perfect sphere. Now, consider how much earth would have to be removed to allow the sea waves to cover the world; therefore, whatever would be removed must be higher than the sea-shore.

--- Page 301 ---
187
OF THE NATURE OF WATER.

### 944.

**THE OPINION OF SOME PERSONS WHO SAY THAT THE WATER OF SOME SEAS IS HIGHER THAN THE HIGHEST SUMMITS OF MOUNTAINS; AND NEVERTHELESS THE WATER WAS FORCED UP TO THESE SUMMITS.**

Water would not move from place to place if it were not seeking the lowest level; and by a natural consequence, it can never return to a height equal to that of the place where it first came to light upon issuing from the mountain. And that portion of the sea which, in your vain imagination, you say was so high that it flowed over the summits of the high mountains, would have been swallowed up and poured out again through the outlets of these mountains for many centuries. You can well imagine that during all the time that the Tigris and Euphrates

### 945.

have flowed from the summits of the mountains of Armenia, it must be believed that all the water of the ocean has passed very many times through these outlets. And do you not believe that the Nile must have sent more water into the sea than currently exists in the entire element of water? Undoubtedly, yes. And if all this water had fallen away from this body of the earth, this terrestrial machine would long since have been without water. From this we may conclude that the water goes from the rivers to the sea, and from the sea to the rivers, thus constantly circulating and returning, and that all the sea and the rivers have passed through the mouth of the Nile an infinite number of times.

--- Page 302 ---
# II.
# ON THE OCEAN.

### 946.

#### WHY WATER IS SALT.
Pliny says in his second book, chapter 103, that the water of the sea is salt because the heat of the sun dries up the moisture and drinks it up; and this gives to the wide-stretching sea the flavor of salt. But this cannot be admitted, because if the saltness of the sea were caused by the heat of the sun, there can be no doubt that lakes, pools, and marshes would be so much the more salt, as their waters have less motion and are of less depth; but experience shows us, on the contrary, that these lakes have their waters quite free from salt. Again, it is stated by Pliny in the same chapter that this saltness might originate...

--- Page 303 ---
because all the fresh and subtle portions which the heat attracts easily being taken away, the more bitter and coarser part will remain, and thus the water on the surface is fresher than at the bottom; but this is contradicted by the same reasons given above, which is, that the same thing would happen in marshes and other waters which are dried up by the heat. Again, it has been said that the saltness of the sea is the sweat of the earth; to this it may be answered that all the springs of water which penetrate through the earth would then be salt. But the conclusion is that the saltness of the sea must proceed from the many springs of water which, as they penetrate into the earth, find mines of salt, and these they dissolve in part and carry with them to the ocean and the other seas, from which the clouds, the begetters of rivers, never carry it up. And the sea would be saltier in our times than it ever was at any time; and if the opponent were to say that in infinite time the sea would dry up or congeal into salt, to this I answer that this salt is restored to the earth by the setting free of that part of the earth which rises out of the sea with the salt it has acquired, and the rivers return it to the earth under the sea.

### 947.

For the third and last reason, we will say that salt is in all created things; and this we learn from water passed over the ashes and cinders of burnt things, and the urine of every animal, and the waste products issuing from their bodies, and the earth into which all things are converted by decay.

But—to put it better—given that the world is everlasting, it must be admitted that its population will also be eternal; hence the human species has eternally been and would be consumers of salt; and if all the mass of the earth were to be turned into salt, it would suffice.

--- Page 304 ---
...would not suffice for all human food; therefore we are forced to admit either that the substance of salt is eternal along with the world, or that it dies and is reborn along with the men who consume it. But as experience teaches us that salt does not die—as is evident by fire, which does not consume it, and by water, which becomes salt in proportion to the amount dissolved in it (and when the water is evaporated, the salt always remains in its original quantity)—it must pass through human bodies and be found again in urine, sweat, or other excretions. This is the same salt that is carried every year into the cities; therefore salt is dug from places where there is urine. Sea hogs and sea winds are salt.

We will say that the rains which penetrate the earth are what lie beneath the foundations of cities and their inhabitants, and it is these rains which, through the internal passages of the earth, restore the saltness taken from the sea; and that the shifting of the sea, which has been over all the mountains, caused the salt to be left there in the mines found within those mountains, etc.

---

## 948.

The waters of the salt sea are fresh at their greatest depths.

---

## 949.

### THAT THE OCEAN DOES NOT PENETRATE UNDER THE EARTH.

The ocean does not penetrate under the earth, and this we learn from the many and various springs of fresh water which, in different parts of that ocean, make their way up from the bottom to the surface. The same thing is further proved by wells dug at a distance of a mile from the said ocean, which fill with fresh water; and this happens because fresh water is thinner than salt water and, consequently, more penetrating.

Which weighs more: water when frozen or when not frozen?

***

**956. I.** Allusion may here be made to the mythological explanation of the ebb and flow given in the Edda. Utgardloki says to Thor (Gylfaginning 48): "When you were drinking out of the horn, and it seemed to you that it was slow in emptying, a wonder occurred which I should not have believed possible: the other end of the horn lay in the sea, which you did not see; but when you go to the sea, you shall see how much you have drunk out of it. And that is what men now call the ebb tide."

Several passages in various manuscripts treat the ebb and flow. In collecting them, I have been guided by the rule to only transcribe those which named some particular spot.

--- Page 307 ---
### ON THE TIDE.

All seas have their high and low tides at the same time, but they appear to vary because the days do not begin at the same time throughout the entire world. For instance, when it is noon in our hemisphere, it is midnight in the opposite hemisphere. At the eastern boundaries of these two hemispheres, the night begins and follows the day; at the western boundaries of these hemispheres, the day begins and follows the night from the opposite side. Therefore, it is concluded that although the aforementioned rising and falling of the sea levels occur at the same time, they appear to vary for the reasons already stated. The waters are then drawn into the channels originating from the sea floors, which branch out inside the body of the earth and correspond to the sources of rivers; these rivers constantly take from the seabed the water that has flowed into it. An immense amount of water is also drawn from the surface of the sea. And if you were to argue that the moon, appearing in the eastern part of the Mediterranean Sea, should begin to attract the sea waters to itself, it would follow that the effects would be seen immediately at the eastern end of that sea. Furthermore, since the Mediterranean Sea is about one-eighth of the circumference of the sphere of water—being 3,000 miles long—and the high and low tides occur only four times in 24 hours, this effect would not align with a 24-hour period unless the Mediterranean Sea were 6,000 miles long. This is because if the displacement of such a vast sea had to pass through the Strait of Gibraltar while following the moon, the current of water through that strait would be so great, and would rise to such a height, that beyond the strait it would flow for many miles into the ocean, causing massive floods and turbulence. Consequently, it would be impossible to sail through it; and immediately after this, the ocean would return the received waters with the same fury back to where it received them.

--- Page 308 ---
<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>PHYSICAL GEOGRAPHY. [959. 960.</header>

It follows, then, that one could never pass through such a strait; yet experience shows that people pass through it at all hours, except when the wind blows in the direction of the current, for then the strong ebb tide increases. The sea does not raise the water level within the straits that have an outlet, but it certainly becomes congested and is delayed before reaching them; consequently, with a furious motion, it later makes up for the time of its delay until the end of its ebb movement.

had received with equal fury to the place they had come from, so that no one ever could pass through those straits. Now experience shows that at every hour they are passed in safety, but when the wind sets in the same direction as the current, the strong ebb increases. The sea does not raise the water that has issued from the straits, but it checks them and this retards the tide; then it makes up with furious haste for the time it has lost until the end of the ebb movement.

In the West, near to Flanders, the sea rises and decreases every 6 hours about 20 braccia, and 22 when the moon is in its favour; but 20 braccia is the general rule, and this rule, as it is evident, cannot have the moon for its cause. This variation in the increase and decrease of the sea every 6 hours may arise from the damming up of the waters, which are poured into the Mediterranean by the quantity of rivers from Africa, Asia and Europe, which flow into that sea, and the waters which are given to it by those rivers; it pours them to the ocean

--- Page 309 ---
...and Calpe, returns to the ocean the waters given to it by those rivers. This ocean, extending between the islands of England and others further north, becomes dammed up and held high in various gulfs. These seas, because their surfaces have moved away from the center of the world, have acquired weight; since this weight exceeds the force of the incoming waters that caused it, the water regains momentum in the opposite direction to its arrival. It then pushes back against the straits that were supplying the water, and it does so most forcefully against the Strait of Gibraltar. For a certain period of time, this strait remains dammed up and holds back all the water newly delivered during that time by the aforementioned rivers. This seems to me to be one of the reasons that could be assigned as the cause of the flow and ebb, as is proven in the 21st [chapter] of the 4th [book] of my theory.

This is intended to represent the earth cut through the middle, showing the depths of the sea and of the earth; the waters start from the bottom of the seas, and branching through the earth they rise to the summits of the mountains, flowing back through the rivers and returning to the sea.

<margin>
Observations in support of the hypothesis (963-969).
</margin>

Leic. 21 b] ->963.<-

The waters circulate with constant motion from the lowest depths of the sea to the highest summits of the mountains, not following the nature of heavy matter; and in this case they act as the blood of animals does, which is always moving from the sea of the heart and flows to the top of their heads; and it is here that veins burst—as one may see when a vein bursts in the nose, that all the blood rises from below.

--- Page 311 ---
964-966.] SUBTERRANEAN WATER COURSES. 197

...from below rises to the level of the burst vein. When the water rushes out of a burst vein in the earth, it obeys the nature of other things heavier than the air, and therefore it always seeks the lowest places. These waters traverse the body of the earth with infinite branching.

### 964.

The same cause which moves the humors in every species of living body and by which every injury is repaired, also moves the water from the deepest depths of the sea to the highest peaks of the mountains, just as the water rises from the lower parts of the vine to the high points where it has been pruned.

### 965.

It is the specific property of water that it is dedicated to being the vital humor of this arid earth; and the cause which moves it through its branching veins, against the natural course of heavy things, is the very same that moves the humors in all species of living bodies. But what is most admirable to those who contemplate it is that it rises from the deepest depths of the sea to the highest summits of the mountains, and pouring out through the burst veins, it returns to the low sea; then once more, it quickly mounts again and returns by the aforementioned descent. Thus, it moves from the internal parts to the external ones, and from the lowest to the highest, turning back when it falls in its natural course. Thus, joined together in a continuous revolution, it goes circling through the passages of the earth.

### 966.

WHETHER WATER CAN RISE FROM THE SEA TO THE TOPS OF THE MOUNTAINS.

The water of the ocean cannot make its way from the bases to the tops of the mountains...

--- Page 312 ---
...which bound it, but only so much rises as the dryness of the mountain attracts. And if, on the contrary, the rain—which penetrates from the summit of the mountain to the base, which is the boundary of the sea—descends and softens the slope opposite to the said mountain and constantly draws the water, like a siphon which pours through its longest side, it must be this which draws up the water of the sea. Thus, if *s n* were the surface of the sea, and the rain descends from the top of the mountain *a* to *n* on one side, and on the other sides it descends from *a* to *m*, without a doubt this would occur after the manner of distilling through felt, or as happens through the tubes called siphons. And at all times the water which has softened the mountain, by the great rain which runs down the two opposite sides, would constantly attract the rain *a n* on its longest side together with the water from the sea, if that side of the mountain *a m* were longer than the other *a n*; but this cannot be, because no part of the earth which is not submerged by the ocean can be lower than that ocean, etc.

### 967.

### OF SPRINGS OF WATER ON THE TOPS OF MOUNTAINS.

It is quite evident that the whole surface of the ocean—when there is no storm—is at an equal distance from the center of the earth, and that the tops of the mountains are farther from this center in proportion as they rise above the surface of that sea; therefore, if the body of the earth were not like that of man, it would be impossible that the waters of the sea—being so much lower than the mountains—could by their nature rise up to the summits of these mountains. Hence, it is to be believed that the same cause which keeps the blood at the top of the head in man keeps the water at the summits of the mountains.

--- Page 313 ---
### 968.

**FURTHER CONFIRMATION OF WHY WATER REACHES THE TOPS OF MOUNTAINS.**

I say that just as natural heat keeps the blood in the veins at the top of the human head—for when a person is dead, the cold blood sinks to the lower parts—and when the sun warms a person's head, the blood increases and rises with such humors that, by straining the veins, it often generates headaches; similarly, the veins that ramify through the body of the earth, driven by the natural heat distributed throughout the entire containing body, keep the water elevated in the veins at the high peaks of mountains. This water, which passes through a closed conduit within the body of that mountain like a dead thing, will not emerge from its original low position because it is not warmed by the vital heat of the primary vein. Furthermore, the heat of the element of fire and, during the day, the heat of the sun, have the power to draw out the moisture from the low places of the mountains and pull it upward, in the same way that the sun draws up the clouds and pulls their moisture from the bed of the sea.

---

### 969.

Many veins of salt water are found at great distances from the sea; this might happen because such a vein passes through some salt mine, like the one in Hungary where salt is extracted from massive caves, almost as if they were quarrying stone.

--- Page 314 ---
## IV.
### OF RIVERS.

970.

**ON THE ORIGIN OF RIVERS.**

*On the way in which the sources of rivers are fed.*

The body of the earth, like the bodies of animals, is intersected with a network of water branches which are all connected and are designed to provide nourishment and life to the earth and its creatures. These originate from the depths of the sea and, after many cycles, must return to it through the rivers created by the bursting of these springs. If you chose to say that the rains of winter or the melting of snows in summer were the cause of the birth of rivers, I could mention the rivers that originate in the torrid countries of Africa, where it never rains—and even less snows—because the intense heat always dissolves into air all the clouds that are driven there by the winds. And if you chose to say that such rivers, which increase in July and August, come from the snows that melt in May and June due to the sun’s approach to the snows on the mountains of Scythia, and that such meltings flow down into certain valleys and form lakes, into which they enter through springs and subterranean passages, the—

--- Page 315 ---
...that these then emerge at the source of the Nile, this is false; because Scythia is lower than the source of the Nile, and furthermore, Scythia is only 400 miles from the Black Sea, while the source of the Nile is 3,000 miles away from the Egyptian sea into which its waters flow.

---

### 971.

**The tide in estuaries.**

Book 9, on the meeting of rivers and their ebb and flow. The cause is the same in the sea, where it is created by the Straits of Gibraltar; and it also occurs because of whirlpools.

If two rivers meet each other along the same straight line, and then together take their course between two right angles, an ebb and flow will occur first in one river and then in the other above their confluence, especially if the outlet for their combined volume is no faster than when they were separate. Here, four cases occur.

---

### 972.

**On the alterations caused in the courses of rivers by their confluence.**

When a smaller river pours its waters into a larger one, and that larger river flows from the opposite bank, the course of the smaller river will bend against the approach of the larger river. This happens because, when the larger river fills its entire bed with water, it creates an eddy in front of the mouth of that river, and thus carries the water poured in by the smaller river along with its own. When a smaller river pours its waters into a larger river which has its current running across the mouth of the smaller one, then its waters will bend in the direction of the larger river's flow.

--- Page 317 ---
### 976.

#### ON THE VIBRATION OF THE EARTH.

<margin>On the alterations in the channels of rivers.</margin>
Subterranean water channels, like those that exist between the air and the earth, are the ones that continuously wear away and deepen the beds of their currents.

---

### 977.

<margin>The origin of the sand in rivers (977. 978).</margin>
A river that flows from mountains deposits a great quantity of large stones in its bed, which still retain some of their angles and sides; as it flows, it carries down smaller stones with more worn angles; that is to say, the large stones become smaller. Farther on, it deposits coarse gravel and then finer gravel, followed by coarse sand and then fine sand. Continuing this way, the water—turbid with sand and silt—reaches the sea. The sand settles on the sea-shores, being cast up by the salt waves, while the silt is of such a fine nature that it seems almost like water. This silt does not stay on the sea-shores but is carried back out by its lightness, because it originated from rotten leaves and other very light materials. Nevertheless, being almost—as was said—of the nature of water itself, it later settles and stays at the bottom of the sea during calm weather. There, because of its fineness, it becomes compact and, due to its smoothness, resists the waves that pass over it. Shells are found in this material, and this is the white clay used for making pottery.

---

### 978.

All the water flowing from the mountains to the sea carries stones from the hills into the sea. Because of the surge of sea-water against the mountains, these stones were thrown back toward the mountains; as the waters advanced and retreated, the stones were tossed about by them, and in rolling, their angles struck together. Then, as the parts that least resisted the blows were worn off, the stones ceased to be angular and became round in shape, as can be seen on the banks of the Elsa. Those stones remained larger which were moved less far from their place of origin.

--- Page 318 ---
...birthplace; and they became smaller the farther they were moved from the aforementioned location, so that in the process they were converted into small pebbles, then into sand, and finally into mud. After the sea receded from the aforementioned mountains, the salt left by the sea, combined with other moisture from the earth, created a bond with this gravel and sand, so that the gravel was converted into stone and the sand into tufa. An example of this can be seen in the Adda where it emerges from the mountains of Como, and in the Ticino, the Adige, and the Oglio coming from the German Alps, and similarly in the Arno from Monte Albano near Monte Lupo and Capraia, where the massive rocks are all composed of conglomerate pebbles of various stones and colors.

--- Page 319 ---
# V.

## ON MOUNTAINS.

The formation of mountains (979-983).

**979.**
Mountains are made by the currents of rivers.
Mountains are destroyed by the currents of rivers.

**980.**
How the northern roots of any Alpine range are not yet petrified; and this is clearly seen where the rivers that cut through them flow toward the north. In the heights of the mountains, these rivers cut through the strata of living stone; yet, where they meet the plains, these same strata are composed entirely of potter’s clay. This is demonstrated in the Val di Lamona by the river Lamona, which, as it emerges from the Apennine Mountains, exhibits these conditions along its banks.

How the rivers have sawn through and divided the sections of the great Alps from one another; and this is revealed by the arrangement of the stratified rocks, for from the summit of the mountain down to the river, the correspondence of the strata can be seen on one side of the river just as on the other. How

--- Page 320 ---
The stratified stones of the mountains are all layers of mud, deposited one above the other by the floods of rivers. The varying thicknesses of the stone strata are created by different floods of the rivers—that is to say, by greater or lesser floods.

### 981.

The summits of mountains rise constantly over a long period of time.
The opposite sides of mountains always draw closer together; the depths of the valleys which are above the sphere of the water always approach the center of the world over a long period of time.
In an equal amount of time, the valleys deepen much more than the mountains rise.
The bases of the mountains always become narrower.
In proportion as the valley becomes deeper, its sides are worn away more quickly.

### 982.

In every hollow at the summits of mountains, the folds of the stone strata will always be found.

### 983.

**OF THE SEA WHICH ENCIRCLES THE EARTH.**
I find that in ancient times, the surface of the earth in its plains was entirely occupied and covered by salt waters, etc.

--- Page 321 ---
984.

Because physical things are much more ancient than written letters, it is no wonder if, in our day, no records exist of the aforementioned seas having occupied so many countries. And even if some records had existed, wars, fires, floods, and changes in languages and laws have destroyed all traces of antiquity. But for us, the testimony of things born in salt waters, which are now found in high mountains far from the seas of that time, is sufficient.

--- Page 322 ---
### VI.

## GEOLOGICAL PROBLEMS.

**985.**

In this work of yours, you must first prove that the shells at an elevation of a thousand braccia were not carried there by the deluge, because they are seen to be all at the same level, and many mountains are seen to rise far above that level. You must also ask whether the deluge was caused by rain or by the swelling of the sea. Then you must show that neither by rain that causes rivers to rise, nor by the swelling of the sea, could the shells—being heavy objects—be pushed by the sea up the mountains, nor be pulled along by the rivers against the natural flow of their waters.

**986.**

**Doubts about the deluge.**

**A DOUBTFUL POINT.**

Here a doubt arises, and it is this: whether the deluge that occurred in the time of Noah was universal or not. And here it would seem not, for the reasons...

--- Page 323 ---
### 987.

**GEOLOGICAL PROBLEMS.**

...seem not, for the reasons that will be given: We have it in the Bible that the aforementioned deluge consisted of 40 days and 40 nights of continuous and universal rain, and that such rain rose ten cubits above the highest mountain in the world. And if it were so, that the rain was universal, it would have covered our earth in its spherical form. A spherical surface is, in every part, equally distant from the center of its sphere; therefore, the sphere of water, being in the state of the said condition, it is impossible for the water upon it to move, because water does not move of itself unless it descends. Consequently, how did the water of such a great deluge depart, if it is proved here that it had no motion? And if it did depart, how did it move if it did not go upward? Here, then, natural reasons are lacking; therefore, to resolve such a doubt, it is necessary to call upon a miracle for help, or to say that such water was evaporated by the heat of the sun.

---

### 987.

**OF THE DELUGE AND OF MARINE SHELLS.**

**That marine shells could not go up the mountains.**

If you say that the shells which are seen in our times within the borders of Italy, far from the seas and at such a great height, were there because of the deluge that left them there, I answer you that, if you believe that such a deluge rose 7 cubits above the highest mountain, as he who measured them wrote, these shells—which always stay near the seashores—should have remained on top of such mountains, and not just a little above the base of the mountains, nor all at the same height, layer upon layer. And if you say that these shells were eager to stay near the seashores and that, as the water rose to such a height, the shells left their original site and followed the rising of the waters up to their highest point, here it is answered that, the shell being an animal...

--- Page 324 ---
...slower; because it does not swim, but instead makes a furrow in the sand by using its sides, and in this furrow it will travel only 3 to 4 braccia each day. Therefore, this creature, with such a slow motion, could not have traveled from the Adriatic Sea as far as Monferrato in Lombardy, which is a distance of 250 miles, in 40 days—as was claimed by the person who kept track of that time. And if you say that the waves carried them there, they cannot support themselves due to their weight, except at the bottom. And if you will not grant me this, at least admit that they would have had to remain at the summits of the highest mountains, and in the lakes that are enclosed among the mountains, such as Lake Lario or Como, Lake Maggiore, and those of Fiesole, Perugia, and similar places.

And if you should say that the shells were carried by the waves because they were empty and dead, I say that where the dead ones went, they were not far removed from the living ones; for in these mountains, living ones are found, which are recognizable by their shells being in pairs. They are found in a layer where there are no dead ones; and a little higher up, a place is found where all the dead ones were thrown by the waves with their shells separated, near where the rivers fell into the sea at a great depth. This is like the Arno, which fell from the Gonfolina near Monte Lupo, where it left a deposit of gravel that can still be seen, and which has bonded together; a single conglomerate has been formed from stones of various regions, natures, colors, and hardness. A little beyond this sandstone conglomerate, a tufa has formed where the water turned toward Castel Fiorentino; farther on, the mud was deposited in which the shells lived, which rose in layers according to how the floods of the turbid Arno poured into that sea. From time to time, the bottom of the sea was raised, producing these shells in layers, as is shown in the cutting at Colle Gonzoli, laid open by the river Arno, which wears away its base; in which cutting...

--- Page 325 ---
988.]

...the Arno which is wearing away the base of it; in which cutting the said layers of shells are very plainly to be seen in clay of a bluish color, and various marine objects are found there. And the land of our hemisphere has risen so much higher than it used to be, in proportion to how much lighter it became than the waters it lost through the rift at Gibraltar and Ceuta; and it rose all the more because the weight of the waters lost from here was added to the earth turned toward the other hemisphere. And if the shells had been carried by the muddy Deluge, they would have been mixed together, separated from one another within the mud, and not in orderly steps and layers—as we see them in our own time.

---

### 988.

<margin>Leic. 9 a]</margin>
<margin>The marine shells were not produced away from the sea.</margin>

As for those who say that shells existed for a long time and were born far from the sea due to the nature of the site and the heavens, which influence and dispose such a place toward the creation of such animals; to them it shall be answered that such an influence could not produce animals all in one line unless they were of the same kind and age—and not the old with the young, nor some with an operculum and others without their covering, nor some broken and others whole, nor some filled with sea-sand and small and large fragments of other shells inside the whole shells that remained open there, nor the claws of crabs without the rest of their bodies, nor the shells of other species stuck to them like animals that moved on top of them; for the traces of their movement still remain upon the outer shell which they once consumed, just as a woodworm does to wood. Nor would there be found among them the bones and teeth of fish, which some call arrows and others serpent tongues.

--- Page 326 ---
Nor would so many parts of various animals be found joined together if they had not been cast there by the sea shores. And the deluge could not have carried them there, because things heavier than water do not float upon the water; and the aforementioned things would not be at such a great height unless they were carried there floating upon the waters, which is impossible due to their weight. Where the valleys do not receive the salt waters of the sea, shells are never seen; as is clearly visible in the great valley of the Arno above Gonfolina, a rock anciently joined with Monte Albano in the form of a very high bank. This bank kept that river dammed up in such a way that before it poured into the sea—which was then at the foot of that rock—it formed two large lakes. The first of these is where today we see the flourishing city of Florence, together with Prato and Pistoia; and Monte Albano continued the rest of the bank as far as where Serravalle is situated today. From the upper Val d’Arno as far as Arezzo, a second lake was created, which poured its waters into the aforementioned lake. It was closed near where Girone is seen today and occupied the entire said upper valley for a distance of 40 miles in length. This valley received on its floor all the earth brought by the water made turbid by it, which can still be seen remaining at a great height at the foot of Prato Magno, where the rivers have not worn it away. Within this earth are seen the deep cuttings of the rivers that have passed there, which descend from the great mountain of Prato Magno; in these cuttings, no vestige of shells or marine earth is seen. This lake was joined with the lake of Perugia.

A great quantity of shells is seen where the rivers pour into the sea, because in such places the waters are not as salty due to the mixture of fresh waters that unite with them. A sign of this is seen where, in ancient times, the Appenini mountains poured their rivers into the Adriatic sea; these, for the most part, show a great quantity of shells among the mountains together with the bluish marine soil.

--- Page 327 ---
...with bluish marine clay; and all the rocks that are quarried in such places are full of shells. The same can be observed to have happened with the Arno when it fell from the rock of Gonfolina into the sea, which at that time was not very far below; for then it was higher than the top of San Miniato al Tedesco, since at the highest summits of that hill, shores full of shells and oysters can be seen within its walls. The shells did not extend toward Val di Nievole because the fresh waters of the Arno did not reach that far.

That the shells were not moved from the sea by the deluge, because the waters coming from the land—even though they drew the sea toward the land—were the ones that struck its bottom. This is because the water coming from the land has a faster current than that of the sea, and consequently is more powerful; it enters beneath the sea water, stirs the bottom, and carries with it all the movable objects it finds there, such as the aforementioned shells and other similar things. And as the water coming from the land is muddier than sea water, it becomes that much more powerful and heavy than the sea water; therefore, I see no way for the aforementioned shells to be drawn so far inland unless they had been born there. If you were to tell me that the river Loire, which passes through France, covers more than eighty miles of countryside when the sea rises because it is a region of vast plains, and the sea rises about 20 braccia, and shells are found in that plain eighty miles away from the sea; here I answer that the ebb and flow in our Mediterranean seas does not vary so much; for at Genoa it does not vary at all, at Venice but little, and in Africa but little; and where it varies little, it occupies little of the land.

The current of river water always rises higher in the place where its course is impeded, just as it does where it is narrowed to pass under the arches of bridges.

--- Page 328 ---
989.

## A REFUTATION OF THOSE WHO CLAIM THAT SHELLS MAY HAVE BEEN CARRIED TO A DISTANCE OF MANY DAYS' JOURNEY FROM THE SEA BY THE DELUGE, WHICH WAS SO HIGH THAT IT EXCEEDED THOSE HEIGHTS.

I say that the deluge could not have carried objects native to the sea up to the mountains, unless the sea had already risen so much as to create floods as high as those places; and this rise could not have occurred because it would create a vacuum. And if you were to say that the air would rush in to fill it, we have already concluded that a heavy substance cannot remain supported above a light one; therefore, by necessity, we must conclude that this deluge was caused by rainwater. If this is the case, all those waters flow toward the sea, and the sea does not flow up the mountains; and as they flow toward the sea, they push the shells away from the seashore and do not pull them toward themselves. And if you were then to say that the sea, raised by the rainwater, carried these shells to such a height, we have already stated that objects heavier than water do not float upon it but remain at the bottom, and they do not move from there except by the impact of waves. And if you claim that the waves carried them to such high places, we have proved that in great depths, the waves at the bottom move in the opposite direction to the motion at the surface; this is made evident by the cloudiness of the sea caused by the earth washed away near the shores. An object lighter than water moves along with its wave and is left at the highest point of the shore by the highest wave. An object heavier than water moves when pushed by its wave both on the surface and at the bottom; and from these two conclusions, which will be fully proved in their proper place, we conclude that surface waves cannot carry shells, because they are heavier than water.

If the deluge had been responsible for carrying shells three hundred or four hundred miles away from the sea, it would have carried them mixed together with various other types of objects heaped in a mass; yet we see at such distances oysters all together, and sea-snails, and cuttlefish, and all the other shells that live together in colonies, being...

--- Page 330 ---
The foolishness and simplicity of those who claim that such animals were carried to places far from the sea by the Deluge; just as another sect of ignorant people asserts that Nature or the heavens created them in such places through celestial influences, as if the bones of fishes grown over a long period of time were not found there; as if one could not count the years or months of their lives in the shells of cockles and snails, just as one does in the horns of bulls and rams, and in the branches of plants that have never been pruned; and having demonstrated by such signs that the length of their lives is manifest, it must be admitted that these animals could not live without moving to seek their food, and in them we see no tool for penetrating the earth and the stone where they are found enclosed. But in what way could one find within a large snail shell the fragments and parts of many other kinds of shells of various natures, if they had not been cast into it by the waves of the sea—while it was already dead upon the seashore—like the other light objects that the sea throws onto the land? Why are so many fragments and whole shells found between layer and layer of stone, if that layer had not already been covered upon the shore by earth cast up by the sea, which then eventually petrified? And if the aforementioned Deluge had carried them to such sites from the sea, you would find those shells at the edge of a single layer, and not at the edges of many; one must then count the winters of the years in which the sea increased the layers of sand and mud brought to it by neighboring rivers, which it discharged upon its shores. And if you wished to say that several deluges occurred to produce such layers and the shells between them, you would also have to affirm that such a deluge happened every year. Furthermore, among the fragments of such shells, it is presumed that such a site was a sea beach, where all the shells are cast up broken and separated, and never in pairs, as they are found alive in the sea with two shells that serve as a cover for one another; and among the layers of the riverbanks and the seashores, fragments are found; and within the boundaries of the stones, shells are found rarely and in pairs, like those that were left by the sea buried alive within the mud, which then dried and, with time, petrified.

--- Page 333 ---
# VII.
## ON THE ATMOSPHERE.

---

995.

**Constituents of the atmosphere.**
The brightness of the air is caused by the water which has dissolved itself within it into imperceptible molecules. These, having caught the light of the sun from the opposite side, reflect the brightness that is visible in the air; and the azure blue which appears there is caused by the darkness that is hidden beyond that air.

---

996.

**On the motion of air.**
The return eddies of wind at the mouths of certain valleys strike upon the waters and scoop them out into a great hollow, carrying the water into the air in a column-like shape with the color of a cloud. I once saw this same thing happen over a sandbank of the Arno, where the sand was hollowed out to a depth greater than a man’s height; the gravel was removed from it and tossed aside over a wide area, appearing in the air in the shape of a massive bell tower. Its summit spread out like the branches of a large pine tree, and it then bent when it encountered the direct wind passing over the mountains.

--- Page 338 ---
For instance, in the discussion regarding the equilibrium of the water mass in the Mediterranean Sea—a subject which, it should be noted, had attracted the interest and study of almost no other observer at that time. The sharp observations in Nos. 985–993 concerning the presence of shells on mountaintops are enough to prove—in my opinion—that it was not in his nature to let himself be led into broad generalizations that went beyond the limits of his own investigations, even when his personal studies yielded such brilliant results.

Most of these Topographical Notes, though suggesting very careful and thorough research, do not, however, as previously stated, necessarily provide indisputable evidence that the research was Leonardo’s own. But it must be admitted that in more than one instance, probability favors this idea.

Among the passages that treat the topography of Eastern locations in some detail, by far the most interesting is a description of the Taurus Mountains; however, since this text is written in the style of a formal report and is associated in the original with certain letters that explain its origin, I have thought it best not to separate it from that context. It can be found under No. XXI (Letters).

It should not surprise us that Florence and its surrounding area, where Leonardo spent his early years, are mentioned nowhere except in connection with the canal projects that occupied his attention for a short time during the first decade of the 16th century. The various passages relating to the construction of canals in Tuscany, which are grouped together at the beginning, are immediately followed by those dealing with canal schemes in Lombardy; after these come notes on the city and vicinity of Milan, as well as on the lakes of Northern Italy.

The notes on several towns in Central Italy that Leonardo visited in 1502, while in the service of Cesare Borgia, are reproduced here in the same order as they appear in the notebook used during those travels (MS. L., Institut de France). These notes have little interest in themselves except for suggesting his itinerary. The maps of the districts drawn by Leonardo at the time are more valuable (see the note for No. 1054). The names on these maps are not written from right to left, but in the usual manner, which allows us to infer that they were made by order of someone else, possibly for the use of Cesare Borgia himself; the fact that they nevertheless remained in Leonardo’s possession is not surprising when we consider the sudden political changes and wartime events of that period. There can be no doubt that these maps, which are published here for the first time, are original in the strictest sense of the word—that is to say, drawn from direct observations of the locations themselves. This is proved, among other evidence, by the fact that we find among his manuscripts not only the finished maps but also the rough sketches and studies for them. It would perhaps be difficult to find, among the many contributions to geographical knowledge published during the 16th century, any maps that come close to these in accuracy and detail.

The interesting map of the world as it was then known, which is among the Leonardo manuscripts at Windsor (published in 'Archaeologia' Vol. XI), cannot be attributed to the Master, as the Marchese Girolamo d'Adda has sufficiently proven; it has therefore not been reproduced here.

--- Page 339 ---
225
TOPOGRAPHICAL NOTES.

---

Leonardo's observations on places in Italy that were made before or after his official travels as a military engineer for Cesare Borgia have been arranged in alphabetical order under Nos. 1034—1054. The most interesting are those relating to the Alps and the Apennines, Nos. 1057—1068.

Most of the passages in which France is mentioned have remained unknown until now, as have those concerning the countries bordering the Mediterranean, which appear at the end of this section. Although these may be considered of more questionable importance regarding the Master's biography than those mentioning places in France, it must be admitted that they are interesting for showing the prominent place that Eastern countries held in his geographical studies. He never once refers to the discovery of America.

### 1008.

A river that is to be diverted from one place to another must be coaxed and not treated roughly or with violence. To do this, a sort of floodgate should be constructed in the river, and then another one further ahead downstream, and in the same way a third, fourth, and fifth, so that the river may discharge itself into the channel provided for it, or so that by this means it may be diverted from the place it has damaged—as was done in Flanders, according to what Niccolò di Forzore told me.

### 1010.

**ON THE MARTESANA CANAL.**

By constructing the Martesana canal, the water of the Adda is greatly diminished because it is distributed across many districts for the irrigation of fields. Here is a remedy, which is to make many...

--- Page 345 ---
springs, because that water which is absorbed by the earth is of no use to anyone, nor does it cause any harm, because it is taken from no one; and by making such springs, the water that was previously lost returns again to be of service and benefit to men.

### 1011.

No canal that branches off from a river will be permanent if the water of the river from which it originates is not completely enclosed, like the Martesana canal and the one that branches off from the Ticino.

### 1012.

From the beginning of the canal to the mill.
From the beginning of the Brivio canal to the Travaglia mill is 2,794 trabochi, which is 11,176 braccia, being more than 3 miles and two-thirds; and here I find the canal is 57 braccia higher than the surface of the water of the Adda.

## 1013.

If it is not reported there that this is to be a public canal, it will be necessary to pay for the land; and the king will pay for it by remitting the taxes for one year.

## 1014.

Estimates and preparatory studies for canals (1014. 1015).

**CANAL.**
The canal which may be 16 braccia wide at the bottom and 20 at the top, we may say is on average 18 braccia wide; and if it is 4 braccia deep, at 4 dinari per cubic braccio, the excavation alone will cost 900 ducats per mile, provided the cubic braccia are calculated in ordinary braccia. But if the braccia used are according to the land measure, where every 4 are equal to 4 ½, and if the mile is understood to be three thousand ordinary braccia, then converted into land braccia, those 3000 braccia are reduced by ¼, leaving 2250 braccia; at 4 dinari per braccio, the mile amounts to 675 ducats. At 3 dinari per cubic braccio, the mile amounts to 506 ¼ ducats, so that the excavation of 30 miles of the canal amounts to 15187 ½ ducats.

It is hardly possible to doubt that the canals spoken of here were intended to be in the Milanese territory. Compare this passage with the draft of a letter by Leonardo to the "President of the Office for Water Regulation" in No. 1350. See also the note to No. 745, line 12.

### 1020.

->**ON THE FORCE OF THE VACUUM CREATED INSTANTANEOUSLY.**<-

I saw in Milan a thunderbolt strike the tower della Credenza on the side that faces north; it descended slowly down that side and immediately separated from the tower, tearing away from that wall a section 3 braccia wide in every direction and two deep. This wall was 4 braccia thick and was built of thin, small ancient bricks; and this was pulled out by the vacuum that the flame of the thunderbolt left behind it, etc.

**1019.** Regarding this passage, AMORETTI remarks (*Historical Memoirs* chap. IX): In the same year we see him designing a mechanism of pulleys and ropes with which to transport the sacred relic of the Holy Nail—which is still venerated there—to a more dignified and secure location, namely the final arch of the central nave of the cathedral. On folio 15 of the codex marked Q. R. in 16, he left a double illustration of this mechanism: one consisting of four pulleys and one of three with their respective ropes, adding the note: *In the Cathedral at the pulley of the Nail of the Cross.*

AMORETTI'S views regarding the mark on the manuscript and the date it was written are, it should be noted, entirely groundless. Manuscript L, in which it appears, dates to the year 1502, and it is very unlikely that Leonardo was in Milan at that time; however, this would not prevent the remark, which is somewhat unclear, from referring to the Cathedral of Milan.

**1020.** Regarding buildings in Milan, see also Nos. 751 and 756, and Plate XCV, No. 2 (explained on p. 52), Plate C (explained on pages 60—62). See also pages 25, 39, and 40.

**1021.** *of atmospheric effects* is missing in the original but may safely be inserted into the context, as the formation of clouds is being discussed prior to this text.

### 1027.

Furthermore, if the lowest part of the bank that lies across the current of the water is made in deep and wide steps, like a staircase, the water—which in its downward course usually falls perpendicularly from the edge of such a place to the bottom and wears away the foundations of this bank—can no longer descend with a blow of too much force; and I find the example of this in the stairs down which the water falls in the fields at Sforzesca at Vigevano, over which the running water falls from a height of 50 braccia.

---

### 1028.

Staircase of Vigevano below La Sforzesca, 130 steps, 1/4 braccio high and 1/2 braccio wide, down which the water falls so as not to wear away anything at the end of its fall; by these steps so much soil has come down that it has dried up a pool; that is to say, it has filled it up, and a pool of great depth has been turned into meadows.

<header>1029. 1030.] ITALY. 237</header>

<margin>Leic. 11b]</margin>
<margin>1029.</margin>
<margin>Notes on the North Italian lakes (1029-1033).</margin>

Just as in many places there are springs of water that rise for six hours and fall for six hours, I myself have seen one above Lake Como called Fonte Pliniana, which increases and decreases in such a way that, when it flows, it powers two mills; and when it fails, it drops so low that it is like looking at water in a deep well.

---

<margin>C. A. 211a; 619a]</margin>
<margin>1030.</margin>

### LAKE OF COMO. VALLEY OF CHIAVENNA.

Up along Lake Como, toward Germany, is the valley of Chiavenna where the river Mera flows into the lake. Here are barren and very high mountains with massive rocks. In these mountains, the water-birds are called cormorants; here grow firs, larches, and pines, and there are deer, ibex, chamois, and terrifying bears. One cannot climb there except on all fours. The peasants go there during the snowy seasons with great ingenuity to make the bears tumble down the cliffs. These narrow mountains hem in the river and lie to the right and left for a distance of twenty miles, all in the same manner. Good inns are found every mile. Along the said river there are waterfalls four hundred braccia high, which are a beautiful sight; and one can live well there for a reckoning of four soldi. A great deal of timber is transported down this river.

### VAL SASINA.

Val Sasina runs toward Italy; it is of almost the same form and nature. Much monkshood grows there, and there are great ruins and waterfalls.

---

**1029. 2. 3.** The fountain is known by this name to this day: it is near Torno, on the eastern shore of Como. The waters still rise and fall with the flow and ebb of the tide as Pliny described it (*Epist.* IV, 30; *Hist. Nat.* II, 206).
**1030. 1031.** From the character of the handwriting, we may conclude that these observations were made in Leonardo's youth; and I should infer from their contents that they were notes made in anticipation of a visit to the places here described, and derived from some person (unknown to us) who had given him an account of them.
**14.** The meaning of *mappello* is unknown [Note: likely monkshood/aconite].

--- Page 356 ---
### VALLEY OF INTROZZO.

This valley produces a great quantity of firs, pines, and larches; it is from here that Ambrogio Fereri has his timber brought down. At the head of the Valtellina are the mountains of Bormio, which are formidable and always covered with snow; marmots are found there.

---

### BELLAGGIO.

Opposite the castle of Bellaggio is the Fiumelatte, which falls from a height of more than 100 braccia from the source where it springs, dropping perpendicularly into the lake with an inconceivable roar and noise. This spring flows only in August and September.

---

### VALTELLINA.

Valtellina, as it is called, is a valley enclosed by high and formidable mountains; it produces much strong wine and so much cattle that the locals conclude that more milk than wine is produced there. This is the valley through which the Adda passes, which first runs for more than 40 miles through Germany; this river breeds the grayling, a fish which lives on silver, of which much is found in the river's gravel. In this region, everyone is permitted to sell bread and wine; the wine is worth at most one soldo per bottle, a pound of veal is one soldo, salt is ten dinari, and butter is the same. Their pound is 30 ounces, and eggs are one soldo for a batch.

---

### AT BORMIO.

The baths are at Bormio. About eight miles above Como is the Pliniana, which rises and ebbs every six hours; its rising provides enough water for two mills and more, while its ebbing causes the spring to dry up. Two miles further up is the town of Nesso, where a river falls with great force through a massive rift in the mountain. These trips should be made in the month of May. The largest bare rocks to be found in these parts are the mountains of Mandello, near the mountains of Lecco and Gravidona toward Bellinzona.

**1051.** Leonardo inserted this passage on the margin of the circular plan of Imola, drawn in watercolor—see Plate CXI No. 1. In the original, the fields surrounding the town are light green; the moat, which surrounds the fortifications, and the windings of the river Santerno are light blue. The sections that appear blackish near the river are yellow ochre in the original. The dark groups of houses inside the town are red. At the four points of the compass drawn in the center of the town, Leonardo has written (from right to left): *Mezzodì* (South) at the top; to the left *Scirocco* (Southeast), *Levante* (East), *Greco* (Northeast), *Settentrione* (North), *Maestro* (Northwest), *Ponente* (West), and *Libeccio* (Southwest). The circle in which the plan is drawn is 42 centimeters across in the original.

At the beginning of October 1502, Cesare Borgia was trapped in Imola by a sudden revolt of the Condottieri, and several weeks passed before he could free himself from this state of siege (see Gregorovius, *History of the City of Rome in the Middle Ages*, Vol. VII, Book XIII, 5, 5).
Aside from this incident, Imola plays no significant role in the history of that period. I therefore consider myself fully justified in connecting this map, which is at Windsor, with the siege of 1502 and with Leonardo’s service to Cesare Borgia, because a comparison of these texts, Nos. 1050 and 1051, raises this hypothesis, I believe, to a certainty.

1052. Most of the places described here lie within the district shown in the maps on Plate CXIII.

| **1054.** This passage evidently refers to the making of maps, such as Plate CXII, CXIII, and CXIV. It is true that there is no mention of such works except in this one passage of MS. L. But this can scarcely be taken as evidence against my view that Leonardo occupied himself very extensively at that time with the construction of maps; and all the less so since the preceding chapters clearly prove that during a time so full of events, Leonardo would only occasionally record his observations on paper in MS. L. | Beside this text we find, in the original, a very indistinct sketch, perhaps a plan of a position. It may be noted that these are the only landscape drawings that occur at all in that manuscript. |

1056—1059.] ITALY.
245

### 1056.
[Leic. 10 b]

At Alessandria della Paglia in Lombardy, there are no stones for making lime except those mixed with countless things born in the sea, which is now more than 200 miles away.

### 1057.
[G. 1 b]
The Alps (1057—1062).

At Monbracco, above Saluzzo—one mile above the Certosa, at the foot of Monte Viso—there is a quarry of flaky stone which is as white as Carrara marble, without any spots, and as hard as porphyry or even harder. My close friend, Master Benedetto the sculptor, has promised to give me a small slab of it for grinding colors on the second day of January 1511.

### 1058.
[Leic. 11 b]

There are springs that suddenly appear due to earthquakes or other accidents and then suddenly disappear. This happened on a mountain in Savoy, where certain forests sank and left a very deep abyss; and about four miles from there, the ground opened up on a certain mountain slope and released a sudden, massive flood of water. This flood scoured an entire valley of its cultivated fields, vineyards, and houses, and caused immense damage wherever it flowed.

### 1059.
[C. A. 86 b; 250 b]

The Arve River near Geneva; a quarter of a mile into Savoy, where the fair is held on Midsummer Day in the village of Saint Gervais.

---

Apparatus Criticus

**1056.** Alessandria . . in Lombardia. 2. mixed . . which.
**1057.** Lines 1, 3—13 R. 1. Monbracco . . Saluzzo, 2. at the foot . . Viso. 4. white. 5. Carrara. 6. spots . . hardness. 7. or more. 8. of which. 9. Master Benedetto. 11. promised to give me.

**1059.** 2. mile. 3. held on Midsummer Day . . village . . Gervais.

---

**1057.** *Saluzzo* at the foot of the Alps south of Turin.

**9. 10. *Master Benedetto the sculptor;*** probably a native of Northern Italy familiar with the place described here. It is unlikely to be the Florentine sculptor Benedetto da Majano. Amoretti had published this passage, and M. Ravaisson, who provided a French translation of it in the *Gazette des Beaux Arts* (1881, page 528), remarks as follows:

> *Could the master sculptor whom Leonardo calls his "close friend" be Benedetto da Majano, one of those who judged with him regarding the placement of Michelangelo's David, and from whom the Louvre recently acquired a bust of Filippo Strozzi?*

To this it may be objected that Benedetto da Majano had already been dead for fourteen years by 1511, the year he is supposed to have made the promise to Leonardo. The colors may have been given to the sculptor Benedetto, and the stone may have been payment for them. From the description of the stone given here, we may conclude that it is repeated from the sculptor's own account of it. I do not see how it is possible to conclude from this observation that Leonardo was actually at the site.

**1059.** An indistinct sketch can be seen next to the text.

### 1076.

Is it better for the water to be raised entirely in a single turn, or rather in two?
The answer is that in a single turn, the wheel could not support all the water that it raises in two turns, because at the half-turn of the wheel it would be lifting 100 pounds and no more; and if it had to lift 200 pounds in a full turn, it could not lift them unless the wheel's diameter were doubled. By doubling the diameter, the time required would also double; therefore, it is better and more cost-effective to make such a wheel half the size, etc.
The descent of the hub must not go so low as to reach the surface of the water, because touching the water would decrease its weight.
And if, on the contrary, the water intake were ten times larger than the pipe through which the water escapes, and if it moved ten times slower than the pipe, what would its function be? The answer is found in the 9th rule of this work, which states that the water would rise to a tenth of the height it previously reached in the pipe from which it first emerged.

---

The way in which the river, by its own flow, should scour the riverbed.

According to the ninth rule of the third book: the faster the water flows, the more it wears away its bed; and conversely, the slower the water, the more it deposits the sediment that makes it turbid.

## 1078.

One *trabocco* is four *braccia*, and one mile is three thousand of these *braccia*. Each *braccio* is divided into 12 inches; and the water in the canals has a fall of 2 of these inches for every hundred *trabocchi*. Therefore, 14 inches of fall are necessary for every two thousand eight hundred *braccia* of flow in these canals. It follows that 15 inches of fall give the required momentum to the currents of the water in the said canals—that is, one and a half *braccia* per mile. And from this, we may conclude that the water taken from the river of Ville-

--- Page 393 ---
Adula, where the Danube rises, extends eastward for a course of 1500 miles; its shortest line is about 1000 miles, and the same, or nearly the same, is the length of that branch of the Adula mountains which changes its name as previously mentioned. To the north are the Carpathians, which define the width of the Danube valley; this valley, as I have said, extends eastward for a length of about 1000 miles and is 200 miles wide in some places and 300 in others. Through the middle of this valley flows the Danube, the largest river in Europe. This Danube runs through the middle of Austria and Albania, and to the north of Bavaria, Poland, Hungary, Wallachia, and Bosnia. The Danube, or Donau, used to flow into the Black Sea, which formerly extended almost to Austria and occupied the entire plain through which the Danube now flows. I maintain that the evidence for this is found in the oysters, cockles, snails, scallops, and bones of great fish that are still found in many places on the high slopes of the aforementioned mountains. This sea was formed by the damming up of the spurs of the Adula mountains, which extended to the east and joined the spurs of the Taurus mountains extending to the west. Near Bithynia, the waters of this Black Sea poured into the Propontis, falling into the Aegean Sea—that is, the Mediterranean—where, over a long period, the current eventually separated the spurs of the Adula mountains from those of the Taurus. Consequently, the Black Sea level fell and revealed the Danube valley with the aforementioned provinces, as well as all of Asia Minor north of the Taurus range, the plains from Mount Caucasus to the Black Sea to the west, and the plains of the Don at the foot of the Ural mountains. Thus, the Black Sea must have dropped about 1000 braccia to uncover such vast plains.

--- Page 394 ---
### III.

## THE COUNTRIES OF THE WESTERN END OF THE MEDITERRANEAN.

---

### 1083.

<margin>The Straits of Gibraltar (1083–1085).</margin>

WHY THE SEA CREATES A STRONGER CURRENT IN THE STRAITS OF SPAIN THAN ELSEWHERE.

A river of equal depth will have as much more speed in a narrower width than in a larger one, as the larger width exceeds the smaller.

This proposition is clearly proved by reasoning confirmed by experience; because, when a mile in length of water passes through a channel one mile wide, where the river is 5 miles wide, each of the 5 square miles will contribute 1/5 of itself to replace the square mile of water missing in the open sea; and where the river is 3 miles wide, each of those square miles will contribute a third of its volume for the loss that occurred in the square mile of the strait, as is demonstrated in *f g h* for the mile marked *n*.

--- Page 395 ---
### 1084.

WHY THE CURRENT OF GIBRALTAR IS ALWAYS GREATER TOWARD THE WEST THAN TOWARD THE EAST.

The reason is that if you were to combine the mouths of all the rivers that discharge into this Mediterranean Sea, you would find the total volume of water to be greater than that which this sea pours through the straits into the ocean. You can see Africa discharging its rivers that run northward into this sea, among which is the Nile, which flows through 3,000 miles of Africa; there is also the Bagrada river, the Schelif, and others like them. Likewise, Europe pours into it the Don and the Danube, the Po, the Rhone, the Arno, and the Tiber; so it is evident that these rivers, along with countless others of less fame, create its great breadth, depth, and flow. Furthermore, the sea is no wider than 18 miles at the westernmost point of land where it divides Europe from Africa.

---

### 1085.

The Mediterranean gulf, as an inland sea, used to receive the principal waters of Africa, Asia, and Europe that flowed toward it. Its waters reached the foot of the mountains that surrounded it and formed its shores; and the summits of the Apennines stood out of this sea as islands, surrounded by salt water. Furthermore, Africa, behind its Atlas Mountains, did not expose the surface of its vast plains—about 3,000 miles in length—to the sky. Memphis was situated on the shore of this sea, and above the plains of Italy, where birds now fly in flocks, fish used to wander in large shoals.

---

### 1086.

The greatest ebb produced by the Mediterranean Sea is near Tunis, being about two and a half braccia, and at Venice it falls two braccia. In all the rest of the Mediterranean Sea, the fall is little or nothing.

--- Page 396 ---
### 1087.

Libya.
Describe the mountains of the shifting deserts; that is to say, the formation of the waves of sand carried by the wind, and of its mountains and hills, such as occur in Libya. You will see examples of this on the wide sandbanks of the Po or the Ticino, or other large rivers.

---

### 1088.

Majorca.
Circumfulgore is a naval machine; it was an invention of the people of Majorca.

---

### 1089.

The Tyrrhene Sea.
Some in the Tyrrhene Sea used this method: they fastened an anchor to one end of the yardarm, and to the other a rope, the lower end of which was fastened to an anchor. In battle, they hooked this anchor into the oars of the opposing vessel and, by the power of a capstan, pulled it to the side. They then threw soft soap and tow, daubed with pitch and set on fire, onto the first side where the anchor was attached, so that, to escape the fire, the defenders of that ship had to flee to the opposite side. By doing this, they aided the attacker, because the galley tilted more easily to the side due to the counterpoise.

--- Page 399 ---
A mountain may have fallen and closed the mouth of the Red Sea, preventing the Mediterranean from having an outlet. The Mediterranean Sea, having thus overfilled, found an outlet through the passage below the mountains of Gades. We have seen a similar thing happen in our own times: a mountain fell seven miles across a valley, closing it up and creating a lake. In this way, most lakes have been formed by mountains, such as Lake Garda, Lake Como, Lake Lugano, and Lago Maggiore. The Mediterranean level dropped only slightly near the borders of Syria because of the passage at Gades, but it dropped significantly at the passage itself. This is because before that opening was created, the sea flowed toward the southeast, and afterward, a downward slope had to be formed so that it could flow through the Straits of Gades.

At point A, the water of the Mediterranean fell into the ocean.

All the plains located between the seas and the mountains were formerly covered by salt water.

Every valley has been created by its own river, and the proportion between one valley and another is the same as the proportion between one river and another.

The greatest river in our world is the Mediterranean river,

which flows from the sources of the Nile to the Western Ocean.

Its highest elevation is in Outer Mauritania, and it travels a course of ten thousand miles before it reunites with its ocean, the father of the waters.

That is: 3,000 miles for the Mediterranean, 3,000 for the known Nile, and 3,000 for the Nile that flows to the East, etc.

--- Page 400 ---
### 1093.

**The Nile (1093—1098).**

Therefore, we must conclude that those mountains are of the greatest height, above which the clouds, falling as snow, give rise to the Nile.

---

### 1094.

The Egyptians, the Ethiopians, and the Arabs, when crossing the Nile with camels, are accustomed to attach two bags to the sides of the camels' bodies—that is, skins in the form shown below. In these four meshes of the net, the baggage camels place their feet.

---

### 1095.

The Tigris passes through Asia Minor and carries with it the water of three lakes, one after another, of various elevations; the highest is Munace, the middle one is Pallas, and the lowest is Triton. Similarly, the Nile originates from three very high lakes in Ethiopia; it runs northward and empties into the Egyptian sea with a course of 4,000 miles, and its shortest and straightest line is 3,000 miles. According to what is known, it issues from the Mountains of the Moon from various and unknown sources. The aforementioned lakes are located about 4,000 braccia above the surface of the sphere of water—that is, 1 and 1/3 miles—allowing for a fall of one braccio for every mile of the Nile's course.

---

### 1096.

Very many times, the Nile and other rivers of great magnitude have poured out their entire element of water and restored it to the sea.

--- Page 401 ---
### 1097.

Why does the flooding of the Nile occur in the summer, coming from torrid countries?

### 1098.

It is not denied that the Nile is constantly muddy when entering the Egyptian sea, and that this turbidity is caused by soil that this river is continually removing from the places it passes; this soil never returns from the sea which receives it, unless the sea throws it back onto its shores. See the sandy desert beyond Mount Atlas, where it was formerly covered with salt water.

### 1099.

**Customs of Asiatic Nations.**

The Assyrians and the people of Euboea accustom their horses to carry sacks which they can fill with air at their pleasure; these they carry in place of the saddle girth, both above and at the side. They are well covered with plates of boiled leather so that they may not be punctured by flights of arrows. Thus, they do not have to worry about a secure retreat when victory is uncertain; a horse equipped in this way enables four or five men to cross over in case of need.

### 1100.

**SMALL BOATS.**

The small boats used by the Assyrians were made of thin willow laths woven over rods also made of willow, and formed into the shape of a small boat. They were daubed with fine powder soaked in oil or turpentine and reduced to a kind of mud which resisted the water. Because pine would split, these remained fresh; and they covered this sort of boat with ox skins to safely cross the river Sicuris in Spain, as Lucan testifies.

--- Page 402 ---
<page-num>266</page-num>
<header>TOPOGRAPHICAL NOTES. [1101—1103.</header>

The Spaniards, the Scythians, and the Arabs, when they want to build a bridge quickly, fasten hurdles made of willow onto bags or skins of ox-hide, and in this way they cross safely.

---
