**1101.** *In eighty-nine.* It is hardly likely that Leonardo means 89 A.D. here. Dr. H. MÜLLER-STRÜBING writes to me as follows on this subject: “Regarding Rhodes, Ross says (*Travels in the Greek Islands, III 70 ff. 1840*), that ancient history provides examples of severe earthquakes at Rhodes, including one in the second year of the 138th Olympiad (270 B.C.); a remarkably violent one under Antoninus Pius (A.D. 138—161) and again under Constantine and later. But Leonardo specifically speaks of an earthquake ‘*in the sea of Atalia near Rhodes*,’ which is unusual. The town of Attalia, founded by Attalus, which is undoubtedly what he means, was in Pamphylia and more than 150 English miles east of Rhodes in a straight line. Leake and most other geographers identify it with the present town of Adalia. Attalia is rarely mentioned by ancient writers—only by Strabo and Pliny—and no earthquake is mentioned. I think, therefore, you are justified in assuming that Leonardo means 1489.” In the detailed catalog of earthquakes in the East by Selale Dshelal eddin Sayouthy (an unpublished Arabic manuscript in the possession of Prof. SCHEFER, Member of the Institute, Paris), mention is made of a terrible earthquake in the year 867 of the Mohammedan Era, corresponding to the year 1489, and it is stated there that a hundred people were killed by it in the fortress of Kerak. There are three places with this name: Kerak on the Sea of Tiberias, Kerak near Tahle on Lebanon, which I visited in the summer of 1876—but neither of these is the place referred to. Possibly it may be the strongly fortified town of Kerak (Kir Moab), to the west of the Dead Sea. There is no mention of this in ALEXIS PERCY, *Memoir on the earthquakes felt in the Turko-Hellenic peninsula and in Syria (Crowned memoirs and memoirs of foreign scholars, Royal Academy of Belgium, Volume XXIII).*

**1103.** See Plate LXXXIII. Compare also page 33 of this volume. The standing male figure at the side is evidently suggested by Michelangelo’s David. In the same place, a slight sketch of horses seems to have been drawn first; there is no reason for...

And around this portico towards the North there should be a lake with a little island in the middle, in which there should be a thick and shady wood; the waters at the top of the pilasters should pour into vases at their base, from which they should flow in little channels.

Starting from the shore of Cilicia towards the South, you discover the beauty of the island of Cyprus.

<margin>W. XVII<i>b</i>]</margin>

### 1104.

From the southern shores of Cilicia, one can see to the south the beautiful island of Cyprus, which was the realm of the goddess Venus; and many, enticed by her beauty, have had their ships and rigging broken among the rocks surrounded by swirling waves. Here the beauty of the pleasant hills invites wandering sailors to refresh themselves among its flowery greenery, where the winds, gathering together, fill the island and the surrounding sea with sweet odors. Oh, how many ships have already been sunk there! Oh, how many vessels broken on the rocks! There one could see innumerable ships: some wrecked and half-covered by the sand, some showing their stern, some their prow, some their keel, and some their side. It will appear like a vision of a judgment day, as if it wanted to resurrect dead ships, so great is the number of them covering the entire northern shore; there the north winds, echoing, make various and frightening sounds.

<margin>The Caspian Sea (1105. 1106).</margin>

### 1105.

Write to Bartolomeo the Turk about the ebb and flow of the Black Sea, and let him find out if such an ebb and flow exists in the Hyrcanean Sea or Caspian Sea.

--- Page 404 ---
# 1106.

**WHY WATER IS FOUND AT THE TOP OF MOUNTAINS.**

From the Straits of Gibraltar to the Don is 3,500 miles, which equals a height of one and one-sixth miles, allowing a fall of one braccio per mile for any water that moves at a moderate pace. The Caspian Sea is much higher; and none of the mountains of Europe rise a mile above the surface of our seas. Therefore, it could be said that the water found on the summits of our mountains comes from the elevation of those seas and of the rivers that flow into them, which are even higher.

---

# 1107.

**The Sea of Azov.**

From this it follows that the Sea of Azov, which borders the Don, is the highest part of the Mediterranean Sea, being 3,500 miles distant from the Straits of Gibraltar, as shown by the navigation chart. It has a descent of 3,500 braccia, which is one and one-sixth miles; therefore, this sea is higher than any mountain in the West.

---

# 1108.

**The Dardanelles.**

In the Bosphorus, the Black Sea always flows into the Aegean Sea, and the Aegean never flows into it. This happens because the Caspian Sea—which lies 400 miles to the east along with the rivers that flow into it—always discharges through subterranean caverns into the Black Sea. The Don and the Danube do the same, so that the waters of the Black Sea are always higher than those of the Aegean; and for this reason, the higher waters always descend into the lower ones, and the lower never into the higher.

--- Page 405 ---
### 1109.

The bridge from Pera to Constantinople: 40 braccia wide, 70 braccia high above the water, and 600 braccia long; specifically, 400 braccia over the sea and 200 resting on land, thus providing its own abutments.

---

### 1110.

If the river is diverted at a breach further upstream, it will never return to its original bed, just as the Euphrates does; and the same may happen at Bologna to anyone who is frustrated by the rising of his rivers.

---

### 1111.

Mount Caucasus, the Comedorum, and the Paropamisadae are joined together between Bactria and India, and give rise to the river Oxus. This river originates in these mountains and flows 500 miles toward the North and an equal distance toward the West, discharging its waters into the Caspian Sea. It is joined by the Dargados, Arthamis, Xariaspes, Dargamaim, Ocus, and Margus, all of which are very large rivers. From the opposite side toward the South rises the great river Indus, which directs its waters for 600 miles toward the South. Along this course, it is joined by the rivers Xaradrus, Hyphasis, Vadris, Vandabal, and Bislaspus from the East, and the Suastus and Coe from the West. Having incorporated these rivers into its waters, it turns and flows 800 miles toward the West; then, hitting the Arbiti Mountains, it forms an elbow and turns Southward, where within 500 miles it reaches the Indian Sea.

---

**1109.** See Plate CX No. 1. In 1453, by order of Sultan Mohamed II, the Golden Horn was crossed by a pontoon bridge laid on barrels (see John Ducas’ History of the Byzantine Empire XXXVIII p. 279). The biographers of Michelangelo, Vasari as well as Condivi, relate that at the time when Michelangelo suddenly left Rome in 1506, he intended to go to Constantinople to serve the Sultan. The Sultan sought to hire him through certain Franciscan monks to construct a bridge connecting Constantinople with Pera. See **VASARI**, *Lives* (Sansoni edition VII, 168): *Michelangelo, seeing the Pope's fury and doubting him, reportedly desired to go to Constantinople to serve the Turk through certain friars of Saint Francis, who wanted him to build a bridge passing from Constantinople to Pera.* And **CONDIVI**, *Life of Michelangelo Buonarroti* chapter 30: *Michelangelo, seeing himself driven to this and fearing the Pope's anger, thought of going to the Levant; especially since he had been recruited by the Turk with great promises through certain friars of Saint Francis, who wanted to use him to build a bridge from Constantinople to Pera and for other matters.* Leonardo’s plan for this bridge was made in 1502. We may therefore conclude that at about that time, Sultan Bajazet II had either announced a competition for this project or had first called upon Leonardo through his agents to carry out the scheme.

--- Page 406 ---
where, after a course of about 100 miles, it reaches the Indian Sea, into which it pours itself through seven branches. On the side of the same mountains rises the great Ganges; this river flows Southward for 500 miles and to the Southeast for a thousand, and the Sarabas, Diarnuna, Soas, Scilo, and Condranunda are its tributaries. It flows into the Indian Sea through many mouths.

### 1112.

On the natives of hot countries.

Men born in hot countries love the night because it refreshes them, and they hate the light because it heats them; therefore, they are the color of night—that is, black. In cold countries, everything is the opposite.

--- Page 409 ---
### ON THE MOVEMENT OF OBJECTS;—HOW TO DETERMINE THE SPEED OF A SHIP PER HOUR.

The ancients used various devices to determine the distance a ship travels each hour. Among these, Vitruvius provides one in his work on Architecture, which is just as unreliable as the others. This method involves a mill wheel that touches the waves of the sea at its outer edges; through its complete revolutions, it describes a straight line representing the circumference of the wheel laid out flat. However, this invention is only valid on the smooth and still surfaces of lakes. If the water moves along with the ship at the same speed, the wheel remains stationary; and if the water moves faster or slower than the ship, the wheel still does not move at the same speed as the ship. Consequently, this invention is of little value. There is another method based on the experience of a known distance from one island to another; this is done with a board or lever struck by the wind, which hits it with greater or lesser speed. This method is found in Battista Alberti.

--- Page 410 ---
Battista Alberti’s method is based on the experience of a known distance from one island to another. However, such an invention does not succeed except on a ship similar to the one where the experiment was conducted; it must have the same cargo, the same sails, the same sail positions, and the same size of waves. But my method serves for every ship, whether powered by oars or sails, and whether it be small or large, long and high, or low, it always works.

---

Methods of staying and moving in water

### 1114.

How an army ought to cross rivers by swimming with air-bags; of the way fish swim; of the way they jump out of the water, as can be seen with dolphins, which seems a marvelous thing—to perform a leap upon something that does not provide resistance, but rather gives way; of the swimming of animals with long bodies, such as eels and similar creatures; of the method of swimming against currents and great waterfalls; of the way round-shaped fish swim; how animals that do not have long hindquarters do not know how to swim; how all other animals with toes on their feet naturally know how to swim, except for man; in what way man must learn to swim; of the method of resting on top of the water; how man must defend himself against whirlpools or eddies in the water that pull him to the bottom; how a man pulled to the bottom must seek the reflected motion that will throw him out of the depths; how one must move with the arms; how one must swim on the back; how and when one can stay under water, which is only when one can hold one's breath; how many may stay somewhat under water with an instrument; how and why I do not write down my method of staying under water, or how long I can stay without eating; and I do not publish or divulge this because of the evil nature of men, who would practice assassinations at the bottom of the seas.

--- Page 411 ---
by breaking the ships at the bottom and sinking them together with the men inside. And although I will teach other methods, they are not dangerous, because the mouth of the tube through which you breathe appears above the water, supported by bags or cork.

---

### 1115.

On naval warfare.

If ships and galleys are in battle, and the ships are winning because of their high crow's nests, you must pull the yardarm up almost to the top of the mast. At the end of this yardarm—specifically the end that projects over the enemy—have a small, reinforced cage attached, covered underneath and around with a thick mattress filled with cotton so that it is not damaged by small cannons. Then, using the capstan, pull down the opposite end of the yardarm, and the cage on the other side will rise so high that it will far exceed the height of the ship's crow's nest, and you will easily be able to drive out the men who are inside it. However, it is necessary for the men in the galley to move to the opposite side to act as a counterweight to the weight of the men placed inside the cage on the yardarm.

--- Page 413 ---
...and this, by the force of oars, will be able—once the first blow has been struck—to be pulled back, and driven forward again with fury to deliver a second blow, and then a third, and as many as are needed to destroy the other ship.

---

**1117.**
**A METHOD OF ESCAPING DURING A STORM AND SHIPWRECK AT SEA.**
You must have a coat made of leather, which should have a double layer across the breast for the width of a finger; it should likewise be double from the waist down to the knee, and the leather must be airtight. When it becomes necessary to jump into the sea, inflate the skirts of your coat through the double layers at the breast, and jump into the water, allowing yourself to be carried by the waves. When you see no shore nearby and have no knowledge of the sea you are in, always keep the air tube that leads into the coat in your mouth; and if once or twice you need to take a breath of outside air, but the foam prevents you, draw a breath of the air from within the coat.

---

**1118.**
If the weight of the sea rests upon its floor, a man lying on that floor with a thousand braccia of water on top of him would have enough weight upon him to be crushed.

---

**1119.**
**On going under water; A method of walking on top of the water.**

### 1120.

Just as a man can glide across a frozen river without moving his feet, so it would be possible to build a car that moves by itself.

---

### 1121.

A definition of why a person who slides on ice does not fall.

---

### 1122.

**On Flying machines (1122—1126).**

When in a flying machine, a man must remain free from the waist upward so that he can balance himself, as one does in a boat, so that his center of gravity and that of the machine may balance each other and be shifted as necessity requires for the changing of its center of resistance.

---

### 1123.

Remember that your flying machine must imitate no other than the bat, because the membrane is what provides the framework or the connection to the structure—that is, the main supports of the wings.

If you were to imitate the wings of feathered birds, they have a more powerful structure because they are pervious; that is, their feathers are separate and the air passes through them. But the bat is aided by the membrane that binds the whole together and is not pervious.

--- Page 416 ---
ON MINING. [1127. 1128.]

### 1127.

Of mining.

If you want to know where a mine is being dug, place a drum in all those locations where you suspect the mine is being made, and on top of said drum put a pair of dice. When you are near the place where the digging is occurring, the dice will jump slightly on the drum because of the blow given underground during the mining.

---

There are some who, having the convenience of a river or a marsh on their lands, have made a large reservoir of water near the place where they suspect the mine is being made; they have dug a counter-mine against the enemy and, having found them, have opened the sluice and drowned a great number of people in the mine.

---

### 1128.

Of Greek fire.

GREEK FIRE.

Take willow charcoal, saltpeter, alcohol, sulfur, pitch with frankincense, camphor, and Ethiopian wool, and boil everything together. This fire is of such—

--- Page 417 ---
281
1129.] ON GREEK FIRE. 281

...intensity that it continues to burn the wood even under water. And if you add to this composition liquid varnish, bituminous oil, turpentine, and strong vinegar, mix everything together and dry it in the sun, or in an oven after the bread has been removed. Then wrap it around hemp tow or other fibers, molding it into a round shape and driving very sharp nails into it from every side; leave only one hole in the ball to serve as a fuse, and then cover it with rosin and sulfur.

Furthermore, this fire, when attached to the end of a long pole—which should have a point of iron one cubit long so that it is not burned by the fire—is useful for avoiding and warding off enemy ships, so as not to be overwhelmed by their charge.

Additionally, if you throw glass vessels full of pitch onto the enemy ships while the men on them are focused on the battle, and then throw similar ignited balls after them, you have the power to burn every ship.

---

### 1129.

A drum with cogs, operated by wheels with springs.

1128. Venturi has provided another short text regarding Greek fire in a French translation (Essai § XIV). He adds that the original text is to be found in MS. B. 30 (?). Libri speaks of it in a note as follows (*History of Mathematical Sciences in Italy Vol. II p. 129*): "The composition of Greek fire is one of the things that has been most sought after and which remains the most uncertain. It is said that it was invented in the seventh century of the Christian era by the architect Callinicus (*Works of Constantine Porphyrogenitus, Leiden 1617, 8vo; p. 172, On Administering the Empire, ch. 48*), and it is frequently mentioned by Byzantine historians. Sometimes it was launched with machines, like a projectile; at other times it was blown through long tubes, like a gas or an ignited liquid (*Anna Comnena, Alexiad, p. 335, book XI. — Tactics of Aelian and Leo the Emperor, Leiden 1613, 4to. part 2, p. 322, Leo's Tactics ch. 19. — Joinville, History of Saint Louis, Petitot collection vol. II, p. 235*). Contemporary writers say that water could not extinguish this fire, but that it could be managed with vinegar and sand.

According to some historians, Greek fire was composed of sulfur and resin. Marcus Graecus (*Book of Fires, Paris, 1804, 4to*) gives several ways of making it which are not very intelligible, but among which one finds the composition of gunpowder. Leonardo da Vinci (*Manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci, vol. B. f. 30*) says that it was made with willow charcoal, saltpeter, brandy, resin, sulfur, pitch, and camphor. But it is probable that we do not know what its composition was, primarily because of the secrecy maintained by the Greeks. Indeed, the Emperor Constantine Porphyrogenitus recommends to his son never to give it to the Barbarians, and to answer them, if they asked for it, that it had been brought from heaven by an angel and that the secret had been entrusted to the Christians (*Works of Constantine Porphyrogenitus, p. 26—27, On Administering the Empire, ch. 12*)."

## XIX.

### *Philosophical Maxims. Morals. Polemics and Speculations.*

Vasari indulges in severe criticisms of Leonardo's religious views. He speaks, among other things, of his "caprices in philosophizing about natural things" and says on this point: "Because of this, he formed such a heretical concept in his mind that he did not adhere to any religion, perhaps valuing being a philosopher much more than being a Christian" (see the first edition of *Le Vite*). But this accusation by a writer in the days of the Inquisition is not very serious—and even less so since, throughout the manuscripts, we find nothing to support it.

Under the heading of "Philosophical Maxims," I have collected all the passages that can give us a clear understanding of Leonardo's ideas of the world at large. It is hardly necessary to observe that there is absolutely nothing in them to lead to the conclusion that he was an atheist. His views of nature and its laws are no doubt very unlike those of his contemporaries, and have a much closer affinity to those that find general acceptance today. On the other hand, it is obvious from Leonardo's will (see No. 1566) that, in the year before his death, he had professed to adhere to the fundamental doctrines of the Roman Catholic faith, and this evidently from his own personal desire and impulse.

The incredible and demonstrably fictitious legend of Leonardo's death in the arms of Francis the First is given, with others, by Vasari and further embellished by this hateful comment: "He showed, nevertheless, how much he had offended God and the men of the world by not having worked in art as was fitting." This last accusation, it may be remarked, is above all evidence of the superficial nature of the information Vasari was in a position to give about Leonardo. It seems to imply that Leonardo was disdainful of diligent labor. Regarding the second point, referring to Leonardo's morality and dealings with his fellow men, Vasari himself cancels it out by asserting the exact opposite in several passages. A further refutation may be found in the following sentence from

--- Page 420 ---
*The letter in which Melzi, the young Milanese nobleman, announces the Master’s death to Leonardo’s brothers:*

> I believe you have been informed of the death of Master Leonardo, your brother and to me the best of fathers. It would be impossible for me to express the grief I have felt at his death; and as long as these limbs of mine hold together, I shall possess a perpetual unhappiness—and rightly so, because he showed me a heartfelt and most ardent love every day. Everyone grieves the loss of such a man, the likes of whom nature no longer has the power to produce, etc.

It is true that, in April 1476, we find the names of Leonardo and Verrocchio entered in the "Libro degli Uffiziali di notte e de’ Monasteri" as breaking the laws; but immediately afterward we find the note "Acquitted on the condition that they be re-summoned" ("Tamburini" was the name given to the complaint boxes of the night police). The acquittal, therefore, did not exclude the possibility of a repetition of the charge. It was in fact repeated two months later, and on this occasion the Master and his pupil were again fully acquitted. Verrocchio was at this time forty and Leonardo twenty-four. The documents referring to this affair are in the State Archives of Florence; they have been withheld from publication, but it seemed to me desirable to give the reader this brief account of the leading facts of the story, as the vague hints of it which have recently been made public may have given the incident an aspect which it did not have in reality, and which it does not deserve.

The passages here classified under the heading "Morals" reveal Leonardo to us as a man whose life and conduct were unfailingly governed by lofty principles and aims. He could scarcely have recorded his stern disapproval and boundless contempt for men who do nothing useful and strive only for riches, if his own life and ambitions had been such as they have so often been misrepresented.

At a period like 그hat, when superstition still exercised unlimited dominion over the minds not merely of the illiterate crowd, but of the cultivated and learned classes, it was very natural that Leonardo’s views on Alchemy, Ghosts, Magicians, and the like should be met with stern disapproval whenever and wherever he may have expressed them; this accounts for the argumentative tone of all his utterances on such subjects which I have collected in Subdivision III of this section. To these I have added some passages which throw light on Leonardo’s personal views on the Universe. They are, without exception, characterized by a broad spirit of naturalism, the principles of which are more strictly applied in his essays on Astronomy, and even more so on Physical Geography.

To avoid repetition, only such notes on Philosophy, Morals, and Polemics have been included in this section as appear as independent texts in the original manuscripts. Several moral reflections have already been given in Volume I, in the section "Allegorical representations, Mottoes and Emblems." Others will be found in the following section. Numbers 9 to 12 in Volume I are also passages of an argumentative character. It did not seem necessary to repeat these and similar passages here, since their direct connection with the context is far closer in the places where they have already appeared than it would be here.

--- Page 422 ---
**1136.**
In many cases, one and the same thing is pulled by two strong forces: namely, Necessity and Power. Water falls as rain; the earth absorbs it out of a necessity for moisture; and the sun evaporates it, not out of necessity, but by its power.

**1137.**
Weight, force, and accidental motion, together with impact, are the four accidental powers through which all the visible works of mortals have their existence and their death.

**1138.**
Our body is subject to heaven, and heaven is subject to the spirit.

**1139.**
Motion is the cause of all life.

**1140.**
And you, man, who observe the marvelous works of nature in this labor of mine, if you judge it a wicked thing to destroy them, then consider it a most wicked thing to take the life of a man. If his physical composition seems to you a work of wonderful skill, consider that this is nothing compared to the soul that dwells within such architecture; for truly, whatever that soul may be, it is a divine thing. Therefore, let it dwell in its own work at its own pleasure, and do not let your rage or malice destroy such a life; for truly, he who does not value life does not deserve it.

--- Page 423 ---
287
1141—1146. PHILOSOPHICAL MAXIMS.

1141.
The soul can never be corrupted by the corruption of the body, but exists in the body like the air that causes the sound of an organ; where, if a pipe is damaged, the wind would cease to produce a good effect.

1142.
Every part has a tendency to reunite with its whole in order to escape from its imperfection.
The soul desires to remain with its body because, without the organic instruments of that body, it can neither act nor feel anything.

1143.
If anyone wishes to see how the soul dwells in its body, let him observe how that body uses its daily habitation; that is to say, if the house is disordered and confused, the body will be kept in disorder and confusion by its soul.

1144.
Why does the eye see a thing more clearly in dreams than the imagination does while awake?

1145.
The senses are of the earth; Reason stands apart from them when in contemplation.

1146.
Every action must be performed through motion.
To know and to will are two human operations.
Discerning, judging, and deliberating are human acts.

--- Page 425 ---
Leaving experience aside, turn away from complaints about our ignorance, which causes us to be carried away by vain and foolish desires to promise ourselves things in her name that are not in her power, claiming that she is deceptive. Men are unjust to complain about innocent experience, frequently accusing her of error and of false evidence.

### 1154.

Instrumental or mechanical science is the noblest and most useful of all, seeing that by means of it all animated bodies that possess movement perform all their actions. These movements originate from the center of gravity, which is placed in the middle, dividing unequal weights; and it involves the scarcity and abundance of muscles, as well as the lever and counter-lever.

### 1155.

**ON MECHANICS.**

Mechanics is the paradise of the mathematical sciences, because through it one reaches the fruits of mathematics.

### 1156.

Every instrument requires being made through experience.

### 1157.

Whoever criticizes the supreme certainty of mathematics feeds on confusion, and will never be able to silence the contradictions of the sophistical sciences, which lead only to eternal shouting.

### 1158.

There is no certainty in the sciences where one of the mathematical sciences cannot be applied, or which are not connected to those mathematics.

--- Page 426 ---
### 1159.

Anyone who, in a discussion, relies upon authority does not use his intellect, but rather his memory. Good learning is born of a good natural disposition; and since the cause is more to be praised than the effect, you should praise a good natural disposition without learning more than a well-learned man without a natural disposition.

---

### 1160.

Science is the captain, and practice is the soldiers.

---

### 1161.

**ON THE ERROR OF THOSE WHO USE PRACTICE WITHOUT SCIENCE.**

Those who fall in love with practice without science are like a pilot who boards a ship without a rudder or a compass, and who can never be certain where he is going.

## II.
### MORALS.

### 1162.

Now you see that the hope and the desire of returning home and to one's former state is like the moth to the light. The man who, with constant longing, always awaits with joy each new spring, each new summer, and each new month and new year—deeming that the things he longs for are too late in coming—does not perceive that he is longing for his own destruction. But this desire is the very quintessence, the spirit of the elements, which, finding itself imprisoned by the soul within the human body, is always longing to return to its sender. And I want you to know that this same longing is that quintessence, the companion of nature, and that man is a model of the world.

### 1163.

O Time, consumer of all things! O envious antiquity, you destroy all things and consume all things with the hard teeth of old age, little by little, in a slow death! Helen, when she looked in her mirror and saw the withered wrinkles made in her face by old age, wept and wondered to herself why she had been abducted twice.

--- Page 428 ---
O Time, consumer of all things, and O envious age, by which all things are devoured!

---

## 1164.

**Death.**
Every loss leaves behind a sense of grief in our memory, except for the ultimate loss, which is death; for death destroys memory itself along with life.

---

## 1165.

**How to spend life.**
O sleepers, what a thing is slumber! Sleep resembles death. Why then do you not work in such a way that after death you may retain the likeness of one who is perfectly alive, rather than making yourself like the wretched dead by sleeping while you are still living?

---

## 1166.

One pushes down the other.
By these square blocks are meant the life and the studies of men.

---

## 1167.

The knowledge of past times and of the geography of the earth is both an ornament and nourishment to the human mind.

---

## 1168.

Lying is so vile that even if it were to speak well of godly things, it would take away from God’s grace. Truth is so excellent that if it praises even the smallest things, they become noble. Beyond a doubt, the relationship of truth to falsehood is the same as that of light to darkness. Truth is in itself so excellent that, even when it focuses on humble and lowly matters, it infinitely exceeds the uncertainties and lies disguised in high and lofty discourses.

--- Page 429 ---
lofty discourses; because in our minds, even if lying should be their fifth element, this does not prevent the truth of things from being the chief nourishment of superior intellects, though not of wandering wits.
But you who live in dreams are better pleased by the sophistical reasons and frauds of wits in great and uncertain things, than by those reasons which are certain and natural and not so far above us.

---

### 1169.

Avoid studies of which the resulting work dies along with the worker.

---

### 1170.

Men are wrong when they lament the flight of time, accusing it of being too swift, and not perceiving that it passes at a sufficient rate; but good memory, with which nature has endowed us, causes things long past to seem present.

---

### 1171.

Learning acquired in youth resists the damage of old age; and if you understand that old age has wisdom for its food, you will conduct yourself in such a way in youth that your old age will not lack nourishment.

---

### 1172.

The acquisition of any knowledge is always useful to the intellect, because it can then drive out useless things and reserve the good;
for nothing can be loved or hated unless it is first known.

---

### 1173.

Just as a day well spent brings happy sleep, so a life well used brings a happy death.

--- Page 430 ---
### 1174.

The water you touch in a river is the last of what has passed, and the first of what is coming. So it is with the present time.
A life well spent is long.

### 1175.

Just as eating without an appetite turns into troublesome nourishment, so study without desire spoils the memory, as it retains nothing that it takes in.

### 1176.

Just as eating against one's will is harmful to health, so study without desire spoils the memory, and it retains nothing it takes in.

### 1177.

On Mount Etna the words freeze in your mouth, and you could make gelatin of them.
Just as iron rusts without exercise, and water putrefies or freezes in the cold, so the intellect spoils without exercise.
You do poorly if you praise, and worse if you criticize a thing, when you do not understand it well.
When Fortune comes, seize her firmly by the forelock, because she is bald behind.

### 1178.

It does not seem to me that coarse men of bad habits and little reasoning power deserve such a beautiful instrument, nor such a variety of mechanisms, as do men of speculation and great reasoning; but rather merely a sack where...

--- Page 431 ---
...a sack in which their food may be stowed and from which it may issue, since they should be judged as nothing more than a passage for food; for it seems to me they have nothing of the human species about them except the voice and the figure, and in all the rest they are much less than beasts.

---

### 1179.

There are some who should be called nothing other than a passage for food, producers of excrement, and fillers of privies, because through them nothing else in the world is produced, nor is any virtue put into practice, since nothing remains of them but full privies.

---

### 1180.

The greatest deception men suffer is from their own opinions.

---

### 1181.

Folly is the shield of shame, just as persistence is the shield of glorified poverty.

---

### 1182.

Blind ignorance misleads us thus and delights us with the effects of lustful pleasures.
{ Because it does not know the true light.
{ Because it does not know what the true light is.
Vain splendor takes away our very being... see how we go into the fire for that splendor, just as blind ignorance misleads us.
O wretched mortals, open your eyes!

---

### 1183.

That which can be lost is not to be called riches; virtue is our true good and the true reward of its possessor. It cannot be lost; it does not desert us, except when life leaves us.

--- Page 432 ---
...unless life leaves us first; as for property and external riches, hold them with trembling; they often leave their possessor in contempt, mocked for having lost their possession.

### 1184.

Every man wishes to make a fortune to give it to doctors, the destroyers of life; they, therefore, ought to be rich.

Man has a great power of speech, the larger part of which is vain and false; animals have but little, but it is useful and true, and a small certainty is better than a great lie.

### 1185.

He who possesses the most must be the most afraid of loss.

### 1186.

He who wishes to be rich in a day will be hanged in a year.

### 1187.

That man is of supreme folly who always lives in want for fear of wanting; and his life flies away from him while he still hopes to enjoy the goods acquired with extreme labor.

### 1188.

If you governed your body according to virtue, you would not crawl on all fours in this world.

You grow in reputation like bread in the hands of children.

### 1189.

Savage is he who saves himself.

### 1195.

**THE OPERATION OF PATIENCE.** | **A SIMILE FOR PATIENCE.**
--- | ---
Patience acts against insults in no other way than clothing does against the cold; for if you increase your layers of clothing as the cold increases, that cold will be unable to harm you. Similarly, increase your patience in the face of great insults, and those insults will be unable to wound your mind.

### 1196.

To speak well of a wicked man is the same as speaking ill of a good man.

### 1197.

Envy wounds with sharp infamy—that is, by detraction—which is a thing that frightens virtue.

### 1198.

We are deceived by our desires, we are misled by time, and death mocks our cares; an anxious life amounts to nothing.

### 1199.

Fear is born sooner than anything else.

### 1200.

Just as boldness is a danger to life, so fear is its security.
Threats are the only weapons of the threatened.
Wherever good fortune enters, envy lays siege to it and attacks it; and when fortune departs, it leaves behind sorrow and regret.
He who walks well rarely falls.

--- Page 435 ---
It is bad if you praise, and worse if you reprove a thing, I mean, if you do not understand the matter well.
It is wrong to praise, and worse to reprimand in matters that you do not understand.

## 1201.

Words which do not satisfy the ear of the hearer weary him or vex him, and the symptoms of this you will often see in such hearers in their frequent yawns; you therefore, who speak before men whose good will you desire, when you see such an excess of fatigue, shorten your speech, or change your subject; and if you do otherwise, then instead of the favor you desire, you will gain dislike and hostility.
And if you wish to see what a man takes pleasure in, without hearing him speak, change the subject of your conversation in talking to him, and when you see him become intent, without yawning or wrinkling his brow or other various actions, you may be certain that the matter of which you are speaking is the one in which he takes delight, etc.

## 1202.

The lover is moved by the beloved object as the senses are by sensible objects; and they unite and become one and the same thing. The work is the first thing born of this union; if the thing loved is base, the lover becomes base.
When the thing joined is perfectly adapted to that which receives it, the result is delight, pleasure, and satisfaction.
When the lover is united to the beloved, he finds rest there; when the burden is laid down, it finds rest there.

## 1203.

The primary fame becomes eternal along with the inhabitants of the city built or enlarged by him.

--- Page 436 ---
All communities obey and are led by their leaders, and these leaders ally themselves with the lords and bind them in two ways: either by family ties or by property. By family ties, when their children are kept as hostages, serving as a security and a pledge of their questionable loyalty; by property, when you have each of them build one or two houses inside your city, from which they may draw some income. You shall have ten cities, five thousand houses with thirty thousand inhabitants, and you will disperse this great gathering of people who stand huddled together like goats, filling every place with foul smells and spreading the seeds of pestilence and death.

And the city will gain beauty worthy of its name, and it will be useful to you through its revenues and the eternal fame of its growth.

## 1204.

To preserve nature’s greatest gift, which is freedom, I have found ways to attack and defend when besieged by ambitious tyrants; and first I will speak of the layout of the walls, and also how communities can support their good and just lords.

--- Page 439 ---
When you have ruined a work of some speculative mind, you do not perceive that you are falling into the same error as a man who strips a tree of the ornament of its branches, which are covered with leaves mingled with scented blossoms or fruit... as Justinus did in abridging the histories written by Trogus Pompeius, who had written in an ornate style all the excellent deeds of his ancestors, which were full of the most wonderful and ornamental passages. Thus, he composed a barren work, worthy only of those impatient spirits who imagine they are losing as much time as that which is actually employed usefully—namely, in the study of the works of nature and of human affairs. But let such people remain in the company of beasts; let their associates be dogs and other predatory animals, and let them join them in constantly hunting... and following innocent animals which, driven by hunger in times of great snow, come to your houses asking for alms as if from their protector.

---

### 1211.

O mathematicians, shed light on such an error!
The spirit has no voice, because where there is a voice there is a body, and where there is a body there is an occupation of space, which prevents the eye from seeing things placed behind that space; therefore, such a body fills all the surrounding air with itself—that is, with its own image.

---

### 1212.

There can be no voice where there is no motion and percussion of the air; there can be no percussion of that air where there is no instrument; there can be no instrument that is incorporeal. This being the case, a spirit can have neither voice, nor form, nor strength; and if it should take on a body, it could not penetrate or enter where the doors are locked. And if anyone should say that by means of air gathered and compressed together, the spirit takes on bodies of various forms, and—

--- Page 440 ---
...speaks and moves with force through that instrument—to this I say that where there are no nerves and bones, no force can be exercised in any movement made by such imaginary spirits. Avoid the precepts of those speculators, for their arguments are not confirmed by experience.

---

## 1213.

Of all human opinions, that which deals with the belief in necromancy must be considered the most foolish. It is the sister of alchemy, which gives birth to simple and natural things; but necromancy is all the more worthy of condemnation than alchemy because it brings forth nothing but what is like itself—namely, lies. This does not happen in alchemy, which serves as the administrator of the simple products of nature. This function cannot be performed by nature itself, because it possesses no organic instruments with which it can work as man does by means of his hands, who in such a role has produced glass, and so on. But this necromancy, like a flag or a flying banner moved by the wind, is the guide of the foolish crowd, which constantly bears witness through its clamor to the infinite effects of such an art. Books are filled with it, asserting that enchantments and spirits can act and speak without tongues, and speak without organic instruments—without which speech is impossible—and can carry the heaviest weights, cause storms and rain, and that men can be turned into cats, wolves, and other beasts, although those who affirm such things are the first to have turned into beasts.

And certainly, if such necromancy truly existed, as is believed by shallow minds, there is nothing on earth that would be of such great power for both the harm and service of man. For if it were true that such an art possessed the power to disturb the calm serenity of the air, converting it into the appearance of night, and creating flashes of light or winds with terrifying thunder and lightning rushing through the darkness, and with violent winds destroying...

--- Page 441 ---
...high buildings and uprooting forests, and with these striking down armies, breaking and shattering them; and, besides this, causing harmful storms, depriving the farmers of the reward for their labors—now what kind of warfare can there be that could so greatly harm an enemy as the power to deprive him of his harvests? What naval battle could be compared to that? I speak of the one who commands the winds and creates ruinous and sinking gales for any fleet—certainly, whoever commands such impetuous powers will be lord of the nations, and no human ingenuity will be able to resist his destructive forces. The hidden treasures and gems buried in the body of the earth would all be revealed to him; no lock or impregnable fortress would be able to save anyone without the will of such a necromancer. He would have himself carried through the air from the East to the West and through all the opposite regions of the universe. But why should I expand further? What is there that could not be done by such a craftsman? Almost nothing, except to escape death. Therefore, the harm and the utility contained in such an art are partly concluded, if it were true; and if it is true, why has it not remained among men who desire it so much, having no regard for any deity? And I know that there are infinite numbers of people who, to satisfy one of their appetites, would ruin God along with the whole universe; and if it has not remained among men, being so necessary to them, it never was, nor is it ever meant to be, according to the definition of the spirit, which is invisible in a body; and within the elements there are no incorporeal things, because where there is no body, there is a vacuum, and a vacuum does not exist within the elements, because it would be immediately filled by the element. <meta>catchword: Turn the page.</meta>

---

<meta>W. An. II. 242 a]</meta> <meta>1214.</meta>

### OF SPIRITS.

We have said, up to this point on the back of this page, that the definition of a spirit is a power conjoined to a body, because by itself...

--- Page 442 ---
...it cannot support itself, nor can it take on any kind of local motion. And if you say that it supports itself, this cannot happen within the elements; because if the spirit is an incorporeal quantity, such a quantity is called a vacuum, and a vacuum does not exist in nature. Even if one were to exist, it would be immediately filled by the collapse of the element in which that vacuum was generated. Therefore, by the definition of weight, which says that gravity is an accidental power created by one element being drawn or pushed into another, it follows that no element has weight within that same element, but it has weight in the element above it, which is lighter than itself. As we see, one part of water has no more gravity or levity than another part of water, but if you draw it up into the air, it will then acquire weight; and if you draw air beneath the water, then the water that is above that air acquires weight. This weight cannot support itself, which makes a collapse inevitable; thus it falls through the water into that place which is a vacuum of such water. The same would happen to a spirit existing among the elements, for it would continually generate a vacuum in whichever element it was located. For this reason, it would be forced into a continuous flight toward the sky until it had exited these elements.

### WHETHER THE SPIRIT HAS A BODY AMID THE ELEMENTS.

We have proved how the spirit cannot exist by itself among the elements without a body, nor can it move by its own voluntary motion, except upward. But for now, we will say how, if such a spirit takes on a body of air, it must inevitably infuse itself within that air; because if it remained as a united mass, it would be separate and would fall due to the generation of a vacuum, as was said above. Therefore, it is necessary, in order to remain within the air, that it infuse itself into a quantity of air. And if it mixes with the air, two problems follow: first, it lightens that quantity of air where it is mixed, and consequently, the lightened air flies upward of its own accord, and will not stay...

--- Page 443 ---
...it remains among the air that is heavier than itself; and besides this, the subtle spiritual essence is dispersed, and its nature is modified, by which that nature loses some of its original power. Added to these there is a third difficulty, and this is that such a body formed of air assumed by the spirit is exposed to the penetrating winds, which are incessantly sundering and dispersing the united portions of the air, revolving and whirling them amidst the rest of the atmosphere; therefore the spirit which is infused in this air would be dismembered or rent and broken up along with the rending of the air into which it was incorporated.

### AS TO WHETHER THE SPIRIT, HAVING TAKEN THIS BODY OF AIR, CAN MOVE OF ITSELF OR NOT.

It is impossible that the spirit infused into a certain quantity of air should move this air; and this is proved by the above passage where it is said: the spirit rarefies that portion of the air in which it incorporates itself; therefore this air will rise high above the other air, and there will be a motion of the air caused by its lightness and not by a voluntary movement of the spirit. And if this air is encountered by the wind, according to the third point of this argument, the air will be moved by the wind and not by the spirit incorporated in it.

### AS TO WHETHER THE SPIRIT CAN SPEAK OR NOT.

In order to prove whether the spirit can speak or not, it is necessary in the first place to define what a voice is and how it is generated; and we will say that the voice is the movement of air in friction against a dense body, or a dense body in friction against the air—which is the same thing. And this friction of the dense and the rare condenses the rare and causes resistance; again, the rare, when in swift motion, and the rare in slow motion condense each other when they come in contact and make a sound and a very great uproar. Sound, or rather the murmur made by the rare moving through the rare...

--- Page 444 ---
...and the sound or murmur made by the rare moving through the rare with only moderate swiftness, like a great flame generating noises in the air; and the tremendous uproar made by the rare mingling with the rare, and when that air which is both swift and rare rushes into that which is itself rare and in motion, it is like the flame of fire which issues from a big gun and strikes against the air; and again when a flame issues from a cloud, there is a concussion in the air as the bolt is generated. Therefore we may say that the spirit cannot produce a voice without movement of the air, and air in it there is none, nor can it emit what it does not have; and if it desires to move that air in which it is incorporated, it is necessary that the spirit should multiply itself, and that cannot multiply which has no quantity. And in the fourth place it is said that no rare body can move if it has not a stable spot from which it may take its motion; much more is it so when an element has to move within its own element, which does not move of itself, except by uniform evaporation at the center of the thing evaporated; as occurs in a sponge squeezed in the hand held under water; the water escapes in every direction with equal movement through the openings between the fingers of the hand in which it is squeezed.

As to whether the spirit has an articulate voice, and whether the spirit can be heard, and what hearing and seeing are; the wave of the voice passes through the air as the images of objects pass to the eye.

## 1216.

Every quantity is intellectually conceivable as infinitely divisible. [Amid the vastness of the things among which we live, the existence of nothingness holds the first place; its function extends over all things that have no existence, and...

--- Page 447 ---
Nature, taking pleasure in creating and making constantly new lives and forms—because she knows that they increase her terrestrial matter—is more willing and more swift in her creating than time is in his destruction. Therefore, she has ordained that many animals shall be food for one another. Since this does not satisfy her desire, she frequently sends out certain poisonous and pestilential vapors upon the vast increases and gatherings of animals, and especially upon men, who increase greatly because other animals do not feed upon them. Once the causes are removed, the effects will cease. This earth, therefore, seeks to diminish its own life, while desiring continual reproduction; and for the reason you have assigned and demonstrated—that effects often resemble their causes—animals are the image of the life of the world.

--- Page 448 ---
Now, this earth seeks to lose its life, desiring continual reproduction; and for the reason you have proposed and demonstrated—that effects often resemble their causes—animals are the image of the life of the world. Therefore, the earth, being possessed of the same desire for reproduction, has ordained that its life should be sustained by the death of its members. And because this does not satisfy its desire, it frequently sends out certain poisonous and pestilential vapors upon the vast increases and gatherings of animals, and especially upon men, who increase greatly because other animals do not feed upon them. Once the causes are removed, the effects will cease. This earth, therefore, seeks to diminish its own life, while desiring continual reproduction; and for the reason you have assigned and demonstrated—that effects often resemble their causes—animals are the image of the life of the world.

## XX.

# Humorous Writings.

Just as Michelangelo’s occasional poems reflect his private life as well as the general disposition of his mind, we may find in the writings collected in this section a transcript of Leonardo’s fanciful nature. We would probably not be far wrong in assuming that he himself had recited these fables in the company of his friends or at the court festivals of princes and patrons. "He was so pleasant in conversation," relates Vasari, "that he drew the souls of people to himself." And Paolo Giovio says in his short biography of the artist: "He was of an extremely friendly, polished, and generous nature, with a face of great beauty; and since he was a wonderful inventor and judge of all elegance and delights—especially theatrical ones—and sang skillfully to the lyre, he was wonderfully pleasing to all princes throughout his entire life." There can be no doubt that the fables are the original offspring of Leonardo’s brain and not borrowed from any foreign source; indeed, the schemes and plans for the composition of fables collected in division V seem to afford external proof of this, if the fables themselves did not make it self-evident. Several of them—for instance No. 1279—are so strikingly characteristic of Leonardo’s views of natural science that we cannot do them justice until we are acquainted with his theories on such subjects; and this is equally true of the ‘Prophecies.’

I have prefixed to these quaint writings the ‘Studies on the life and habits of animals,’ which are singular for their peculiar aphoristic style, and I have transcribed them in exactly the order in which they are written in MS. H. This is one of the very rare instances in which one subject is treated in a consecutive series of notes, all in one manuscript, and Leonardo has also departed from his ordinary habits by occasionally not completing the text on the page where it began. These brief notes of a somewhat mysterious bearing have been placed here simply because they may possibly have been intended to serve as hints for fables or allegories. They can scarcely be regarded as preparations for a natural history; rather, they would seem to be extracts. On the one hand, the names

--- Page 450 ---
*The descriptions of some of the animals seem to prove that Leonardo could not have been recording his own observations here; on the other hand, the notes on their habits and lives appear to me to focus precisely on what must have interested him most—so far as it is possible to form any complete assessment of his nature and tastes.*

*In No. 1293, lines 1–10, we have a sketch of a plan for grouping the Prophecies. I have not, however, used it as a guide for their arrangement here because, in the first place, the texts are not so numerous as to make the suggested classification useful to the reader, and also because in reading the long series as they occur in the original, we may follow the author's mind; here and there, it is not difficult to see how one theme suggested another. I have, however, regarded Leonardo's scheme for the classification of the Prophecies as applicable to that of the Fables and Jests, and have adhered to it as far as possible.*

*Among the humorous writings, I might perhaps have included the 'Rebusses,' of which there are several in the collection of Leonardo's drawings at Windsor; it seems unlikely to me that many or all of them could be solved today, and the manuscripts throw no light on them. Nor would I be justified if I intended to include in the literary works the well-known caricatures of human faces attributed to Leonardo—of which, it may be observed incidentally, the majority are in my opinion undoubtedly fake. Only two have necessarily been included due to their presence in the text, which I wished to reproduce: Vol. I page 326, and Plate CXXII. It can scarcely be doubted that some satirical intent is conveyed by the drawing on Plate LXIV (text No. 688).*

*My reason for not presenting Leonardo to the reader as a poet is the fact that the maxims and morals in verse which have been attributed to him are not found in the manuscripts, and Prof. Uzielli has already proved that they cannot be by him. Consequently, it would seem that only a few short verses can be attributed to him with any certainty.*

--- Page 451 ---
# I.

## STUDIES ON THE LIFE AND HABITS OF ANIMALS.

1220.

**THE LOVE OF VIRTUE.**

The goldfinch is a bird of which it is said that, when it is brought before a sick person, if the patient is destined to die, the bird turns its head away and never looks at him. But if the patient is to recover, the bird never takes its eyes off him; instead, it is the cause of curing him of all his illness.

The love of virtue is similar. It never looks upon anything vile or base; rather, it always dwells on honest and virtuous things and finds its home in a noble heart, just like birds in green woods upon flowering branches. And this love shows itself more in adversity than in prosperity, acting like a light that shines brightest where it finds the darkest place.

1221.

**ENVY.**

We read of the kite that when it sees its young ones in the nest becoming too fat, it pecks at their ribs out of envy and keeps them without food.

--- Page 452 ---
### CHEERFULNESS.
Cheerfulness is characteristic of the cock, which rejoices over every little thing and crows with varied and playful movements.

---

### SADNESS.
Sadness resembles the raven, which, when it sees its young born white, departs in great grief and abandons them with mournful lamentations; it does not feed them until it sees at least a few black feathers on them.

---

### PEACE.
We read of the beaver that when it is pursued, knowing it is hunted for the potency of its medicinal testicles and being unable to escape, it stops. To be at peace with the hunters, it bites off its testicles with its sharp teeth and leaves them for its enemies.

---

### RAGE.
It is said of the bear that when it goes to the beehives to take their honey, and the bees begin to sting him, he leaves the honey and rushes to take revenge. Wanting to avenge himself on all those that sting him, he succeeds in avenging himself on none; thus, his rage turns into madness. Flinging himself on the ground, he vainly irritates his foes with his paws and feet while trying to defend himself.

---

### GRATITUDE.
The virtue of gratitude is said to be most present in the birds called hoopoes. Recognizing the benefits of life and nourishment they received from their father and mother, when they see them grow old, they build a nest for them, brood over them, and feed them. They pull out their parents' old and worn feathers with their beaks and, with a certain herb, restore their sight so that they return to a healthy state.

--- Page 453 ---
### AVARICE.

The toad feeds on earth and always remains lean because it never eats its fill; such is its fear that it might run out of earth.

### INGRATITUDE.

Pigeons are a symbol of ingratitude; for when they reach an age where they no longer need to be fed, they begin to fight with their father. This struggle does not end until the young bird drives the father out, takes the hen for himself, and makes her his own.

### CRUELTY.

The basilisk is so utterly cruel that when it cannot kill animals with its venomous gaze, it turns toward herbs and plants; by fixing its sight upon them, it withers them up.

### GENEROSITY.

It is said of the eagle that it is never so hungry that it does not leave a portion of its prey for the birds surrounding it. Since these birds are unable to feed themselves, they must necessarily attend the eagle, as this is how they obtain their food.

### DISCIPLINE.

When the wolf prowls stealthily around a livestock pen and happens to misstep so that he makes a noise, he bites his own foot to punish himself for such a mistake.

### FLATTERERS OR SIRENS.

The siren sings so sweetly that she lulls the sailors to sleep; she then climbs onto the ships and kills the sleeping men.

--- Page 456 ---
## 1231.

**CONSTANCY.**
Constancy may be symbolized by the phoenix which, knowing by its nature that it must be renewed, has the constancy to endure the burning flames that consume it, and then it is born again.

**INCONSTANCY.**
The swallow serves as a symbol for inconstancy, for it is always in motion because it cannot endure even the slightest discomfort.

**CONTINENCE.**
The camel is the most lustful animal there is and would follow a female camel for a thousand miles; yet, if it were kept constantly with its mother or sisters, it would never touch them; so well does it know how to restrain itself.

## 1232.

**INCONTINENCE.**
The unicorn, because of its lack of self-control and its inability to master itself for the pleasure it finds in young women, forgets its ferocity and wildness; setting aside all suspicion, it goes to a seated girl and falls asleep in her lap, and in this manner the hunters capture it.

**HUMILITY.**
The highest example of humility is seen in the lamb, which submits itself to every animal; and when lambs are given as food to imprisoned lions, they submit to them as if to their own mother, so that it has often been seen that the lions are unwilling to kill them.

## 1233.

**PRIDE.**
The falcon, because of its arrogance and pride, wishes to lord it over and overpower all the other birds of prey, and it always desires to be alone; it has often been seen that the falcon will attack the eagle, the queen of birds.

--- Page 457 ---
**ABSTINENCE.**
The wild ass, when it goes to the well to drink and finds the water muddy, is never so thirsty that it will not abstain from drinking and wait until the water becomes clear again.

**GLUTTONY.**
The vulture is so addicted to gluttony that it would travel a thousand miles to eat a carcass; for this reason, it follows armies.

## 1234.

**CHASTITY.**
The turtle-dove is never unfaithful to its mate; and if one dies, the other observes perpetual chastity and never again rests on a green branch, nor ever again drinks clear water.

**UNCHASTITY.**
The bat, owing to its unbridled lust, observes no universal rule in mating; instead, males with males and females with females pair together indiscriminately, just as they happen to meet.

**MODERATION.**
The ermine, out of moderation, eats only once a day; it would rather let itself be captured by hunters than flee into a muddy lair, so as not to stain its purity.

## 1235.

**THE EAGLE.**
The eagle, when it is old, flies so high that it scorches its feathers, and Nature allows it to renew its youth by falling into shallow water. And if its young cannot endure the sight of the sun, it will not feed them; it will not accept any bird that does not wish to die. Animals that greatly fear...

--- Page 459 ---
## THE SWAN.

The swan is white without any spots, and it sings sweetly as it dies, a song that ends its life.

## THE STORK.

This bird, by drinking saltwater, purges itself of illness. If it finds its mate unfaithful, it abandons her; and when it grows old, its young ones brood over it and feed it until it dies.

---

### 1238.

## THE GRASSHOPPER.

This creature silences the cuckoo with its song. It dies in oil and revives in vinegar. It sings during the most intense heat.

## THE BAT.

The more light there is, the blinder this creature becomes, and the more it looks at the sun, the more it is blinded. This represents Vice, which cannot remain where Virtue exists.

## THE PARTRIDGE.

This bird transforms from female to male and forgets its former sex. Out of envy, it steals eggs from others and hatches them, but the hatchlings follow their true mother.

## THE SWALLOW.

This bird gives sight to its blind young using the herb celandine.

---

### 1239.

## THE OYSTER.—FOR TREACHERY.

When the moon is full, this creature opens itself completely. When the crab sees this, it throws a stone or a stalk of seaweed inside so that the oyster cannot close again, and thus it becomes food for the crab. So it is for one who opens his mouth to tell his secret: he becomes the prey of an untrustworthy listener.

--- Page 460 ---
### THE BASILISK.—CRUELTY.

All snakes flee from this creature; but the weasel attacks it by means of rue and kills it.

---

### THE ASP.

This carries instantaneous death in its fangs; and, so that it may not hear the charmer, it stops its ears with its tail.

---

### THE DRAGON.

This creature entangles itself in the legs of the elephant, which falls upon it, and so both die; and in its death, it is avenged.

---

### THE VIPER.

She, in pairing, opens her mouth and at last clenches her teeth and kills her husband. Then the young ones, growing within her body, rend her open and kill their mother.

---

### THE SCORPION.

Saliva, spit out when fasting, will kill a scorpion. This may be likened to abstinence from greed, which removes and heals the ills that result from gluttony and opens the path to virtue.

---

### THE CROCODILE.—HYPOCRISY.

This animal catches a man and straightway kills him; after he is dead, it weeps for him with a mournful voice and many tears. Then, having finished its lament, it cruelly devours him. It is thus with the hypocrite, who, for the smallest matter, has his face bathed with tears, but shows the heart of a tiger and rejoices in his heart at the woes of others while wearing a pitiful face.

---

### THE TOAD.

The toad flees from the light of the sun, and if it is held there by force, it puffs itself out so much as to hide its head below and shield itself from the rays. Thus does the foe of clear and radiant virtue, who can only be forced to face it with a puffed-up spirit.

--- Page 462 ---
## 1245.

### THE ELEPHANT.

The huge elephant has by nature what is rarely found in humans: namely, integrity, prudence, justice, and the observance of religion. This is because when the moon is new, these animals go to the rivers, and there, solemnly purifying themselves, they bathe; and having thus saluted the planet, they return to the woods. And when they are ill, lying on their backs, they fling plants toward the sky as if they intended to offer a sacrifice. They bury their tusks when they fall out due to old age. Of their two tusks, they use one to dig up roots for food, and they keep the point of the other for fighting. When they are overcome by hunters and exhaustion defeats them, the elephants pull out these tusks and use them to ransom themselves.

---

## 1246.

They are of gentle minds and recognize dangers; and if an elephant finds a man alone and lost, it kindly puts him back on the path he had lost, provided it finds the man’s footprints before it sees the man himself. It fears betrayal, so it stops and blows, signaling this to the other elephants; they then form a troop and proceed cautiously.

These animals always travel in troops; the oldest goes in front, and the second oldest remains last, and in this way, they enclose the troop. They feel shame and do not engage in mating except at night and in secret, and they do not return to the herd after mating until they have first washed themselves in the river. The females do not fight like other animals. The elephant is so merciful that by nature it is very unwilling to hurt those less powerful than itself; and if it encounters a flock of sheep in its path,

---

## 1247.

...so that it may easily climb out. They greatly dread the squealing of pigs and flee in retreat; and at such times, an elephant does no less damage with its feet to its own kind than to its enemies. They delight in rivers and are always wandering around them, though because of their great weight they cannot swim. They devour stones, and the trunks of trees are their most pleasant food. They hate rats. Flies delight in their scent and settle on their backs; the elephant then wrinkles its skin, making the folds tight, and crushes them.

### 1248.

When they cross rivers, they send their young downstream, while they stand upstream to break the rapid current of the water so that the flow does not carry the young away. The dragon flings itself under the elephant’s body, ties its legs with its tail, and with its wings and arms also grips its ribs; it then cuts the elephant's throat with its teeth. The elephant falls upon it, and the dragon bursts; thus, in its death, it is avenged upon its enemy.

## THE DRAGON.

These dragons travel together in companies and intertwine themselves like roots; with their heads raised, they cross marshes and swim to where they find better pasture. If they did not join together in this way,

### 1249.

they would drown; thus, they survive through unity.

## THE SERPENT.

The serpent is a very large animal. When it sees a bird in the air, it draws its breath so strongly that it pulls the birds into its mouth. Marcus Regulus, a consul of the Roman army, was attacked and nearly defeated by such an animal along with his army. This animal, after being killed by a siege engine, was measured at 123 feet—that is, 64 and a half braccia. Its head rose above all the trees of a forest.

--- Page 467 ---
**THE WEASEL.**
When this beast finds the lair of the basilisk, it kills it with the smell of its urine; and this smell, indeed, often kills the weasel itself.

**THE CERASTES.**
This has four small, movable horns; so, when it wants to feed, it hides its entire body under leaves except for these little horns. As they move, they look like small, playing worms to the birds, which immediately swoop down to peck at them. The Cerastes then suddenly coils around them in a circle and devours them.

**THE AMPHISBOENA.**
This has two heads, one in its proper place and the other at the tail, as if one place were not enough from which to discharge its venom.

**THE IACULUS.**
This stays in trees and launches itself like a dart; it pierces through wild beasts and kills them.

**THE ASP.**
The bite of this animal has no cure, except for the immediate amputation of the bitten parts. This pestilential animal has such an affection for its mate that they always travel together. If, by misfortune, one of them is killed, the other pursues the killer with incredible speed. It is so focused and eager for revenge that it overcomes every obstacle; passing through entire armies, it seeks to harm only its enemy. It covers any distance, and it is impossible to escape it except by crossing water or by very swift flight. It has deep-set eyes and large ears, and it is moved more by hearing than by sight.

**THE ICHNEUMON.**
This animal is the mortal enemy of the asp. It is native to Egypt, and when it sees an asp near its territory, it immediately runs to the mud or slime of the Nile and covers itself entirely with it. Then, once dried...

--- Page 468 ---
itself in the sun, it smears itself again with mud. Continuing this process one layer after the other, it creates three or four coatings like a suit of armor. Then it attacks the asp and fights well against it, so that, waiting for the right moment, it catches the asp by the throat and destroys it.

**THE CROCODILE.**

This is found in the Nile; it has four feet and lives on land and in water. No other terrestrial creature but this is found to have no tongue, and it only bites by moving its upper jaw. It grows to a length of forty feet, has claws, and is armed with a hide that can withstand any blow. By day it stays on land, and at night it stays in the water. After feeding on fish, it falls asleep on the bank of the Nile with its mouth open. A bird called the trochilus, a very small bird, immediately runs to its mouth, hops inside among its teeth, and goes about pecking out the remains of the food. By stimulating the crocodile with this pleasurable sensation, the bird encourages it to open its mouth completely, and so the crocodile falls asleep. When the ichneumon sees this, it suddenly flings itself into the crocodile's mouth, pierces its stomach and bowels, and finally kills it.

**THE DOLPHIN.**

Nature has given such knowledge to animals that, besides being aware of their own advantages, they know the disadvantages of their enemies. Thus, the dolphin understands the power of the cut from the fins located on its back, and it knows how tender the belly of the crocodile is. Therefore, in a fight, the dolphin thrusts itself beneath the crocodile, rips open its belly, and so kills it. The crocodile is terrifying to those who flee, but a total coward toward those who pursue it.

**THE HIPPOPOTAMUS.**

This beast, when it feels itself over-full, goes about seeking thorns or the remains of cut reeds, and there it rubs a...

--- Page 469 ---
vein until it breaks, and having bled as much as he needs, he plasters the wound with mud and returns to the shore. He has a form almost like a horse with long haunches, a split hoof, a twisted tail, and the teeth of a wild boar; his neck has a mane. His skin cannot be pierced, except when he is bathing. He feeds on plants in the fields and enters them backwards so that it may appear as though he has come out.

### THE IBIS.

This bird resembles a crane, and when it feels itself ill, it fills its craw with water and makes an injection of it with its beak.

### THE STAG.

These creatures, when they feel themselves bitten by the spider called a daddy-long-legs, eat crabs and free themselves of the venom.

---

### 1261.

### THE LIZARD.

This creature, when fighting with serpents, eats sow-thistle and is cured.

### THE SWALLOW.

This bird restores sight to its blind young with the juice of the celandine.

### THE WEASEL.

This animal, when hunting rats, first eats rue.

### THE WILD BOAR.

This beast cures its ailments by eating ivy.

### THE SNAKE.

This creature, when it wants to renew itself, casts off its old skin, beginning with the head; it changes in one day and one night.

### THE PANTHER.

This beast, even after its bowels have fallen out, will still fight with the dogs and hunters.
