HUMOROUS WRITINGS. [1262—1264.]

## 1262.

### THE CHAMELEON.

This creature always takes the color of the object on which it rests; therefore, along with the leaves on which they perch, they are often devoured by elephants.

---

### THE RAVEN.

When this bird has killed the chameleon, it purifies itself with laurel.

---

## 1263.

Moderation restrains all vices.

The ermine would rather die than soil itself.

---

### OF FORESIGHT.

The rooster does not crow until he has flapped his wings three times; the parrot, when moving among branches, never places its feet where it has not first placed its beak. Vows are born when hope dies.

Motion follows the center of gravity.

---

## 1264.

### MAGNANIMITY.

The falcon preys only upon large birds, and would rather die than eat meat that has an unpleasant smell.

--- Page 472 ---
## 1267.

A FABLE.

A dog was lying asleep on the skin of a sheep. One of his fleas, sensing the odor of the greasy wool, decided that this must be a place for a better life, and one more secure from the dog’s teeth and claws than where he currently fed on the dog. Without further thought, he abandoned the dog and entered the thick wool. There, with great effort, he began trying to push through to the roots of the hairs; but after much sweating, he found the task to be useless, because the hairs were so thick that they almost touched one another, and there was no space where the flea could bite the skin. Therefore, after long labor and fatigue, he began to wish to return to his dog; however, since the dog had already left, the flea was forced, after long repentance and bitter tears, to die of hunger.

---

## 1268.

A FABLE.

The vain and wandering butterfly, not satisfied with being able to fly comfortably through the air, was overcome by the alluring flame of a candle and decided to fly into it. Its playful movement was the cause of sudden sorrow, because its delicate wings were consumed in the flame. The wretched butterfly fell, completely scorched, to the foot of the candlestick. After much weeping and repentance, it dried the tears from its wet eyes, and raising its face, it said: "O false light, how many like me must you have miserably deceived in times past! And if I truly wished to see the light, should I not have distinguished the sun from the false glare of dirty tallow?"

---

A FABLE.

A monkey, finding a nest of small birds, approached them with great joy. Since they were already old enough to fly, he was only able to catch the smallest one. Filled with happiness, he took it in his hand and went to his home; and having begun to look at the bird...

--- Page 473 ---
this little bird, he began to kiss it; and out of excessive love, he kissed it so much, turned it about, and squeezed it until he killed it. This is said for those who, by failing to discipline their children, let them come to mischief.

---

### 1269.

### A Fable.

A rat was besieged in his little dwelling by a weasel, which with unwearied vigilance awaited his destruction, while he watched his imminent peril through a small hole. Meanwhile, a cat came by and suddenly seized the weasel, immediately devouring it. Then the rat, having offered a sacrifice to Jove of some of his store of nuts, thanked His divinity profoundly and came out of his hole to enjoy his lately lost liberty; but he was instantly deprived of it, together with his life, by the fierce claws and teeth of the cat.

---

### 1270.

### A Fable.

An ant found a grain of millet. The seed, feeling itself captured, cried out to her: "If you will do me the great favor of allowing me to fulfill my desire to be born, I will give you a hundred of myself." And so it was done.

A spider found a bunch of grapes which, for its sweetness, was much visited by bees and various kinds of flies. It seemed to him that he had found a very convenient place for his deception; and having lowered himself down by his delicate thread and entered his new habitation, he placed himself every day in the openings made by the spaces between the grapes. There, he fell like a thief upon the wretched creatures that were not on guard against him. But after a few days had passed, the vintager gathered that bunch of grapes and, putting it together with the others, it was trodden with them. Thus, the grapes were a snare and a deception for the treacherous spider just as they were for the deceived flies.

An ass, having fallen asleep on the ice of a deep lake, his heat dissolved the ice; the ass awoke under the water to his great misfortune and immediately drowned.

--- Page 475 ---
no; I want to hide myself in some secret place, and there pass my life in tranquil repose.” And having remained hidden for several months, one day he returned to the air, and issuing from his sheath, saw that he had turned into the likeness of a rusty saw, while his surface no longer reflected the resplendent sun. With useless repentance he vainly deplored the irreparable damage, saying to himself: “Oh, how much better it was to employ my lost edge of such exquisite keenness at the barber's! Where is that lustrous surface? Certainly, this vexatious and unsightly rust has consumed it!”
The same thing happens to those minds which, instead of exercise, give themselves up to sloth. Like the razor mentioned above, they lose the keenness of their edge, and the rust of ignorance spoils their form.

---

### A FABLE. ###

A stone of considerable size, recently uncovered by the water, lay on a certain elevated spot where a delightful grove ended by a stony road. Here it was surrounded by plants decorated with various flowers of different colors. And as it saw the great quantity of stones gathered together in the roadway below, it began to wish it could let itself fall down there, saying to itself: “What am I doing here with these plants? I want to live in the company of these, my sisters.” And letting itself fall, its tumbling course ended among those longed-for companions. After it had been there for some time, it began to find itself in constant distress under the wheels of carts, the iron-shod feet of horses, and the feet of travelers. This one rolled it over; that one trod upon it. Sometimes a piece of it was broken off; at other times it lay covered with mud or the dung of some animal; and it looked back in vain at the spot it had left, that place of solitary and tranquil peace.
Thus it happens to those who choose to leave a life of solitary contemplation and come to live in cities among people full of infinite evils.

--- Page 477 ---
...so that, having finished its course upon a hill, it found itself to be of no less size than the hill that supported it; and it was the last of the snow to be melted by the sun that summer. This is said for those who, by humbling themselves, are exalted.

---

### 1275.

The cedar, desiring to produce a beautiful and large fruit at its summit, carried this out with all the strength of its sap; but this fruit, once grown, was the cause of the tall and straight treetop bending down.

The peach tree, envying the great quantity of fruit she saw produced by the neighboring walnut tree, determined to do the same. She loaded herself with her own fruit in such a way that the weight pulled her up by the roots and broke her down to the level ground.

---

[sketches of trees]

->cedar. peach-tree. walnut-tree. fig-tree. fig-tree. elm-tree.<-

---

The walnut tree, displaying the richness of its fruit to travelers along a road, was pelted with stones by every man.

The fig tree, while it had no fruit, was ignored by everyone; wishing to be praised by men by producing fruit, it was bent and broken by them.

The fig tree, standing near the elm and seeing that the elm's branches were without fruit, yet had the boldness to block the sun from its own unripe figs, said with reproaches: "O elm, are you not ashamed to stand before me? Just wait until my children reach a mature age, and you will see where you find yourself!" When those children had matured, a troop of soldiers arrived and, to take the figs, completely tore, stripped, and mangled the tree.

--- Page 478 ---
...figs were all torn off her, and her boughs cut away and broken. Then, when she was thus maimed in all her limbs, the elm asked her, saying: "O fig tree! Which was better: to be without offspring, or to be brought by them into such a miserable state?"

### 1276.

The plant complains of the old, dry stick that has been placed by its side and of the dry stakes that surround it. One keeps it upright; the others protect it from bad company.

### 1277.

A FABLE.

A nut, having been carried by a crow to the top of a tall bell tower and released into a crack—thereby escaping the deadly grip of the bird's beak—prayed to the wall. It appealed to the grace God had given the wall by making it so high, great, and rich with such beautiful bells of such honorable tone, and begged the wall to help it. Since the nut had been unable to fall beneath the green branches of its old father and lie in the fertile earth covered by his fallen leaves, it begged the wall not to abandon it. The nut explained that when it was in the beak of the fierce crow, it had made a vow that if it escaped, it would end its life in a small hole.

At these words, the wall, moved to compassion, was happy to shelter the nut in the spot where it had fallen. Within a short time, the nut began to split open and put forth roots into the cracks of the stones, pushing them apart and sending shoots out of its cavern. These soon rose above the building, and as the twisted roots grew thicker, they began to force the walls apart and tear the ancient stones from their old places. Then the wall, too late and in vain, bewailed the cause of its damage; and in a short time, the tree broke open and ruined a great part of its structure.

--- Page 481 ---
She awaited eagerly some friendly bird who might act as a mediator for her wishes. Presently, seeing a magpie near her, she said to him: "O gentle bird! By the memory of the refuge you found this morning among my branches, when the hungry, cruel, and rapacious falcon wanted to devour you; and by that repose you have always found in me when your wings craved rest; and by the pleasure you have enjoyed among my boughs while playing with your companions or making love—I entreat you to find the gourd and obtain some of her seeds from her. Tell her that I will treat those that are born of them exactly as though they were my own flesh and blood. In this way, use all the persuasive words you can think of for this purpose; though, indeed, since you are a master of language, I need not teach you. And if you will do me this service, I shall be happy to have your nest in the fork of my boughs, along with your entire family, without the payment of any rent."

Then the magpie, having made and confirmed certain new stipulations with the willow—principally that she should never allow any snake or polecat upon her—cocked his tail, lowered his head, and flung himself from the bough, throwing his weight upon his wings. Beating the fleeting air, moving inquisitively here and there while using his tail as a rudder to steer, he reached a gourd. Then, with a handsome bow and a few polite words, he obtained the required seeds and carried them to the willow, who received him with a cheerful face. When he had scraped away a small amount of earth near the willow with his foot, he planted the grains in a circle with his beak. In a short time, these began to grow, and as they increased and spread their branches, they began to occupy all the boughs of the willow, while their broad leaves deprived it of the beauty of the sun and sky. Not content with so much evil, the gourds next began, by their awkward weight, to drag the ends of the tender shoots down toward the earth with strange twisting and distortion.

--- Page 482 ---
Then, being much annoyed and shaking itself in vain to make the gourds fall off on its own, and raving fruitlessly for several days in such a delusion—because the strong and firm connection denied it that wish—it saw the wind passing by and commended itself to it. The wind blew hard, and then the old and hollow trunk of the willow split into two parts down to its very roots. Having fallen into two pieces, the willow mourned for itself in vain and realized it had been born to never have any good fortune.

--- Page 484 ---
### 1282.

Wine, the divine juice of the grape, finding itself in a golden and richly wrought cup on the table of Mahomet, was puffed up with pride at such great honor. Suddenly, however, it was struck by a contrary thought, saying to itself: "What am I doing, and why do I rejoice? Do I not perceive that I am near my death? Must I leave the golden dwelling of this cup to enter the foul and stinking caverns of the human body, and there be transformed from a fragrant and sweet liquor into foul and wretched urine? And as if such an evil were not enough, must I also lie for so long in hideous receptacles with other stinking and corrupt matter cast out from the human intestines?" It cried out toward heaven, demanding vengeance for such an insult, and pleading that an end finally be put to such contempt; and that, since that country produced the most beautiful and best grapes in the entire world, they should at least not be turned into wine. Then Jove caused the wine drunk by Mahomet to elevate his spirit toward his brain; and it contaminated his mind in such a way that it made him go mad, and he gave birth to so many errors that, when he returned to his senses, he made a law that no Asian should drink wine. Thus, the vines and their fruits were thereafter left unmolested.

As soon as wine enters the stomach, it begins to simmer and swell; then its spirit begins to abandon the body and turns toward the sky. It reaches the brain, which causes the soul to divide from the body; it then begins to contaminate the man and make him rave like a madman, leading him to commit irreparable errors, such as killing his own friends.

### 1283.

A craftsman often went to visit a nobleman without having any specific request to make. The nobleman asked him what he was doing there. The man replied that he came there to enjoy a pleasure that his lordship could not have;

--- Page 487 ---
...because I love you, and I do not want you to give others a bad impression of yourself by speaking ill of me, your friend, as you have done to me. Therefore, by no longer being intimate, it will appear that we have become enemies; then, when you speak ill of me as is your habit, you will not be blamed as much as if we were still close friends.

### 1288.

A man was arguing and boasting that he knew how to perform many various and impressive tricks. Another person among the bystanders said, "I know a trick that will make anyone I choose pull off their breeches." The first boaster, finding himself without breeches at the time, said, "You won't make me pull mine off, and I'll bet a pair of hose on it." The one who proposed the game accepted the challenge, produced several pairs of breeches, and pulled them across the face of the man who bet the hose, thereby winning the wager.

A man said to an acquaintance, "Your eyes have turned a very strange color." The other replied, "It happens often, but you haven't noticed it." "And when does this happen to you?" the first asked. The other replied, "Every time my eyes see your ugly face; from the shock of such an unpleasant sight, they suddenly turn pale and change to a strange color."

A man said to another, "Your eyes have all changed to a strange color." The other replied, "It is because my eyes are looking at your strange face."

A man said that in his country, the strangest things in the world were born. The other replied, "Since you were born there, you confirm that this is true by the strangeness of your own ugly appearance."

### 1289.

An old man was publicly insulting a young man, boldly showing that he did not fear him. The young man replied that the old man's advanced age served as a better shield for him than either his tongue or his strength.

--- Page 488 ---
## 1290.

A JEST.

A sick man, finding himself at the point of death, heard a knock at the door. When he asked one of his servants who was knocking at the entrance, the servant replied that it was a woman calling herself Madonna Bona. Then the sick man, lifting his arms to heaven, thanked God with a loud voice; he then told the servants to let her in quickly, so that he might see one good woman before he died, since in all his life he had never yet seen one.

## 1291.

A JEST.

A man was asked to get out of bed because the sun had already risen. He replied: "If I had as long a journey and as much business to attend to as he does, I would also be up by now; but since I have such a short way to go, I do not wish to get up yet."

## 1292.

A JEST.

A man, seeing a woman prepared to hold the target for a jousting match, looked at the shield and, considering his spear, exclaimed: "Alas! This is too small a workman for such a large shop."

--- Page 492 ---
**[Of the Avaricious.]**
There will be many who will eagerly and with great care and diligence pursue that thing which would always terrify them if they only knew its harmful nature.

**[Of those men who, the older they grow, the more avaricious they become; whereas, having but little time left, they should become more generous.]**
We see those who are regarded as being the most experienced and judicious, even when they have the least need for things, seek and hoard them with the greatest greed.

**[Of the Ditch.]**
Many will be busy working to remove material from a thing which will grow in proportion to how much is taken away.

**[Of a Weight placed on a Feather Pillow.]**
And it will be seen in many bodies that by raising the head they visibly swell; and by laying the raised head down again, their size is immediately diminished.

**[Of catching Lice.]**
And there will be many hunters of animals who will have fewer the more they catch; and conversely, they will have more the fewer they catch.

**[Of Drawing Water with two Buckets on a single Rope.]**
And many will be kept busy, for the more they pull the object downward, the more it will escape in the opposite direction.

**[Of the Tongues of Pigs and Calves in Sausage-skins.]**
Oh, what a filthy thing, that we should see the tongue of one animal inside the backside of another.

**[Of Sieves made of Animal Skins.]**
We shall see the food of animals pass through their skins in every direction except through their mouths, and penetrate from the opposite side down to the level ground.

### [[Of Metals.]]

That which shall be brought forth out of dark and obscure caves will put the whole human race in great anxiety, peril, and death. To many who seek them, after many sorrows they will give delight, and to those who are not in their company, death with want and misfortune. This will lead to the commission of endless crimes; this will increase and persuade wicked men to assassinations, robberies, and treachery, and because of it, everyone will be suspicious of his partner. This will deprive free cities of their happy condition; this will take away the lives of many; this will make men torment each other with many artifices, deceptions, and treasons. O monstrous creature! How much better would it be for men if everything were to return to Hell! For this, the vast forests will be devastated of their trees; for this, endless animals will lose their lives.

### [[Of Fire.]]

One shall be born from small beginnings which will rapidly become vast. This will respect no created thing; rather, by its power, it will transform almost everything from its own nature into another.

### [[Of Ships which sink.]]

Huge bodies will be seen, devoid of life, carrying in fierce haste a multitude of men to the destruction of their lives.

### [[Of Oxen, which are eaten.]]

The masters of estates will eat their own laborers.

### [[Of beating Beds to remake them.]]

Men will be seen so deeply ungrateful that they will turn upon that which has sheltered them for no cost at all; they will load it with so many blows that a great part of its inside will come out of its place and will be turned over and over within its body.

### [[Of Things which are eaten and which are first killed.]]

Those who nourish them will be killed by them and afflicted by merciless deaths.

Animals will be seen upon the earth who will always be fighting among themselves.

1330.

## WHY DOGS WILLINGLY SMELL EACH OTHER’S HINDQUARTERS.

This animal hates the poor because they eat bad food, and it loves the rich because they have good meals, especially meat. The excrement of animals always retains some of the quality of its origin, as is shown by the feces. . . .

Now dogs have such a highly sensitive sense of smell that they can detect with their noses the quality remaining in such feces; and to prove this is true, if they find them in the streets, they smell them, and if they detect the quality of meat or something else inside, they take them, and if not, they leave them. To return to the question, I say that if they recognize through such smells that a dog is well-fed, they respect him, because they judge him to have a powerful and rich master; and if they do not smell such quality, they judge that dog to be of little account and to have a poor and wretched master, and therefore they bite such dogs just as they would bite their master.

---

1331.

The circular methods of moving earth are very useful, since the men never stop; and it is done in several ways. In one of these, men carry the earth on their shoulders; in another, with chests; and others with a cart. The one who carries it on his shoulder must first have the tub filled on the ground and loses time putting it on his shoulder; the one with the chests loses no time.

---

1332.

If Petrarch loved the laurel so much, it was because it is good with sausage and tuna; I cannot value their nonsense.

<vocab>blank page</vocab>

--- Page 518 ---
...hesitatingly have declared that some unknown Italian engineer must have been, at that time, employed in Armenia in the service of the Egyptian Sultan, and that Leonardo had copied his documents. Under this hypothesis, however, we would have to state that this unknown writer must have been so much of one mind with Leonardo as to use the same style of language and even the same lines of thought. This explanation might—as I say—have been possible, if only we had the finished letters. But why should these rough drafts of letters be regarded as anything else than what they actually and obviously are? If Leonardo had been a man of our own time, we might perhaps have attempted to account for the facts by saying that Leonardo, without having been in the East himself, might have undertaken to write a romance set in Armenia, and at the request of his publisher had made landscape sketches to illustrate the text.

I feel bound to mention this singular hypothesis as it has actually been put forward (see No. 1336 note 5); and it would certainly seem as though there were no other possible way of evading the conclusion to which these letters point, and their impact on the life of the master—absurd as the alternative is. But if, on a question of such importance, we are justified in suggesting theories that have no foundation in probability, I could suggest another which, compared with that of a fiction by Leonardo, would be neither more nor less plausible; it is, moreover, perhaps the only other hypothesis that can be devised to account for these passages, if it were possible to prove that the interpretation suggested by the documents themselves must be rejected from the outset: namely, might Leonardo not have written them with the intention of mystifying those who, after his death, should try to decipher these manuscripts with a view to publishing them? But if, in fact, no objection that will stand the test of criticism can be brought against the simple and direct interpretation of the words as they stand, we are bound to regard Leonardo’s travels in the East as an established fact. There is, I believe, nothing in what we know of his biography to contradict such a fact, especially as the details of his life for a few years are wholly unknown; nor need we be at a loss for evidence which may serve to explain—at least to some extent—the strangeness of his undertaking such a journey. We have no information as to Leonardo’s history between 1482 and 1486; it cannot be proved that he was either in Milan or in Florence. On the other hand, the tone of this letter does not require us to assume an absence longer than a year or two. For, even if his appointment (offitio) as an engineer in Syria had been a permanent one, it might have become untenable—perhaps by the death of the Defterdar, his patron, or by his removal from office—and Leonardo, upon his return home, may have kept silent regarding an episode which probably had ended in failure and disappointment.

From the text of No. 1379, we can hardly doubt that Leonardo intended to make a secret excursion from Rome to Naples, although as far as has been known until now, his biographers never allude to it. In another place (No. 1077) he says that he had worked as an engineer in Friuli. Are we to doubt this statement too, merely because no biographer has so far given us any information on the matter? In the geographical notes, Leonardo frequently speaks of the East, and though such passages...

--- Page 519 ---
provide no direct proof of his having been there, they show beyond a doubt that, next to the Nile, the Euphrates, the Tigris, and the Taurus mountains held a special interest for him. As further proof of the futility of the argument that there is nothing in his drawings to show he had traveled in the East, we find on Plate CXX a study of oriental heads of Armenian type—though, of course, this may have been made in Italy.

If the style of these letters were less sober, and the expressions throughout less strictly to the point, it might be possible to regard them as a romantic fiction instead of a narrative of fact. Indeed, we only have to compare them with such obviously fanciful passages as No. 1354, Nos. 670–673, and the Fables and Prophecies. It is unnecessary to discuss the subject any further here; such explanations as the letter requires are given in the footnotes.

The drafts of letters to Lodovico il Moro are very remarkable. Leonardo and this prince were certainly far less closely connected than has previously been supposed. It is impossible that Leonardo could have remained so long in the service of this prince because the salary was good, as is commonly stated. On the contrary, it would seem that what kept him there, despite his desperate need for the money the prince owed him, was the hope of one day being able to carry out the project of casting the ‘gran cavallo’.

The text is written in mirror script, requiring horizontal reversal for standard reading. The manuscript includes complex diagrams and marginal notes typical of Leonardo's scientific notebooks.

| <br>The recent disaster that occurred in these northern parts of ours, which I am certain will terrify not only you but the entire world, which | <margin>Drafts of Letters and Reports referring to Armenia (1336. 1337).</margin> <br>[3]The recent disaster in our Northern parts which I am certain will terrify not you alone but the whole world, which |

1336. Lines 1—52 are reproduced in facsimile on Plate CXVI.
1. *Diodario*. This word is not found in any Italian dictionary, and for a long time I searched in vain for an explanation of it. My wife’s childhood memories provided the desired clue. The main town of each Turkish Vilayet, or province—such as Bursa, for example, in Asia Minor—is the residence of a **Defterdar**, who presides over the financial affairs of the province. **Defterdar hane** was, in former times, the name given to the Ministry of Finance at Constantinople; the Minister of Finance to the Porte is now known as the *Malliè-Nazri*, and the *Defterdars* are his subordinates. A **Defterdar** at the present day is merely the head of the finance department in each provincial district. Regarding my suggestion that Leonardo’s *Diodario* might be identical to the **Defterdar** of former times, the late M. C. DEFRÉMERIE, Arabic Professor and Member of the Institute of France, wrote to me as follows: "Your conjecture is perfectly well-founded; *diodario* is the equivalent of *devadar* or, more exactly, *dēvâtdâr*, the title of an important high office in Egypt under the Mamluks."

The word, however, is not of Turkish but of Perso-Arabic derivation. Literally, **Defter** (Arabic) means folio; **dar** (Persian) means bookkeeper or holder, which is the English equivalent; and the concept is that of a deputy in command. During the Mamluk supremacy over Syria, which coincided in date with Leonardo’s time, the office of **Defterdar** was the third most important in the State.

*Sultan of Babylon*. The name Babylon was commonly applied to Cairo in the Middle Ages. For instance, BREIDENBACH, in *Itinerarium Hierosolyma* page 218, says: "At last we reached Babylon. But this is not that Babylon which stood on the far shore of the river Chebar, but that which is called the Egyptian Babylon. It is close to Cairo, and the two are but one and not two towns; one half is called Cairo and the other Babylon, which is why they are called together Cairo-Babylon; originally the town is said to have been named Memphis and then Babylon, but now it is called Cairo." Compare No. 1085, 6.

Egypt was governed from 1382 until 1517 by the Burji or Circassian dynasty of the Mamluk Sultans. One of the most famous of these, Sultan Qaitbay, ruled from 1468 to 1496; during his reign, the Mosque of Qaitbay and the tomb of Qaitbay near the Okella Qaitbay were built in Cairo, preserving his name to this day. Under the rule of this great and wise prince, many foreigners, particularly Italians, found employment in Egypt, as can be seen in the ‘Travels of Josaphat Barbaro’, among other travelers. "Next to Leonardo (as I learn from Prof. Jac. Burckhardt of Basel), Qaitbay’s most helpful engineer was a German."

who in about 1487 superintended the construction of the Mole at Alexandria. Felix Fabri knew him and mentions him in his *History of the Swabians*, written in 1488."

3. *The new accident that occurred*, or as Leonardo first wrote and then erased, *a new accident has occurred*. From the context, this must refer to an earthquake, and indeed these were frequent at that period, particularly in Asia Minor, where they caused immense damage. See No. 1101 note.

4. The text here breaks off. The following lines are a fresh beginning of a letter, evidently addressed to the same person, but, as it would seem, written at a later date than the previous text. The numerous corrections and amendments amply prove that it is not a copy from any account of a journey by some unknown person; but, on the contrary, that Leonardo was particularly anxious to choose such words and phrases as might best express his own ideas.

5. *Parts of Armenia*. See No. 945, note. The extent of Armenia in Leonardo's time is only approximately known. In the 15th century, the Persians governed the Eastern, and the Arabs the Southern portions. Arabic authors—as, for instance, Abulfeda—include Cilicia and a part of Cappadocia in Armenia, and Greater Armenia was the tract of that country known later as Turcomania, while Armenia Minor was the territory between Cappadocia and the Euphrates. It was not until 1522, or even 1574, that the whole country came under the dominion of the Ottoman Turks, in the reign of Selim I.

The Mamelook Sultans of Egypt seem to have taken a particular interest in this, the most Northern province of their empire, which was even then in danger of being conquered by the Turks. In the autumn of 1477, Sultan Kaït Bey made a journey of inspection, visiting Antioch and the valleys of the Tigris and Euphrates with a numerous and brilliant escort. This tour is briefly alluded to by MOODSHIREDDIN p. 561; and by WEIL, *History of the Abbasids* V, p. 358. An anonymous member of the suite wrote a diary of the expedition in Arabic, which has been published by R. V. LANZONE (*Travels in Palestine and Syria by Kaid Ba, 18th sultan of the 2nd Mamelook dynasty, made in 1477. Arabic text*. Turin 1878, without notes or commentary). Compare the critique on this edition by J. GILDEMEISTER in *Journal of the German Palestine Society* (Vol. III p. 246—249). Lanzone's edition seems to be no more than an abridged copy of the original. I owe to Professor Schéfer, Member of the Institute, the information that he is in possession of a manuscript in which the text is fuller and more correctly given. The Mamelook dynasty was, as is well known, of Circassian origin, and a large proportion of the Egyptian Army was recruited in Circassia even as late as the 15th century. That was a period of political storms in Syria and Asia Minor, and it is easy to suppose that the Sultan's minister, to whom Leonardo addresses his report as his superior, had a special interest in the welfare of those frontier provinces. To mention only a few historical events of Sultan Kaït Bey's reign, we find that in 1488 he assisted the Circassians to resist the encroachments of Ala-eddoulet, an Asiatic prince who had allied himself with the Osmanli to threaten the province; the consequence was a war in Cilicia by sea and land, which broke out in the following year between the contending powers. Only a few years earlier, the same province had been the scene of the so-called Caramenian war in which the united Venetian, Neapolitan, and Slavonic fleets had been engaged. (See CORIALANO CIPPICO, *On the War of the Venetians in Asia from 1469—1474*. Venice 1796, p. 54) and we learn incidentally that a certain Leonardo Boldo, Governor of Scutari under Sultan Mahmoud—as his name would indicate, one of the numerous renegades of Italian birth—played an important part in the negotiations for peace.

*You sent me*. The address *you* (tu) to a personage so high in office is singular and suggests personal intimacy; Leonardo seems to have been a favorite with the Diodario. Compare lines 54 and 55.

I have endeavored to show, and I believe that I am also in a position to prove with regard to these texts, that they are drafts of letters actually written by Leonardo; at the same time I must not omit to mention that shortly after I had discovered

---

these texts in the Codex Atlanticus and published a paper on the subject in the *Zeitschrift für bildende Kunst (Vol. XVI)*, Prof. Govi put forward this hypothesis to account for their origin:

> *Regarding the information about Mount Taurus, Armenia, and Asia Minor contained in the other fragments, it was taken from some contemporary geographer or traveler. From the imperfect index accompanying those fragments, one might conclude that Leonardo intended to make a book of them, which was never completed. In any case, it is impossible to find in these passages any evidence of a journey by Leonardo to the East, nor of his conversion to the religion of Muhammad, as some would claim. Leonardo passionately loved geographical studies, and in his writings one often encounters itineraries, directions, or descriptions of places, map sketches, and topographical drafts of various regions; it is therefore not strange that he, being the skilled narrator he was, had proposed to write a kind of Romance in epistolary form, setting the plot in Asia Minor, about which the books of that time, and perhaps some traveler friend of his, had provided him with some more or less fantastic elements. (See Transunti della Reale Accademia dei Lincei Vol. V Ser. 3).*

It is hardly necessary to point out that Prof. Govi fails to name the sources from which Leonardo could be supposed to have drawn his information, and I may leave it to the reader to pass judgment on the anomaly involved in the hypothesis that we have here a fragment of a Romance, written in the form of a correspondence. At the same time, I must admit that the solution to the difficulties proposed by Prof. Govi is, under the circumstances, certainly the easiest way of dealing with the question. But we would then be equally justified in supposing some more of Leonardo's letters to be fragments of such romances; particularly those where the addresses can no longer be identified. Still, regarding these drafts of letters to the Diodario, if we accept the Romance theory proposed by Prof. Govi, we are also compelled to assume that Leonardo intended from the start to illustrate his tale; for it takes only a glance at the sketches on Pl. CXVI to CXIX to perceive that they are connected with the texts; and of course the rest of Leonardo's numerous notes on matters pertaining to the East, the greater part of which are published here for the first time, may also be somehow connected with this strange romance.

7. *Città de Calindra (Chalindra).* The position of this city is so exactly determined, between the valley of the Euphrates and the Taurus range, that it should be possible to identify it. However, it can hardly be the same as the seaport of Cilicia with a somewhat similar name—Celenderis, Kelandria, Celendria, Kilindria, now the Turkish Gulnar. In two Catalonian Portulans in the Bibliothèque Nationale in Paris—one dating from the 15th century by Wilhelm von Soler, the other by Olivez de Majorca in 1584—I find this place called Calandra. But Leonardo's Calindra must certainly have been located further to the Northwest, probably somewhere in Kurdistan. The fact that the geographical position is so carefully determined by Leonardo seems to prove that it was a place of no great importance and little known. It is singular that the words first written in line 8 were *divisa dal lago* (Lake Van?), later altered to *dall' Eufrates*.

*Nostri confini*, and in line 6 *proposito nostro*. These refer to the frontier and to the affairs of the Mamelook Sultan. Lines 65 and 66 throw some light on the purpose of Leonardo's mission.

8. *I corni del grã mõte Tauro.* Compare the sketches Pl. CXVI—CXVIII. As long as it is impossible to identify the location of Calindra, it is very difficult to decide with any certainty which peak of the Taurus is meant here; and I greatly regret that I had no prior knowledge of this puzzling topographical question when, in 1876, I was pursuing archaeological inquiries in the Provinces of Aleppo and Cilicia, and had to travel for some time in view of the imposing snow-peaks of Bulghar Dagh and Ala Tepessi.

9—10. The opinion expressed here regarding the height of the mountain would be meaningless unless it had been written before Leonardo moved to Milan, where Monte Rosa is so conspicuous an

--- Page 526 ---
the sun always fall upon it on its East side, four hours before daylight; and being made of the whitest stone, it shines resplendently and serves the same purpose for these Armenians as bright moonlight would in the midst of the darkness. Because of its great height, it rises above the highest level of the clouds by a distance of four miles in a straight line. This peak is seen from many places toward the West, illuminated by the sun for a third of the night after sunset. This is the same object which we, when with you in calm weather, formerly supposed to be a comet. It appears to us in the darkness of night to change its form, being sometimes divided into two or three parts, and sometimes appearing long or short. This is caused by the clouds on the horizon of the sky which interpose themselves between part of this mountain and the sun; by cutting off some of the solar rays, the light on the mountain is interrupted by various intervals of clouds, and therefore the form of its brightness varies.

object in the landscape. 4 *hours before* seems to mean, four hours before the sun's rays penetrate to the bottom of the valleys.

11. *Very white stone.* The Taurus Mountains consist in great part of limestone.

14. *With you.* Leonardo had at first written "us" as though his meaning had been: This peak appeared to us to be a comet when you and I observed it in North Syria (at Aleppo? at Aintab?). The description of the curious reflection in the evening, resembling the "Alpine-glow," is certainly not an invented fiction, for in the next lines an explanation of the phenomenon is offered, or at least attempted.

19. The next 33 lines are evidently the contents of a connected Report or Book, but not of one which he had at hand; more probably, indeed, of one he intended to write.

20. *Persuasion of faith*, of the Christian or the Mohammedan faith? We must suppose the latter, at the beginning of a document addressed to so high a Mohammedan official. *Sermon* probably stands as an abbreviation for *preaching* (Latin *praedicatio*) in the sense of praise or glorification; very probably it may mean some such initial doxology as we find in Mohammedan works. (Compare line 40.)

26. 28. The phrasing of this is too general for any guess as to its meaning to be worth risking.

30. *Destruction of the mountain.* Of course by an earthquake. In a catalogue of earthquakes, entitled *The Discovery of the Earthquake in the Description of the Earthquakes*, and written by Jalal ad-Din

Syouthy, the following statement occurs: "In the year 889 (1484 A.D.) there were six shocks of earthquake at Aleppo. They were excessively violent and threw the inhabitants into consternation." I owe this communication to the kindness of Prof. Ch. Schéfer, Member of the Institute, to whom this unpublished Arabic MS. belongs. The preceding entries refer to two earthquakes in Cairo, in 1476 and 1481: the following ones indicate a time at which Leonardo was, certainly, living in Milan.

40. *New prophet*, l. 33, *prophet.* Mohammed. Leonardo here refers to the Koran:

> In the name of the most merciful God.—When the earth shall be shaken by an earthquake; and the earth shall cast forth her burdens; and a man shall say, what is the matter with her? On that day the earth shall declare her news, for your Lord will inspire her. On that day men shall go forward in distinct classes, so that they may see their works. And whoever has done good the weight of an ant, shall see it. And whoever has done evil the weight of an ant, shall see it. (The Koran, translated by G. Sale, Chapter XCIX, p. 452).

36. *Cutting of the Taurus Mountains.* The Euphrates flows through the Taurus range near the influx of the Kura Shai; it rushes through a rift in the wildest cliffs from 2000 to 3000 feet high and runs on for 90 miles in 300 falls or rapids until it reaches Telek, near which at a spot called Gleikash, or the Hart's leap, it measures only 35 paces across. Compare the map on Pl. CXIX and the explanation of it on p. 391.

53—94. The facsimile of this passage is given on Pl. CXVII.

54. The preceding sketch of a letter, lines 5. 18, appears to have remained a fragment when Leonardo received pressing orders which caused

--- Page 530 ---
Do not be distressed, O Devatdar, by my delay in responding to your eager request, because the matters you asked me about are of such a nature that they cannot be properly expressed without a lapse of time; especially since, in order to show the cause of so great an effect, it is necessary to describe the nature of the site with accurate form, and through that description you will then be able to easily satisfy yourself regarding the aforementioned request.

I will omit the description of the shape of Asia Minor, and which seas or lands define the boundaries of its size, because I know that the diligence and care of your studies have not left you without such knowledge; and I will proceed to describe the true shape of Mount Taurus, which is the cause of such a stupendous and harmful marvel, and which serves the purpose of our task. This Mount Taurus is what many say is the ridge of Mount Caucasus; but, having wished to clarify this for myself, I wanted to speak with several of those who live above the Caspian Sea, who show that to be the true Mount Caucasus; for although their mountains have the same name, these are of greater height. They confirm this because "Caucasus" in the Scythian tongue means "highest peak," and in truth there is no knowledge of any mountain of such great height in either the East or the West. The proof that this is so is that the inhabitants of the countries to the West see the rays of the sun illuminating a great part of its summit for as much as a quarter of the longest nights, and the same occurs for those countries situated to the East.

### Quality and size of Mount Taurus.

The shadow of this ridge of the Taurus is of such height that, when the sun is at its meridian in the middle of June, its shadow reaches—

--- Page 533 ---
Its shadow extends as far as the borders of Sarmatia, a distance of twelve days; and in the middle of December, it reaches as far as the Hyperborean mountains, which are a month's journey to the North. The side that faces the wind is always free from clouds and mists because the wind, which is split upon striking the rock, closes again on the far side of that rock; in its motion, it carries the clouds with it from all directions and leaves them where it strikes. It is also constantly struck by lightning due to the vast quantity of clouds that accumulate there, which has left the rock completely shattered and filled with massive debris. At its base, this mountain is inhabited by a very wealthy population and is full of beautiful springs and rivers; it is fertile and abounds in all good produce, particularly in the areas facing South. However, after climbing about three miles, one begins to find forests of great fir trees, beeches, and other similar trees. Beyond these, for a distance of three more miles, there are meadows and vast pastures; and all the rest, up to the beginning of the Taurus, is covered in eternal snows.

***

77. Sudden storms are equally common on the heights of Ararat. It is hardly necessary to observe that Ararat cannot be the place meant here. Its summit is shaped like the crater of Vesuvius. The peaks sketched on Plate CXVI—CXVIII are probably views of the same mountain, taken from different sides. Near the solitary peak on Plate CXVIII, these three names are written: *goba, arnigasar, carūda*, which are most likely the names of different peaks. Plate CXVI and CXVII are in the original on a single sheet folded down the middle, 30 centimeters high and 43 1/2 wide. On the back of one half of the sheet are notes on *peso and bilancia* (weight and balance); on the other are the ‘prophecies’ printed under Nos. 1293 and 1294. It is evident from the arrangement that these were written later, in the space that had been left blank. These pages are reproduced in facsimile on Plate CXVIII. In Plate CXVI—CXVIII, the size is smaller than in the original; the map of Armenia, Plate CXVIII, is slightly enlarged on Plate CXIX. On this map, we find the following names, starting from the top right: *pariardes mō* (for Paryadres Mons, Armenian Parchar, now Barchal or Kolai Dagh; Trebizond is on its slope).

Aquilone—North, *Antitaurus Antitaurus \\\\ psis mō* (probably intended for Thospitis = Lake Van, Armenian Dgov Vanai, Tospoi, and the mountain range to the South); *Gordis mō* (Mountains of Gordyaea), the source of the Tigris; *Oriente*—East; *Tigris*, and then, to the left, *Eufrates*. Then, above to the left, *Argeo mō* (now Erdshigas, an extinct volcano, 12,000 feet high); *Celeno mō* (undoubtedly Sultan Dagh in Pisidia. Celeno is the Greek town of Kελαιναί—see Arrian I, 29, I—now the ruins of Dineir); *oriente*—East; *africo libezco* (for libeccio—Southwest). In the middle of the Euphrates river on this small map, we see a shaded portion surrounded by mountains, perhaps to indicate the flood mentioned in line 35. The tributary to the Euphrates shown winding from the highlands of ‘Argeo’ on the West is the Tochma Su, which joins the main river at Malatie. I have not been able to discover any map of Armenia from the 15th or 16th century in which the course of the Euphrates is laid out with anything like the accuracy displayed in this sketch. The best I have seen is the Catalonian Portolan by Olivez de Majorca, executed in 1584, and it is far inferior to Leonardo da Vinci’s.

1337.] LETTERS.

What we have seen and experienced here has caused such great harm to people that I cannot imagine how such evil could be increased further. We endured this for the space of ten hours. First, we were attacked and assaulted by the violence and fury of the winds; to this was added the collapse of great mountains of snow, which filled all these valleys and shattered a large part of our city. Not satisfied with this, fortune sent sudden floods of water that submerged the entire lower part of the city. Beyond this, there was a sudden rain—or rather, a destructive storm filled with water, sand, mud, and stones, all tangled together with roots, stems, and logs from various plants. Everything flying through the air descended upon us. Finally, a fire broke out that seemed to be driven not just by winds, but carried by ten thousand devils; it has burned and destroyed this entire country, and it has not yet stopped. Those few of us who remain are left with such dismay and fear that, like stunned people, we hardly dare to speak to one another. Having abandoned all our work, we stay huddled together in the ruins of certain churches—men and women, children and adults, mixed together like herds of goats. Out of pity, the neighbors have helped us with food supplies, though they were previously our enemies. And if...

***

11. *Of our city* (Leonardo first wrote *of this city*). From this we may infer that he had at some time lived in the place in question, wherever it might be. Under the title of "Description of a landscape near Lake Como," we do in fact find, among other loose sheets in the Codex Atlanticus, certain texts referring to Alpine valleys (see Nos. 1030, 1031 and note p. 237). In the arrangement of the loose sheets from which the Codex Atlanticus was formed, these happen to be placed close to this text. The compiler stuck both on the same folio sheet; if this is not the reason for Dr. Jordan choosing such a title (Description, etc.), I cannot imagine what it could have been. It is, at any rate, a merely hypothetical statement. The designation of the population of the country around a city as "the enemy" (nemici) is hardly appropriate to Italy in the time of Leonardo.

17. *Certain ruins of churches.* Either Armenian churches or mosques, which were not uncommonly referred to as churches. *Males and females united together* implies a violation of the usually strict rule regarding the separation of the sexes.

18. *The neighbors, our enemies.* The town must then have stood quite close to the border of the country. Compare 1336, line 7: *neighbors at our borders*. Dr. M. Jordan has already published lines 4–13 (see *The Painter’s Book, Leipzig*, 1873, p. 90; his reading differs from mine).

VOL. II. DDD

--- Page 540 ---
The page is blank and contains no legible text or illustrations.

--- Page 541 ---
The tempestuous sea does not bellow so loudly when the northern blast strikes it, with its foaming waves between Scylla and Charybdis; nor do Stromboli or Mount Etna, when their sulfurous flames, having been forcibly confined, rend and burst open the great mountain, thundering stones and earth through the air together with the flames they vomit.

Nor when the inflamed caverns of Mount Etna, rejecting the ill-restrained element, vomit it forth back to its own region, driving furiously before it every obstacle that comes in the way of its impetuous rage.

Unable to resist my eager desire and wanting to see the great [quantity] of the various and strange shapes made by formative nature, and having wandered some distance among gloomy rocks, I came to the entrance of a great cavern, in front of which I stood for some time, astonished and unaware of such a thing. Bending my back into an arch, I rested my left hand on my knee and held my right hand over my downcast and contracted eyebrows; often bending first one way and then the other to see whether I could discover anything inside, though this was prevented by the deep darkness within. After having remained there some time, two contrary emotions suddenly arose in me: fear and desire—fear of the threatening dark cavern, and desire to see whether there were any marvelous thing within it.

***

### 1340.

Most illustrious Lord, having now sufficiently considered the specimens of all those who proclaim themselves skilled contrivers...

--- Page 544 ---
10. In times of peace, I believe I can provide perfect satisfaction and equal any other in architecture and the design of both public and private buildings, as well as in conducting water from one place to another.

Item: I can execute sculpture in marble, bronze, or clay, and likewise in painting I can do whatever may be done, as well as any other, whoever he may be.

Furthermore, the bronze horse may be taken in hand, which will bring immortal glory and eternal honor to the happy memory of your father the Prince, and to the illustrious house of Sforza.

And if any of the aforementioned things should seem impossible or unfeasible to anyone, I offer myself as most ready to perform the experiment in your park, or in whatever place may please your Excellency, to whom I commend myself with as much humility as I possibly can, etc.

### 1342.

You would like to see a model which will prove useful to you and to me, and also be of use to those who will be the cause of our usefulness.

--- Page 546 ---
My Lord, knowing your Excellency's mind to be occupied, I remind your Lordship of my small matters and the arts put to silence, fearing that my silence might cause your Lordship to feel scorn for my life in your service. I hold myself constantly ready to obey. Regarding the horse, I will say nothing because I know the times are difficult. I remind your Lordship how I was still owed two years' salary, along with the two skilled workmen who were constantly in my pay and at my expense, so that in the end I found myself having advanced about 15 lire for the said work. I desire to create works of fame by which I could show those who come after me that I have been active everywhere; but I do not know where I could better bestow my work than in your service. Having been occupied with earning my living, and not having been informed of what the situation is, I find myself in this position. Remember the commission to paint the small rooms; I brought this to your Lordship's attention, only requesting it of you.

Magnificent Commissioners of Buildings, understanding that your Magnificences have decided to commission certain great works in bronze, I will remind you of a few things: first, that you should not be so hasty or so quick to award the contract, lest by such haste it becomes impossible to select a good design and a capable master; and some man of little merit might be chosen, who by his incompetence may cause your successors to reproach your era.

--- Page 547 ---
...judging that this age was poorly supplied with men of good judgment and good masters, seeing that other cities, and especially the city of the Florentines, have been endowed at almost the same time with such beautiful and grand works of bronze, among which are the doors of their Baptistery. This Florence, like Piacenza, is a place of passage where many foreigners gather; these people, seeing works that are beautiful or good, receive a favorable impression for themselves. They believe that city to be supplied with worthy inhabitants, seeing the works that bear witness to that opinion. On the contrary, I say that seeing so much metal used so poorly, it would be less of a shame to the city if those doors were made of simple wood; for the low cost of the material would not seem to deserve a great expenditure of craftsmanship, whereas...

The principal features sought for in cities are the cathedrals, and as one approaches them, the first things that appear to the eye are the doors through which one passes into those churches.

Take care, gentlemen of the Commission, that too much haste in your desire to settle the contract for such a great work—as I hear you have ordered—does not cause that which is done for the honor of God and of men to turn into a great dishonor to your judgment and to your city. For since it is a worthy place of passage, there is a gathering of innumerable foreigners; and this dishonor would occur if, through your lack of diligence, you placed your trust in some boaster who, through his deceits or through favor shown to him here, might obtain such a work from you. By this, he would bring long and immense infamy upon himself and upon you. I cannot help but be angry when I think of what kind of men those are who have discussed wanting to enter into such an undertaking without considering their own sufficiency, to say nothing more; one is a master of pottery, another of armor...

--- Page 548 ---
...one is a bell-founder, another a bell-ringer, and one is even a bombardier. Among them is one in his Lordship's service who boasted that he was a close friend of Messer Ambrosio Ferrere, who has some authority and has made him certain promises. If this were not enough, he said he would mount his horse, go to his Lord, and obtain such letters of recommendation that you could never refuse to give him the work. But consider what masters of real talent, fit for such projects, are reduced to when they must compete with men like these. Open your eyes and look carefully so that your money is not spent on buying your own disgrace. I declare to you that from this place you will procure nothing but mediocre works by inferior and unskilled masters. There is no capable man here—and you may believe me—except Leonardo the Florentine, who is making the bronze equestrian statue of Duke Francesco. He has no need to seek notice, because he has enough work to last his entire lifetime; and I doubt, since it is such a massive undertaking, whether he will ever finish it.
The miserable, hardworking students... with what hope can they expect a reward for their merit?

---

### 1347.

There is one whom his Lordship invited from Florence to do this work and who is a worthy master, but he has so much business that he will never finish it; and you can imagine what a difference there is to be seen between a beautiful object and an ugly one. Quote Pliny.

### 1348.

**To my most Illustrious and most Reverend Lord, my only Patron.**
**To Lord Ippolito, Cardinal of Este, my most respected Lord.**
**At Ferrara.**
**Most Illustrious and most Reverend Lord, I humbly commend myself to you.**
I arrived from Milan only a few days ago, and finding that an elder brother of mine...

--- Page 549 ---
...refuses to carry out a will made three years ago when our father died—as well as, and no less, because I would not fail in a matter I consider most important—I cannot refrain from requesting of your most Reverend Highness a letter of recommendation and favor to Ser Raphaello Hieronymo, who is currently one of the illustrious members of the Signoria before whom my case is being argued. More particularly, it has been placed by his Excellency the Gonfaloniere into the hands of the said Ser Raphaello, so that his Worship may decide and conclude it before the festival of All Saints. And therefore, my Lord, I entreat you as urgently as I know how and am able, that your Highness will write a letter to the said Ser Raphaello in that skillful and affectionate manner which your Highness can use, recommending to him Leonardo Vincio, your most devoted servant as I am called, and shall always be; requesting and urging him not only to do me justice but to do so with speed. I have not the least doubt, from many reports made to me, that since Ser Raphaello is most affectionately devoted to your Highness, the matter will turn out according to my wishes. This I shall attribute to your most Reverend Highness's letter, to whom I once more humbly commend myself. And may you be well.

Florence, September 18, 1507.
Your most Reverend Lordship's
Humble servant,
Leonardus Vincius, painter.

I am afraid that the small return I have made for the great benefits I have received from your Excellency may have...

--- Page 551 ---
Magnificent President, I...

Magnificent President, having frequently recalled the offers made to me many times by your Excellency, I have taken the liberty of writing to remind you of the promise made to me at my last departure—specifically, the possession of those twelve inches of water granted to me by the most Christian King. Your Lordship knows that I did not enter into possession because, at the time it was granted to me, there was a water shortage in the canal, both due to the great drought and because the outlets had not yet been regulated. However, I was promised by your Excellency that once such regulation was completed, I would obtain my objective. Afterward, understanding the canal to be in order, I wrote several times to your Lordship and to Messer Girolamo da Cusano, who has the deed of that gift in his possession; I also wrote to Corigero, but I never received a reply. Now I am sending Salai, my pupil and the bearer of this letter, to whom your Lordship may explain in person everything that has transpired, for which I petition your Excellency. I believe I shall be there this Easter, as I am near the end of my lawsuit, and I will bring with me two paintings of Our Lady that I have begun, which I have brought to a very advanced state in my spare time. I have nothing else to add.

My Magnificent Lord, the love that your Excellency has always shown me and the benefits I have continually received from you are always before me...
I suspect that the poor return I have made for the great benefits I have received from your Excellency may have made you somewhat angry with me, and this is why I have never had a response to the several letters I have written to your Excellency. Now I am sending Salai there to inform your Lordship that I am almost at the end of my litigation with my brothers, and that I expect to be there this Easter and bring with me two paintings of Our Lady of different sizes. I began these for the most Christian King, or for whomever you please; I would be very glad to know...

--- Page 552 ---
...to know where, on my return from there, I shall have to reside, because I do not wish to cause any more trouble for your Lordship; and also, having worked for the most Christian King, whether my salary is to be continued or not. I am writing to the President regarding the water rights that the king granted me, which I had not been put in possession of because of the shortage in the canal caused by the great drought and because its outlets were not regulated. However, he certainly promised me that as soon as the regulation was made, I would be put in possession of it. I therefore pray that, if you should meet the said President, you would be kind enough—now that the outlets are regulated—to remind him to have me put in possession of that water, since I understand it is largely within his power. Nothing else occurs to me; I am always at your command.

---

Good day to you, Messer Francesco. How can it be, in God's name, that of all the letters I have written to you, you have never answered one? Just wait until I come there, by God, and I shall make you write so much that perhaps you will become sick of it.

---

My dear Messer Francesco, I am sending Salai there to learn from His Magnificence the President what has become of that regulation of the water which was ordered for the canal outlets at the time of my departure. His Magnificence the President promised me that as soon as this regulation was completed, my matter would be settled. It has now been some time since I heard that the canal was in order, as were its outlets, and I immediately wrote to the President and to you, and then I wrote again, but I never received an answer. Therefore, please deign to answer me regarding what has happened; and since the matter is not yet settled, would you please, for my sake, take the trouble to urge the President a little, as well as Messer Girolamo da Cusano, to whom you will commend me and offer my service to his Magnificence.

**1351. 1353.** It is clear from the contents of these notes that they refer to Leonardo’s residence in Rome in 1513–1515. Nor can there be any doubt that they were addressed to Leonardo’s patron at the time: Giuliano de’ Medici, third son of Lorenzo the Magnificent and brother of Pope Leo X (born 1478). In 1512 he became the head of the Florentine Republic. The Pope invited him to Rome, where he settled; in 1513 he was named a patrician with much splendid ceremony. The medal struck in honor of the event bears the words MAG. IVLIAN. MEDICES. Leonardo also uses the title “Magnifico” in his letter. Compare also No. 1377.

Gino Capponi (*History of the Florentine Republic*, Vol. III, p. 139) thus describes the character of Giuliano de’ Medici, who died in 1516: *He was the best of the family, of a peaceful life, a great spender, keeping ingenious men around him, and he wanted to try every new thing.*

See also Gregorovius, *History of the City of Rome*, VIII (book XIV. III, 2): *The castles in the air of princely greatness, to which the Pope had wanted to raise him, crumbled. Giuliano was the noblest of all the Medici of that time, a man of inner direction, unsatisfied by life, a dark figure passing like a shadow in the midst of the sunny splendor of Leo X’s glory.* Giuliano lived in the Vatican, and it may be safely inferred from No. 1352 l. 2, and No. 1353 l. 4, that Leonardo did the same.

From the following unpublished notice in the Vatican archives, which M. Eug. Müntz, librarian of the Ecole des Beaux-Arts, Paris, has done me the favor of communicating to me, we get a more accurate view of Leonardo’s relation to the often-named Giorgio Tedesco:

> Note of the allowances to be paid by me in the name of our illustrious Lord Bernardo Bini and company of Rome, and first to his most illustrious consort every month 800 ducats.
>
> To Leonardo da Vinci for his allowance 33 ducats, and 7 ducats more to the same for the allowance of Giorgio Tedesco, which are 40 ducats in total.

From this we learn that seven ducats formed the German’s monthly wages, but according to No. 1353 l. 7, he pretended that eight ducats had been agreed upon.

--- Page 554 ---
...would acquire the Italian language. He always promised to do so, but never actually would. I also did this because Giovanni, the German who makes the mirrors, was in the workshop every day and wanted to see and understand everything that was being done and made it known outside, criticizing it severely. Furthermore, he ate with the members of the Pope’s guard and then went out with them carrying guns to kill birds among the ruins; this continued from after dinner until evening. If I sent Lorenzo to urge them to get on with the work, he would become angry and say that he did not want so many masters over him, and that his work was for Your Excellency’s Wardrobe. Two months passed in this manner, and so it continued. Later, finding Gian Niccolò of the Wardrobe, I asked him if the German had finished the work for your Magnificence, and he told me it was not true—he had only given him two guns to clean. Afterward, when I pressed him further, he left the workshop and began to work in his room, wasting much time making another vise, files, and other screw-driven tools. There he worked on silk-twisting machines, which he would hide whenever one of my people entered, accompanied by a thousand curses and insults, so that none of my men wanted to go in there anymore.

I was so greatly rejoiced, my most Illustrious Lord, by the long-awaited recovery of your health that my own illness has almost fled from me. But I am very sorry that I have not been able to completely satisfy Your Excellency’s wishes because of the malice of that German deceiver, for whom I left nothing undone that I believed might please him. Secondly, I invited him to lodge and live with me, so that I could constantly see the work he was doing and easily correct any errors. Besides this, he would have learned the Italian language, through which he could easily speak without an interpreter; and his money was always given to him in advance of the...

--- Page 555 ---
time when due. Afterwards, his request was to have the finished models made of wood, just as they were to be made of iron, which he wanted to take back to his own country. But I refused him this, telling him that I would give him a drawing of the width, length, thickness, and shape of what he had to make; and so we remained on bad terms.

The second issue was that he set up another workshop with a vise and tools in the room where he slept, and there he worked for others. Afterward, he went to dine with the Swiss Guard, where there are idle people, and he surpassed them all in that regard. Most of the time, two or three of them would go out together with guns to shoot birds among the ruins, and this continued until evening.

At last, I discovered how this Master Giovanni the mirror-maker was the one who had caused all of this for two reasons. The first is because he had been heard saying that my arrival here had deprived him of the conversation and favor of your Lordship, which always... The other is that he said the room belonging to this iron-worker suited him for making his mirrors. He proved this because, besides making that man my enemy, he made him sell everything he owned and leave his workshop to him, where he now works with many laborers making numerous mirrors to send to the fairs.

***

### 1352.

I was so greatly rejoiced, my most illustrious Lord, by the long-awaited recovery of your health that my own illness has almost fled from me; I say, God be praised for it. But it vexes me greatly that I have not been able to completely satisfy Your Excellency’s wishes because of the malice of that German deceiver, for whom I left nothing undone that I believed might please him. Secondly, I invited him to lodge and live with me, by which means I could constantly see the work he was doing...

--- Page 556 ---
a table at the foot of one of these windows, where he could work with a file and finish the items manufactured below; in this way, I would constantly see the work he did, and it could be corrected with greater ease.

---

### 1353.

This other man has hindered me in my anatomy studies by criticizing them before the Pope, and likewise at the hospital; he has filled this entire Belvedere with workshops for mirrors or for laborers, and he did the same in Maestro Giorgio’s room. He claimed that eight ducats were promised to him every month, beginning from the first day he set out on his journey, or at the latest when he spoke with you, and that you accepted these terms.

Seeing that he rarely stayed in the workshop and that he consumed a great deal, I sent word to him that, if he liked, I would make a deal with him for each individual thing he produced, and I would pay him whatever fair valuation we could agree upon. He took counsel with his neighbor and gave up his room, selling everything, and went to find...

---

### 1354.

Dear Benedetto de' Pertarti. When the fierce giant fell because of the bloody and miry ground, it seemed as if a mountain had fallen; consequently, the countryside was shaken as if by an earthquake, causing terror to Pluto in hell. From the great blow, he lay upon the level ground for a while, stunned. Suddenly the people—believing he had been killed by a lightning bolt—returned in a great crowd, like ants running frantically over the body of a fallen oak; so these people ran over his massive limbs, scarring them with frequent wounds; whereby...

--- Page 557 ---
### 1355.

The giant, having regained his senses and feeling himself almost entirely covered by the crowd, suddenly let out a roar that sounded like a terrifying clap of thunder. He placed his hands on the ground and raised his terrifying face; then, putting one hand to his head, he found it full of men and boys clinging to it, like the tiny creatures that sometimes hide among the hair. Shaking his head, he sent the men flying through the air like hail when it is driven by a whirlwind, and many of these men were killed by those who fell on top of them. Then he stood up, treading under his feet the great crowd, and stood as tall as a tower.

The holes of a pipe should, by their nature, exceed the number of a man's fingers. These holes should be closed by keys and be covered by keys that are of the same width and length. Through these holes, the escaping air performs its function or whistle, just as you will hear later regarding the keys.

---

One can make the air enter the bellows by way of holes, and by way of pipes, and by way of openings, and by way of trumpets, and by way of organ pipes, and by way of reed pipes.

---

One can make the air enter and exit through the same path, and one can make the air enter and exit through different paths and with different pipes.

--- Page 561 ---
The giant, being roused and feeling himself almost covered by the multitude, suddenly perceives the smarting of the stabs and sent forth a roar which sounded like a terrifying clap of thunder. Placing his hands on the ground, he raised his terrible face; and having lifted one of his hands to his head, he found it full of men and rabble sticking to it like the tiny creatures that are often found there. Therefore, with a shake of his head, he sends the men flying through the air just as hail does when driven by the fury of the winds. Many of these men were found to be dead, having been stepped on by those who were standing upright upon him, stamping with their feet.

And clinging to his hair and striving to hide within it, they behaved like sailors in a storm who run up the ropes to lower the sails and lessen the force of the wind.

News of things from the East. Be it known to you that in the month of June there appeared a giant who came from the Libyan desert, raging like ants, struck down by the harsh peasant.

This great giant was born on Mount Atlas and was a hero, and he had to fight against the Egyptians and Arabs, Medes and Persians. He lived in the sea on whales, sperm whales, and ships.

Mars, fearing for his life, took refuge under the protection of Jove.

And at the great fall, it seemed as though the entire province quaked.

**1358.** Below this text we read *gusstino* = Giustino and in another passage on the same page Justin is quoted (No. 1210, l. 48). The two have, however, no real connection.

--- Page 563 ---
There are few whom his vices have disgusted; rather, he only dislikes those men whose natures are contrary to such vices. Many hate their fathers and break off friendships with those who reprove their vices; they do not want to see examples that contradict their behavior, nor do they want any human advice.

And if you find anyone who is virtuous and good, do not drive him away from you; honor him, so that he does not have to flee from you and retreat into hermitages, caves, or other solitary places to escape your treachery. If such a person is found, honor him, because these are your earthly gods; these are the ones who deserve statues and images from you. However, I remind you that their images should not be eaten by you, as is still done in some regions of India; for when the images perform some miracle according to them, the priests cut them into pieces—since they are made of wood—and give them to everyone in the country, though not without payment. Each person then finely grates his portion and puts it on the first food they eat; thus they believe by faith that they have eaten their saint, and they believe that he will then protect them from all dangers. What do you think of your own species here, Man? Are you as wise as you believe yourself to be? Are these things to be done by men?

Francesco d’Antonio.
Bernardo di Maestro Jacopo.

**1368.** Leonardo writes his name this way on a sheet with various short notes, evidently to test a pen. Compare the signature with those in Nos. 1341, 1348, and 1374 (see also No. 1346, l. 33). The form “Lionardo” does not appear in the original signatures. The Portrait of the Master in the Royal Library at Turin, which is reproduced—slightly reduced in size—on Pl. I, has two lines of writing underneath in the original; one in red chalk consisting of two or three words is partly rubbed out: *lionardo it . . . lm (or lai?)*; the second, written in pencil, is as follows: *made by himself when quite old*. In both of these, the writing is very similar to the Master’s, but is certainly only an imitation.

**1369.** This date is on a drawing of a rocky landscape. See *Chronique des Arts* 1881 no. 23: *Did Leonardo da Vinci go to the Righi on August 5th, 1473?* a letter by H. de Geymüller. The next following date in the manuscripts is 1478 (see No. 663).

**1370.** While the letters in the manuscript notes of 1473 and 1478 are very decorative, this note and the texts on anatomy on the same sheet (for instance No. 805) are in the same simple handwriting that we see on Pl. CXVI and CXIX. No. 1370 is the only dated note from the years between 1480 and 1489, and the characters are in all essential points identical to those we see in the latest manuscripts written in France (compare the facsimiles on Pl. CXV and p. 254), so that it is hardly possible to determine the exact date of a manuscript from the style of the handwriting, if it does not show the stylistic peculiarities found in the few notes dated before 1480.—Compare the facsimile of the manuscripts from 1479 on Pl. LXII, No. 2; No. 664, note, Vol. I p. 346. This already shows a marked simplicity compared to the formal calligraphy of 1478.

The text No. 720 belongs to the year 1490; No. 1510 to the year 1492; No. 1459, No. 1384, and No. 1460 to the year 1493; No. 1463, No. 1517, No. 1024, 1025, and 1461 to the year 1494; Nos. 1523 and 1524 to the year 1497.

**1371.** *I wrote here.* Leonardo does not say where; yet we may assume that it was not in Milan. Amoretti writes in *Memorie Storiche*, chapter XIX: *It seems, therefore, that Leonardo departed from here to go to Florence not in 1499 but in 1500, after the return and imprisonment of the Duke; and it is therefore probable that he spent the months of the new and uncertain government with his friend Francesco Melzi at Vaprio, where he could study nature better than elsewhere, and especially the waters, and the Adda specifically, which had already been the subject of his hydrostatic research.* At that time Melzi was only six years old. The next date is 1502; to this year belong No. 1034, 1040, 1042, 1048, and 1053. The note No. 1525 belongs to the year 1503.
