**1372.** This statement regarding Ser Piero’s age contradicts the *Summary of the tax return of Antonio da Vinci* (Leonardo’s grandfather), who describes Ser Piero as being thirty years old in 1457; it also contradicts the *Summary of the tax return of Ser Piero and Francesco*, sons of Antonia da Vinci, where Ser Piero is mentioned as being forty in 1469. These documents were published by G. UZIELLI, *Researches concerning L. da Vinci, Florence*, 1872, pp. 144 and 146. Leonardo was, as is well known, an illegitimate son. His mother ‘La Catarina’ was married in 1457 to Acchattabriga di Piero del Vaccha da Vinci. She died in 1519. Leonardo never mentions her in the Manuscripts. In the year of Leonardo’s birth, Ser Piero married Albiera di Giovanni Amadoci, and after her death at the age of thirty-eight, he married again, taking Francesca, daughter of Ser Giovanni Lanfredi, who was then only fifteen. Their children were Leonardo’s half-brothers: Antonio (born 1476), Ser Giuliano (born 1479), Lorenzo (born 1484), a girl, Violante (born 1485), and another boy, Domenico (born 1486); Domenico’s descendants still exist as a family. Ser Piero married for the third time to Lucrezia di Guglielmo Cortigiani, by whom he had six children: Margherita (born 1491), Benedetto (born 1492), Pandolfo (born 1494), Guglielmo (born 1496), Bartolommeo (born 1497), and Giovanni (date of birth unknown). Pierino da Vinci the sculptor (about 1520—1554) was the son of Bartolommeo, the fifth of these children. The dates of their deaths are not known, but we may infer from the passage above that they were all still living in 1505.

**1373.** It may be noted that this and the previous text are the only mentions Leonardo made of his father; Nos. 1526, 1527, and No. 1463 are from the year 1504.

**1374.** He writes this on the first page of the manuscript. The title is on the preceding cover sheet as follows: *Book titled "Of Transformation," that is, of one body into another without the reduction or increase of matter.*

**1375.** No. 1528 and No. 1529 belong to the same year. The text in Vol. I, No. 4 belongs to the following year, 1509 (1508 in the old style calendar); so also does No. 1009. Nos. 1022, 1057, and 1464 belong to 1511.

**1376.** No. 1465 belongs to the same year. No. 1065 bears the next date, 1514.

--- Page 570 ---
Whatever may have been the fate of the manuscripts and notebooks of the preceding years—whether they were destroyed by Leonardo himself or have been lost—it is certainly strange that nothing exists to inform us about his life and activities in Milan earlier than the consecutive series of manuscripts which begin in the year 1489.

There is nothing surprising in the fact that the notes regarding his pupils are few and meager. Except for the record of money transactions, only very exceptional circumstances would have prompted him to make any written observations on the people with whom he was in daily contact, among whom, of course, were his pupils. Of them all, none is so frequently mentioned as Salai, but the character of the notes does not—as it seems to me—justify us in supposing that he was anything more than a sort of factotum for Leonardo (see 1519, note).

Leonardo's quotations from books and his lists of titles provide nothing more than a hint regarding his occasional literary studies or recreations. It was evidently no part of his ambition to be deeply read (see Nos. 10, 11, 1159), and he more than once expressly states (in various passages which will be found in the preceding sections) that he did not recognize the authority of the Ancients on scientific questions, which in his day was held to be supreme. Archimedes is the sole exception, and Leonardo frankly admits his admiration for the illustrious Greek, to whose genius his own was so much akin (see No. 1476). All his notes on various authors, except those which have already been inserted in the previous section, have been arranged alphabetically for the sake of convenience (1469–1508).

The passages next in order contain accounts and inventories, principally of household property. The publication of these—often very trivial entries—is only justifiable as proof that the wealth, the splendid lifestyle, and the lavish expenditure which have been attributed to Leonardo are altogether mythical; unless we propose the very improbable hypothesis that these notes regarding cash on hand, expenses, and receipts refer throughout to an exceptional state of his affairs, namely, when he was short of money.

The memoranda collected at the end (No. 1505–1565) are, in the original, in the usual style of writing, from left to right. Besides, the style of the handwriting is at variance with what we would expect it to be if Leonardo himself had actually written these notes. Most of them are found alongside undoubtedly authentic writing of his. But this may be easily explained if we take into account the fact that Leonardo frequently wrote on loose sheets. He may, therefore, have occasionally used paper on which others had made short memoranda, for the most part, it would seem, for his use. At the very end, I have provided Leonardo's will from the copy of it preserved in the Melzi Library. It has already been printed by Amoretti and by Uzielli. It is not known what has become of the original document.

--- Page 571 ---
1379.

Find Longhi and tell him that you are waiting for him in Rome and that you will go with him to Naples; have the donation paid to you and take the book by Vitolone, as well as the measurements of the public buildings. Have two covered boxes made to be carried by mules, but bedspreads will be best; there are three of these, one of which you will leave at Vinci. Obtain the linings of the gratings from Giovanni Lombardo, the linen draper of Verona. Buy tablecloths and towels, . . . and shoes, 4 pairs of hose, a doublet made of selvage and skins to make new ones; the lathe of Alessandro. Sell what you cannot carry with you. Get from Jean de Paris the method of painting in tempera, the method of using white salt, and the way to make tinted papers; folded in many layers.

---

1379. The mysterious-looking words, quite distinctly written in line 1: *ingol, amor a, ilopan a* and on line 2: *enoiganod al* are obviously in cipher and the solution is a simple one; by reading them backwards we find for *ingol*: logni—probably *Longhi*, evidently the name of a person; for *amor a*: *a Roma* (to Rome), for *ilopan a*: *a Napoli* (to Naples). Leonardo has done the same in two passages dealing with certain secrets of his art, Nos. 641 and 729, the only other places in which we find this cipher employed; we may therefore conclude that he used it for the sake of secrecy.

There can be no doubt, from the tone of this passage, that Leonardo planned a secret excursion to Naples. Nothing has previously been known of this journey, but the significance of the passage will be easily understood by referring to the following notes, from which we may infer that Leonardo actually had plans at the time for traveling further than Naples. From lines 3, 4, and 7, it is evident that he intended, after selling everything that was not easily portable, to leave a chest in the care of his relatives at Vinci. His luggage was to be packed into two trunks especially adapted for transport by mules. The exact meaning of many sentences in the following notes must necessarily remain obscure. These brief remarks on small and irrelevant affairs and so forth are, however, of no historical value. The notes referring to the preparations for his journey are more intelligible.

2. *Libro di Vitolone* see No. 1506 note.

7 and following. It would seem from the text that Leonardo intended to receive instructions in painting on paper. It is hardly necessary to point out that the art of illumination was quite separate from that of painting.

**1410. Caravaggio**, a village not far from the Adda between Milan and Brescia, where Polidoro and Michelangelo da Caravaggio were born. This note is shown in facsimile on Plate XIII, No. 1 (above, to the left). On Plate XIII, No. 2 above to the right, we read *cerovazo*.

**1413.** This note is written between lines 23 and 24 of text No. **710**. Corduso, Cordusio (*curia ducis*) = Cordus in the Milanese dialect, is the name of a Piazza between the Via del Broletto and the Piazza de' Mercanti in Milan. During the time of Ludovico il Moro, it was the center of the town. The people named here were members of the noble Milanese family de' Fossani; Ambrogio da Fossano, the contemporary painter, had no connection with them.

**1414. lines 4—10.** This passage clearly refers to events in Milan at the time of the overthrow of Ludovico il Moro. Amoretti published it in the '*Memorie Storiche*' and added extensive notes.

6. *Visconti. We cannot guess which Visconti this was among so many of that name. Arluno relates that at that time the houses of the Visconti, the Castiglioni, the Sanseverini, and the Botta families were destroyed, and it is not improbable that their owners were insulted and killed. The same chronicler lists many members of the Visconti family who, because they celebrated the Duke's return to Milan, were arrested by the French and dragged to France as state prisoners; among these were Messer Francesco Visconti and his son Battista.* (AMORETTI, *Mem. Stor.* XIX.)

8. *Borgonzio or Brugonzio Botta was the administrator of the ducal revenues under Ludovico il Moro; upon the Duke's flight, Borgonzio's house was also ransacked by French partisans.* (AMORETTI, as cited above.)

**1424.** Lines 1–3 are written in the original between lines 3 and 4 of No. 292. However, the meaning is not clear in that context. It is almost impossible to guess the meaning of the following sentence.
2. 3. *Gherardo Miniatore*, a famous illuminator, 1445–1497, to whom Vasari dedicated a section of his Lives (Vol. II pp. 237–243, Sansoni edition 1879).
5. *Bernardo*, possibly the painter Bernardo Zenale.

**1427.** The sketch on the same page as this text represents two poles crossing each other. At the ends of the longest are the letters *c* and *d*. The meaning of the passage is not made any clearer.
**1428.** This seems to be a reflection on the flying machine (compare p. 271).
**1429.** Leonardo perhaps refers to the Gauls under Brennus, who placed his sword in the scales when the tribute was weighed.

**1436.** 3. *Giordano.* Jordanus Nemorarius, a mathematician from the beginning of the 13th century. No details of his life are known. The title of his main work is: *Arithmetica decem libris demonstrata*, first published in Paris in 1496. In 1523, there appeared in Nuremberg: *Liber Jordani Nemorarii de ponderibus, propositiones XIII et earundem demonstrationes, multarumque rerum rationes sane pulcherrimas complectens, nunc in lucem editus.*

**1441.** 6. *Marc Antonio*, see No. 1433.
**1443.** Alessandro Farnese, who later became Pope Paul III, was appointed Cardinal of San Cosimo and San Damiano in 1493 by Alexander VI.
**1444. 1.** *Pandolfino, Agnolo*, of Florence. To this day, it remains uncertain whether he or L. B. Alberti was the author of the famous work *On the Governance of the Family*. It is more likely that Leonardo referred to this work by the words *the book*, as no other book is known to have been written by Pandolfino. This being the case, Leonardo’s allusion is significant evidence in favor of Pandolfino’s authorship (compare No. 1454, line 3).
**7.** The works of Lactantius were published frequently in Italy during Leonardo’s lifetime. The first edition, published in 1465 at the monastery of Subiaco, was also the first book printed in Italy.
**15.** *Sansovino, Andrea* — the sculptor; 1460—1529.
**25.** Leonardo here probably refers to the map, not created by him (See p. 224), which is in the collection of his manuscripts at Windsor and was published in *Archaeologia* Vol. XI (see p. 224).

11. 13. *Brera*, now the Palace of Sciences and Arts. Until 1571 it was the monastery of the order of the Umiliati and afterwards of the Jesuits. *De ponderibus*, compare No. 1436, 3.
12. *San Lorenzo*. A church in Milan, see pp. 39, 40 and 50.
13. 24. *Groups*. See Vol. I p. 355, No. 600, note 9.
16. The Portinari were one of the great merchant families of Florence.
23. *Fondamento* is commonly used by Leonardo to mean a ground plan. See for instance p. 53.
25. *Meteorology*. By this Leonardo undoubtedly means the four books of the *Meteorologica*. He must refer here to a manuscript translation, as no Italian translation is known to have been published (see No. 1477 note).
26. *Vitolone* see No. 1506, note. *Library of Pavia*. One of the most famous Italian libraries. After the victory of Novara in April 1500, Louis XII had it transported to France as a trophy of victory!

**1450.** 1—12 R. 4. Niccolo. 5. Zasti. 7. Heir of. 11. Niccolo.

**1453.** Marcantonio Colonna.

---

**1450. I.** *Giuliano Gondi.* Ser Piero da Vinci, Leonardo’s father, lived until 1480 in a house belonging to Giuliano Gondi. In 1498, this was demolished to make room for the fine Palace built on the Piazza San Firenze by Giuliano di San Gallo, which still exists. In the *Summary of the Tax Assessment of Ser Piero da Vinci*, 1480, Leonardo is not mentioned; it is evident, therefore, that he was living elsewhere. It may be noted incidentally that in the *Tax Assessment of Giuliano Gondi* of the same year, the following mention is made of his four eldest sons:

*My son Lionardo, age 29, does nothing; Giovambatista, age 28, is in Constantinople; Billichozo, age 24, is in Naples; Simone, age 23, is in Hungary.*

He himself was a merchant of gold filigree (*we operate a silk craft shop . . . we do a little business in Naples*). As he was 59 years old in 1480, he certainly would not have been alive at the time of Leonardo’s death. But Leonardo must have been on intimate terms with the family until the end of his life, for in a letter dated June 1, 1519, in which Francesco Melzi, writing from Amboise, announces Leonardo’s death to Giuliano da Vinci in Florence (see p. 284), he says at the end, "Give me your reply through the Gondi family" (see UZIELLI, *Researches*, *throughout*).

Most of the other names on the list belong to well-known Florentine families.

**1452.** See No. 844, note, p. 130.

**1453.** In July 1506, Pope Julius II gave Donna Lucrezia della Rovere, the daughter of his sister Lucchina, in marriage to the youthful Marcantonio Colonna, who, like his brothers Prospero and Fabrizio, became one of the most famous Captains of his family. The Pope gave him Frascati and presented him with the palace he had built, while a Cardinal, near the church of Santi Apostoli, which is now known as the Palazzo Colonna (see GREGOROVIUS, *History of the City of Rome*, Vol. VIII, book XIV I, 3; and COPPI, *Colonna Memoirs*, p. 251).

**1454.** Much of No. 1444 is repeated in this memorandum.

2. Vasari states that Leonardo invented mechanical birds that moved through the air. Compare No. 703.

**1456.** This fragmentary note is written on the margin of a drawing of two legs.

**1457.** This note occurs on a pen and ink drawing made by Leonardo as a sketch for the celebrated large cartoon in the possession of the Royal Academy of Arts, in London. This cartoon is commonly supposed to be identical with the one described and praised by Vasari, which was exhibited in Florence at the time and which now seems to be lost. Mr. Alfred Marks, of Long Ditton, in his valuable paper (read before the Royal Society of Literature, June 28, 1882) “On the St. Anne of Leonardo da Vinci,” has provided proof that the cartoon now in the Royal Academy was executed earlier in Milan. The note given here, which is written on the sheet containing the study for the said cartoon, clearly has no reference to the drawing on which it is written but is obviously from the same date. Although I have no opportunity here to discuss this question of the cartoon, it seemed important to me to point out that the style of the writing in this note does not confirm the opinion previously held that the Royal Academy cartoon was the one described by Vasari, but, on the contrary, supports the hypothesis put forward by Mr. Marks.

**1458.** *St. Mary Magdalen’s day.* July 22.

6. *Marco*, probably Leonardo’s pupil Marco d’Oggionno; 1470 is supposed to be the date of his birth and 1540 the date of his death.

*Who was living with me.* We may infer from this that he left the master shortly after this, his term of study perhaps having expired.

**1465. 2. *Giovan;*** it is not likely that Leonardo would have called Giovan' Antonio Beltraffio "Giovanni" in this note and "Antonio" at another time, as in No. 1464, while in No. 1458, line 16, we find *Giovan' Antonio*, and in No. 1436, line 6, *Beltraffio*. Possibly the Giovanni mentioned here is Leonardo's less famous pupil Giovan Pietrino (see No. 1467, 5).

**2. 3. *Francesco de' Melzi*** is often mentioned; see No. 1350.

**3. *Salai.*** See the note for No. 1519.

**4. *Lorenzo.*** See No. 1351, line 10 (p. 408). Amoretti provides the following note in *Mem. Stor. XXIII: 1505. Tuesday evening, the 14th of April. Lorenzo came to stay with me; he said he was 17 years old . . on the 15th of the said April, I received 25 gold scudi from the treasurer of Santa Maria Nuova.* He asserts this is derived from a manuscript marked S, in quarto. This manuscript seems to have vanished without a trace; Amoretti himself had not seen it, but copied from a selection of extracts made by Oltrocchi before the Leonardo manuscripts were taken to Paris by the first French Republic. According to this, Lorenzo must have been born in 1487. The sculptor Lorenzetto was born in 1490. Amoretti was led by the passage above to make the following absurd observations:
> *This Lorenzo, who was his constant companion afterward, at least while he remained in Italy—could he be Lorenzo Lotto of Bergamo? We know this talented painter was one of Vinci's excellent pupils (?).*

**Il Fanfoia,** perhaps a nickname. Cesare da Sesto, Leonardo's pupil, seems to have been in Rome during these years, as we learn from a drawing of his in the Louvre.

**1466.** This appears to be an account for two assistants. The name of the second is barely legible. The year is not given. Nevertheless, the note is of chronological value. The first line tells us the date the note was recorded, January 3rd, and the...

### 1469.

1468. 2. preface. 4. Arrigo.
1469. 1-25 R. 1. arithmetic. 2. philosophers. 3. bible. 4. letters. 5. decade. 6. decade. — blind man of Ascoli. 7. decade. — the Great.

---

The observations that follow refer to events of the previous month 'of last year' (*dell' anno passato*). Leonardo cannot, therefore, have written this in Florence, where the year was, at that period, calculated as beginning in the month of March (see Vol. I, No. 4, note 2). He must then have been in Milan. What is more important is that we thus learn how to date the beginning of the year in all the notes written at Milan. This clears up Uzielli's doubts: *In Milan, the year was made to begin from the Incarnation, that is, March 25th, and from the Nativity, that is, December 25th. It seems probable to us that Leonardo would have chosen the style that was in use in Florence.* (Ricerche, p. 84, note.)

1467. 5. See No. 1465, 2.

1469. The late Marquis Girolamo d'Adda published a highly valuable and interesting dissertation on this passage under the title: *Leonardo da Vinci and his Library, notes of a bibliophile (Milan 1873. Edition of only 75 copies; privately printed).* In the autumn of 1880, the Marquis d'Adda showed me a considerable mass of additional notes prepared for a second edition. This, as he then intended, was to be released after the publication of this work of mine. After the much-regretted death of the elder Marquis, his son, the Marquis Gioachino d'Adda, was so generous as to place these manuscript materials at my disposal for the present work, through the kind intervention of Signor Gustavo Frizzoni. The following passages, with the initials G. d'A., are printed from the valuable notes in that publication; the manuscript additions I have marked with an asterisk (*). I did not, however, think myself justified in reproducing here the acute and interesting observations on the contents of most of the rare books listed here.

1. "*The noble work of arithmetic in which all matters pertaining to commerce are treated, composed and compiled by Piero Borgi of Venice*", quarto. At the end: "*In the illustrious city of Venice on the 2nd of August, 1484, the present work was completed.*" Signatures a—p, in quires. There is, however, another similar work by Filippo Calandro, 1491. Regarding the latter, one should consult Federici: Memorie Trevigiane, Fiore di virtù: page 73. "*A small book composed in a beautiful style around 1320 and printed several times in the 15th century (later reprinted even more recently).* The members of the Accademia della Crusca include it in the series of classic linguistic texts. See Gamba, Razzolini, Panzer, Brunet, Lechi, etc. (G. D'A.)

2. "*Natural History by C. Pliny the Second, translated from the Latin language into Florentine by Cristoforo Landino & the work of Nicolas Jenson, French printer, in the year of salvation 1476. Venice*" folio. — Diogenes Laertius. Begins: "*The book of the lives of philosophers, etc.: Printed in Venice by Bernardino Celerio of Lovere, 1480*", quarto (G. D'A.).

3. "*The illustrated vernacular Bible (by Nicolò di Mallermi) Venice . . . . August 1, 1471 (by Wendelin of Speyer)*" 2 volumes folio, in 2 columns of 50 lines; or another edition of the same Mallermi version, Venice 1471, and also: "*Venice by Gabriel de Piero 1477*", folio; 2 volumes; Ottavio Scotto of Monza 1481," "*Venice 1487 by Joan Rosso of Vercelli*", "*1490 Giovanni Ragazzo of Monteferrato at the request of Lucantonio di Giunta, etc.*" — Theophrastus's Lapidary? Mandeville: "*The Great Lapidary*", Italian manuscript version?... It cannot be Georgius Agricola, because he was born in 1494; perhaps Albertus Magnus: *On Minerals.* * It could be a translation of the Latin poem (Book of Stones or on Gems) by Marbodius of Rennes (died 1123), translated by himself into French from the Greek of Evax, King of Arabia, a famous physician who had composed it for the Emperor Tiberius. Marbodius wrote his first for Philip Augustus, King of France. There are also prose translations. "*The Lapidary, or the power and virtue of precious stones, herbs, and animals.*" (G. D'A.)

4. Vegetius?... Frontinus?... Cornazzano?... We believe it is more likely Valturius. This book must have been one of Leonardo's favorites, as it was a book of science and art at the same time.

<note>The following notes are presented in two columns in the original text.</note>

The printed editions are as follows: The first: “*Robertus Valturius on military matters, 12 books dedicated to Sigismondo Pandolfo Malatesta . . . Johannes, a native of Verona: son of Nicolaus the surgeon and physician: Master of the art of printing: was the first to print this most elegant book on military matters in his own homeland with letters and illustrated figures. In the year 1472.*” folio, without page numbering.

The second edition is from Bologna, 1483, reprinted in Paris in 1532, and then again in 1533. Paolo Ramusio translated it into Italian and published it again in Verona with the type of Paganino, also in folio, 1483 (the smaller format prints), and Louis Meigret translated it into the French language in 1555 in Paris.

“*Milan by Leon. Pachel & Ulric. Scinzenzeler 1484*” in quarto.

**5—7.** We have various versions of the *Decades* of Titus Livy, printed in the 15th century. The “*Titus Livy translated into the vernacular (by Ruggiero Ferario) Rome, Ulrich Han in 1476,*” one tome in 3 volumes, folio. “*Bologna by master Antonio of Bologna 1478,*” small folio. “*Venice, Octaviano Scoto 1481,*” small folio. Venice, Bartholomeo de Alexandria & Andrea de Asula 1485. Bartholomeo de Zanis, 1490, etc.” See the Manual by Brunet or, better yet, the Repertorium by Hain and the work by Panzer. (G. D’A.)

**5.** “*Arnaldus de Villa Nova & Johannes de Mediolano, Salernitan Rule of Health, 1480,*” in quarto, or “*A most useful treatise regarding the preservation of health, etc. composed by the most illustrious and excellent philosopher & doctor of medicine Master Ugo Benzo of Siena, etc.*”, in quarto, Gothic type without page numbers and without the printer's name. At the end: “*This work was completed in Milan by the care and diligence of Pietro de Corneno of Milan, 1481, on the day before the Kalends of June. During the reign of Giovanni Galeazzo Sforza Visconti, our most invincible prince.*” See Sassi-Argelati. Part I, vol. I, p. 575. (G. D’A.)

**6.** *L’Acerba* (from acervus, a heap), the well-known poem by Francesco Stabili, the astrologer and enemy of Alighieri. Numerous editions from the 15th and 16th centuries. It is a true encyclopedia in verse, filled with very bold ideas that cost the unfortunate thinker his life at the stake in 1347. In this poem are found outlined the origins of many modern discoveries, and in particular the circulation of the blood, two centuries before Michael Servetus. Of the first edition of Brescia, Ferrandus, undated, in folio, only one copy is known to exist in the Spencer Library. See Dibdin. (G. D’A.)

**7.** “*Here begins the book called 'Of Life' etc., composed by Albertus Magnus, most excellent philosopher, etc. Naples, Bernardino de Gerardinis of Amelia, 1478,*” in quarto. Another edition from “*Bologna by Bazalino di Bazaliero, 1493,*” in quarto, Gothic type. (G. D’A.)

Bernardino de Gerardinis of Amelia, 1478,” in quarto. Another edition from “Bologna by Bazalino di Bazaliero, 1493,” in quarto, Gothic type. (G. D’A.)

**8.** Perhaps “Guido dalle Colonne,” also called “of Chauliac.” “*The Surgery of Guy de Chauliac. Prescriptions of Turra de Castello and the baths of Porretta, etc. Venice, by the order and at the expense of Octaviano Scoto, by the care and skill of Boneto Locatelli, 1498*” in folio, Gothic type: a very rare treatise on surgery. It had several French translations and, in the 15th century, also an Italian version without place, name, or date, and another in the Castilian language in 1498. See: Brunet, Panzer, Hain, and Mendez. — L. Guil. de Saona, New Rhetoric, St. Albans, in quarto, 1480 (Laurentius Guilelmus). It is among the rarest of books (Brunet, Volume V, column 137). St. Albans, Albani Villa, Verulamium, an English town in the county of Hertfordshire, the birthplace of Francis Bacon. (G. D’A.)

**9.** *On Agriculture*. “*The book of agriculture by Pietro Crescentio,*” the first edition of this Italian version written in the 14th century and a linguistic text cited by the Accademia della Crusca. “*Florence by me, Nicolaus Laurentii, a German of the diocese of Breslau, in the year 1478,*” in folio.— *Cibaldone*: The subjects treated in this most singular little book are: fruits, herbs, bloodletting, medicine, lust, bathing, etc. Almansor-ebn-Isahck was governor of Khorasan, and Rhazes dedicated his twelve books of medicine to his name, which in summary contain the entire medical system of the Arabs. This last edition in quarto, Brescia by Don Baptista de Farfengo, consists of six unnumbered leaves, semi-Gothic characters in 2 columns. (G. D’A.)

**10.** *Quadriregio* (the book called) by Federigo Frezzi, a Dominican. It is a religious-moral-scientific poem in terza rima. Among the imitators of the Divine Comedy, he is one of the best, “not unworthy of following behind Dante,” says Quadrio. This poem is today unjustly neglected and almost unknown, even though in times far from our own it was nobly printed several times. It had at least seven editions from 1481 to 1515 and contains beauties of the first order. *The edition that was most likely among Leonardo's books we believe to be the one from Milan, 1488, by Zarotto.* A copy is in the Ambrosian Library among the 15th-century books donated by G. Porro; — perhaps rarer than many others.

“*Illustrated Fables of Aesop,*” in quarto, illustrated, without note of time or place; or the edition of Venice by Manfredo da Monferrato, in quarto, illustrated, 1481 and 1490; or also: “*Brescia by Boninum de Boninis 1487,*” in quarto with 67 beautiful woodcut figures; “Rome, Silber 1483,” Venice, Manfredo Bo-

In the Cicognara Catalog, we have a detailed description of this extremely rare volume. (G. D'A.)

11. *"Donatus in Latin & Italian: Printed in Venice at the expense of Johannis Baptistae de Sessa in the year 1499, in quarto."* — *"The Psalter of David in the vernacular language (by Malermi, Venice in 1476,"* in folio, without notes. (G. D'A.)

12. Compare No. 1210, 48.—The version by Girolamo Squarzafico: *"The book of Justinus diligently placed into the mother tongue. Venice at the expense of Johanne de Colonia & Johanne Gheretzen...1477,"* in folio. — *"Marsilio Ficino, Platonic Theology, or on the immortality of souls, Florence, by Ant. Miscominum 1482,"* in folio, or perhaps some Italian version of this same book in manuscript. (G. D'A.)

13. Perhaps *"The Trojan History of Guido,"* or the *"handbook"* of *"Guido da Monterocherii,"* but more probably *"Guido d'Arezzo,"* whose book: *"Micrologus, or the discipline of the art of music"* Leonardo could have owned in manuscript; copies of these exist in many libraries, and it was later printed in 1784 by Gerbert.

There are many editions of the sonnets of Burchiello the Florentine, printed in the 15th century. The first and most rare and sought after: *"Here begin the sonnets, etc. (by Christoforo Arnaldo)"*, in quarto without page numbers, catchwords, or signatures, from 1475, and perhaps even from 1472, according to Morelli and Dibdin, etc. (G. D'A.)

14. An Italian version of the *"Doctrinal de Sapience"* by Guy de Roy, and perhaps even the original in the French language.—

By Luigi Pulci, although in the edition: *"Florence 1479"* in quarto it says: *"The Driadeo composed in octave rhyme by Lucio Pulcro,"* Other editions of the 15th century, *"Florence, Miscomini 1481, in quarto, Florence, at St. Jacob de Ripoli, 1483,"* in quarto and *"Antoni de Francesco, 1487,"* in quarto and *"Francesco di Jacopo 1489,"* in quarto and still others from Venice and without any notes, etc. (G. D'A.)

15. One of the editions of the Morgante printed in the 15th century, etc.—

Which of the works of Francesco Petrarch it might be would be difficult to guess, but probably the Canzoniere. (G. D'A.)

16. These are the travels of the knight "Mandeville," an English gentleman. He wrote his book in the French language. It was printed repeatedly in the 15th century in French, English, Italian, and German; they count perhaps more than 27 editions from the 15th century, of which we know of 8 in French, four in Latin, six in German, and many others in the vernacular. (G. D'A.)

17. Platina (Bartolomeo Sacchi) the Italian version *"of honest pleasure, & health (& of food) Venice (without printer's name) 1487,"* small quarto in Gothic type. (G. D'A.)—Compare No. 844, 21.

18. *The Manganello:* An excessively lively satire against women in imitation of Juvenal's Sixth Satire. *Manganello* is not only the title of the little book, but also the name of the author who was a "Milanese." Of this extremely rare little book, which seems to have been printed in Venice by Zoppino (Nicolò d'Aristotile, called Zoppino), without a date but from the very first years of the 16th century, and perhaps older, as we shall see later, only two copies are known to exist in Europe between public and private libraries. (G. D'A.)

19. *"Chronicle of Desidero"*, it seems one should read instead *"Chronicle of Isidoro"*; and in this case it would mean the *"Chronicle of Isidoro"* so much in vogue at that time *"The Chronicle of Saint Isidoro the Younger begins, with some additions taken from the text & histories of the Bible & from the book of Paulus Orosius . . . . Printed in Ascoli in the house of the Reverend Master Pascale . . . . by the hand of Guglielmo de Linis of Germany 1477"* in quarto of 157 leaves. It is the first book printed in Ascoli and the first edition of this chronicle, which is today quite rare. No less rare is the edition from Cividale del Friuli, 1480, and also that of Aquila, 1482, also in quarto. See Panzer, Hain, Brunet, and P. Deschamps. (G. D'A.)

20. *"The epistles of Ovid translated into prose. Naples, Sixt. Riessinger"*, in quarto, or: *"Epistles in the vernacular 1489,"* in quarto in two columns *"printed in the city of Brescia by Priest Baptista de Farfengo,"* (in octaves) or: *"The book of the Epistles of Ovid in vernacular rhyme by Master Dominico de Monticelli of Tuscany. Brescia, Farfengo,"* in Gothic quarto (in vernacular rhyme), 1491, and also the version by Luca Pulci. *Florence, Mischomini, 1481,* in quarto. (G. D'A.)

21. See 1. 4.

22. "Johannes de Sacrobosco," or "Goro Dati," or "Tolosano da Colle," of which there were multiple editions in the 15th century. (G. D'A.)

<header>MISCELLANEOUS NOTES. [1474—1476]</header>

<margin>L. 94b]</margin>

### 1474.

The Archimedes belonging to the Bishop of Padua.

---

<margin>W. 191a]</margin>

### 1475.

Archimedes gave the quadrature of a polygonal figure, but not of the circle. Therefore, Archimedes never squared any figure with curved sides. That is, he squared the circle minus a portion so minimal that the intellect can barely imagine it—namely, a portion as small as a visible point.

---

<margin>Br. M. 279b]</margin>

### 1476.

If anyone had discovered the ultimate power of the cannon in all its varieties and presented such a secret to the Romans, how swiftly they would have conquered every land and overcome every army; and what reward could have possibly equaled such a great benefit! Even though Archimedes had greatly damaged the Romans during the siege of Syracuse, he was never denied the offer of immense rewards from those same Romans. During the capture of Syracuse, Archimedes was diligently sought after and was found dead; there was no greater lamentation made in the Senate and among the Roman people than if they had lost their entire army. They did not fail to honor him with a burial and a statue, a project headed by Marcus Marcellus. After the second ruin of Syracuse, the burial place of Archimedes was rediscovered by Cato among the ruins of a temple; whereupon Cato had the temple rebuilt and the tomb most honorably restored... and regarding this, it is written that Cato said he took pride in nothing so much as in having honored Archimedes with that monument.

1488—1491.] NOTES ON BOOKS AND AUTHORS.

S. K. M. III. 93 a]

### 1488.

Master Stefano Caponi, a physician, lives at the pool; he has Euclid’s *On Weights*.

> Master Stefano Caponi, a physician, lives at the pool, and has Euclid *De Ponderibus*.

K.² 2 a]

### 1489.

Fifth Book of Euclid. First definition: a part is a quantity of a magnitude smaller than the greater magnitude, provided that the smaller measures the greater;

> 5th Book of Euclid. First definition: a part is a quantity of less magnitude than the greater magnitude when the less is contained a certain number of times in the greater.

A part properly so called is that which is multiplicative—that is, which, when multiplied by any number, reconstructs its whole exactly;

> A part properly speaking is that which may be multiplied, that is when, being multiplied by a certain number, it forms exactly the whole. A common aggregate part.....

A common aggregate part is that which, however many times it is taken, results in more or less than its whole, whereby it is necessary that, with the help of another different quantity, it remakes its whole, and for that reason it is called aggregate.

Second definition. Multiplicity is greater than the smaller when the smaller measures it;

> Second definition. A greater magnitude is said to be a multiple of a less, when the greater is measured by the less.

Above we defined the lesser extreme, and here the greater is defined; the part

> By the first we define the lesser [magnitude] and by the second the greater is defined. A part is spoken

K.² 2 b]

### 1490.

is spoken of in relation to the whole, and within these two extremes lies their entire relationship, and they are called multiples.

> of in relation to the whole; and all their relations lie between these two extremes, and are called multiples.

S. K. M. III. 16 b]

### 1491.

Hippocrates says that the origin of our seed derives from the brain, and from the lungs and the testicles of our parents, where the final decoction is made; and all the other members provide their substance to this seed through perspiration, because no path is evident by which they might reach that seed.

> Hippocrates says that the origin of men’s sperm derives from the brain, and from the lungs and testicles of our parents, where the final decocture is made, and all the other limbs transmit their substance to this sperm by means of expiration, because there are no channels through which they might come to the sperm.

MISCELLANEOUS NOTES. [1492—1495.

### 1492.

Lucretius in the third book of *De Rerum Natura*. Hands, nails, and teeth were the weapons of ancient man (165).
They also use as a standard a bunch of grass tied to a pole (167).

---

### 1493.

**1492.** *Lucretius, De Rerum Natura libri VI* was first printed around 1473, at Verona in 1486, at Brescia in 1495, at Venice in 1500 and 1515, and at Florence in 1515. The numbers 165 and 167 noted by Leonardo at the end of the two passages seem to indicate page numbers, but if so, none of the editions just mentioned can be the one intended here, nor do these numbers refer to the verses in the poems of Lucretius.

**1493.** *Ammiani Marcellini historiarum libri qui extant XIII*, published at Rome in 1474.

**1494.** “*Mundini anatomia. Mundinus, Anothomia (sic). Mundini praestantissimorum doctorum almi studii ticiensis (sic) cura diligentissime emendata. Printed at Pavia by master Antonius de Carcano 1478,*” in-folio; reprinted: “*Bologna, Johan. de Noerdlingen, 1482,*” in-folio; “Padua by Matthæum Cerdonis de Vuindischgretz, 1484,” in-4to; “Leipzig, 1493,” in-4to; “Venice, 1494,” in-4to and there “1498,” with illustrations. These illustrations, however, are not, as has been claimed, the first to be introduced into a treatise on anatomy. In the *Fasciculus Medicinae* of Johannes de Ketham, which reproduces the *Anatomia* of Mondino, also printed in Venice by J. and G. de Gregoriis, 1491, in-folio, there are woodcuts (said to be designed, though not engraved, by Andrea Mantegna) of large size, which were reproduced several times in the following years. This 1491 edition of the *Fasciculus* is among our books and could very well be the volume on anatomy noted by Leonardo. (G. D’A.)

**1495.** Lines 3—5 are written in the margin next to the title line of the text provided, complete as No. 19.

### 1496.
The heirs of Maestro Giovanni Ghiringallo have the works of Pelacano.

### 1497.
The catapult, as Nonius and Pliny say, is an instrument invented by those, etc.

---

### 1498.
I have found in the Histories of the Spaniards how, in the wars they had with the English, there was Archimedes of Syracuse, who at that time lived in the company of Ecliderides, King of the Cirodastri. In maritime combat, he ordered that the ships should have long masts, and upon their tops he placed a small yard-arm forty feet long and one-third of a foot thick; at one end was a small anchor, and at the other a counterweight. Attached to the anchor were twelve feet of chain, and after that chain as much rope as would reach from the chain to the base of the top, which was fastened with a small cord; from that base it was sent down to the base of the mast, where a very strong winch was placed, and there the end of that rope was fixed. But to return to the function of this machine, I say that beneath the said anchor was a fire, which with a very great noise threw down its sparks and a rain of flaming pitch; these, raining down upon the top, forced the men who were there to abandon the said top. Then, the anchor having been lowered with its sharp hooks, it caught the edges of the top; and immediately the cord placed at the base of the top to support the rope that went from the anchor to the winch was cut, and pulling the ship...

---

**1496.** 1. Maestro Giovanni. 2. Ghiringallo have. 3. Pelacano.
**1498.** 1. how... wars they had. 2. The following words are written on the margin: which was fastened with a small cord. 11. base... base of the mast. 12. where a [mast] winch. 13. base. 14. with very great. 15. noise... its sparks and rain. 16. to the top forced. 17. abandon... top... lowered the anchor with the sharp hooks (?). 18. top and immediately. 19. MISCELLANEOUS NOTES. [1499—1503.

### 1499.

Theophrastus on the ebb and flow of the tide, on eddies, and on water.

---

### 1500.

Tryphon of Alexandria, who lived in Apollonia, a city of Albania (163).

---

### 1501.

Messer Vincenzio Aliprando, who lives near the Inn of the Bear, has Giacomo Andrea’s Vitruvius.

---

### 1502.

Vitruvius says that small models are of no use for determining the effects of large ones; I intend to demonstrate below that this conclusion is false. I will do so primarily by citing the very same arguments he uses to reach that opinion—namely, the experiment with the auger. He shows that if a man’s strength can drill a hole of a certain diameter, a hole of double that diameter will not be made by doubling that man’s strength, but will require much more. To this, one can very well respond by pointing out that an auger

---

### 1503.

of double the size cannot be moved by double the strength, because the surface area of any body of a similar shape is four times greater when the dimensions are doubled, as shown in the two figures *a* and *n*.

---

1499. The Greek philosophers had no opportunity to study the phenomenon of the ebb and flow of the tide, and none of them wrote about it. The movement of the waters in the Euripus, however, was a puzzling problem to a few of them.

1500. Tryphon of Alexandria, a Greek Grammarian of the time of Augustus. His treatise *On the Defects of Words* appeared first at Milan in 1476, in Constantin Laskaris’s Greek Grammar.

--- Page 603 ---
453
NOTES ON BOOKS AND AUTHORS.
1504—1507.]

### 1504.

#### ON SQUARING THE CIRCLE, AND WHO IT WAS THAT FIRST DISCOVERED IT BY ACCIDENT.

Vitruvius, measuring miles by means of the repeated revolutions of the wheels that move vehicles, extended over many stadia the circumferential lines of the circles of these wheels. He learned of them from the animals that moved the vehicles. But he did not recognize that this was the means of finding a square equal to a circle, which was first discovered by Archimedes of Syracuse; for the multiplication of the radius of a circle by half of its circumference produced a rectilinear quadrilateral equal to the circle.

### 1505.

Virgil says that the shield was blank and without glory, because among the Athenians true glory, confirmed by testimonies...

### 1506.

### 1507.

Witelo, at Saint Mark’s.

1505. 1. without glory. 2. Athenians glory confirmed by testimonies from... 3. connected, crossed, and joined by softening(?).

1504. *Vitruvius*, see also Nos. 1113 and 343.
10. Compare No. 1475.

1505. The end of the text cannot be deciphered.

1506. (*Witelo, Vitellion, Vitellon*) Witelo. Regarding this thirteenth-century author on optics and perspective, one should consult Luca Pacioli, Paolo Lomazzo, Leonardo da Vinci, etc., and among modern authors, Graesse, Libri, Brunet, and the Memoirs published by Prince Boncompagni, as well as "On the spelling of the name and the homeland of Witelo (Vitellion), a note by Maximilian Curtze, professor at Thorn," where the many existing manuscripts in the libraries of Europe are described. Bernardino Baldi, in his "Lives of Mathematicians," a manuscript held by Prince Boncompagni, has a biography of Witelo. This writing by Baldi bears the date August 25, 1588. He was later discussed by Friedrich Risner and Johannes Regiomontanus in the preface to Alfraganus, and by Jean Borrel and Girolamo Cardano in "On Subtlety," which notes Witelo's errors. According to Baldi, he lived around the year 1269, but according to Reinhold, he flourished in 1299, having dedicated his work to a friar, William of Moerbeke, who lived at that time.

Regarding a manuscript of Witelo's optics cited by Luca Pacioli, there is a second copy by Curtze, with additions by Prince Boncompagni and illustrations by Cavalier Enrico Narducci. In the "Catalogue of manuscripts possessed by Don Baldassare of the Boncompagni princes," compiled by the same Narducci (Rome, 1862), under No. 358, we find cited: Witelo, "Perspective," a fourteenth-century manuscript. The "Perspective of Witelo," a Thuringian-Pole, is cited twice by Paolo Lomazzo in the "Treatise on the Art of Painting." Witelo or Vitello or Witelo. His book was printed in folio at Nuremberg in 1535; the second edition is from 1551, also from Nuremberg, and a third from Basel, 1572. (See Historical Investigations... on the Visconti-Sforza Library of the Castle of Pavia... edited by G. D’A., Milan 1879. Part I. Appendix p. 113. 114).

1507. Another manuscript of this "Perspective" by Witelo is found noted in the "Bibliographical Canon of Nicholas V," preserved at the Magliabechiana, in a copy of the original likely sent by Parentucelli to Cosimo de' Medici (Magliab. cod. sign. I VII, 30, pages 193 to 198). It comes from the Convent of Saint Mark and was transcribed by the Florentine friar Leonardo Scuberti of the Order of Preachers, who was also the librarian of the public Medici library in Saint Mark’s (See Historical Investigations... edited by G. D’A. Part I, p. 97).

**1519.** This note, from about the year 1494, is the earliest mention of Salai, and the last is from the year 1513 (see No. 1465, 3). From the various notes in the manuscripts, he seems to have been Leonardo's assistant and servant only, and hardly a painter himself. At any rate, no signed or otherwise authenticated picture by him is known to exist. Vasari speaks somewhat doubtfully on this point.

**1520.** This note allows us to determine the date of the manuscript in which it is found. In 1495, the 4th of September fell on a Friday; the contents of the manuscript do not allow us to assign it to a much earlier or later date (Compare No. 1522, and Note).

**1522.** See Nos. 1384 and 1517.

### 1546.

<margin>Ash. I. 18b]</margin>
<margin>Notes by unknown persons among the MSS. (1546–1565).</margin>
By divine mercy, cardinal of the title of [n] of the holy Roman Church, commonly called, to the venerable religious brother Johannes Mair of Nusdorf, of the Order of Preachers of the German province, of the Vienna convent, our resident chaplain, eternal greeting in the Lord. Zeal for religion, duly and [unwaveringly?] practiced, honesty, and the merits of other praiseworthy integrity and virtues, for which you are commended to us by a trustworthy testimony—namely, that of the Master of the Order of happy memory, Leonardo de Mansuetis of Perugia, with his seal . . . . giving to you for . . . . works of virtue a resident (?) place and time; whose successor likewise in the office of administrator, who confirmed the gift (?) of his predecessor and gave it anew, and many others [praised] who laud your works, induce us to grant you a liberal favor; hence it is that we, desiring.

### 1547.

<margin>W. XII b]</margin>
| | |
|:---|:---|
| Johannes Antonius di Johannes Ambrosius de Bolate; ²He who loses time and does not acquire virtue; ³the more the mind thinks, the more it becomes saddened; ⁴Wealth has no power over virtue; he who abandons honor to acquire wealth; ⁵Fortune is of no value to one who does not exert himself; ⁶He makes himself happy who follows Christ; ⁷a perfect gift is not obtained without great pain; ⁸Our triumphs and our pomps pass away; ⁹gluttony, sleep, and idle luxury have banished every virtue from the world, ¹⁰so that it is almost lost from its course; our nature is conquered by habit; ¹¹Now it is necessary that you shake off your sloth; The master said that by sitting on down, ¹²one does not come to fame, nor under blankets; without which, he who consumes his life ¹³leaves such a trace of himself on earth as smoke in the air or foam in the water. | Johannes Antonius of Johannes Ambrosius de Bolate. He who lets time pass and does not grow in virtue, the more I think of it the more I grieve. No man has it in him to be virtuous who will give up honor for gain. Good fortune is valueless to him who knows not toil. The man becomes happy who follows Christ. There is no perfect gift without great suffering. Our glories and our triumphs pass away. Foul lust, and dreams, and luxury, and sloth have banished every virtue from the world; so that our Nature, wandering and perplexed, has almost lost the old and better track. Henceforth it would be well to rouse yourself from sleep. The master said that lying on down will not bring you to Fame; nor staying beneath the quilts. He who, without Fame, burns his life to waste, leaves no more vestige of himself on earth than wind-blown smoke, or the foam upon the sea. |

---

<note>1546–1566. All these texts are written in the ordinary way from left to right.</note>
<note>1547. 1. Ambrosius. 3. thinks . . is saddened. 4. leaves honor . . acquire wealth. 5. exerts himself. 6. he . . Christ. 7. perfect gift. 8. pass. 9. and idle . . of the. 10. course . . lost . . habit. 11. it is necessary thus that you shake off sloth . . master who sitting. 12. one comes nor under blankets. 12. who his . . consumes. 13. traces . . leaves . . or in the water the foam.</note>

**1546.** The meaning of this document, which is very difficult to decipher and is written in unintelligible Latin, is that Leonardo di Mansuetis recommends the Rev. Mair of Nusdorf, chaplain at Vienna, to some third person; and says also that something, which had to be proved, has been proved. The rest of the passages on the same leaf are undoubtedly in Leonardo’s hand. (Nos. 483, 661, 519, 578, 392, 582, 887 and 894.)

**1547.** From the last sentence we may infer that this text is by the hand of a pupil of Leonardo’s.—On the same sheet are the notes Nos. 1175 and 715 in Leonardo’s own handwriting.

<margin>C. A. 164 $b$; 490 $b$]</margin>
### 1560.

How well learned art corresponds to Nature! Vinci would have given it a soul, as he granted it everything else. He did not wish for it to be more similar; it is already a second self: the loving Moor possesses her soul.

---

**1557.** The notes in Greek, Nos. 1557, 1558, and 1562, are closely connected with one another, but the meaning of some words is very uncertain, making a translation impossible.

**1559.** AMORETTI, in *Mem. Stor. XXIV*, cites the first three lines of this letter as being by Leonardo. However, the style of the handwriting does not support this theory, and the contents support it even less. I would consider it instead a rough draft of a letter by the young Melzi. I have not succeeded in completely deciphering the 13 lines of this text. Amoretti reads the beginning as *Canonica di Vaprio*, but *Vaprio* seems to me a very doubtful reading.

**1560.** These three epigrams on the portrait of Lucrezia Crivelli—a painting by Leonardo that must have been lost very early on—seem to have been dedicated to Leonardo by the poet. Leonardo used the back of the sheet for notes on geometry.

1561. 1. information. 2. of the horses to(?). 3. of the equerries (?). 5. painter (?) of the King P. 6. Amboise Amboise. 7. Amboise Amboise.

---

1561. **1.** *The book of Master Egidius on the pulses, composed regarding the womb (with the commentary of Gentilis de Fulgineo)* published in 1484 at Padua, in 1494 and in 1514 at Venice, and in 1505 at Lyons.
**2.** This text appears to be in a handwriting different from that in the note in line 1. Here the reading is not as simple as Amoretti presented it in *Mem. Stor. XXV: To Monsieur Lyonard, Painter to the King, for Amboise.* He also says that this address is from the year 1509, and Mr. Ravaisson remarks: *"From this inscription it seems one can infer that Leonardo was then in France, at the court of Louis XII . . . To conclude, I believe it is not proven that Leonardo da Vinci did not make a trip of a few months to France under Louis XII, between the spring of 1509 and the autumn of 1510."*—I must confess that I myself have not succeeded in completely deciphering this French writing, of which two words remain doubtful to me. But this much seems quite evident: this is not the address of a letter at all, but a certificate or note. I believe *Amboise* [line 6] is the signature of Charles d'Amboise, the Governor of Milan. If this explanation is correct, it can be easily explained by the contents of Nos. 1350 and 1529. The note in line 1 was perhaps added later by another hand; and Leonardo himself afterwards wrote some geometrical explanations on the same sheet. I must also point out that the statement that this sheet belongs to the year 1509 has absolutely no foundation in fact. There is no clue whatever for assigning a precise date to this note.

--- Page 618 ---
# 1565.

16th Century, Book 6 of *The City of God*, concerning the Antipodes.

---

# 1566.

**Leonardo's Will.**
Be it known to all persons, present and future, that at the court of our Lord the King at Amboise, there appeared personally before us Messer Leonardo da Vinci, painter to the King, currently residing at the place called Clos Lucé near Amboise. Considering the certainty of death and the uncertainty of its hour, he has acknowledged and declared in the said court before us—to which he has submitted and now submits—that he has made and ordained, according to the tenor of this document, his testament and the declaration of his last will as follows. First, he commends his soul to our Lord God, to the Glorious Virgin Mary, to Saint Michael, and to all the blessed Angels and male and female Saints of Paradise.

Item. The said Testator desires to be buried within the church of Saint Florentin at Amboise, and that his body be carried there by the chaplains of that church.

Item. That his body be accompanied from the said residence to the said church of Saint Florentin by the college of the said church—namely, by the Rector and the Prior, or by their Vicars and the chaplains of the church of Saint Denis of Amboise, as well as the Friars Minor of the said place. And before his body is carried into the said church, this Testator desires that three high masses be celebrated in the said church of Saint Florentin with deacon and sub-deacon; and on the day these three high masses are said, thirty low masses of Saint Gregory shall also be said.

Item. In the said church of Saint Denis, a similar service shall be celebrated as described above.

Item. In the church of the said friars and religious minors, a similar service shall be held.

Item. The aforementioned Testator gives and grants to Messer Francesco da Melzo, nobleman of Milan, in remuneration for the services and favors done to him in the past...

--- Page 619 ---
and all of the books the Testator currently possesses, along with the instruments and portraits related to his art and profession as a painter.

Item. The same Testator gives and bequeaths forever to Battista de Vilanis, his servant, one half—that is, a moiety—of his garden located outside the walls of Milan, and the other half of the same garden to his servant Salai. In this garden, the aforementioned Salai has built a house, which shall likewise remain forever the property of the said Salai, his heirs, and successors; this is in recognition of the good and kind services that the said de Vilanis and Salai, his servants, have rendered him from the past until now.

Item. The said Testator gives to his waiting woman, Maturina, a cloak of high-quality black cloth lined with fur, a cloth gown, and two ducats to be paid only once; this is likewise in recognition of the good services rendered to him by the said Maturina until now.

Item. He desires that sixty torches be carried at his funeral by sixty poor men, who shall be given money for carrying them at the discretion of the said Melzo; these torches shall be distributed among the four aforementioned churches.

Item. The said Testator gives to each of the aforementioned churches ten pounds of wax in the form of thick candles, which shall be placed in the said churches to be used on the day those services are celebrated.

Item. He directs that alms be given to the poor of the Hôtel-Dieu and to the poor of Saint Lazare d'Amboise; for this purpose, the sum of seventy Tours shillings shall be given and paid to the treasurers of that fraternity.

Item. The said Testator gives and bequeaths to the said Messer Francesco Melzo, who is present and accepting, the remainder of his pension and the total sum of money owed to him from the past until the day of his death by the receiver or treasurer-general, M. Johan Sapin, as well as each and every sum of money he has already received from the aforementioned Sapin regarding his said pension, provided he dies before the said Melzo and not otherwise; these funds are currently in the Testator's possession at the said place of Cloux, as he states. He likewise gives and bequeaths to the said Melzo all of the clothes he currently possesses at the said place of Cloux, both in recognition of the good and

--- Page 620 ---
remuneration for the good and kind services done by him from the past until now, as well as in payment for the time and labor he may incur regarding the execution of this present will, all of which, however, shall be at the expense of the said Testator.

And he orders and desires that the sum of four hundred scudi del sole, which he has on deposit in the hands of the treasurer of Santa Maria Nuova in the city of Florence, be given to his biological brothers living in Florence, along with all the interest and profit that may be due up to the present time from the aforementioned treasurers to the aforementioned Testator on account of the said four hundred crowns, since the day they were given and consigned by the Testator to the said treasurers.

Item. The said Testator desires and orders that the said Messer Francesco de Melzo shall be and remain the sole and exclusive executor of the will of the aforementioned Testator, and that this said testament shall take its full and complete effect. Regarding everything narrated and stated here, to have, hold, keep, and observe, the said Messer Leonardo da Vinci, as the established Testator, has bound and binds by these presents his heirs and successors with all his goods, movable and immovable, present and future, and has expressly renounced and renounces by these presents each and every thing to the contrary. Given at the said place of Cloux in the presence of Magister Spirito Fleri, Vicar of the church of Saint Denis at Amboise, M. Guglielmo Croysant, priest and chaplain, Magister Cipriane Fulchin, Brother Francesco de Corton, and Francesco da Milano, a member of the Convent of the Minorites at Amboise, witnesses summoned and called for this purpose by the judgment of the said Court, in the presence of the aforementioned M. Francesco de Melzo, who, accepting and consenting, has promised by his faith and his oath, administered personally into our hands, never to act, speak, or go against this. And it is sealed at his request with the royal seal established for legal contracts at Amboise, and as a sign of truth.

Given on the 23rd day of April, 1518, before Easter.

And on the 23rd day of the same month of April, 1518, in the presence of M. Guglielmo Borian, Royal notary in the court of the Bailiwick of Amboise, the aforementioned M. Leonardo da Vinci gave and granted by his testament and ordinance of last will mentioned above, to the said M. Baptista de Vilanis, who was present and accepting, the water right which

--- Page 621 ---
The late King Louis XII, of pious memory, previously gave to the same da Vinci the stream of the canal of Santo Cristoforo in the Duchy of Milan, to be enjoyed by the said de Vilanis forever, in such a way and manner as the said Lord made the gift in the presence of M. Francesco da Melzo, a gentleman of Milan, and myself.

And on the aforementioned day in the said month of April in the said year 1518, the same M. Leonardo da Vinci, by his last will and testament, gave to the aforementioned M. Baptista de Vilanis—who was present and accepting—each and every piece of furniture and household utensil currently at the said place of Cloux, provided that the said de Vilanis survives the aforementioned M. Leonardo da Vinci, in the presence of the said M. Francesco Melzo and me, the Notary, etc. Borean.

# APPENDIX

## I.

### THE HISTORY OF THE MANUSCRIPTS.

1. Leonardo, by his will, expressly bequeathed all his manuscripts and drawings to his younger friend Francesco Melzi, who took them back to Milan. Four years after Leonardo's death, Alberto Bendedeo wrote from Milan to Alfonso d'Este, Duke of Ferrara: "And because I have mentioned the house of Melzi, I inform Your Excellency that a brother of the man who competed in the joust was a student of Leonardo da Vinci, and his heir, and possesses many of his secrets and all of his opinions . . . I believe he has those small books of Leonardo's on anatomy, and many other beautiful things." See G. Campori, *Nuovi Documenti*.

When Vasari visited Milan—probably in 1566—he saw Leonardo's manuscripts in Francesco Melzi's possession and wrote as follows: "Leonardo . . . wrote in an awkward script, with letters formed by the left hand in reverse; and anyone who is not practiced in reading them cannot understand them, because they can only be read with a mirror. A large part of these papers on human anatomy are in the hands of Messer Francesco Melzi, a Milanese gentleman, who in Leonardo's time was a very beautiful youth and much loved by him, just as today he is a handsome and noble old man; he treasures these papers and keeps them like relics, together with the portrait of Leonardo of happy memory. To anyone who reads those writings, it seems impossible that that divine spirit could have reasoned so well about art, muscles, nerves, and veins, and with such diligence in every detail. There are also in the hands of . . . ., a Milanese painter, some writings by Leonardo, also in characters written with the left hand in reverse, which treat of painting and the methods of drawing and coloring. Not long ago, this man came to Florence to see me, desiring to print this work, and he took it to Rome to find a publisher; I do not know what happened after that." (Ed. Sansoni, IV. 37).

In another place, Vasari mentions that he himself possessed some drawings by Leonardo.

Lomazzo, the Milanese painter, writes in 1590 (*Idea del Tempio della pittura*, 2nd Ed., p. 15):
"But above all these writers, Leonardo Vinci is worthy of memory; he taught the anatomy of human bodies and of horses, which I have seen in the possession of Francesco Melzi, divinely drawn by his own hand. He also demonstrated in figures all the proportions of the members of the human body; he wrote on the perspective of lights, on the method of drawing figures larger than life, and many other books . . . But of so many things, none are found in print; they exist only in his own hand, and a large portion of them have come into the hands of Pompeo Leoni, sculptor to the Catholic King of Spain, who obtained them from the son of Francesco Melzi; and some of these books have also come into the hands of Signor Guido Mazenta, a most virtuous doctor, who holds them very dear."

2. In the short anonymous biography of Leonardo which, as it seems to me, must have been written somewhat earlier than Vasari's *Lives* (published by Milanesi, *Arch. Stor. Ital.* XVI), the manuscripts are mentioned in these terms: "(Leonardo) returned to Milan and afterwards went to France in the service of King Francis, where he took many of his drawings; some of these he also left in Florence in the Hospital of Santa Maria Nuova with other mat-"

--- Page 631 ---
<page-num>481</page-num>
<header>THE HISTORY OF THE MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

*...yielded to the prayers of the Duke of Savoy and other princes who requested it of him: he has refused more than six hundred ducats for it.*

**3.** The manuscripts in the possession of the Mazenta brothers had, it seems, been gradually reduced to three. Guido Mazenta, whose name can be seen in the manuscript he gave to Cardinal Borromeo (see Bibliography No. 2), died in 1613.

In 1636, Count Galeazzo Arconati—who is mentioned in Mazenta’s report—presented twelve manuscript volumes by Leonardo to the Ambrosian Library at Milan. The formal deed of gift may be seen, translated into French, in the *Cabinet de l’Amateur*, 1861, pages 53–59. In the catalogue of these manuscripts, the binding is described more specifically than the contents. The following twelve manuscripts were included in this gift: 1. the Codex Atlanticus (Bibliography 38); 2. a manuscript now lost, but described as follows:
*The second is an ordinary folio book, the size of standard cut paper. It is bound in wood covered with red leather, decorated with borders and gold flowers. The volume is composed entirely of vellum leaves and begins with these words, written in red:* TABLE OF THE PRESENT WORK. *Eight unnumbered leaves follow. The numbering begins on the next leaf, which has an ornament at the top that says:* Most Excellent Prince, *etc., and the pagination continues to the one hundred and twentieth leaf: eighty-seven for the text, three blank, and the rest consisting of various colored drawings, the first of which is titled:* Solid Sphere, *and the last:* Hollow Hexagonal Side-Pyramid; *at the bottom of the leaf is a Greek text expressing the same thing.*

**3.** *The third is a quarto book, bound in vellum, on the spine of which one reads the following words:* BY LEONARDO DA VINCI; *it consists of exactly one hundred leaves, but the first is missing; on the second there are some black cherries, leaves, and colored fruits. Inside the volume, at leaf 49, five leaves of various drawings have been inserted, mostly polearms, and at the end another small volume (volumetto) of various mathematical figures and birds, consisting of eighteen leaves, which has been sewn into the same vellum binding.*

Bibliography 3, 4. Ashburnham II and B: The appendix (*volumetto*) is now lost; the last mention of it occurs in Venturi’s *Essai* (1796). Compare No. 1465, Note 4.

**4.** Manuscript of 114 leaves (see Bibliography 5, 6, Ashburnham I and A).

**5.** *The fifth is another similar book, quarto, covered like the previous one, consisting of fifty-four leaves. On the first are drawn various comical heads, and on the last are four columns of text, written in reverse. It is marked on the spine* LEONARDO DA VINCI.

This description corresponds with the manuscript, Bibliography 28 Tr. 6.

6. Manuscript; see Bibliography 25, E.
7. Manuscript; see Bibliography 22, F.
8. Manuscript; see Bibliography 26, G.
9. Three manuscripts bound in one volume; see Bibliography 8–10, H^1, H^2, H^3.
10. Two small manuscripts bound in one; see Bibliography 13, 14.
11. Manuscript; see Bibliography 18, L.
12. Manuscript; see Bibliography 27, M.

In 1674, Count Orazio Archinti presented a manuscript by Leonardo to the same library, consisting of three small notebooks in one volume; Bibliography 32–34.

In 1790, Stefano Bonsignori made a short catalogue of the manuscripts in the Ambrosian Library at Milan. It includes: 1. MS. C. A, see Bibliography 28; 2. MS. B and Ashburnham II, see Bibliography 3, 4; 3. MS. Ashburnham I and A, Bibliography 5, 6; 4. MS. D, Bibliography 31; 5. MS. E, Bibliography 25; 7. MS. G, Bibliography 26; 8. MSS. H^1, H^2, H^3, Bibliography 8–10.

The descriptions of the others are too vague and brief to allow us to identify any existing manuscripts by them: 6. *Miscellany; hydrostatics, etc. It is a small octavo, in rough cardboard.* 9. *Miscellany. Motion, machines, small machines for boring crystals, etc. It is a sixteenmo, bound in parchment.* 10. *Miscellany in sixteenmo, in rough cardboard.* 11. *Miscellany. Rough sketches, motion, etc. It is a sixteenmo, parchment* (see Dozio, *On the Writings... of Leonardo da Vinci*, Milan 1871, pages 21–24). It will be noted that one fewer manuscript is named here than in the deed of gift from Count Arconati; on the other hand, a Manuscript D, not previously mentioned, is now included. The fifth manuscript of Arconati's list is evidently missing from this list. The volume given to the Ambrosian Library by Cardinal Borromeo in 1603 (Bibliography 2, C) also seems to have been omitted. It is evident, then, that we cannot determine exactly how many of these manuscripts were to be found in the Ambrosian Library in the year 1796. At the suggestion of Bonaparte, the Directory of the French Republic transported many works of art from Italy to France. This much, at any rate, is certain: in August 1796, the Codex Atlanticus was in the Bibliothèque Nationale; and "Twelve small manuscripts by Leonardo da Vinci, on the sciences" were in the Institut National (Institut de France). The authors of the catalogue of the paintings and manuscripts removed from the Ambrosian Library—Peignon, *commissaire de guerre*, and Citizen Tinet, *agent des Arts* (dated Milan, May 24, 1796)—either do not mention Leonardo’s manuscripts at all, or—which is more probable—include them under the following somewhat vague designation: "The Portfolio of the works of Leonardo da Vinci." It is certain, on the other hand, that in 1815 the commissary of the

VOL. II.

### 8. H.<sup>3</sup>

*The three small notebooks H<sup>3</sup>, H<sup>2</sup>, and H<sup>1</sup> are bound in one volume. From the soiled condition of the sheets at the beginning and end of each section, it is evident that Leonardo had used them separately. The cover is made of parchment and is marked "H" twice on the outside and once on the inside, and "Q" on the back of the first sheet. Inside the back cover is the mark Q<sup>a</sup>, and on the penultimate sheet is N N 48, likely indicating the number of sheets originally belonging to H.<sup>3</sup> Manuscripts H<sup>2</sup> and H<sup>3</sup> are numbered throughout. The sheets of H<sup>3</sup> are also numbered 1—47 below the text in a reversed position. The numbers provided here are located above the text.* | 47<sup>a</sup> A | 47<sup>b</sup> 1389 | 48<sup>a</sup> Ma | 48<sup>b</sup> "tell me if ever," sketch of a man's head in profile | 49<sup>a</sup>, 49<sup>b</sup> Mn | 50<sup>a</sup> 670 | 50<sup>b</sup>, 51<sup>a</sup> notes | 51<sup>b</sup> 689 | 52<sup>a</sup> 999 | 52<sup>b</sup> 736 | 53<sup>a</sup> 1264, 690 | 53<sup>b</sup>—55<sup>a</sup> Mn | 55<sup>b</sup>

### 20. S. K. M. I.¹

On the first sheet is a note, written in German:* **"Leonardo da Vinci, the greatest painter of the Italian school, born in 1452 at Vinci, entered the service of Duke Valentin Borgia in 1502 as a military engineer, and died in 1519."** — *This volume and the two others now in the Forster Library of the South Kensington Museum, London, were given to Mr. Forster by Lord Lytton, who is said to have purchased them in Vienna for a small sum. The title of the treatise on sheet 1ᵃ is provided in* **1374**, *Note; no other subject is discussed on the 38 sheets that make up this manuscript.* 1ᵇ 1374 I wish to reduce the thickness of a board to a given thickness without changing its width | I ask how much it increases in its length, etc. | 4ᵃ, 6ᵃ, 8ᵇ, 11ᵃ, 12ᵃ, 16ᵇ are blank. — *On the last sheet 39ᵃ is the mark 46.*

->28. Tr.<-

*Marked S inside the cover and on the first sheet. At the beginning of the volume is the following note:*
> January 5, 1783. This small codex by Leonardo da Vinci belonged to Mr. Don Gaetano Caccia, a nobleman from Novara but residing in Milan, who died on January 9, 1752, in the parish of San Damianino La Scala. I, Carlo Trivulzio, purchased it from the said nobleman around the year 1750, together with a gold quinarius of Giulio Maporiano and a few other items I no longer remember, giving him in exchange a silver repeating watch that I had bought used two years earlier for sixteen gigliati; in truth, it was of excellent quality, so this small codex effectively cost me six or seven gigliati.

## 29. Leic.

*Bound volume in a leather cover. On the first five sheets before the beginning of the original MS are the following Notes. On 1^a marked in pencil 596: This treatise on the nature, weight, and motion of water... has never been printed. On the reverse of the modern title may be found an extract from the life of Leonardo da Vinci by Dufresne, in which this volume is particularly mentioned. It appears from the title page (although the name of the owner has been erased) that it belonged to Giuseppe Ghezzi, a prominent painter in Rome.* — W. Roscoe. 1^b + | 2^a + | 3^a Original Book || On the Nature, weight, and motion of Water, || Composed, written, and illustrated in his own || Left-handed handwriting || By the Distinguished Painter and Geometer || Leonardo da Vinci || During the time of Ludovico il Moro, while he was || conducting the waters of the Martesana Canal || from the Adda to Milan. || This is authenticated by the specific mention made of it by Raffaelle du Fresne in the Life of the said Leonardo, described in his book printed in Paris by Giacomo Longlois in the year 1651, entitled || Treatise || On Painting || Acquired "by the great power of gold" (these words cover an erasure), to enhance || the laborious collections of his study || by || Giuseppe Ghezzi, Painter in Rome || 3^b (*in another handwriting*) Da Vinci used to write left-handed, according to the Hebrew custom, in which manner those sixteen volumes we have already mentioned were written; and since the handwriting was good, it could be read quite easily using a large mirror. It is probable that he did this so that everyone might not read his writings so easily. The project of the Martesana canal gave him the opportunity to write a book on the nature, weight, and motion of water, full of a great number of drawings of various wheels and machines for mills, to regulate the course of the waters and to raise them upward. | 4 + | 4^a (*in an earlier handwriting*) Book written by Leonardo da Vinci which treats of the sun, the moon, the course of water, bridges, and motions | 4^b + | *The organization of this MS is somewhat unusual. At the top of many pages there are title lines, placed here between single quotation marks, giving the numbers of "cases" (casi) or subjects treated on the page. Most of these cases are introduced by "Come" (How, or that).* 1^a 864 | 1^b 1082, 901 | 2^a 902, Pl. CVIII, No. 5 | 2^b How to empty a pond that flows into the sea, Ph on the moon | 3^a A, 985 | 3^b A | 4^a 300, 1060 | 4^b A | 5^a 957, 971, 919, 907, moon | 5^b A | 6^a 2nd book on the diversity of water waves | 6^b 958, A, 977 | 7^a on the water of the moon, Ar | 7^b A, O | 8^a A, 386, Pl. ### 30. Mz.

*The grey cardboard cover is original. The sheets are numbered twice: once in Leonardo's handwriting and once by a more recent hand. The original numbers are provided here in brackets because they are not consecutive; they were subsequently altered:* o' **728** | 1a (3) **1154, P** | 1b **Ge P** | 2a (4) **P** | 2b **V** | 3a (6) **1122, V** | 3b **V** | 4a (6?) , 4b **V** | 5a (8 altered to 7) | 5b **V** | 6a (9 altered to 8) | 6b, 7a (10 altered to 9) | 7b **V** | 8a (12 altered to 11) **1168, V, Ge** | 8b, 9a (13 altered to 12) **V** | 9b **1124** | 10a (14 altered to 13) **705** | 10b, 11a (15 altered to 14) | 11b **V** | 12a (16 altered to 15) **1123** | 12b **V** | 13a (17 altered to 16) **V, 1125, 381** | 13b baga, **V** | o" **1428, and the architectural drawing reproduced in Vol. II, p. 67.**

### 31. D.

*Marked D on both the inside and outside of the grey cardboard cover, and S inside the back cover. Four blank sheets are at the beginning. This manuscript discusses the eye. The following texts are a selection of the headings.* 1a Why nature did not create equal power and capacity in the visual faculty | 1b Why the rays of luminous bodies appear so much larger the further they are from their source | 2a Whether the image or likeness has a fixed location upon the eye or not . . . how the straight path of the converging images bends upon entering the eye | 2b How the images of any body that pass through a small opening to the eye are imprinted upside down on its pupil, yet the sense perceives them upright | 3a How right-hand objects do not appear on the right to the visual faculty unless their images pass through two intersections | 3b How images are necessarily presented to the visual faculty with two intersections | 4a Why a mirror swaps the right and left sides of the images of objects | 4b That it is true that every part of the pupil possesses visual power | 5a On the eyes of nocturnal animals | 5b The pupil of the eye changes into as many different sizes as there are variations in the brightness or darkness of the objects presented before it | 6a The image of the sun is unique in the entire sphere of water that sees and is seen by the sun, but it appears divided into as many parts as there are eyes of animals that see the surface of the water from different locations | 6b The pupil of the eye possesses visual power throughout its entirety and in every one of its parts | 7a How the pupil receives the images of things placed before the eye only from light and not from the object itself | 7b Why a right-hand object does not appear on the left in the eye | 8a **71** | 8b A demonstration of why the eye sees things placed in the lateral spaces behind itself | 9a On the human eye | 9b Why luminous bodies show their boundaries full of straight luminous rays | 10a On the images of objects that pass through narrow openings into a dark place | 10b On the images of objects diffused through the air. — *At the end are four blank sheets, two of which bear the mark S.*

### 32. K.¹

*The three manuscripts K¹, K², and K³ are bound in one volume with a leather cover inscribed LEONARDI || VINCI || in gold letters. The sheets of each manuscript are numbered separately. Inside the cover are the marks K and 13. On the first sheet is the inscription:*

> Autograph commentaries || of Leonardo da Vinci || the most famous || Painter and Architect || which were given as a gift || to the Ambrosiana Library || by Count Horatio Archinto || a most devoted student || of the Liberal Arts || In the year 1674 ||

*Then follow four blank sheets.* 1a A, and the mark 44 | 1b Ma | 2a **1067** | 2b Ge | 3a Divide the treatise on birds into 4 books, etc. | 3b—14a **V** | 14b *sketch of a male figure* | 15a **+** | 15b, 31a Ge *but in black chalk* | 31b—48a in ink | 47b has the mark O O 47 | 48b **+** |

### 38. C. A.

*This best known and most voluminous volume is composed of loose sheets of various sizes, each folio containing one or more sheets of original manuscript. The mounting is the same as in Volume W. L. Those sheets that have notes on both sides are not fixed by their back to the folio sheets, but are set into a paper frame. The numbering of sheets refers only to the folios. For the sake of identification and to make it easier to compare the writing on the opposite sides of the same sheet, I have introduced here, in addition to the numbers of Leoni’s folio sheets, second numbers which refer to the separate original sheets.—In the following description, I thought it desirable to avoid giving detailed accounts of the contents of sheets that do not relate to the various subjects of the present publication, especially since the order of the sheets, being entirely accidental, sheds no light at all on the connection between the various stu-*
